[p. vii]

## Vorwort — Foreword

In presenting to the public a work long cherished, much pondered in the mind, and thought through again and again, I cannot refrain from prefacing it with a few words about the great diversity of human ways of thinking in relation to the task here at hand. Whoever has looked about with any attentiveness in life, whoever has observed the infinitely manifold directions in which the human spirit moves when it is a matter of demanding or receiving some more definite account of divine things, and thus also of the soul, will first be struck by the fact that, where one might expect a deep sorrow over the mystery hovering here, indeed a burning longing for the resolution of questions that have the truly and profoundly human as their subject, must dwell everywhere in humanity, one finds on the contrary, and cannot conceal from oneself, that in the far greater mass of human

[p. viii] natures there prevails a high degree of indolence, not to say indifference, precisely in these regards. Of souls in whom, already at an early stage in the development of their spirit, the inborn striving for self-knowledge allows no rest, of souls who, driven ceaselessly by inner longing, seek that sustenance which Dante already called "the bread of angels" — of these there has only ever been a small and counted number.

And indeed in our own days a peculiar artificiality of life has accumulated around the human being! — and just as one who swims in a raging surf and must strain all his senses merely to reach the saving shore, in that moment can think of nothing else at all, since all representations that were otherwise the most important to him are now involuntarily pushed far back — so now a multitude of people find themselves so pressed and held fast in the roaring bustle of industrial, commercial, statistical, economic, and political interests, that any quiet gazing inward, any deeper reflection upon what must ultimately remain the most important questions for the soul, becomes for them almost an impossibility. No less great, on the other side, is the number of those in whom the constant struggle with the tormenting want of life, in whom the lack of all spiritual cultivation and nourishment, no longer allows the urge of that higher longing and the desire for the resolution of that higher task to arise in any way. It is therefore really only a smaller number in whom the need, the urge, the seeking of the spirit for understanding of itself constitutes an altogether essential stirring of life, ceaselessly and continually, and it is to these that the message of these pages is addressed, always hoping that from that presently unreceptive mass, individuals will again emerge in whom the need lying everywhere at the foundation reawakens and drives them toward this sustenance, or rather into the depths of their own being. — But even the souls that remain, with their full longing and their persistent search for self-understanding, follow in these endeavours the most varied paths and byways. Were I to characterise the byways in a few words, I would already here point to a distinction that in all the following considerations of the soul must be emphasised as the most significant and consequential — that is, the distinction between the unconscious and conscious impulses of our soul. On the one hand, we must regard it as a byway when the attempt is made to unveil completely, by the decisive force of the conscious working of the spirit, the domain of the soul that in the end always remains in a certain respect mysterious and mystical, and to demonstrate it in all its radiations, so that, as it were, the mysterious and unconscious element of the soul as such is to be entirely abolished and no longer tolerated; on the other hand, however, we consider no less misguided and lost the direction that will not grant to the clear, fully conscious consideration of the revelations of the soul its absolutely due right, and would seek in feeling and the presentiment of something utterly incomprehensible alone the sufficient vocation of the researcher. The latter is the byway of those called mystics, of whom it suffices to have named Jakob Boehme, who, despite a truly deep and genuine feeling, especially of the soul's relation to God, yet lacks everything that a higher scientific — that is, the ultimately alone satisfying — cognition may demand. Into the first byway many of our more recent psychologists of the schools of Hegel and Herbart have fallen. — According to my conviction, one cannot treat of the soul in the right sense without taking up this unconscious, and thus also to the separating, dividing understanding unknown, element as something indefinable, as it were a given x, into the reckoning of concepts, and precisely for this reason no one will be able to deny that the dialogues of a Plato, which always rest upon this unconscious and mysterious<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> [p. ix] [p. x] Only in this way will the contemplation of the highest and the divine attain that simplicity and naturalness which Lichtenberg once divined when he wrote the remarkable words: "Should it then be so entirely certain that our reason can know nothing of the supersensible? Should not the human being be able to weave his ideas of God just as purposefully as the spider weaves its web for catching flies?"

[p. xi] foundation and continually refer back to it, penetrate far more deeply into the mysteries of the actual life of the soul than, say, the compendium of psychology of a Herbart.

It is, however, by no means intended here that these byways are trodden only by those who, as teachers and writers, pursue the mystery of the life of the soul; on the contrary, we find that this diversity of erring direction also runs through those who, more or less frequently, in quiet communion with themselves, consider, or at least think about, the concerns of the soul. In these minds too the representations waver manifoldly, now entirely toward the side of dark feelings, preparing that which, where it becomes dominant, is termed fanaticism, or they crystallise prematurely into certain solid, homespun concepts, according to which, for instance, the inner being of the human is thought of as a kind of clockwork, in which the soul counts merely as one of the artificially inserted wheels. — Whoever wishes to inquire among people in this regard will sometimes hear remarkable notions, and one will not infrequently be able to perceive views here which, in relation to the soul, differ very little from the opinions of peoples who represent to themselves the highest mystery of God as some kind of fetish.

Yet just as among the learned and the teachers there has always been a certain number who, carefully pursuing the conscious and honouring the unconscious, have striven earnestly and beautifully after the mystery of the soul, so too among the unlearned multitude there are always souls to be found who, in this spirit, with earnestness and love, turn again and again in their own way to these high things, however often the everyday of life has had to divert them from these thoughts. To all those, then, who love to think in this direction, may the following pages be especially commended. They will find here the closely compressed fruit of many years of study; they will observe that the author was, in the highest sense, in earnest about an earnest task; and they must acknowledge that he has endeavoured to set down, in the simplest manner, always in the most genetic sequence possible, and free from the fetters of scholastic methods, as the faithful result of much-considered intuition, as if in a sanctuary, that which had ripened in his contemplation during the purest hours. May what is thus given be received in the same spirit, and be of pure consequence for the inner life of many!

[p. xii] [p. 1] The key to the cognition of the essence of conscious soul-life lies in the region of the unconscious. All difficulty, indeed all apparent impossibility, of a true understanding of the mystery of the soul becomes clear from this vantage point. Were it an absolute impossibility to find the unconscious in the conscious, the human being would have to despair of attaining cognition of his soul, that is, of arriving at true self-knowledge. If this impossibility is only an apparent one, then it is the first task of the science of the soul to set forth in what manner the spirit of the human being is able to descend into these depths.

Before any such attempt is made, however, it must be considered to what extent the unconscious life of the soul may rightly be called the basis of the conscious.

That by far the greatest part of the realm of our soul-life rests continually in unconsciousness, the first glance into inner life can teach us. At any given time, while we are really conscious of only a few representations, we possess thousands of representations that are entirely withdrawn from consciousness, that are not known at this moment and yet are there, and consequently show that the greater part of the life of the soul falls into the night of the unconscious.

[p. 2] Later, when the remarkable cycle of the life of representations comes up for discussion — a cycle I first introduced into the teachings of psychology in the third volume of my System of Physiology — we shall recognise that in this regard the life of the soul may be compared to a great stream flowing ceaselessly onward, which is illuminated by sunlight, that is, by consciousness, at only a single small point. Already by the fact, then, that the greater part of the thoughts of our consciousness always sinks back into the unconscious and can only from time to time and individually re-enter consciousness, the unconscious life of the soul is characterised as the basis of the conscious. But the relation goes far deeper still. All life of the soul, the entire world of our innermost spiritual existence, which we very well distinguish in our consciousness from everything external, rests upon the unconscious and forms itself only out of it. The human being, as an individual, lives his life in three quite essentially different conditions:

1. as an egg perceivable only microscopically, consisting of concentric envelopes, 2. as something germinating within the egg, that is, an embryo, and 3. as a human being proper.

The first is the entirely bound (latent) existence of a germ, given to the healthy maternal organism from birth onward in its most hidden depths, still so inseparable from the maternal life, so devoid of all perceptible changes of life, remaining always only equal to itself through several decades, and thus naturally also without the slightest trace of higher psychic manifestations. Thereupon, awakened by peculiar vital influences of a masculine soul standing in opposition to the maternal one, the second period of life of the child-to-be begins in the mother's womb. According to the most remarkable inner laws of its own innermost unconscious being, the originally simple differentiates itself into oppositions, and the infinite richness of human formation shapes and enlarges itself, yet this formation is still very, indeed exceedingly, different from that of the mature human being.

[p. 3] An embryo germinating within the egg, surrounded by envelopes, all its organs destined for later interaction with the world still turned inward toward the interior of the envelopes — thus it presents itself in this period of life, and nothing is here, either, that would betray in this egg-creature any dawning of consciousness. In our matured soul there is therefore nothing that could in and of itself be called a memory of that life, a reminiscence from our second period of life. All existence there is accordingly an unconscious yet, according to wondrous laws, ceaselessly forming, shaping life of great wisdom.

With birth the third and properly human period of life begins, and as a wide outer world enters into manifold interaction with the organism, there gradually dawns in the dark, hitherto unconscious regions of life — that is, of the soul — a faint distinction between one's own being and an alien being, and little by little, with periodically ever-recurring sinking back into unconscious life, as the journey of life approaches, the peculiar world of the self-conscious, feeling, willing, and knowing soul develops out of that earlier unconscious condition.

This, in the most fleeting outline, is the essence of our development as human being, as soul. Only if we hold fast strictly to the cognition of this, only if we take care not to think anything arbitrary into it, only if we look faithfully and persistently within ourselves and arrive at what I might call a spiritual reconstruction of our existence — from the conscious being back into the unconscious — may we hope to find that which I called at the outset the key to the cognition of conscious soul-life, namely the understanding of the unconscious through consciousness. This path of contemplation is difficult but not impossible. We proceed here in the spiritual as we proceed in the corporeal, when we study and come to know the organic development that we ourselves live through and yet do not know, from observation of another; and this path we shall therefore pursue throughout the present investigation.

[p. 4] The older researchers, from Aristotle onward, though still as good as entirely uninformed about the organic processes of unconscious human articulation and development, nevertheless followed this path far more faithfully than the moderns. The soul always appears to them first as a formative, shaping, nourishing essence of the living, in which cognition develops only later. Aristotle says very beautifully: "the soul is the first actuality of a natural articulated body" 1.

Error and discord entered these teachings only when one separated the soul from life, indeed sought to introduce the most abstruse concepts about life itself. When one considers that at the end of the seventeenth century physiological views such as those of a Fr. Hoffmann, who regarded plants as lifeless because they possessed no heart, found adherents, one also understands how it was possible to dispute whether animals had a soul and at what time the soul actually entered the child from without — indeed, that one dared to posit a deus ex machina [god from the machine], the so-called vital force, from whose absolute authority the organism was supposed to arise and sustain itself, while the soul was to be regarded merely as a<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> On the Soul, Book 2, Chapter 1.

[p. 5] stranger inserted only later into this housing. It was evidently in part a misunderstood theological tendency which, in dark minds, made it appear a profanation that the same thing which feels and thinks in us should also direct our formation and nourishment — a tendency which saw in this connection at the same time the ground of an impossibility of maintaining the immortality of the soul. Therefore something other than the soul had to be active in developing the organs of our body; therefore the question could arise whether animals, at least the more imperfect ones, possessed a soul or not; and of a plant-soul, since one hardly wanted it acknowledged that plants had life, there was now no question at all. A prejudice of this kind must, however, seem all the more astonishing, since the strictest theologians of earlier times — I recall only Thomas Aquinas<sup>1</sup> — had no hesitation in attributing to the soul, just as in one direction so simultaneously along nourishing and

<sup>1</sup> He distinguishes in the soul potentiae vegetativae, sensitivae, intellectivae, appetitivae, motivae [vegetative, sensitive, intellectual, appetitive, and motive powers].

the last, cognition, so in another direction the first, formation — since by them all the less could a contradiction against a doctrine of immortality be perceived herein, as they divined in advance that through what we call death, in a soul that also co-determines organic formation, only a transformation of the life of the soul could be posited — namely an abolition of a certain direction of that life — but by no means a complete extinction of its fundamental idea.

Meanwhile, as I said, it was also only in part this reason that prevented the recognition of the soul as the first actuality of a natural articulated body; on the other hand, it was the dividing wall between the unconscious and the conscious by which one was induced to sever from the domain of the soul whatever lay entirely outside of consciousness. Thus, because we have no consciousness of how we originate, how we grow, how we internally nourish and transform ourselves, but do have consciousness of everything in us that is called sense-sensation, representation, and stirring of the will, only the latter processes alone could truly belong to the soul, while everything else remained inexplicable from the standpoint of the soul, and therefore was to fall to a special agency, a peculiar unknown potency — a so-called vital force.

[p. 6] Consciousness, clear thinking, or at least the capacity to have distinct sense-representations and to be determined by them, was so infallibly regarded in silence as a credential of the soul that one would not accept as soul anything to which this capacity could not be attributed.

But what was the consequence when one surrendered to this illusion and posited a special vital force for the formative and nourishing processes of the organism, and a special soul for feeling, cognition, and will? One introduced two different souls, or two essentially different fundamental forces, into the organism, which yet proved itself as a unity beyond dispute in origin, life-direction, and self-feeling. For vital force or formative drive, however one may wish to name this something, will always and in every manifestation have to be not something moved from without but something moving itself from out of itself, and will precisely thereby have to be called something partaking of the divine, since it is everywhere proper to the divine not to be moved but to move itself and other things from out of itself.<sup>1</sup> A

<sup>1</sup> Here the beautiful passage from Plato (Phaedrus, p. 245): "Every soul is immortal. For that which is ever in motion is immortal; but that which moves another and is moved by another, having a cessation of movement, has also a cessation of life. Only that which moves itself, since it never abandons itself, never ceases to be in motion; nay rather, to all else that is moved, this is the source and beginning of movement. But the beginning is ungenerated."

[p. 7] different thing, however, was also essentially not to be said of the soul itself, and it is easy to see that one must drive oneself about in an eternal circle when one seeks marks by which to arrive at lasting, tenable distinctions between vital force and soul in this regard. The vital force, however subtly or however massively one may wish to conceive it, will always remain something moving itself from out of itself, something individual, something driven only by the breath of the divine — in a word, a kind of soul; the soul, however much one may in thought separate it from the palpable organism, and distinguish it from what one calls organic life, will always show itself in our consciousness most closely linked with all the forms of our life; and indeed some physiologists have therefore already arrived at the very natural assumption that the soul is in the end only a heightened vital force appearing in its highest efficacy — against which nothing would need to be said, so long as one only connects the right concept with this word.

It is, however, in order to make comprehensible and sharply demonstrate the unity and not the duality of the essence underlying our entire existence, that something else must also be attended to. For when one adduces as proof of the difference between the soul and that which determines the processes of formative and nourishing life, that nothing of the latter can reach consciousness, one does not consider that while much indeed goes on in the organism that does not as such really and immediately reach consciousness, yet nothing goes on in it that does not at least indirectly exert an influence upon consciousness. Even the entirely unconsciously carried out processes by which the organism grows and develops in the embryo — do they not all bear a relation to consciousness, in that they create the organs that shall in the future receive, preserve, and modify representations? This applies still more to the processes that we shall henceforth call the partially unconscious ones, such as blood circulation, growth, secretion, and so forth, in the human being who has attained consciousness; for is it not they that indirectly influence consciousness here as well, and do not some of them also here become directly represented to consciousness in pathological conditions? — In this respect, too, it is therefore vain to search for a firm dividing wall between soul and vital force. As impossible as it would be in general that, if these two energies were something truly and in every way different, the sensations of sense-life should benefit the soul and the soul itself should be able to act upon the body, so certain is it that everything points us to the conclusion that only a single principle of the living, only something moving itself from out of itself —

[p. 8] an entelechy with Aristotle, or an idea according to Plato, or a psyche, a soul, in a word a divine something, call it what one will — can be the fundamental condition of every manifestation of life and therefore also of every formation.

In this connection it has often been pointed out that the words for this secret principle of all life so commonly coincide with the designations for breath and exhalation — as anima, spiritus, pneuma, and the like — and it has been supposed that this figurative designation was derived merely from breathing, as one of the most enduring and unceasing manifestations of life; I should, however, venture the conjecture that the choice of this designation has yet another sense, and that far more than of the breath and the breathing of the body, the reference is symbolically to the breath of the divine which creates the body. Already the very remarkable expression of Genesis — "and He breathed into his nostrils the breath of life; and so the human being became a living soul" — evidently has this sense, just as "inspiring" and "being inspired" can only be referred to this. Then there connects itself very beautifully to this way of thinking the thought that only that living being from which this divine breath sounds forth perceptibly as something divine in deed and word — resounds

[p. 9] through it (per-sonare [to sound through], as the voice of the ancient actor through his mask) — gives us alone the concept of an individual determining itself according to a higher faculty of perception (reason [Vernunft]), that is, the concept of a person.

Thus everything points to the fact that in every living thing whatsoever, as the one thing, as that by which a living being is conditioned at all, as that which we must regard as the ground of its actuality, we recognise a divine something, which we designate with the name of the idea of its existence, or (as soon as in this idea some kind of consciousness has developed) with the name of the soul.

The capacity of our spirit by means of which we may in thought separate many things that in actuality can never be separated, does indeed allow us to distinguish in any being, as Aristotle says: 2 "one part as matter, which in and of itself is not a definite something; another, form and shape, according to which a something is now named; and thirdly that which consists of these. But matter is potentiality, and the determination of form is actuality." Where<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> On the Soul (trans. Weiße), Book 2, Chapter 1.

[p. 10] it concerns a living, that is, a self-developing, self-preserving, self-nourishing, self-moving being, that which is first determined by that divine element in which we recognise the ground of its individual existence will always be form. But the determination of form, which Aristotle rightly designates as the actuality of the object, is also that which, although continually modified, is alone essentially held fast and appears enduring through that divine element during the whole of life, whereas the matter — what Aristotle calls potentiality — is in continual change, always escaping and always having to be replaced by other matter. If we therefore call the divine element, which contains the ground of an individual existence, the idea or the soul; the possible, at which this idea comes to appearance, the matter or aether; and then the actuality, as which it lives itself forth, the form — then we have indeed three moments of a living existence, of which, however, we must well consider that we can distinguish them as different only in the understanding, but that we may never assume an objective separation among them anywhere. A form without any matter and without any idea by which it would be determined is an absurdity; a matter that existed at all without existing in any form is likewise an absurdity; and an idea, a soul, that did not express itself in activity in any form, can have no existence at all. Precisely for this reason it is to be regarded as the highest development of the self-knowing idea that it becomes capable of thinking these distinctions, which are in themselves impossible; and precisely for this reason there also lies so much danger in these thoughts, because the human being is so easily led to grant them some kind of reality, in which case, however, only an abstruse and thoroughly unsatisfying representation of the world and of the human being can thereby be occasioned.

[p. 11] In these considerations there is thus at the same time designated the truly and properly human standpoint, the one alone illuminated and illuminating for all dealings with science and life; for that a divine something, self-moving, self-developing, exists, is told us by the deepest inmost essence of our spirit, as well as by observation of the world and of every living thing, just as certainly as that a matter exists which in thousandfold forms conditions the phenomena of our inner and outer life. We are also permitted, for a clearer development of our thinking, to grasp and to name separately in the mind those primordial, in and of themselves eternally united, moments of the highest divine essence; and an abstraction from actuality to this extent is the true realm of the metaphysics accessible to us. What, by contrast, goes beyond these distinctions — all attempts to determine and explain the highest relations of the totality of the divine, of the absolute God in his necessity for revelation in a world-appearance — they will always lack any immediate cognition and will lose themselves in one way or another in wavering, nebulous, and barren regions.

The present investigations shall maintain themselves entirely upon the standpoint just indicated: to trace the divine in our interior, in its unfolding from the unconscious to the conscious, shall be our essential occupation in what follows; and we now declare once more with definiteness that the key to this cognition of the essence of conscious soul-life can be found only in the region of the unconscious.

[p. 12]

## I. Vom unbewußten Leben der Seele — I. On the Unconscious Life of the Soul

It is a peculiar sensation that we have when, in our self-conscious thinking, we make clear to ourselves with what characteristic and lofty lawfulness and beauty, in us and in other living beings, long before all thinking, the becoming-actual and the preservation of our formation is guided. The more deeply we penetrate here into the depths of the formative processes, the higher rises our reverence before this governance! Whoever has followed step by step, with unwavering steadiness, the crystallisations of the primal divisions of but a single organism; whoever has seen how, through infinite repetitions of the single primal form of the microscopic first egg-vesicle, a peculiar cell-formation arises which is everywhere the foundation from which then vessels, nerves, muscles, bones, each according to definite radiations and metamorphoses, emerge — to such a one it must gradually become intelligible what a wisdom, power, and beauty a self-individualising divine element is able to reveal, still without any self-consciousness. It is then even unavoidable that we arrive at the question: "Can the free activity of the self-conscious soul rise to a height equal to the beauty, the richness of inner perfection, of that which an unconscious governance of the psychic principle daily and hourly unfolds before our eyes?" Everything that has been said about the relationship and opposition of nature and art can be drawn upon here, and one will always have to convince oneself that the inner perfection and highest purposiveness of the formations wrought by this unconscious infinitely surpasses everything and anything that the conscious spirit is able to produce in a similar way. Indeed, when it then becomes clear to us that everything we call the science of the conscious soul is only a tracing and a seeking out of the relations and laws that ceaselessly operate in the unconscious governance of the various living things around and within us, from the celestial body to the blood corpuscle, there arises for us a peculiar cycle of the world of ideas, which develops from the unconscious to consciousness, and as such yet again seeks at its highest point the unconscious, and finds itself satisfied only in the fullest possible understanding of it.

[p. 13] In these considerations, remarkable relationships concerning the human being itself also open up for us. For when we have convinced ourselves that the formation of this our organism — quite apart from all conscious life that is later to unfold from it and within it — presents to us a perfection, a manifoldness, an inner purposiveness that we find in no other organism in this way, then this must fill us with a peculiar reverence for the human being as such, a reverence that will be quite independent of whatever the human being as a conscious individual may have become in particular. In the individual, therefore, who as a conscious spirit has developed ever so meagrely, indeed who has entirely lost within himself his dignity as a self-conscious being, we recognise nonetheless a wisdom, a purposiveness, indeed a certain beauty of inner life, which must fill us with an admiration all the greater the further we ourselves have advanced in the knowledge of these phenomena.

[p. 14] From this veneration, which, even without yet having fully passed into cognition, pervades and must pervade the human being toward this unconscious, much in the representations of humanity is explained, and indeed already in the most ancient times. The peculiar reverence for the child's nature, even before a higher self-conscious life has developed in it; the aversion to the killing of human beings; indeed, among the Hindu, the aversion to the destruction of all animal life; and among so many peoples even the veneration of human form, indeed even of certain animal forms, as something divine. To be sure, the further back cognition still was, the more misunderstandings made themselves known in these representations, for here what was only a divine element — a single ray of the one absolute God whom we can only divine — was often taken as divinity itself, and precisely here the source of the actual error of pantheism has opened up. This pantheism, the opinion that many particular things could already be an absolute divinity, stands in complete opposition to that which one might perhaps best be justified in calling entheism — that is, the cognition of the divine in everything — and as clear as it must be that this entheism designates the sole truly healthy intuition of the world, so certain is it that a complete pantheism, like actual atheism, is really too absurd ever to have been able to satisfy the human being in the full sense of the word, given even the slightest development of intelligence.

Moreover, our theology of today, like a very widespread kind of physiology, still moves, in its teaching on things of this kind, in a peculiar circle. The excellence and wisdom of the divine in the formations of nature, and likewise in the organisation of the human being, is acknowledged; its study is even recommended for increasing the cognition of the divine in general; and yet a distinction is drawn between this unconscious governance of a self-individualising divine element and the conscious divine which we perceive in the developed human soul, as between two things utterly opposed. That first unconscious is then again, as against the soul, addressed as vital force, and this latter then appears now degraded to a mere mechanism, now perhaps also conceived as especially daemonic, so that in the end not much was lacking before one would have directly opposed it as the revelation of the evil, satanic principle to the conscious psychic as the revelation of the good and truly divine principle — while yet the inner, truly divine perfection of all productions of that first unconscious governance cannot be denied. These are aberrations that are here only indicated in passing, but which actually stray further from the truth than those of so-called pantheism itself.

[p. 15] Now I said already at the outset that it is difficult, in the region of conscious soul-life, to grasp in truth the concept of unconscious suffering and doing of the soul, but that yet again it is precisely here alone that the key to a true psychology can be found. Let us therefore try at first to advance by attending to how much, even within the conscious state of our soul, moves and completes itself only as something unconscious. Thus it is, for example, beyond any doubt that the muscles which serve the movement of breathing obey, through the effects of nerve-life spreading to them, the volition of our conscious soul-life. We can arrest these movements for a time, we

[p. 16] can intentionally accelerate, interrupt, strengthen, or weaken them, and perceive therein their full dependence upon our self-conscious soul. Nevertheless, these movements occur as a rule and continually, throughout our entire life, for the most part completely unconsciously, and make it intelligible to us that between consciousness and unconsciousness there lies a very mobile boundary, and that the conscious and the unconscious are radiations of one and the same unity. Perhaps even more striking is this in all movements that serve any skill. Here, entirely in the region of consciousness, and executed by muscles wholly subject to volition, what we call "learning," "practice," is nothing other than an effort to bring something that belongs to consciousness back into the region of the unconscious. One may think of the pianist: each individual finger-placement, finger-flick, is originally voluntary and must first be called forth by intentionally and individually willed nervous impulse upon the appropriate muscles. If it is now called forth many times and renewed again and again, it gradually passes in its particular complication entirely into the realm of the unconscious and is so withdrawn from consciousness that it no longer needs to be individually thought at all; rather, the representation of realising certain tonal sequences in general is already sufficient to call them forth quite unconsciously in their totality and in any desired temporal succession just as surely as the breathing movements follow without our thinking of them. The same holds for the learning of our most essential locomotion — walking — and for a hundred other things; from which it clearly follows that in ability, just as in knowledge, the reaching over from the conscious into the unconscious truly belongs to the height of human perfection.

[p. 17] The latter is an observation that deserves the full attention of the psychologist and has not yet been pursued to a sufficient degree, although E. Stahl had already very decidedly drawn attention to some things of this kind. It is certainly very remarkable that for doing, for ability, for the art of the human being, paths quite similar to those for knowing, for knowledge, for science, are here prescribed. Just as it denotes an ever greater height of science the more deeply the conscious cognition of the human being penetrates into the perception of the ideas and laws that unconsciously operate in our own organism and in that of the world-appearance around us — and just as it is therefore also the highest task of the doctrine of the soul to penetrate into the regions where soul-life still proves itself effective entirely without consciousness — so too does every ability become truly art only when all doing, insofar as it is to serve a certain purpose of the will, is again carried out unconsciously in and of itself, and precisely thereby favours the highest ease of every production; for only then does it become superfluous for the soul to think specially and intentionally of all the individual acts of will that are necessary to carry out any intended deed, so that now, with the will to achieve it, only the purpose needs to hover before it purely and vividly, in order to see the artistic activity for the attainment of this purpose set in fresh and easy motion.

If, moreover, we turn again to what we call in conscious soul-life knowing, cognising, we now understand, in attending to its emergence from the unconscious, why already Plato represented all learning-to-know as a remembering, as a "finding within"; thus finding where hitherto there was no knowledge, and where this

[p. 18] truth, this thought, yet was, like the unconscious embryo in the conscious mother — a process on account of which Socrates so often wished the development of the thought to be regarded as an obstetric act. All of this points with certainty to the rich, peculiar world that we carry darkly in our interior, and every reflection of this kind must at once be able to make the remarkable relationship between consciousness and the unconscious more distinct for us. An even brighter light, moreover, can be thrown upon this if we wish to think of the gradual emergence of inborn particular tendencies of the conscious soul. Here it is shown at the same time how far back we must go in the history of the idea of our existence — and thus precisely into the realm of unconscious existence — if we wish to arrive at the discovery of the first grounds of the particularity of this existence. I recall here first how many quite distinctive traits even of conscious soul-life can propagate themselves from parents to children, how many particular tendencies of the spirit, many special inclinations, many artistic endowments, become in this way the property of persons in whom, moreover, they often emerge quite late, although in disposition they must have been present in them from the start. Now let one make vivid to oneself the completely unconscious condition of the soul at the time when, in the first formative periods of the egg, such transmissions alone were possible. Let one make clear to oneself how here in the soul of the embryo, while it expresses itself solely and entirely as a formative, developing, substance-attracting and substance-distributing power, all those later-manifesting spiritual tendencies of conscious life are already actually prefigured unconsciously! And one will have before oneself one of the most remarkable and, for the history of the relationship between consciousness and the unconscious, most instructive moments. Certainly, from such things it must become clear how through and through our conscious soul-life rests upon the region of the unconscious and proceeds from it; how it is quite properly the first creative act of the idea living itself forth as soul, still entirely unconsciously, to found the admirable manifoldness of the organism; and how also then, when in the reflection of the idea in this creation consciousness has opened up, the unconscious radiation of that divine element is the inexhaustible spring from which ever new and new enrichments of consciousness arise.

[p. 19] Precisely because we thus recognise it as so supremely important for the science of the soul to penetrate as deeply as possible into the understanding of the idea unconsciously governing within us, it is here first of all unconditionally required to draw in sharper strokes the history of the developing organism, and especially the human one. It is particularly necessary in this to clearly apprehend the essence of the process of development, though not necessarily every individual modification of it — an insight that has only become possible through the careful investigations of the most recent times, still entirely foreign to the layperson and indeed to many physicians as well. Only from this clarity of insight will the inference back to the peculiarity with which an unconsciously formative soul-life expresses itself in general become truly possible. Had E. Stahl, to whom the thought had already come in the seventeenth century that "it is only the soul that is the truly creative and formative element of the organism," been able at his time to grasp clearer representations of this forming and the true relationship of an idea to its living-itself-forth in a form, and had he not been held back from purer intuitions by the assumption of a certain materiality of the soul,

[p. 20] then the whole essence of these relationships would have had to disclose itself to him already. For the distinction between conscious and unconscious soul-life had indeed dawned upon him, and he said very aptly: "The unconscious and involuntary in the organism occurs indeed also ratione or logo [according to reason], but not ratiocinio or logismo [by ratiocination]" — a cognition that so filled and satisfied him that he looked down with a certain contempt upon the physiology of his contemporary F. Hoffmann, and even fell into decided differences with Leibniz, the latter having certainly grasped the soul in and of itself in its immateriality more correctly than he, but since the soul's relation to the organism was more remote to him, still assumed a second entelechy, the force of movement, besides the soul in the organism — which Stahl was bound to reject, since the concept of the unity of the organism had once truly dawned upon him and become clear.

### a. Vom Wesen der ersten Bildungsvorgänge des menschlichen Organismus — a. On the Essence of the First Formative Processes of the Human Organism

A real misfortune that accrued to psychology from the fact that in recent times it was mostly men who occupied themselves with its elaboration who had no concepts at all, or only the most imperfect ones drawn from books, of the formative processes and the life of the organism, lay in the fact that, when they wished to make clear to themselves what they called the relation of soul and body, only an approximate image of the entire articulated manifoldness of the adult body hovered before them, and they entirely lacked the right representation of its simplest, earliest conditions. Already Aristotle says: "And for this reason the consideration of the soul belongs to the natural scientist, either in its entirety or as so constituted." How should the right cognition of the conscious in us come forth, if the cognition and tracing of the unconscious had not preceded, and on account of lacking prior knowledge could not precede! Just as, therefore, in morphology it is only through the study of developmental history that the doctrine of the relation and significance of the organs has become more fully apparent, so too will one recognise the manner in which a divine element —

primal image of a being prior to all existence — actuality — in a copy, and which relations then continually subsist between primal image and copy, only when one seeks out the conditions where the relations and formations are still simpler, whereas one will grasp them far more with difficulty where they have already actually attained an infinite manifoldness and complication.

As we now therefore turn to these considerations, one fact is immediately to be set forth as the most important and made clear, from which alone already infinite light can be gained for insight into all similar relations — a fact that has truly been unveiled only by the most recent researches, and which, had men such as Aristotle and E. Stahl already been acquainted with it, would have opened up for them the understanding of the unconscious forming of the soul in fuller measure. This fact reads: the original complete equality of all elementary parts of the organism; or the truth that all enlargement of the articulated structure in the living body is determined by infinitely manifold repetitions of one and the<sup>1</sup> [p. 22] Simplest of all, and so it is primordial cells, which

<sup>1</sup> On the Soul, Book 1, Chapter 1.

same simplest fundamental form. The simplest of all forms, however, is the pure sphere — infinitely minute hollow spheres, vesicles — which, as organic unities (monads), found the multiformity of all organic formation. Actualised a thousand and a thousandfold, the idea thus posits itself in such a monad, and every primordial cell of the organism is accordingly always only the repetition of that first primordial cell — that egg-germ, egg-vesicle — with which the whole organism began; precisely for this reason, however, every one of these primordial cells is also in its particular way the expression of the idea of the whole, and thereby in its particular way possessed of its own life.

Let one think through the thought of such an articulation, and one will find that herewith in truth a tremendous step has been taken toward the understanding of life in general and of the relation of the soul to the articulated body in particular! It was precisely the lack of this cognition that led the older researchers, and still some more recent ones, to search in absurd fashion for a seat of the soul, as if only at one point, like the spider in the middle of its web, the soul were fixed in the organism and from there set in motion the mechanical apparatus of the body! Whoever, by contrast, has rightly grasped how all fundamental form of the organism rests upon the uncountable repetition of the one fundamental form, and how every cell is the repetition of the first egg-germ, and how it itself, precisely thereby, always again actualises the fundamental idea, or is precisely thereby in itself possessed of its own life — such a one now regards with altogether different eyes the whole that builds itself up from all these repetitions. Only through this representation, however, does every more highly developed and especially the human organism become truly elevated for us to the concept of a small world, a microcosm — a concept that otherwise can be thought by most people only as a

[p. 23] simile, and consequently inadequately. If, on the other hand, we make clear to ourselves that really the very first germ-vesicle of the organism appears only as a single thing, as a monad; that then already during its first further development thousands of new such monads develop from the germ-vesicle; indeed, that the whole body of the gradually forming embryo consists only of repeated vesicle-forms — cells — from which brain and nerves, muscles and bones, sense organs and organs of formation and nourishment gradually arrange themselves according to a higher plan of the totality of the idea, while at the same time millions of restlessly arising and perishing monads circulate about as blood corpuscles; indeed, that also of the primordial vesicles or cells that have already concreted into larger structures, many thousands cease during every revolution of the earth to belong to the organisation, detach themselves and are destroyed, while other thousands continually form themselves anew and join the existing ones; and that now in each of these millionfold formed cells the original idea of life of the organism has always actualised itself in a peculiar way — then a concept of life is first produced in us that we can in the true sense call a worthy one, and which presents and proves the apparently simple and quietly persisting living body to be a thoroughly agitated sea of constant perishing and becoming, quite in the same sense as a system of celestial bodies — in short, as I said above, truly as a microcosm.

In these processes, then, we see the first unconscious working of that divine idea which is to live itself forth as soul, as it here emerges under given conditions upon a simple albuminous fluid, and indeed emerges formatively with the same necessity as, upon the hovering water-droplet of the atmosphere, when warmth diminishes, the idea of a six-rayed crystallisation of the snowflake appears; and we recognise at once: this first unconscious working must in general manifest itself as twofold; on the one hand, it appears as a ceaselessly repeating, ever newly positing itself in one and the same primal form, so long as any further formation of the organism takes place at all; on the other hand, it is a higher one, aiming at the presentation of the totality of a manifoldly articulated organism. One could therefore say that the opposition of matter and form touched upon above repeats itself here in the material itself once more, inasmuch as the endless repetition of the primordial cell in millions of monads possessed of their own life, or cells, represents, as it were, the material of the organism, while the various modifications of these infinitely accumulated cells according to the higher schema of our entire organic formation first establish the form, and thereby — as Aristotle quite rightly says — "the actuality of the whole living formation."

[p. 24] Not only these spatially formative relations, however, are important to observe here, but equally the moment of time deserves our particular attention. For if the creative activity of this divine element within us, which in its fuller development we designate by the name of the soul, were merely a stroke-like, only once effective, lightning-like appearing and not continuing in time, it would in nothing differ from actual crystallisation or the formation of a member of the earth-organism; but it is in a certain measure enduring, it is transformative, always destroying and newly forming, it is thus also, precisely through these repetitions, preserving the organism, and in this unconscious revelation of a higher organism there emerges a most remarkable property, which relates to time just as that first formative moment related to space.

[p. 25] It is now this property in which once again very important conditions for the development of the higher conscious soul-life arise. For just as it was previously shown that, with respect to the spatial appearance of the organism, an infinite multitude of particulars are always subordinated to the formation of a higher, a total form, so it is proper and necessary to that progressive, preserving effect, to that temporal creating of the unconscious divine element in the organism, to subordinate all individual time-moments of its existence to something higher, to a general time of its existence. For this divine element, which as such necessarily also partakes of the predicate of eternity, always reveals its essence, one might say, in a fraction of this eternity, in a part of time perpetually dividing itself into past and future, which we call, as it were, its relative eternity — that is, its lifetime. But precisely because every past and every future of the living organism are integrating parts of a whole — namely, fractions of a relative eternity — they must also always stand in the most exact relation to one another; the preceding must point most determinately to the following, and the present just as determinately to the past; and herein lies the higher ground of that relation of times which we shall later designate in consciousness as memory and foresight.

Since, therefore, all growing, all forming, all destroying, and all re-forming — in a word, all this unconscious becoming — always reveals the firmest and most exact relations of the preceding to the succeeding and of the succeeded to the previously existing, although it itself proves itself only in constant flight between past and future, without actual present, this foresight and this memory in it must indeed be called firmer and more certain than in the conscious sphere; and it must become an important task, here where the unconscious of the soul is to be considered, to set forth what is the nature of this unconscious memory and unconscious foresight. Only later will it emerge that an actual present — that is, the finding of a true resting point between past and future — is possible only in the conscious spirit, but that therein also time's flight is overcome and eternity is grasped. Here, however, in the development of the conscious spirit, once the present is attained, past and future are darker, while in the unconscious the actual present is indeed absent, but the relations between past and future are all the more intimate and certain.

[p. 26] In any case, it is now of the highest importance that it become entirely and completely clear to us what significance is contained in this foresight and memory, lying hidden in the darkness of the unconscious creating of the idea and yet so precisely proving itself; and it will be necessary first to go somewhat into detail here in order to build up the concept thereof quite clearly and completely. Only when this has succeeded will it be possible to open an understanding of what passes from it into the conscious life of the soul — indeed, to show how this conscious life finds itself through and through grounded precisely by this Promethean and Epimethean element of the unconsciously creating idea. Not without deep wisdom had the Greeks already connected the myth of Prometheus and Epimetheus with everything that is called the higher cultivation of humanity.

[p. 27] The doctrine, hitherto so entirely neglected, of this Promethean and Epimethean element of the unconscious will emerge most clearly for us if we first direct our gaze somewhat more sharply upon the history of all unconscious organic life — upon the mysterious, silent further-forming of the plant world, or upon the more restless, more animated life and activity of the animal world. In every stirring and in every form we shall here, if we are attentive in spirit, be able to understand that something lies hidden everywhere by which something past, something previously existing, is pointed back to, and by which something further forming itself, something future, is prefigured. Thus the first divisions of the plant-germ point to the kind and position of later leaves, thus the leaves to the kind and position of the flower-crown, and already the first rudiment of the blossom shows the definite articulation of a structure from which at the beginning of its life the entire plant emerged, and which, although unconsciously, has yet remained so well in its memory that it reproduces it entirely at the height of its life — that is, the seed. Indeed, if we observe life more closely, we see that there must absolutely, in its striving forward, remain a feeling, an unconscious memory, of what was previously present; otherwise it could not be explained how, at the pinnacle of a development, after manifoldly traversed phases, something could recur just as the germ was formed from which the formation began (for example, the egg or the seed); and conversely, we recognise that a definite, though unconscious, presentiment must live in it of where its course of formation is to be directed and what it is to strive for — otherwise the surely progressive course, the regular preparation of many phenomena that can in themselves only constitute transitional periods and always subordinate themselves to ever higher purposes, would be quite inexplicable. The more one thinks one's way into all of this, the more determinately one recognises that with extraordinary firmness the after-feeling of the previously existing and the fore-feeling of the coming expresses itself here unconsciously, the more one must gain the conviction that everything we call memory, recollection, in conscious life, and far more everything we call foreseeing, foreknowing, in this region, falls far behind the firmness and certainty with which, in the region of unconscious life, this Epimethean and Promethean principle, this faculty of memory and presentiment, still without any consciousness of a present, asserts itself. When in lower animals the lost limb regenerates itself most perfectly, as it were from the image of the lost one firmly retained in unconscious memory; when in the initially merely microscopic human egg, during its gradual development into the mature human being, the image of the human organisation in general, indeed of the maternal or paternal organisation in particular, remains so unforgotten through series of years that more and more, in quite gradual succession, the image of that first stock at last truly emerges most distinctly; when the seed preserved dry for a millennium retains the form of the plant from which it stems with such distinctness that, as soon as moisture, nourishment, and warmth are offered to it, it is able to reproduce that form with all the particulars of the microscopic cell-structure — then a powerful Epimethean element is quite unmistakable here. When, on the other hand, while the embryo seems to be unable to have the slightest presentiment of the atmosphere into which it shall later enter, within it the wonderful tissue of the lung-structure is already prepared with the greatest completeness, into which this atmosphere is yet to penetrate only after birth; when the secretions covering the eggs of the night-moth flow more strongly as soon as a more severe winter is approaching; when the seeds of so many plants develop their flight-apparatus, by which they are later to be carried onward by the wind and dispersed, long beforehand within the seed-pod — all this again indicates most determinately the power of the Promethean and the certainty of unconscious foresight.

[p. 28] [p. 29] Two great facts we have thus far gained for the knowledge of the inner life of the soul: first, that what in our soul, as an unconscious, truly creatively produces the copy of the primal image, completes the structure of this copy by ceaselessly positing in life infinite repetitions of the one first type of the monad, the primordial cell, so that accordingly in this regard every organism truly appears as a world of restlessly arising and perishing monads, as a world of unities which, however, are always again subordinated to a higher unity, and in which therefore the same concentrating relation of multiplicity to unity obtains upon which, in the highest sense, all consciousness of the spirit rests. Secondly, we have become aware of the steady inner connection between past and future in the history of this unconscious revelation of the idea in life, as organism, and have come to know the Promethean element of the beginning and the Epimethean element of the become, recognising in it at once, on the one hand, the most essential condition of this life, and on the other the preparation for that which, when consciousness has opened, is given the name of memory and foreknowledge.

In all of this, however, the foregoing has by far not exhausted what science otherwise has to say about the governance of the unconscious of the soul in every detail of a human existence; it is rather furthermore of the highest interest: 1. that it be shown in what way not merely in the whole and general the creating of an unconsciously self-manifesting divine element determines that which we call a human organisation, but that, since infinite stirrings, radiations, articulations, and developments even of the conscious soul become intelligible only through the inner manifoldness of organic articulation and through the various reflections that arise for the soul from this, above all a more detailed picture must also be sketched of the origin and significance of this organic articulation in general. 2. Since the unconscious governance of the idea within us determines not merely the preservation and further formation of a single individual life, but also the multiplication of individuals — that is, the preservation and further formation of the species — and since this too stands in the most important reciprocal effect upon the conscious spiritual life of the soul, inasmuch as we shall find that a consciousness can develop only under the condition of the life of the individual in the plurality of the species — a more definite account must be given at once of how the idea unconsciously effects the multiplication of the species. 3. And finally, it remains to show more definitely what, in the soul itself, even when a consciousness has developed, still falls to the mysterious depth of the unconscious. All of this, then, must now gradually come to consideration, before we enter the circles that were formerly often regarded as alone belonging to the doctrine of the psyche — namely, the regions of the conscious spirit. First, then, follows here:

[p. 30]

[p. 31]

### b. Betrachtung der ersten durch unbewußtes Walten der Idee geseşten Gliederung des Organismus in verschiedene Systeme — b. Consideration of the First Articulation of the Organism into Different Systems, Effected by the Unconscious Governance of the Idea

Among the many unintelligible and irresponsible things with which the older psychology burdened itself, the doctrine of the relation between body and soul stands near the top, insofar as by this one did not so much have in mind the relation between the idea, the divine primal image in and of itself, and the copy impressed in aetherial substance, also in and of itself, but rather understood — or rather really did not understand, or at least did not rightly understand — the relation between different regions of soul-life as it expresses itself partly consciously, partly unconsciously. Thus, for example, one reckoned it as a relation between body and soul when one described the relation that obtains between the thinking-functions of the brain that reach consciousness and the unconscious digestive functions of the stomach; one said: the thinking of the soul is influenced by the nourishing life, the spirit by the blood-life of the body, and so forth, and did not consider that here and in all similar cases one had before one no opposition of soul and body at all, but only an opposition between different, now conscious, now unconscious, regions of the self-manifesting soul, or of a divine primal image living itself forth temporally and organically. This falsely so-called doctrine of soul and body — two factors whose interaction, moreover, could not at all be brought to clarity — has already by itself alone spread unspeakable error in psychology, and it can only be illuminated, and really entirely set aside, if the doctrine of the articulation of the different spheres and systems of life in the human being is brought to complete clarity; I say set aside, because of that which can truly be called the relation of body and soul, primal image and copy, psychology least of all needs to take notice. For psychology is concerned only with life, the life in which the idea, the soul, expresses itself, lives itself forth; in everything living, however, idea and aetherial substance are, as something actu — in fact eternally — inseparable, always to be grasped only in undivided unity. If we therefore really separate in thought the matter, as Aristotle calls it, from the form of life, if we take in separate consideration all the chemical elements that in eternal flight pass through the form of organic life — carbon, calcium, oxygen, nitrogen, hydrogen, sodium, iron, chlorine, and so on — what has all this in and of itself to do with life, what with the processes of the soul and of the spirit? All this first becomes living in the human sense through the fact that the idea seizes it, that it orders it into organic form; but then, and so long as it serves this form, it is also no longer to be separated from what Aristotle calls form; it is this form everywhere body and soul at once, and only the diversities of this form are what were sometimes falsely opposed as body and soul.

[p. 32] If, on the other hand, in the ordinary sense one opposes body and soul — for example, thinking and the stirrings of feeling on the one hand, and muscular movement and the circulation of blood on the other — one has only two different spheres of soul-life, on the one hand conscious, on the other unconscious, where in each, idea and aetherial substance work in inseparable union.

Against all these errors one will be protected if the representation of the articulation of the different spheres of life is given adequately and completely — that articulation as it gradually emerges from the governance of an unconscious, and according to higher divine order, and as it is ever maintained, though also in the most intimate living-together of the members. For one must here too begin by understanding that all the distinctions set forth and established here — say, as individual organic systems — can only be admitted as artificial ones for the facilitation of overview and understanding. It would be absurd to think that a vascular system, a nervous system, a respiratory and digestive system could have any reality in isolation; only in their co-operation, in their totality with all the others, are they real. But if one holds this representation fast in its deepest ground, then the contemplation of one after another can not only be permitted, but will here become especially necessary, in order to bring to the greatest possible clarity the different psychic significance — the way each of these systems asserts itself in a special manner, now consciously, now unconsciously, in the life of the more highly developed soul. To be sure, of the enormous material that the more recent studies of developmental history of the organism have furnished, only a cursory overview can presently be given; but it must nevertheless be said that indeed even the smallest physiological fact can never be entirely without psychological significance, and that the more the psychologist descends into these depths, the richer the yield he will bring to the surface for the circle of his knowledge.

[p. 33] What, then, we first have to direct our attention to is the process by which the individual radiations contained in the divine primal image as it reveals itself organically live themselves forth in different organic systems. To be recalled here first of all is again that Carus, Psyche.

[p. 34] each and every material of formation is given only by the fact that the very same primal form, the same monad or primordial cell in which the life-idea of the organism is first spatially posited as a microscopic egg-germ, repeats itself immeasurable times in the course of development, so that the developing organism initially appears through and through as a structure of uncountable primordial cells or monads, emerging from the fluid element, each of which, possessed of its own life within itself, again completes for itself its life-cycle, arises, perishes again, and is replaced by new monads — structures that, the nearer to the very beginning of general formation, are all the more perfectly alike among themselves, and the further from it, all the more individually and specially modified and so fused into larger structures that their individuality entirely perishes in these. An ever stronger, more decided imprinting of individuality is indeed altogether the essence and the aim of all revelation of an idea. Inasmuch, therefore, as certain series of these monads represent one, and others another, radiation of the idea, in the structure of these primordial cells — extending more and more spatially and fusing more and more here and there — individual life-circles emerge that we designate with the name of organic systems, and in which either merely a radiation of unconscious soul-life is formed, or the appearance of a radiation of soul-life that shall in the future reveal itself as consciousness is Prometheanly prepared.

Before, however, we pursue all these particular developments more closely, attention must be drawn to a peculiarity of these processes that must be called extremely characteristic of the manner of creating of our divine primal image; this is: the wondrous, lightning-like, positional, creative, enlarging character of all the first formations (crystallisations) of our and similar organisms.

[p. 35] Physiology here enumerates phenomena that must have something fairytale-like for everyone who approaches such things for the first time. It can, for example, give some notion of the rapidity of such processes when I mention that our own body, at its embryonic beginning, enlarges in the space of a single lunar revolution by more than 500 times its length, or about 25,000 times its circumference, and that even in the following second lunar revolution it increases at least fiftyfold in mass, while at the same time, as cell after cell ranges itself within, the outer form of the body, like the articulation of the individual inner organic systems, is continually produced with the most extraordinary purposiveness and delicacy. Microscopic observation of developing animal organisms has here especially illuminated the science, and has everywhere spread natural representations of the relatively truly enormous rapidity of such formative histories; and when we now consider such things in a higher sense, it must make it fully clear to us what extraordinary power, even in this regard, a divine element living itself forth entirely and utterly unconsciously is able to manifest in such processes.

Already this power proper to the divine psychic principle, this absolute mastery and permeation of matter at a time when this psychic element, entirely absorbed in itself, forms as if dreaming, or — because it does not yet think in thoughts — must think in forms, brings us, when we now rightly follow it with consciousness, a great step closer to self-knowledge and to the understanding of our soul. It is, however, remarkable also to perceive that this rapidity of the self-manifestation of the idea decidedly diminishes the more the actual goal of this life is already to be

[p. 36] considered as reached. Already the above example shows how soon the rapidity of the developmental process subsides; but when we follow the history of life further, we find, before even the first quarter of the lifetime, the cessation of general growth, and in later periods more and more rigidity, regression, and atrophy — processes that then likewise do not fail to let a decided reflection be perceived in the states of conscious soul-life, and which prove that, over against the infinite of the idea, every finite revelation of it can only be something imperfect and must sooner or later dissolve again within itself and be lost.

If we now proceed further to the formation of particular systems and particular structures in this organic becoming, one phenomenon is still especially to be highlighted that was indeed already indicated in general earlier, but on account of its higher spiritual significance is now to be designated more closely: we mean the fusing of those first given unities — the primordial cells produced by ever-repeated positing of the idea — into ever higher wholes. For as certain as it is that everything in the organism begins with cell-structure, so certain is it that in all higher formations — such as nerve-fibres, muscle-fibres, vessels, and membranes — these primordial cells in ongoing formation completely fuse with one another, perish as individuals, and thus show already in the unconscious what ultimately in the conscious becomes a higher task of life — namely, the perishing of the particular in the general. Remarkable and significant, however, is that those primal forms do not everywhere wholly disappear, but persist as such at two kinds of places: namely, where something quite low, merely elementary, is represented — thus in the circulating blood as so-called blood corpuscles, thus also in the ever-renewing cells of the epithelia, and so on, on the one hand; and on the other hand, where the elementary must persist as the highest, as primal structure, in order always to remain capable of being polarised in a thought-like way by the idea — that is, in nerves and brain.

[p. 37] The latter observation leads us at once to the organic system most peculiarly remarkable for psychology among all organic systems — namely, the nervous system. We perceive here, not only in the human being but in the entire series of all true animals, that as soon as the unconscious governance of the psychic principle, for the various needs of bodily existence, causes multiple organic systems to emerge from the first semi-fluid mass of those primordial cells, then, amid this rapid progress of an ever further emerging differentiation, at certain places the substance really remains almost entirely in that most delicate, semi-fluid quality as it first exhibited the constitution for the generality of the organism everywhere. Here, then, a mass accumulates which, precisely because it does not diverge into other disparate structures, because it is not really organically polarised into something else, retains the capacity to be altered, polarised in its tension, continually by the finest, most spiritual radiation of the idea — that is, already by unconscious feeling, as well as by conscious thought. If one grasps this significance of such a formation in a truly objective way, one will have to comprehend most perfectly its enormous importance and how all higher psychic development in life rests upon this hinge alone; and precisely for this reason everyone tries quite in vain to make fully intelligible to himself the way in which a soul of higher energy can corporeally live itself forth, if he has not previously attained sufficient insight into these formative processes. Only that which in itself is still most indifferent, only the most delicate, semi-fluid, elementary material of the organism, is what can be suited to being thoroughly determined and permeated by the finest inner currents, stirrings, differentiations that the living-in of the idea into appearance calls forth; and just as, the more this mass still shows itself in the constitution of the whole organism, the more rapid is the speed with which a manifold organic formation realises itself therein, so too a formation in which henceforth, without further organic transformation, every slightest retuning of the inner divine principle is to live itself forth merely in altered current- and tension-relations of inner energy, can only be one that perfectly preserves and presents that semi-fluid elementary cell-substance throughout the whole of life. Through this cognition, then, we are placed in a position now to comprehend, according to its inner ground, what external observation had long clearly established — namely, that the higher or lower dignity of the divine fundamental thought of an organism — in a

[p. 38] word, its more or less energetic soul — must characterise itself more than by anything else through the kind and constitution of its nervous system, and therein continually express itself. Whether a higher degree of centralisation in the nervous system presents itself; whether in mass and formation one part of it — the brain — predominates over its rays spread through the organism in mass and refined formation; whether these rays — the nerves — are drawn finer and more numerous, or coarser and more seldom — all this must accordingly be thoroughly characteristic and important for bringing about a possibility that in the life of the soul that centralisation emerge upon which alone, as will later appear, the possibility of consciousness rests.

[p. 39] Precisely because, however, in the nervous system what is most original, most pure — as it were, the organism within the organism — is given, it can also present itself only withdrawn into the innermost, so that it is henceforth impossible for it to stand in immediate interaction with what lies outside the organism. For this reason, on the one hand, intermediate members must form themselves; formations must arise that have the significance of mediation — transmitting effect from outside upon the nerve: for this the sense organs are destined (for what immediately touches the nerve, as in injuries, produces only a suffering condition of it — pain) — or structures that transmit effect from the nerve to the exterior, and for this the organs of movement, the musculature, serve in particular. On the other hand, however, formations will also arise that complete the withdrawal of the nerve, presenting a more perfect isolation of it and especially of its central masses; and for this the skeleton and especially the nerve-skeleton is destined.

Already through this, then, the concept of a greater manifoldness of organic systems necessarily required for the self-manifestation of the soul arises for us. Because, however, such a manifoldness can only live itself forth gradually in time, and a constant exchange of substances with the outer world is as indispensable to it for material existence as the interaction with the outer world in sensations and reactions of the idea is for spiritual existence, special systems must develop for that exchange — indeed, precisely these will necessarily first gain more definite form. The division also of these systems destined for material exchange — which, however, as having likewise emerged from the unconscious governance of the idea, can never lack the psychic signature — is determined: on the one hand by the necessity of substance-intake, on the other by the necessity of substance-excretion, and thirdly by the necessity of inner substance-processing. Digestive system, respiratory and secretory system, and vascular system thus emerge with definiteness in the manifoldness of the organic cell-structure. But since the appearance of the individual organism in and of itself is also destined to be a transient one, and the idea of it is granted a more enduring existence only through immeasurable repetitions from generation to generation in the idea of the species, the organism, just as it itself grew out of a preceding one, must also contain within itself the possibility of a new generation, whereby yet another special system — namely, that of the continuation of the species — is founded. Through all this, then, the necessity of a great manifoldness of organic formation is at once revealed, among which, however, always only one is the centre, one the highest purpose of all the others, and only one will be that which can be called purely psychic, the purest form of the self-manifestation of a divine element — the nervous system. It is extraordinarily important for all understanding of soul-life to grasp these relations quite sharply.
