Just as God reveals himself through the entire world-appearance, but according to our cognition most purely in noble human natures, so the soul, the divine fundamental thought of the human being — it reveals itself, lives itself forth, to be sure in the full totality of the human organism, but most immediately in the nervous system of the same. These relations one must contemplate long, strive to grasp them within oneself in deep inner reflection, and only then can one perhaps bring them to clearer cognition. Ultimately, to be sure, it is altogether indispensable that one has arrived at pure and clear conviction concerning the relation between the ideal, which causally conditions an appearance, and the material, at which the ideal can come to appearance. In my System of Physiology I pointed out how it is here that the physics of the organism passes over into metaphysics, since here a separation must be thought that lies outside of and above actuality. Actuality — we ourselves — the world — everything — has an existence only in that it is at once and in inseparable union idea and substance. Nevertheless, we are able in our own ideal being, in our spirit, to distinguish — by placing ourselves above nature (proceeding metaphysically) — between these two things inseparable in themselves, and we now call the one: idea — the image of being prior to all existence — the God-thought — the primal image as such — the eternally self-identical, timeless

[p. 40] [p. 41] and spaceless, moved only in a divine, not a spatial manner; the other: substance, or better (from aei theo [to be in eternal motion]) aether — the eternally movable and eternally actually moved, the conditioning of time and space through this movement; and so we hold more or less apart in thought what in truth and in essence is eternally united and inseparably joined.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> This most primordial of all oppositions — this opposition of idea and aetherial substance, or aether, as I call it — has also been most determinately recognised by the most primordial of all philosophies, the Indian; and it is important to recall this, not as an authority — for rational cognition cannot acknowledge authority — but only to show that already so early an untroubled reflection upon the highest riddle of the world had to lead to a result such as cannot present itself otherwise to our time either, given the ripest consideration. Already among the Hindus, then, a distinction is drawn (see v. Bohlen, Old India, Part 2, p. 311 et seq.): 1) eternal matter, the natura naturans [nature naturing], the aether as Prakriti or Mulaprakriti; and 2) natural reason, the idea determining and conditioning the aether — Purusha. Indeed, already there the distinguishing of the I, as Buddhi — also as world-soul — and from these two, as a third, self-consciousness (Ahankara) emerges.

[p. 42] Here, however, it is particularly to be recalled that this inseparability of idea and aetherial substance is by no means to be thought of in such a way that one and the very same element is always and eternally, or even only for a longer time, united with one and the very same idea, or always determined by one and the same idea; on the contrary, it lies necessarily in the concept of the eternally movable and eternally actually moved aetherial substance that a constant fleeing and drawing must take place here, that the same idea must live itself forth in ever new aether — so that every idea perpetually lives itself forth through ever other metamorphoses of the aether, through other and always new substances. Thus there presents itself to us an eternal drawing and fleeing of the elements, now slower, now faster, now imperceptibly, now en masse, but never standstill, never absolute persistence, never a thoroughly enduring union of the same potencies — and, in a word, herein the ground of the eternal transformation of the world.

I cannot, of course, enter here into expounding these fundamental intuitions more fully, or even lose myself in polemic against those who think differently. There are truths that the human being must find within himself — truths to which the human being, as Goethe once said, must be "organised upward"; and whoever has not attained this will not be convinced of it from without; and therefore let all this be left to the conscience of truth in the reader. Perhaps it will be the success of the following presentations of soul-life to convince more and more that only by grasping it at this root can a purer understanding also be attained here.

If, however, we proceed further in the development of the inner differentiation and articulation of the organism, guided and determined by an unconscious, then particular attention must also be paid to how in the various organic systems, whose necessity in general we set forth above, at the same time certain peculiar directions of soul-life must come to quite particular appearance. We found, namely, that purely as the self-purpose of soul-life only the nervous system could be regarded, while all other systems relate entirely to the relations of the individual to the outer world. Only the nervous system, then, is purely psychic, is in itself an indifferent, resting whole showing only peculiar, mysterious currents similar to magnetic and galvanic ones. But also the remaining systems with their structures, as they all formed themselves out of an original, essentially identical with the semi-fluid of the nervous system, general substance, arise through that unconscious governance of the idea and its living-in of itself into organic matter, and they too are in that respect psychic — have a particular, only at first unconscious soul-life, and can later, by means of the conscious life in the nervous system, also be brought, at least in part, to consciousness. It is important already here to follow these different radiations of psychic existence somewhat more closely in detail, so that it may become clear how thereby certain — I would like to say — particular souls, or soul-circles within the soul, are founded, upon the correct cognition of which chiefly depends that which one has usually, and, as has already been shown above, wrongly, called the doctrine of the interaction between body and soul.

[p. 43] Those particular provinces, then, into which the organism articulated itself, were, in accordance with the above, next to those most closely akin to the nervous system — the sensory and locomotive structures and the skeleton — those of nourishment, partly those of substance-intake and substance-distribution, partly those of substance-decomposition and substance-expulsion. The latter are distinguished [p. 44] Thus

partly into those that kill what is external and use it for nourishment, such as the secretion of bile and similar ones, and partly into those whose secretion liberates and vivifies what is internal, such as respiration. Finally, the continuation of the species becomes the task of a special organic system. As certainly as each of these provinces, each of these systems, arises through a particular unconscious governance of the soul, so certainly must there exist and remain for each a particular dominion in the peculiarly interior stirrings of the soul, and it will thereby be able to impart to consciousness, when this has developed, a distinctive colouring from this quarter. In this way, then, those peculiarly felt moods of conscious soul-life (feelings) will arise which appear as reflected upon consciousness from organic processes that were themselves in turn conditioned only by certain unconscious psychic tendencies. One can regard these moods as particular circles in which one and the same feeling manifests itself now heightened, now diminished, now toward a plus-side, now toward a minus-side. Only provisionally shall we indicate the most important occurrences of this kind; later there will be occasion for a more detailed consideration of them.

Thus, for example, as regards the sphere of nourishment, on its psychic side there lies above all that feeling which is grounded, on the plus-side, in a life-fresh assertion of existence, and, on the minus-side, in its atrophy and misery — with which, then, even in conscious soul-life, a great manifoldness of states subsequently begins. The fullness of a vigorous blood-life and healthy, strong activity of the heart in the organic is accompanied by — or rather is itself — an unconscious mood of the soul which, upon the development of consciousness, is felt as courage and vitality. Conversely, a sunken blood-life, greater blood-loss, weakness of the heart's movement and slackness of its texture are reflected in the psychic under the form of dejection, fear, and a feeling of general powerlessness and incapacity. — It is also easy to perceive that it is quite indifferent from which side — whether purely from the organic or purely from the psychic — these retunements are initiated. A sustained, circumstantially aroused state of fear and faintheartedness calls forth the aforementioned morbid states of blood-life, and conversely — and everything indicates how very much we have reason always and ever to regard both as one. — It is the same with the sphere of substance-intake. The life in the digestive system, through which a new fullness of elementary substance is brought into the organism, is expressed in the psychic by the comfort or the torment of the actual feeling of a somehow actualised existence; states that manifest themselves in the agreeable, in the feeling of satiation and in the pleasant taste of what produces this condition, or in the disagreeable, through the feeling of privation, of hunger, of thirst, and in the unpleasant impression of those elements that are not perfectly suited to nourishment — and so propagate themselves into the highest conscious sphere as well. The latter, however, always only insofar as a nervous system participates; for what actually thirsts and hungers, or lives in a state of satiation, is by no means the nervous system itself — that is, the soul preparing itself for consciousness — but rather modifications of the wholly unconscious, and here therefore of the digestive system — that is, modifications of that life-structure and actualisation of the unconscious soul-circle destined to supply and permeate the organism with new substances. Even the plant can thirst or be satiated, but it does not possess the capacity to heighten this dark sensing to any kind of true sensation, and therefore arrives neither at the feeling of the agreeable in satiation nor at the feeling of the disagreeable in thirst.

[p. 45] [p. 46] In contemplations of this kind one immediately perceives that language really lacks a word to designate aptly this kind of stirrings of unconscious soul-life as such. We must resort to the strangest circumlocutions if we wish to make ourselves at all understood about what we actually mean by this. It is also very natural that such designations are found, or rather formed, only late in language. For I have already pointed out above that the cognition of the unconscious in consciousness is everywhere the last and highest achievement of science, just as in respect of ability the highest art arises only when ability again becomes unconscious. Precisely because it is only in the finest and deepest investigations that this penetration of the conscious into the realm of unconscious existence becomes the task, the need for word-formations of this kind also emerges only late in language. In my System of Physiology, at the beginning of the third volume, I first drew attention to this in greater detail, and there proposed (after having shown how already Bacon had felt a need for these speech-forms) to use the word "sensing" [Erfuehlung] — perceptio — and thus to designate linguistically the unconscious feeling of the soul that is as yet living itself forth only in organic forming. Once this designation has been granted its right of citizenship in physiological and psychological matters, one will immediately find oneself truly advanced in all considerations. The sensing of the soul in the life of the blood-vascular system or of the digestive system is therefore that which alone actually conditions all those moods whose reflex in conscious life we have just cited as courage or faintheartedness, as the feeling of satiation and the feeling of privation, and so on; for although we in the conscious spirit decidedly receive these sensations themselves through the nervous system, their cause can obviously not be sought in it, and again this cause can be nothing other than that now-acknowledged unconscious feeling of the state in which these other, non-nervous systems find themselves. Sensation, conscious feeling, is always possible only in the life of the properly and solely purely psychic system — that is, in nerve-life; but the nervous system does not live merely in itself; it is also the central point for all the remaining systems through which it enters into interaction with the outer world; it can therefore take up into itself the states of these mediating systems, thereby also transmits the sensings of one system to the other, and is hence also alone capable of elevating their sensings to sensations.

[p. 47] Sensing, then, the plant has; sensing has every primordial cell, every non-nervous structure in animal as in human being; indeed, even the receptivity of the nerve, so long as no complete centricity of nerve-life has been developed, or when it has been abolished again, can be nothing other than sensing. Thus, for example, it is not to be said of the embryo that it feels sensation, and just as little of the newborn as soon as, in brainless malformations, the central station of the nervous system had not been formed at all; rather, in both cases there is here only an unconscious receiving and transmitting of stimulus — a sensing,

a perceptio, but no sensatio [sensation].

I wish herewith also to remark that, just as there has hitherto been lacking a definite linguistic designation for these unconscious self-feelings, we also possess no definite word for what I above called the unconscious memory of the organism of its past and the equally unconscious foresight of its future, and must all the more seek to form one so that it may later become easier to set forth how in conscious existence so much else develops from these faculties and gradually forms itself further out of the unconscious as conscious. It is now quite interesting to perceive that of these Promethean and Epimethean feelings, whose essentiality for the entire formative process of an organism I have already discussed above, only for the former — because it never fully attains consciousness in the human being at all, but always persists in its peculiar darkness — has a somewhat definite designation, namely the word "presentiment" [Ahnung], "divining" [Vorahnen], long since been found (although even this still denotes a certain consciousness of the future); whereas the latter, which we know more clearly in its conscious form (memory), but which in its unconscious form has never before been noticed, entirely lacks a special designation. If, therefore, a special word-formation is to be introduced here as well, then the linguistic form "innering" [Innerung] for the unconscious sensing of the past, just as "presentiment" [Ahnung] for the unconscious fore-sensing of the coming, would certainly be the most apt; and I therefore note here once and for all that sensing [Erfuehlung], innering [Innerung], presentiment [Ahnung], in this measure and in distinction from sensation [Empfindung], memory [Erinnerung], and foreseeing [Voraussehen] or foreboding [Vorahnen], in the present considerations, wherever the occasion arises, are always to be so employed.

[p. 48] Proceeding further in the consideration of the articulation of the organism into its particular systems and their particular sensings, the system of respiration and secretion now presents itself as the next in line. Both are in a certain sense opposed to nourishment; the former has the significance of permeating the interior with fresh vital aether, so that it may continually evaporate again with pleasure into the general; in the latter a liquid flowing-away of the interior itself predominates, and often with the significance of killing what is external so that it may then serve to nourish what has killed it. So also the psychically sensing side presents itself quite differently in these two. That dark feeling which, when it presses toward consciousness, is felt as courage, energy, joyfulness, lightness of movement, proceeds just as much from the respiratory system — so long as it operates in free, pure activity — as its opposite, fear, timidity, anxiety, appears when the breath is constricted, oppressed. Without respiration the sensings that are the ground of those named feelings would remain entirely foreign to us, and the more a creature is pervaded by respiration, the more it is governed by these sensings. Examples of the latter are given by insects and birds, from whose fickleness — made possible only by vigorous respiration — we have even named a characteristic of many a human feeling. As for secretion, the ones that kill what is external, appropriating it to the organism, are here the most significant. Just as these processes themselves are far more hidden and withdrawn from volition than respiration, so their sensings also remain more remote from consciousness than those of the latter; yet from here, too, a multitude of unconscious sensings pass into consciousness and there take on a killing, hateful colouring. The expression of a bitter mood is just as characteristic for the relation to the peculiar sensings of the agitated

[p. 49] [p. 50] liver system as that expression of fickleness for the sphere of the most vigorous respiration. Such contemplation of the homonymous in these states is well suited to making clearer the formation of the conscious out of the unconscious — of which far more detailed discussion must take place later — and especially to letting one see ever more clearly what is signified by what is commonly designated as the influence of the body upon the soul and of the soul upon the body; for one recognises in this that usually nothing more is thereby meant to be expressed than the influence of one organic system upon another, and especially the effect of dark sensings upon conscious feeling and upon the knowing soul, and conversely. If, therefore, for example, through blood-loss the life of the heart and vessels and secondarily also the life of the lungs is lowered in tone, then at the same time the minus-side in the peculiar sensings of these systems is posited, and since this cannot but produce a retuning in the fundamental idea of the organism — of which these individual ones are only partial ideas — a decidedly different mood also spreads over the region of conscious thinking, feeling, and willing — a mood of dejection, faintheartedness, and weakness that can go as far as fainting (the vanishing of consciousness). In such things, therefore, there by no means emerges a special dominion of the body as something in any way independent over against the soul — for of this there can be all the less talk the more it is understood that the body is only the appearance of the soul itself — but rather a certain effect of blood- and respiratory life upon the more purely psychic nerve-life. Finally, quite special occasion for contemplations of this kind is given by the sphere of sexual life — of the life for the continuation of the species. In this

system, developing later than the others, the opposition of individual life to the life of the species is to be brought out most decidedly; in it the entire new creature separates itself from the old; in it therefore rests all the pleasure of a newly opening life and all the pain of one that is perishing. At the same time it forms, more than the other systems — to which it is not opposed as one particular to another particular, but to which it, insofar as it is the reproducing whole, stands as one particular over against a totality — a greater self-enclosedness within itself, and its sensings can therefore govern the entire organism in the most distinctive manner. In the animal world we therefore see the entire individual existence most frequently dependent upon this system alone. The animal attains sexual union, and in many cases the cycle of its life is thereby closed. In the human soul there lies, precisely for this reason, in this region the possibility of the highest intensification of inner well-being, inner pleasure — for which the language gives a special word — "voluptuousness" [Wollust] — which is nothing other than the communication of the most vital, highest sensing of the unconscious sphere of the sexual system to the highest conscious sphere of the nerves; indeed, in the conscious soul, from here, ever more rising and spiritualising itself, the possibility of the mightiest of all passions is given —

[p. 51] and precisely that which includes the highest happiness and the highest pain — that is, love.

If we now survey, after only a brief account has been given of the history of the delimitation of the various organic provinces and of the various sensing of an unconscious psyche, the manifoldness of these facts as a whole, the following important propositions result for the doctrine of the life of the soul:

1. The unconscious governance of the idea determines an articulation of the bodily formation into different systems, in each of which a particular ray of the ideal psychic existence actualises itself.

[p. 52] 2. The truly pure soul-system, from whose dim feelings [Erfühlungen] consciousness alone can develop at a higher concentration, is the nervous system.

3. In every other organic system, the soul in and of itself is capable only of a particular circle of unconscious feelings, which can be communicated to consciousness only by virtue of the fact that branches of the pure soul-system interweave themselves within them, take up their feelings, and thus appropriate them to the nerve centre.

4. In the recognition of the original manifoldness of these systems and their particular feelings, we thus find the first foothold for obtaining a proper intuition of the inner manifoldness of the various circles of the soul that originally inheres in every higher soul-life. Long before we are conscious of a multiplicity of representations and feelings, the soul lives itself forth unconsciously as something manifold, and only a clear insight into the diversity of these its circles of life, which only late find their centre in the awareness of one's own I, can afford us an adequate representation of soul-life in general; in short, we must here too once again convince ourselves of the truth of that proposition with which we opened all these reflections: "The key to the cognition of the conscious life of the soul lies in the region of unconsciousness."

### c. Von dem wesentlich Unbewußten des Vorganges, durch welchen innerhalb der Gattung die Individuen vervielfältigt werden — c. On the essentially unconscious character of the process by which individuals are multiplied within the species

In the foregoing it has become clear to us how, in consequence of the unconscious desire-to-become [Werdelust] of a divine idea, an organism manifold in itself actually comes into being, and indeed comes into being through immeasurably repeated self-positing in the form of a primal form, a monad, a primal cell. But how from one organism one or more new independent organisms emerge, and how ever new ideas can thus live themselves forth so that the species may be preserved, is furthermore a question of the highest importance for psychology, inasmuch as it must thereby be decided in what manner the multiplication of souls may be conceived. We find creatures in which nearly every monad, every primal cell of their interior, is capable of becoming a new independent organism; we find others that can be divided by a cut, each half completing itself, and where formerly one soul held sway, now two souls manifest themselves. These processes appear at first sight most mysterious. Can an idea divide itself? Can a forcibly mechanical separation cause one soul to become two, indeed many? These questions must be settled in order to comprehend not only the infinite multiplication of souls in the animal kingdom, but also the manifesting of more and ever more human souls destined for consciousness.

[p. 53] One must begin here by making it perfectly clear to oneself in what relation the species stands to the individual in general. The species [species] is, namely, in and of itself something purely ideal, not existing as something actual, something fully appearing in space, but rather revealing itself perpetually only in the multiplicity of individuals. This relation of the species then repeats itself in a certain way in that of the individual to its elementary parts, the primal cells. In the origination, continued development, destruction, and re-formation of these primal cells, the former continually lives itself forth in just the same way as the species lives itself forth through individuals; for indeed here too, properly speaking, only the entire sum of all the millions of such monads, which ever again vanish and in the continuation of life ever again arise, would constitute the actual individual, whereas the individual that we apprehend as a single temporal appearance (I may, for example, consider the human being now as child, now as man, now as old man) always remains but a fragment of the whole ideal individual. The idea of the species thus lives itself forth by realising itself possibly infinite times in single individuals, just as conversely the idea of the individual lives itself forth by positing itself possibly infinite times as a single primal cell, as a monad. Of a division of ideas there can therefore be no more question in the multiplication of individuals, or monads, than the idea of a triangle divides itself by a multitude of particular triangles becoming actual. Just as, moreover, every triangle that becomes actual, precisely because in it the idea now becomes manifest temporally and spatially in the ever-flowing stream of the elements, and consequently becomes manifest under always somewhat different conditions, will also invariably differ in some respect, even if perhaps infinitely little, from every other triangle that has become actual, so too every realisation of the idea of the species as an individual, and every realisation of the idea of the individual as a monad, will in some way, even if perhaps infinitely little, differ from every other. According to all this, one can now summarise these truths once more in the following proposition: the idea of the species, the idea of the single being, and the idea of the monad, that is, of an elementary formation of the single being, are everywhere first to be distinguished. To each of these ideas belongs the possibility of revealing itself in infinitely manifold ways. In a circle of beings there can be infinitely manifold species, in a species immeasurably many individuals, in an individual immeasurably many monads. The higher the circle of beings in which this triad of different ideas reveals itself, the more will each of them diverge from the others. In humanity, every individuality is essentially different from all others and from the totality of the human race, and every primal cell or monad is again essentially different from the whole human being.

[p. 54] [p. 55] It is furthermore clear that the higher a circle of beings is, the more determinately individuality will reveal itself within it, and if we cast a glance in this regard upon all living things around us, we find, with respect to continued procreation and the ever-new realisation of ideas, very remarkable and important differences indeed. In the lowest organisms, where everything pertaining to the soul still rests deep in unconsciousness, everything actual is still so indifferent that between individual and primal cell only a very slight difference obtains. If the individual is to be multiplied here, it requires merely either the forcible severing, or the detachment — occurring through the idea's own unconscious drive — of one or more primal cells, and these individual primal cells at once behave as a whole, just as the broken-off pieces of a piece of magnetic iron likewise immediately behave like the larger piece, displaying their own south and north pole and their own attractions and repulsions, and so forth. Just as one may also say of the larger piece of magnetic iron that it is still not a true whole, not an individual, but only a fragment of a member of the organism of the earth, so too individuals whose generation and multiplication is already possible in such a manner possess only slight selfhood, and every development of the idea toward any kind of consciousness remains here utterly

Indeed, just as unthinkable. It is remarkable, however, that precisely because of this lower significance in the whole, the ease of production merely through division and detachment of primal cells stands in quite direct proportion to the enormous multiplication of such beings. Here belong the examples from the world of infusoria, where one can calculate that a creature can multiply in the course of a few hours into millions of similar creatures, and the like. The desire-to-become [Werdelust] of these ideas, with regard to the number of particulars, stands directly in inverse proportion to the power and significance of the individuals thereby called forth. If, on the other hand, we turn to the higher circles of life, of which humanity itself is the highest, we encounter an essentially different and very remarkable relation. It is there the higher energy of the idea of the individual that posits a powerful difference between the totality of the organism and its elementary parts; a difference from which it follows that although individual elementary parts, by multiplying, can indeed continue to develop and supplement the organism itself, they are absolutely no longer capable, alone and in and of themselves, of repeating the whole organism in the way that the division of the monad-infusorian, or the severed piece of the nais, or the bud of the hydra could accomplish. All the more powerfully, by contrast, does the idea of the species prove itself here, and a new individual can always arise only as the idea of the species itself posits itself anew; which is why in actuality this positing also becomes possible only in that, not emerging from one individual, but always only through the cooperation of two individuals, whose duality in each case represents the species (hence language has very astutely formed the word "mating" [Begattung]), the new arises, and indeed arises as an elementary part, that is, a primal cell of one individual, assumes the concept of the egg-germ, so that now not merely the very same idea of that organism of which this primal cell was originally a fragment, but a particular expression of the idea of the species as such, may live itself forth and manifest.

[p. 56] [p. 57] While, therefore, in that first case the individual can truly procreate and multiply itself, as its idea continually posits itself anew in elementary parts that need only be separated in order to reveal the same idea again as a new organism, in the second, higher case it is always only the species — which for its representation always requires at least two individuals — that behaves in a procreating and self-multiplying manner. If one makes this relation truly clear to oneself, much is thereby to be gained, partly for understanding the significance of sexual procreation, partly for understanding the higher circle of life altogether. That the idea of an organism which can become manifest only inasmuch as something in itself merely ideal — the species — continually lives itself forth and posits itself anew and distinctively within it, must be of higher significance than an idea that can already multiply itself at any fragment of its own organism, and live itself forth anew, yet always again only as the same, is surely clear in and of itself. But equally, sexual procreation now presents itself in a higher significance, for in it the two actual, real organisms that procreate represent something ideal — the idea of the species, which in and of itself never appears corporeally — and thus give occasion for yet another one or several of the infinite ideas of individuals contained in the concept of the species henceforth actually to come into appearance.

In this regard, the clarifications that the advances of recent physiology have provided concerning the course of human development are very instructive indeed, but they also first receive their correct interpretation from this standpoint. It is namely remarkable that in all higher creatures that procreate only through the species, and especially in human beings, first likewise certain primal cells form, each of which has the significance of developing in the future a whole new individual from itself; these are the egg-vesicles of the female ovaries. To this extent, then, higher organisms also appear to repeat those lower ones, where individual primal cells separate themselves and the idea of the whole creature immediately lives itself forth in them again; here, however, there is no longer any question of such immediate development; never does a new human individual arise from such a monad unless the essence of the species has previously manifested itself in it by its coming into immediate, yet in and of itself always utterly unconscious, contact with at least one of those living primal cells that are secreted in the male body as spermatozoa. If one thus knows that the new individual actually proceeds neither from the woman nor from the man, but rather, as a particular individual revelation of the idea of the species, is posited only through a certain unconscious cooperation of both, so that always from two factors a third emerges as something utterly new and distinctive, then the remarkable independence of the newly manifesting idea from the two individual procreating ideas is also immediately comprehensible. Already that remarkable law of the equal number of the sexes discovered by Hufeland, in which humanity is invariably maintained independently of such various circumstances of procreation, can be understood only from this standpoint; and only from here does it also become comprehensible how, although something kindred is always communicated by the procreating souls to the newly manifesting idea, yet every newly emerging idea must also always betray something, and often an extremely strongly pronounced, original-primordial quality.

[p. 58] [p. 59] With every act, therefore, in which the idea of the species of humanity embodies itself, becomes actual, yet another of the infinite ideas comprised in the total idea of humanity begins to live itself forth, and that the manner in which humanity realises itself in that act — whether it be a powerful and significant one, or a weak and slight one — remains of the greatest importance for calling forth an idea either of great energy and beauty, or of weak and slight energy, and letting it enter into actuality, is subject to no doubt whatsoever.

Since it has thus become generally clear how, only by means of a process that likewise belongs to the sphere of unconscious soul-life (for what in the sexual union falls within the sphere of consciousness of the procreators has absolutely nothing in and of itself to do with that contact by which the new life is conditioned — this latter moreover occurs one to two days later than the mating and always entirely within the interior of the female organism), ideas from the infinite number of those comprised in the idea of humanity continually begin to live themselves forth, it remains now to draw attention to some particular circumstances that come into consideration here.

First, namely, it also marks the higher dignity with which the desire-to-become [Werdelust] of the idea of humanity manifests itself in the ever-new realisation of its individuals, in comparison to lower circles of life, that on the one hand a far more limited productivity in point of number occurs here; and on the other hand, that here the possibility of the unconscious act and contact of the aforementioned primal parts — representing the species — is necessarily bound to a certain maturity of life of the individuals and to the prior development of consciousness. That enormous multiplication of individuals, where one and the same meagre idea of life multiplies itself millionfold in a short time, as it makes itself felt either through immediate division and detachment of primal cells or through procreation on lower levels of the animal kingdom, is entirely foreign to humanity, and repeats itself here at most and solely in the rapid multiplication of primal cells within the individual, particularly in its earliest life-processes (of which mention was made above) and indeed throughout the entire continued development of the organism. Secondly, as regards the maturity of life required of the individuals that condition the actual procreation, here again a very remarkable relation between the conscious and the unconscious emerges. For when, stimulated by the entirely unconscious contact of the primal cells, a new form of the idea of humanity begins to live itself forth, and indeed at first, as we have found, likewise quite unconsciously, then at these earlier stages of its living-itself-forth — stages at which lesser organisms already begin to multiply without measure — it is in itself utterly incapable of procreation; rather, it must necessarily first be unfolded to full consciousness, and only then will it be mature enough to occasion, in the encounter with another likewise conscious idea, that unconscious contact through which yet another new idea can be called into life. Here too, then, this cycle from the unconscious through the conscious and back again to the unconscious appears once more.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> So much time passes in the human being before the spermatozoa reach the egg-vesicle; in some animals, such as the roe deer, weeks pass from mating to that contact, or the actual fertilisation.

[p. 60] [p. 61] Finally, one cannot enter more deeply into the consideration of the manner in which infinite individuals in humanity continually realise themselves anew without endeavouring at the same time to arrive at a more precise insight into the causes of their enormous diversity; for even though it is clear on higher grounds that nothing that has become actual in the world can be entirely equal to another, there nevertheless emerges in human souls and human forms of life a diversity that, were we to compare millions upon millions, would only become ever more distinct and powerful.

A more careful reflection upon this diversity of human souls will then teach us that it always has a twofold ground: one that lies in the original divine thought of humanity, and one that is given through the circumstances under which these thoughts live themselves forth. The idea of humanity, as species, must, precisely inasmuch as it is an idea of higher divine energy than any other of which we have experience, already contain within itself infinite possibilities of individual ideas, and precisely because of that higher energy in general, the differentiation of these individualities will be all the more strongly grounded already in this, its original divine thought itself; indeed it is really, just as a larger number distinguishes itself from a smaller by the quantity of units it comprises, precisely this containing of a greater differentiation of particulars that is the principal document of the higher energy of the fundamental thought of humanity as such. A primordial opposition we first notice, which runs through all this infinity of individual ideas comprised within humanity: it is an opposition in which the supreme dualism of the world — idea and aether, Purusha and Prakriti of Hindu philosophy, form and matter — repeats itself, namely the opposition of the masculine and the feminine. Perpetually, therefore, in its constant rebirth, humanity diverges into two essentially equal halves of the masculine and the feminine, and perpetually this rebirth itself proceeds again, from the ever-renewing union of these separated halves, in that unconscious manner which has been set forth further above. Only in this indispensable necessity of dividing a higher whole first symmetrically into two great oppositions within its unity lies the sole sufficient ground for that remarkable equal number of the sexes, recognised and first demonstrated by Hufeland only in its teleological bearing — an equal number which is therefore by no means proper to all other genera of living beings, among which the preponderance of number is found falling now on one side, now on the other.

[p. 62] Within this first opposition running through the whole of humanity, manifold further oppositions emerge, and these too are partly grounded immediately in the originality of the idea of the individuals themselves, partly everywhere heightened and awakened by the diversity and mobility of life. Thus a multitude of circles within circles forms, but always a definite law presents itself: that the more strongly the conscious life of the spirit develops, the more decidedly the opposition between individuals stands out, and the more distinctly the manifoldness of human natures makes itself felt. For that most primordial of the oppositions in humanity, which is entirely and wholly grounded through the unconscious —

[p. 63] namely the opposition of the masculine and the feminine — it follows from this law that, because in the masculine the higher conscious spirit is especially destined to develop, the diversity of individuals in the male sex must also be more strongly grounded and more manifestly revealed than in the female; and equally this law holds for the circles of the different ages of life, indeed for the essentially different stems of humanity, separated by the influence of the earth's nature. In the most indifferent age of childhood, individuals are still less different from one another, whereas in the age when the conscious spirit emerges most powerfully, individualities diverge the widest, just as they are, for that very reason, also first capable in this time of the strongest attraction toward one another. As regards the stems of humanity, which, according to the four conditions of the planet that steadily revolve around the earth — day and night, morning and evening twilight — fall into the four great divisions of day-peoples, night-peoples, and eastern and western twilight-peoples, it is naturally the day-peoples in whom the day of the soul, consciousness, most fully unfolds, and for that reason the peculiarities of individuals among them diverge most strongly, while among the night-peoples (Negroes) they are decidedly more uniform already in the most primordial endowments of the soul.

If the decline in the sharpness of individuality according to the energy of the circles of life is already very striking within humanity, it becomes far more so when one casts a glance from this standpoint at the circles of life of the animal world. Only in humanity does what, following our earlier derivation, we call "personality" still reign as the pinnacle of all individuality; in the animal world, by contrast, those pri-<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> See my System der Physiologie, vol. 1, p. 118.

[p. 64] mordial oppositions of individuality disappear more and more, the lesser the idea of life of the species becomes; an ever more decided sameness characterises an endless repetition of the same form of life, and even the opposition of sex finally dies away in the deepest regions and is sometimes preserved only in the opposition of the organs of procreation, which are then united in one and the same individual.

It has been said, however, that the stronger individual diversity of the particular forms of life is not given entirely and solely by the distinctiveness of the first divine thought, but that the interaction with other forms of life — what we understand by conflict with the outer world — likewise has a powerful share in allowing the distinctiveness in the manner of an idea's living-itself-forth to emerge more strongly. Here, too, however, it depends quite especially on the inner higher or lower significance of the circle of beings to which the individual belongs, whether such external circumstances can contribute much or little to the sharpening of individuality. The higher the energy of an idea, the further its history reaches, and the greater also is the circle of possibilities within which its manifestation is able to change.

The manner, therefore, in which an organism is surrounded during its development, how it is acted upon, what relates to it in a promoting and favourable, or in a hindering and harmful way, can and must alter its distinctiveness in the most significant manner, and just as its spatial appearance, so too its soul-life, indeed already as unconscious soul-life, is retuned in the most remarkable fashion depending on the very different influences that act upon it. All organisms, all souls of a higher order, therefore have a larger circle of possible deflections and fluctuations within and around them, and conversely. In the human being, in the individual

[p. 65] human soul, the diversity is enormous that can be occasioned, given the same original endowment, by differences in influences, and indeed already by influences during the time of the first unconscious formation; in the insect, the worm, and in all individual souls of similarly lesser significance, even the most diverse influences can call forth no great and very essential diversity.

May these reflections now suffice to give a concept of the manner in which soul after soul, in immeasurable succession, manifests itself within the most diverse species, and may they also provide a provisionally satisfying demonstration of the grounds that determine the diversity of individual souls.

### d. Von Dem, was in einer ihrer selbst bewußt gewordenen Seele immer noch dem Neiche des Unbewußtseins angehört — d. On that which, in a soul that has become conscious of itself, still belongs to the realm of the unconscious

Whoever has followed the foregoing reflections with attention, whoever has now made clear to oneself how we ourselves — much as a crystal unconsciously crystallises according to the idea of its geometric form — come into being, arise, and continually exist through an entirely unconscious governance of the idea, that is, of the originally divine in us, will now also be able to convince oneself more closely of the power that, alongside the conscious spirit, the unconscious must forever have and retain in us. To develop and strengthen this conviction in detail will be especially the task of the present section. Above all, however, it seems important for this purpose to point out more fully that it is not merely in one way, but in several forms, that the unconscious of our soul- life manifests itself.

[p. 66] To some extent, indeed, the foregoing could already draw attention to such differences within the unconscious; at present, however, when we wish to look back thither from the height of the conscious spirit, and intend once more to survey comprehensively all forms of unconscious activity of the inborn divine within our being, it cannot escape us that such distinctions must necessarily be made here for the completion of a scientific cognition.

First, namely, we are compelled to acknowledge that there is a region of the life of the soul into which truly no ray of consciousness whatsoever penetrates — and this we can therefore call the absolutely unconscious. This absolutely unconscious, however, extends either still over all governance of the idea in us alone, and then we call it the general. Thus we found it in embryonic existence — it was the still exclusively formative governance of the idea, of the idea that, for this very reason, we cannot properly yet designate here by the name soul. Or else the absolutely unconscious is no longer the sole and exclusive character of all soul-life; rather, a consciousness has developed in some way, the idea has truly become soul, but even so all processes of the formative, destructive, and re-shaping life remain entirely without participation of consciousness, and such an unconscious is therefore no longer a general one but only a partial one. Over against the absolute or sheer unconscious, moreover, as it is recognised now as general, now as partial, stands the relatively unconscious, that is, that realm of a soul-life that has indeed already come to consciousness, which however for some period of time has now become unconscious again, yet always also returns again to consciousness — a realm that will at all times, even in the fully matured soul, encompass the greatest part of the world of the spirit, because at any given moment we can actually grasp and hold present only a comparatively small part of the whole world of our representations.

[p. 67] In the following reflections it will be our principal task to depict more fully the relation of that absolute, yet only partial, unconscious, as it subsists alongside what can reach consciousness and actually does reach it. As for the absolute and indeed generally unconscious of the soul during the embryonic period of formation — that is, that wondrous life in which the idea, as the divine fundamental thought of an entire human existence, so mysteriously and enclosed within itself, yet Promethean-like unfolds the whole remarkable structure of the organism in which the conscious spirit is later to stir and develop — upon it rests truly the veil of Isis, which can never genuinely be lifted for consciousness; nevertheless, analogy and comparison lead us even in this regard to be able to know that one and the same intelligence governs there as here, and indeed governs as a truly unconscious thinking.

More comprehensible to the conscious spirit, to be sure, is what we have called above the partially unconscious; for once the ray of consciousness has been kindled, in a manner of which we shall speak later, it also immediately makes the unconscious designated here far more objective within us. Thus, for instance, only the kindled light makes the night truly distinct and recognisable to us in its darkness. Within the domain of this partially absolute unconscious, then, everything still falls that belonged to it in the generally and absolutely unconscious as well — all formative processes,

[p. 68] everything that goes by the name of growth, nutrition, the life of the blood, respiration, secretion — all belongs to it, while upon the system that we have called the purely psychical one, upon the nervous system and the senses, the domain of conscious soul-life develops exclusively and completely. In this connection, moreover, it must never be overlooked that this form of the unconscious, too, is always a ray of the same soul that in another region truly manifests itself as consciousness, and precisely because it truly belongs to the same soul, all of its re-tunings must also in some way make themselves felt throughout all regions of soul-life generally, and thus in some way even in consciousness. What we call the world of feeling of the spirit is rendered explicable to us chiefly by these reflexes. Thus we find, for example, only for this reason that a predominant life of digestion disturbs the mobility and lightness of the life of representation, that an altered mood of the blood-life does not remain without influence upon the mood of the spirit, and so forth — because the stronger sounding from those dark regions up into the bright regions of consciousness makes itself felt there only all too distinctly in manifold feelings. As we have already remarked earlier, it was precisely for this reason that one often sought, under unrefined concepts in general, in such effects of the unconscious and the conscious, erroneously only evidence for the different kinds of commerce between the bodily and the spiritual, while now, after the foregoing clarifications, one can very easily perceive that in such relations, properly speaking, one is always and solely permitted to speak of the commerce between certain regions of unconscious and certain other regions of conscious soul-life, both of which are always only different rays of the same divine and one. One may perhaps seek to make clear to oneself the close relation of this seemingly lesser — that is, the partially unconscious — to the higher, that is, to pure consciousness, to the matured spirit, by means of the image of comparing the expression of the full conscious soul-life, say, to the luminous spire of one of those Gothic cathedrals that attract the eye through the richness of their ornamentation and the heaven-aspiring quality of their overall form, but which could neither shine in their beauty and be maintained, nor be borne aloft in their height, if the ground resting invisibly deep in the earth (here the simile of the completely unconscious) did not support them everywhere, and the inner artful joinery of masonry and ironwork did not fasten them throughout. Truly in quite the same way as that resplendent exterior depends on the inconspicuous foundation of a building, all the high and highest qualities of conscious soul-life depend on a thousand relations to the unconscious of the soul, and just as that spire of the cathedral falls irretrievably if but a single iron clamp breaks or a cornerstone of the foundation gives way, so too the most brilliant manifestations of the spirit vanish at once when the unconscious working of the soul — as it, for instance, directs the bloodstream of the heart or governs the alternation of breathing — encounters even the slightest obstacle. All this is ordinarily by no means sufficiently considered, or, when it is considered, is ascribed to a lamentable dependence of the spirit upon the body, whereas to the eye that is able to apprehend these phenomena in their totality, it must appear entirely as a beautiful and necessary sign of the common grounding of both spheres of soul-life, the conscious and the unconscious, in one and the same divine essence or idea.

[p. 69] Certainly, these subjects are of the most enormous significance for the possibility of a truly scientific psychology, and precisely for this reason a great part of the present work will make it its especial task to shed a brighter light upon just these matters.

[p. 70] We therefore state here once more expressly that whoever succeeds in any measure, in the conscious world of the spirit developed upon the height of the unconscious, in apprehending those wondrous and mysterious processes of the unconscious world of the soul — processes upon which the conscious world of the spirit, like a luminous rainbow upon a dark wall of rain, maintains itself only lightly hovering — has already been helped in the essential part of his cognition, and imperceptibly, the more he penetrates into these subjects, the more significant results will present themselves to him.

Let one therefore first direct all attention to penetrating with one's conscious thinking more and more into the unconscious processes of our soul-life; let one recall again and again that at a time of embryonic life, when all higher spiritual endowments must already be present in potentia [in potentia], and already the distinctive nerve- and head-formation that is to become the particular bearer of these endowments is for the most part unfolded, the soul in all this governs only unconsciously — and one will arrive at translating, as it were, this thinking of the soul without consciousness, in the forming and re-forming of our organism, into conscious soul-life. Already Schelling said very beautifully of the governance of nature in general: "All motion and activity, all stirrings of life, even those of nature, are but an unconscious thinking, or take place in the form of thinking; the more the lawful manifests itself in nature, the more spiritual its workings appear; the optical phenomena are already entirely a geometry whose lines the light draws, and the perfected theory of nature would be that by virtue of which the whole of nature would resolve itself into an intelligence." And who could acknowledge the world as appearance, as revelation of a divine, without being deeply imbued with the necessity of the inner intelligence of all natural life!

[p. 71] Let one therefore now first make it perfectly clear to oneself how even that soul in which the light of self-consciousness has in part actually arisen, nevertheless by far remains for the greater part enveloped in the night of unconsciousness. If one then further examines the distinctiveness of the various circles of this unconscious — the circles of formative life, of the ongoing building and destroying — and compares them with what we survey as conscious, then several otherwise important moments of distinction between the conscious and the unconscious will soon clearly emerge, and first of all one will become convinced that, as far as that realm of the unconscious extends, necessity also prevails, while directly with the dawning of consciousness, freedom also establishes itself. From this point, therefore, oppositions immediately date themselves that are of the greatest significance for all further understanding of soul-life. Everything that unconsciously takes original form within us lives itself forth as the revelation of a divine, whose determining ground lies just as much outside our individual existence as its cognition is also never fully attained by the individual spirit. In these regions, therefore, there is no question of individual will, nor can there be, for will presupposes cognition, and here there is as yet no cognition; rather, an unconditional necessity, foreign to our cognition and will, is here posited, which, inasmuch as it is still the sole determining ground for the soul that is for the greatest part unconscious, must also at the same time exert a certain influence upon the freedom of that portion of the soul which has developed to consciousness. It is precisely thereby posited that, just as our entire psychical existence perpetually hovers between unconsciousness and consciousness, so too a constant oscillation between caprice and compulsion, freedom and necessity, remains inalterably allotted to us throughout the whole of life.

[p. 72] How great, accordingly, are the disclosures that from this standpoint immediately present themselves for the entire realm of psychology, everyone will at once perceive who has but once attempted to make clear to oneself how the conflict between compulsion and caprice, or necessity and freedom, makes itself felt through the whole universe; and what is it that gives the individuality of the human being, seemingly so weak and insignificant in comparison to the revolving celestial bodies and the raging elements, this powerful significance and this sublime station, if not the consciousness that all those enormous masses obey a mute law of necessity, while this one individual alone is illuminated by the light of freedom!

But once again here too it must now be taken into consideration what was in part already discussed above, namely that we have only conditional reason to elevate freedom above the realm of necessity. For it has already been shown that the unconscious, and precisely for that reason what is determined by necessity, contains within itself again — precisely because it is by its essence something divine — a security, a wisdom, and a beauty to which consciousness and what is free, even at their highest height, can never quite attain to this degree. Where conscious thinking wavers and perhaps twice strikes upon the false and once upon the true, and wills the right, there the unconscious governance of the idea proceeds with the greatest decisiveness, and with deeper, in our sense, unconscious wisdom, upon its precisely measured course, and often fashions its essence with a beauty that in its entire scope can never be grasped by conscious life, let alone imitated.

[p. 73] Only when, therefore, in consciousness and in freedom, reverence for the unconscious and the necessary arises once more, does it become possible to give all these reflections that consequence which we must demand all the more, since it was already made clear earlier that knowledge, when from consciousness it penetrates the unconscious, just as surely attains its highest goal, as capability first becomes the highest art when from the conscious it is able to become once again an unconscious.

The psychologist has accordingly, with respect to the realm of the unconscious, above all to make clear to himself in what manifold, distinctively combined manner — thoroughly pervaded by retention [Innerung] of what has gone before and presentiment of what is to come — the life-idea of our existence ceaselessly manifests itself in the processes of the formation and re-formation of our organism. The more he here penetrates into the cognition of the particulars of this unconscious world, the more important results will present themselves to him. One of the first observations that will force itself upon him will be that the inherently eternal essence of the soul manifests itself in the unconscious more than in consciousness, inasmuch as in the former there is not a moment's standstill, no interruption, but rather an absolutely unceasing current of activity throughout the whole of life, whereas consciousness is not capable of this constancy but, for reasons that must come under consideration when we reflect upon conscious life, is in need of a periodic return into the unconscious — a return that we designate by the name of

[p. 74] sleep. From this follows the remarkable — and, because this path of reflection has hitherto been altogether neglected, never before especially appreciated — result that in the entire domain of unconscious soul-life the concept of fatigue does not exist at all, but rather that it first appears only where the unconscious is found especially combined with the conscious, or where it is a matter of the conscious alone. Thus the streams of fluids within us flow ceaselessly on, and unceasingly the pulse of the heart beats, and the lungs breathe, and the glands secrete, and there is no standstill and no fatigue in all these vital manifestations of the unconscious sphere of soul-life — a circumstance that must appear all the more remarkable to us when we consider how quickly other muscles tire when they have long been in activity, and how all other processes of consciousness within us require a constant interruption and renewed refreshment. For the same reason we then also find that many other concepts, likewise borrowed from conscious soul-life, are absolutely inapplicable to the unconscious — for example, the concept of gradual learning, of practising, of proficiency, and so forth.

For whatever takes place in the realm of the unconscious, whatever thereby belongs to necessity, requires no laborious learning, no practising to bring it to proficiency; easily and immediately everything is here exercised and accomplished that the essence of precisely this determinate being demands, and just as the crystal crystallises in precisely this way because it absolutely cannot do otherwise, so too the higher organism requires for its unconscious vital processes no special preparation; rather, it exercises them without further ado, because they simply belong to its vital distinctiveness. All of this is of extraordinary importance for the natural assessment of soul-life, and will be of the greatest advancement for better insight especially when we come to the consideration of the vital processes in the animal soul.

[p. 75] However, when it was mentioned above that the conscious distinguishes itself from the unconscious in the same relation as the free from the necessary, this does not refer merely and solely to some deed or activity of mature life; rather, we must at the same time recall what already followed essentially from the history of the primitive development of the organism, proceeding through an absolutely and generally unconscious governance of the idea — namely, that the necessity and the compulsion, by virtue of which every individual must be a particular and distinctive one and every soul can develop only as a particular one, spring solely and alone from the realm of the unconscious. That is roughly what Goethe calls the daemon [Daemon] within us, of which he says:

"According to the law by which you entered, / So must you be, you cannot flee yourself, / And no time and no power dismembers / The minted form that living unfolds itself."

Inasmuch, therefore, as it is clear from the foregoing that all conscious soul-life gradually develops only out of the absolutely unconscious of the idea, that only from the dark feeling [Erfühlung] does self-feeling and ultimately self-consciousness emerge, as from retention [Innerung] comes remembrance, and from necessary activity the free deed, so it must now also be perceived how a certain first necessary distinctiveness of the unconscious becomes the condition that likewise all individual stirrings of the conscious soul assume from the unconscious a definite abiding colouration, a certain necessary distinctiveness, and that is precisely what we usually designate by the name of the innate endowments of the soul — that is, those standing in exact and direct proportion to the bodily organisation — which then, inasmuch as they also essentially assert themselves in conscious soul-life, will soon come up for closer discussion in the section on conscious soul-life.

[p. 76] Yet not only the absolutely unconscious, inasmuch as it is the basis from which consciousness later unfolds, and inasmuch as it also still subsists alongside consciousness, is to be acknowledged in the soul, but also the relatively or secondarily unconscious, into which the conscious periodically always returns again. Just like the entirely unconscious, namely, all feelings and cognitions that have once already come to consciousness but then lie slumbering unconsciously in the soul again, continually act upon conscious soul-life, as upon what we have called the absolutely unconscious soul-life; what is ordered and beautiful acts beneficently and promotingly, what is crude and unbeautiful acts disturbingly and hinderingly.

If we now find this secondarily and only periodically unconscious united with the primarily and absolutely unconscious entirely in one life of the soul, and if the absolutely unconscious is precisely what essentially conditions the formation and re-formation of the organism, then it now also becomes clearly apparent why such once-conscious but now again unconscious stirrings of the soul can and must still quite essentially and continually exert an influence upon the nourishment and continued development of the organism. An example will make what we mean here clearer and more comprehensible. Imagine the cultivated human being who has arrived at a refined intuition of the beautiful and the true. In his soul, whose absolutely unconscious working has already built up and created for him a fortunate organisation and continually maintains it, there rest a multitude of representations, sensations, thoughts, of which only a few come to his consciousness at the same moment; nevertheless, all these treasures, now not known, are ever unforfeited to him, and at every moment this inner wealth continually acts upon the individual conscious stirrings of the soul that the moment's life happens to bring about, in such a way that each of these too can now be nothing other than beautiful and true, precisely because the fullness of the relatively unconscious soul-life had already long possessed just this character through and through. But this is not all! This relatively unconscious also acts upon the absolutely unconscious that presides over the forming and re-forming of the organism; the formation itself becomes different in such an individual, the features of the countenance acquire a certain clarity and what we call a noble expression, and a shimmer of this influence spreads over the entire organisation. Now imagine the opposite: a crude individuality sunk in the lowest interests of life, already through its primitive and absolutely unconscious developed in a coarser organisation, common and unbeautiful in all its intuitions and thoughts; it too will at every moment be conscious of but few representations, yet the repulsive, half-animal distinctiveness of this entire relatively unconscious soul-life will continually not only drag down all individual expressions of the conscious psyche and impress upon them a common, unworthy character, but that relatively unconscious will likewise communicate itself to the absolutely unconscious (strictly speaking, the expression "communicate" is only a figurative one to make oneself understood, since both are in essence truly one), and so this character will not fail to give the already originally less fortunate organisation an even coars-

er, more ignoble expression; the features will incline more and more toward the animal, indeed the most solid structures such as the skeleton will assume a distinctive character. will take on an alien

I think one cannot possibly follow these reflections with attention without becoming convinced that the path here taken — that is, the manner of constantly tracing the relations between the conscious and the unconscious in the unity of the soul — alone can lead us to a deeper understanding of the entire life of the soul. Manifold phenomena that would otherwise remain utterly incomprehensible to us become in this way clear and perfectly intelligible to us; but especially the manner in which representations too — that is, stirrings of conscious soul-life — can act upon formative processes, that is, upon re-tunings of unconscious soul-life, and conversely, formative conditions will constantly act upon representation, becomes comprehensible from this alone. We illustrate this too at once with a few examples. Thus it is a well-known experience that not merely the sensation, but already the vivid representation of familiar fluids — of lemon juice, for instance — calls forth an increased secretion of saliva just as definitely as, on the other hand, some object of anger almost instantaneously produces an effusion of bile, to such an extent that even other mild secretions — for example, in nursing mothers, the milk — can immediately thereby assume a sharp, even poisonous quality. In both cases these alterations in the aforementioned secretion occur, to be sure, quite unconsciously, but yet always only because conscious and unconscious soul-life ultimately always remain essentially one, and because that re-tuning of the unconscious formative life which brings about the change in secretion is entirely the homologue of what in consciousness becomes objective under the form of those

[p. 79] representations. The bile secretion, the formation of poison, namely, are truly in the unconscious the homologue of what anger is in the conscious, and when the wrathful representation is excited, this immediately calls forth in the unconscious those secretions as well. Likewise it is with the sensation of a piquant taste, whereby those secretions are immediately summoned that alone are capable of neutralising those pungencies. This then necessarily continues further; the actual taste sensation need not even yet be present at all — we need only rouse the representation of it, and at once, precisely because this representation is quite exactly connected with the unconscious life of that secretion, the function of secreting is also immediately there as soon as the representation awakens. Just so, conversely, these stirrings can also begin in the unconscious and, reflecting upon consciousness, reappear, as it were transfigured, in certain homologous representations. Thus, for example, an atmosphere corrupted by charcoal fumes, affecting the sleeper, acts inhibitingly upon the respiratory process of the lungs, and immediately anxious representations arise in the dreaming consciousness of the sleeper — of monsters pressing down suffocatingly upon the chest, and so forth. Likewise, all the effects of medicines usually called exclusively psychical (that is, those such as opium, henbane [Hyoscyamus], and the like, which affect the conscious sphere of soul-life) are comprehensible only in this way; for they excite in the unconscious a re-tuning that is equal to that which, through certain conscious psychical states, can in turn be produced in the unconscious, and by the reverse path, then, the medicine, through primary affection of the unconscious, secondarily and in polar fashion calls forth the alteration in consciousness. For we know that, for example, the thicker, more carbonised, poorly aerated blood, through its relation to innervation, immediately conditions a depressed state of cerebral life passing into sleep, just as conversely depressed states of the spirit and habituation to much sleeping in turn produce that blood-condition. If now opium and henbane in larger doses produce a more carbonised condition of the blood, they will also in this antagonistic manner immediately have a stupefying effect upon the spirit, and so on.

[p. 80] Just as, however, we earlier came to know necessity and immediacy (that is, the immediate doing and being-able, without prior practice needed and without ensuing fatigue) as especially remarkable attributes of the originally unconscious, so now, as an essential and very important attribute of all unconscious soul-activity, there is further to be considered a remarkable property of the same, for which we may be permitted to use the word generalisation [Verallgemeinerung] — that is, the intimate connection with the general of the world that essentially subsists in the unconscious, or as one may also say, the being-incorporated, so to speak, into the general that is especially perceptible in it. or

Indeed, the cognisant spirit very soon convinces itself that the world-whole has and must have an inner organic coherence, and that every organism, even though it appears for a certain time as something individual, is yet really only a part — an organ —

a partial organism of a higher whole. Nevertheless, with the awakening of this cognisant self-conscious spirit, the feeling of individuality, of being-for-oneself, and thus of being in a certain sense separated from the world-whole, is immediately given; the opposition between an I and an outer world stands out sharply from this moment on, just as thereby (as we

[p. 81] earlier remarked) the opposition of necessity and freedom was given. The unconscious, on the other hand, although its striving must be ceaselessly directed toward maintaining a certain independence of its own organism, so that precisely at the pinnacle of its existence the cognisant spirit may develop from it, is far removed from this sharp opposition; in it, the general existence of the world still flows on immediately, and in it stir all the fibres of connection by which the individual is everywhere and perpetually linked and must be linked to the whole. Now, to consider this carefully in the observation of organisms and especially in this psychological regard is of the most extraordinary importance. For the further removed any organism remains from self-consciousness, the less, accordingly, will its individuality be developed on the whole, and the more immediately must its unconscious being-comprised in a general organism be conceived, and the more dependent upon the latter's manner of living-itself-forth will it be, and all the more only feeling [erfühlend], retaining [innernd], and presaging [ahnend] all the vital processes of that general. On the one hand, then, when we hold fast to this cognition, very much at once becomes clear that concerns the history of the lower organisms known to us: one understands why protorganisms, plants, and lower animals are still wholly given over to the changes of telluric life, why their inner formation, unconsciously anticipating as it were, always develops in a manner appropriate to the moods of the circle of life in which they are comprised, so that from them, for example, certain prognostications of atmospheric changes and the like become discernible, of which our consciousness in and of itself has absolutely no closer knowledge. On the other hand, however, the most remarkable results also emerge from this for the correct assessment of the psychical processes of our own orga-

[p. 82] nism. For it follows that, since our psyche too originally persists entirely, and later still for the most part, at the stage of unconsciousness, in it too, insofar as it is unconscious, not only its own vital conditions are felt and determined through retention and presentiment, looking backward and forward, but that it, as a partial idea, first of humanity and, more remotely, of the world-whole, must be pervaded, now more closely, now more distantly, unconsciously by all the stirrings of the soul of humanity and of the souls of the world. It is necessary, however, to make it perfectly clear to oneself from the outset here too that this being-pervaded can in part occur only in extremely remote relations, yet that it must nonetheless certainly exist in some degree really and truly. After all, even that which can be demonstrated mechanically between masses as attraction permeates the world in quite a similar manner, now perceptibly, now imperceptibly. If one wishes to make this clear to oneself, one should consider how, for example, it admits of no doubt that, just as the gravitating of the celestial bodies toward one another — that is, their mutual attraction — is a certain fact, so too every small freely floating mass attracts the other larger one in proportion to its mass just as it is attracted by it. The falling stone, therefore — that is, the small body floating in the atmosphere that is so powerfully attracted by the so much larger earth — must nonetheless for its part also attract the earth, although of this attraction, because of its infinite slightness, we can perceive just as little as, say, of the propagation of the shock of any artificial explosion through the whole of the telluric mass — a propagation concerning which, however, the English mathematician Babbage has already conducted such interesting calculations, pointing to the infiniteness of every such effect. In a similar manner, accordingly, it is also to be conceived that the entire realm of unconscious life in us is somehow affected and must be affected by the whole circle of life of humanity, of terrestrial life, indeed of the life of the universe, and precisely for the reason that it decidedly appears as an integrating part of that totality; only the manner in which this unconscious is affected must, to be sure, be quite infinitely various.
