While, for example, from the movements of the celestial bodies outside the earth, with the exception of the sun and the moon, so little enters into the feeling of our interior that we may compare it to the attraction of the earth toward the falling stone, and while the remote masses of humanity remain just as foreign to our feeling, the changes in the electrical and magnetic moods of our planet, as well as those of the atmosphere, penetrate just as deeply into our unconscious life as the changes in the life of human beings standing so much nearer to us. Indeed, in this sphere the reciprocal relations are often even the most essential of all, and yet they are all originally only unconscious, although under certain circumstances much from this nocturnal realm can very well actually be communicated to the conscious region, so that one may already surmise from here what we shall later have to set forth more closely — namely, how that strange far-seeing, in dream or waking, of relations to terrestrial or celestial events, or to the destinies of human beings, those strange phenomena of magnetic rapport between distant persons, and so much that has remained an inexplicable riddle to ordinary psychology, can find its complete elucidation only by means of these reflections. In this respect, even the consideration of embryonic life in relation to the life of the mother already offers very remarkable moments. In the still veiled embryonic human being, namely, consciousness has not yet awakened at all; the absolute and generally unconscious still prevails throughout, and precisely for this reason its relation to the circle of life of the mother enclosing it is the most intimate. Unconsciously, the stirrings of maternal life pervade such a developing individual, and only from this standpoint does the remarkable case of so-called maternal impression [Versehen], undeniably occurring under certain conditions, become truly comprehensible to us alone — a process that more than anything else makes it clear how delicate the bond can be that links two lives to one another. Since even in our conscious existence we can become aware that not infrequently some vivid representation of the conscious soul relating to a particular part of the body can instantly and involuntarily produce in that part a certain sensation or even a definite change of function (examples include the sensation we can have in the eye when we vividly imagine a knife stabbing into the eye, or likewise the sensation of watering in the mouth when vividly imagining a sliced lemon, and the like), it thus appears, going even much further, in the case of maternal impression in the pregnant woman, that a vivid representation by the mother of some injury or disfigurement in general does not so much affect the mother's own life, which has become freer through the development of consciousness, but very decidedly does affect the still unconscious life of the child, intimately connected with the mother's life, actually affecting this life and producing there a disfigurement similar to the one seen. The reason for this then obviously lies not alone in the fact that the formative life of the child still exists in a far greater delicacy and mobility, but rather and principally in the fact that unconsciousness in the embryo is still an absolute and complete one, and that therefore what we called the being-incorporated with the general or higher must here necessarily prove itself most intimately. For this reason the English proverb is not at all untrue that says: "The education of the child begins nine months before birth;" for indeed it lies precisely in that greater unconsciousness and therefore more intimate being-fitted into a higher whole, that the embryo must necessarily take more part in everything that moves the mother in whose womb it rests than the born human being can take part in what thereafter occurs around it. How important applications of this law must also arise, moreover, for the psychical life of the animal world, is now self-evident.

[p. 83] [p. 84] [p. 85] It follows, however, also for conscious human soul-life from the foregoing, a very remarkable result, one that has hitherto not been recognised in this sense at all. For since, as mentioned above, everything that belongs to conscious soul-life does not always persist in consciousness but periodically ever returns again into the unconscious, now for this that has periodically become unconscious too, that law for the unconscious in general must attain its validity and contribute to placing what was once in consciousness and later returns there, so long as it is unconscious, always more in rapport with the general than it was before, and thereby also always somewhat changing it within itself. This observation is immediately of exceptional importance for many processes of soul-life. Everyone will discover in themselves the experience that some impression, some representation, when it has long rested unconsciously in the soul and is now called back into consciousness, or, according to the inner lawful movement of soul-life (of which more must be said later), of its own accord again a-

wakens, will upon this re-emergence always have become different in some respect, and will from then on no longer fully resemble its earlier form. In some cases the representation has perhaps been potentiated within itself, having gained in beauty, grandeur, and manifoldness of relations; in others it will have, as it were, regressed and lost in beauty, fullness, and power. Here, then, is where partly the relations to the essential nature of the individual itself, and partly the more intimate rapport that all unconscious soul-life has with the whole of outer nature, must have acted upon the re-formation and development of the representation. The nobler and higher, therefore, the fundamental idea of this existence, the finer and more versatile the rapport with the macrocosm, the more certainly will the representation, the thought, increase in development through that submersion into the unconscious; the lesser the idea and the weaker the general rapport, the more readily can the individual representation thereby also undergo a regression. It is therefore not without deeper reason when already from many artists, poets, and thinkers the remark has been heard: "Only a long holding of a fundamental thought for some significant work in the soul has always favoured its ripening — and every attempt to bring such a thought too quickly to actual presentation in a work of art or science has always harmed the inner completion of such a work;" for even though in such matters the frequent renewed conscious thinking-through truly and essentially advances the work, yet here the emphasis is surely always still more upon that unconscious growth of the representation in the interior. After all, it holds true, moreover, not merely of individual representations, thoughts, and sequences of thoughts that a return to the unconscious elevates and strengthens them; it holds equally of the individual in general, of the entire fundamental idea of the organism. The ancient myth of Antaeus, the son of the earth, who gained new strength from every contact with his mother, repeats itself, with respect to the unconscious, in every human being; in particular, the undeniably refreshing quality that sleep — that is, the periodic return into unconsciousness — has for the conscious soul rests principally upon this ground. How often will one find that a thought that would not become entirely clear to us, or a relation that could not be discovered, after an often only brief sinking into restful sleep, suddenly stands clear before consciousness; indeed, that even individual memories that had perhaps faded through length of time can suddenly, after such a brief vanishing of consciousness, once again stand clear and distinct before the soul: all relations that become comprehensible only through our knowing that in the unconscious a greater, so to speak, generalisation of life prevails, and that therefore whatever plunges into this unconsciousness must also necessarily partake in some way of this generalisation.

[p. 87] Not only, however, does the conscious soul-life change through its submersion into the unconscious in respect of the increase of the general rapport, but the thereby gained enhancement of its energy and productivity has also another and very essential bearing. Precisely because the unconscious is the original, and because its living-itself-forth is most intimately fused with the life of the all, and in particular because, as we have shown above, in the unconscious the concept of fatigue does not exist at all, the slackening, the fatigue, that overtakes all conscious life when its activity has continued for a longer time must also necessarily be diminished as soon as the soul for some time, as it were, relinquishes this conscious-

[p. 88] ness and now returns entirely to the circle of unconsciousness. For all activity is thereby at once, as it were, restored to its original state; it has re-entered that form of psychical life where there can be no question of learning, practising, or unlearning, but where everything happens out of its own inner, that is, divine plenitude of power; and with this, then, it is also necessarily connected that the slackening, the fatigue, which became possible only where the individual life had attempted to raise itself out of the current of the general for a time as something particular, is here now immediately, through the relinquishing of this particularity, truly for the most part or entirely cancelled and removed. The fact that nothing more restores from a strenuous conscious activity of the soul, nothing more diminishes the slackening that has set in for the conscious soul-life, than an often only brief sleep; the fact of the invigoration that our conscious soul-life daily experiences through the regularly returning nightly sleep; indeed, the fact that after the most violent agitations of conscious life, a supervening faint — that is, precisely a temporary total relinquishment of consciousness — often best calms the exhausted life and renders it capable of renewed exertions of strength: all this had long been known, and physicians had drawn from it many an important conclusion for their art, yet the explanation of such a process was lacking, and finds here for the first time its complete demonstration.

### e. Von Dem, was im unbewußten Seelenleben an krankhaften Zuständen vorkommen kann — e. On that which can occur in the way of morbid conditions in the unconscious life of the soul

After it had emerged from the earlier investigations that the primitive unconscious, inasmuch as it is to be called the first revelation of the idea — that is, of a divine and self-existingly powerful being — is incapable of fatigue, just as it requires no gradual practising of its vital activities, the question necessarily arises whether the concept of being ill [Kranksein] can in any way be applied to this life. In truth we find at once that in the primitive and absolutely unconscious there can be absolutely no question of illness. For illness, inasmuch as its concept rests upon the fact that within an organism, alongside the idea of life that conditions the innermost essence of this organism, yet another principle, another and indeed foreign idea, asserts itself, and that thereby the life especially proper to this organism is impaired and disturbed, always presupposes a certain freedom to leave the originally prescribed course of life and to deviate in some way from the path of life no longer prescribed with iron necessity. Precisely this is the reason that, the further we go back in the scale of beings, the more we remove ourselves from the concept of freedom, the more we see the occurrence of illness diminish. Among all creatures known to us, the human being has the melancholy privilege of being able to exhibit the greatest manifoldness of diseases; already in the series of animals the frequency of falling ill and the manifoldness of morbid conditions decreases; in plants there is no longer any question of the most important forms of disease of the higher creatures — of fever and inflammations; and in telluric as in cosmic organisms the concept of illness then ceases altogether. In precisely the same measure, then, as the idea of life rises to consciousness and thereby also to freedom, the predisposition to and the actuality of falling ill also grows; for being destroyed, dying, is indeed, because they are temporal and not eternal, the fate of all organisms, but such things then happen just as little through illness as a human being dies through illness when the fall of a rock crushes him or he otherwise perishes by violent means. While all this thus points to the fact that unconscious soul-life by its nature should not be subject to illness, this nevertheless seems to stand in the most decided contradiction to the fact that in the human organism precisely those systems and organs that have the least share in consciousness and are entirely governed by the unconscious psyche fall ill far more frequently and diversely than those that have especially awakened to consciousness. By far the greatest frequency of illness makes itself noticeable in the life of the blood, in the life of the digestive apparatus, of the glandular system, of the organs of secretion, and so forth, and precisely the spheres of life most fully and actually alone truly awakened to consciousness — the system of nerves together with the spinal cord and brain — are far more seldom the essential seat of illness. This contradiction is, however, only an apparent one. For we must consider that all illness is actually a general one, that once it comes to pass that in the previously normal organism a special organism of disease develops, nothing in the former can remain completely normal. The organism is a totality; it is possible only as such; and as soon, therefore, as this totality is no longer moved by one principle, as soon as a second, foreign principle asserts itself within it, the primitive idea of life can nowhere any longer manifest itself entirely in its proper essence, nowhere can a completely untroubled normal state any longer take place. Wherever, therefore, in us a seemingly ever so local illness develops, never is this or that structure alone ill; rather, the whole human being is ill and only especially suffering at this or that part. Often the disturbances to general well-being are truly only very slight, yet some disturbances — afflictions to some degree — must nevertheless be present everywhere, for otherwise the organism could not truly exist as a whole within itself.

[p. 89] [p. 90] [p. 91] Here, now, it is where, in accordance with the sequence in the dignity of the different circles of life that are found in the totality of the organism, we become aware of a certain gradation in the disposition to falling ill. We see that the higher circles of life, in which the idea lives itself forth most purely and intimately, resist the foreign idea of the organism of disease the most, assert their integrity most decidedly, while the lower circles of life — those through which the possibility of an interaction with the outer world and of the constant renewal of the elements of the organism is given — are also most open to these impressions of the outer world and most readily subordinate themselves to a foreign idea. It therefore already presupposes a high degree of illness when the nervous system, or the brain itself — that is, the innermost region of conscious life — is to be altered in any significant way; whereas with every, even milder, illness, the activity of the blood-system, of nutrition, of secretion, and so forth, tends to be affected. Not, therefore, because these regions serve the unconscious soul-life, but because in the once-diseased organism the lower and more outward-facing regions, which are precisely the region of unconscious soul-life, must also fall more under the foreign principle — that is, here the idea of illness — do we find these unconsciously living regions more subject to illness than the conscious ones. Likewise, it is not the higher — the conscious in us — that is less immediately seized by illness because it is the conscious, but because in it the independence, the freedom, of the organism is most decidedly documented. On the other hand, the inner divine nature of the unconscious, its original foreignness to the concept of illness, its originally inexhaustible health, and at the same time its distinctive, we might say, unconscious wisdom, manifests itself especially in the fact that all the movements of the organism that oppose the principle of illness and strive to lead the sick life back to a healthy condition belong only to unconscious soul-life.

[p. 92] Here we thus arrive once more at a very remarkable aspect of the unconscious, which likewise from this standpoint gains a wholly new light and, for the first time, its true explanation, inasmuch as it thereby at the same time becomes most clearly apparent that illness actually has no power over the realm of truly unconscious soul-life, and that the proposition is perfectly correct that the concept of illness does not exist in and of itself in the utterly unconscious governance of a divine, just as in the moral sense the concept of evil does not; both — illness, the physical evil, as well as evil, the psychical sickness —

first appear with the concept of caprice, of greater independence, of freedom. We said, accordingly, that the unconscious psychical in us is what continually negates illness the most — even though it suffers the most from it — most decidedly counteracts it, and precisely for this reason gradually brings about its annihilation in thousands of cases. To this belongs, then, by no means solely what in this regard from the unconscious at times does come to a dim consciousness and what we are accustomed to designate by the name of the instinct for the choice of the right means for self-healing, but far more than this, that wondrous inner and secret stirring of unconscious life, that so-called healing power of nature [Naturheilkraft], or that physician within the human being, whereby illnesses are gradually undermined, whereby what physicians call "crises" are brought about, and whereby, often by means of quite distinctive turns of organic activity, health is restored with striking rapidity. Precisely for this reason, these remarkable processes emerge all the more decisively the more consciousness is pushed back, the less it is stimulated by sensory impressions, the more perfectly calm and turned inward upon itself the organism remains. Often consciousness must even vanish entirely, as in deep sleep or in fainting; and only then do these phenomena emerge. These processes are in every respect important, but especially for the physician, and can be fully understood only once one has grasped that it is consciousness that actually conditions illness, and that the absolutely unconscious also knows nothing of illness. Just as, then, with respect to consciousness we shall find that beneath all clearly thought and felt representations lies something dark yet most determinate and firm, something unerring — what we therefore very aptly call "conscience" [Gewissen] — which, in regard to the deviations that are called evil, always points back to the right and pure centre, so does the unconscious organic being, which in itself knows nothing of illness, lie at the foundation of everything that is directed against falling ill and is ever endeavouring to restore health — what we have hitherto usually found designated by the name of the healing power of nature. It must moreover be expressly noted that this unconscious governance for the restoration of the organism to its healthy natural condition does not emerge solely in actual illnesses, but that it makes itself felt equally in every external injury. An injury, a wound, a bone fracture is in itself not an illness — that is, it is indeed an affliction of the life properly suited to the organism, but not in consequence of a distinctive foreign idea of illness that co-inhabits the organism, but rather in consequence of the violent action of some power of the outer world directly. However, both the injury and the illness now demand and stimulate a definite doing of the injured or afflicted organism. The processes that are employed, also for the restoration from an injury, in the unconscious life, are therefore no less remarkable than those of the healing activity of the unconscious psyche in actual illnesses; indeed, they are of a wisdom that at every step summons the physician who carefully follows them to admiration. Already the simple closing of an injured vessel and the staunching of the bleeding is in this respect a most important process. How gradually the flow of blood in the injured vessel-channels takes another direction and thereby relieves the pressure against the injured sites, how the wall of the channel itself gradually contracts, how through the coagulating of the blood the distinctive structure called the thrombus forms, and how now distinct vegetative processes are stimulated through whose influence, without anything of it coming to consciousness, the closing of the wound is completed, while at the same time entirely new vessel-channels form and the circulation of the blood, perhaps at first entirely interrupted by the injury in the damaged part, is in this way completely restored — all of this invites the most manifold reflections. With similar remarkableness proceeds the healing of a bone fracture, the re-attachment of severed parts, and, in lower animals, the replacement of entirely lost limbs. All are distinctive stirrings of the unconscious psyche, and if I said above that properly the highest task of knowledge can only be to penetrate with consciousness into the depths of the unconscious soul-life of the world, it is especially the task of medical knowledge to follow these unconsciously healing-artistic stirrings and, for the purpose of their greatest possible intentional promotion — often also in order to imitate them as far as possible in suitable cases and especially to bring them about — to bring them to the clearest knowledge.

[p. 93] [p. 94] [p. 95] And so let this be enough for now to give, in quite general terms, an adequate representation of the relation of illness to unconscious soul-life; how important the aspects that a history of illnesses themselves would have to offer, were it treated from this standpoint, can here only be noted in passing.

[p. 96]

## II. Vom bewußten Leben der Seele — II. Of the Conscious Life of the Soul

If it is to be called the first unfathomable wonder that a world has actually come into being, that it has arisen — been created — then it is the second, equally to be acknowledged, to be contemplated in its consequences and equally impossible to fathom in its origin — that there is a consciousness — that certain ideas do not merely exist as thoughts of a Higher, Divine, Eternal Being, but that these thoughts themselves can, under certain conditions, attain within themselves to self-contemplation, to consciousness, to knowledge of the world, of themselves, and of God, and that thereby the second world can arise, can be created within us — a world after the natural world — the world of spirit. The act by which consciousness discloses itself within the idea, within that which primordially is unconscious, or what amounts to the same for us, existing and reigning within the universal divine consciousness — the act by which the idea, from the moment of becoming conscious, is no longer merely something divine but also feels itself as something divine, and has received the possibility of knowing ever more clearly about the world, about itself, and about God — this act will forever remain for us one that is absolutely inexplicable, one that can only be acknowledged, discussed in its conditions and consequences, but absolutely cannot be demonstrated in its ultimate ground. Consciousness appears as it says in Genesis: "and there was light!" How the light came to be, in what manner light arose from absolute darkness — this is as impossible to say as how consciousness emerges from the absolute unconscious. For all that, one could, in order to further understanding, quite well compare these relations of the spiritual world, founded through the becoming-conscious, in many respects with relations of the natural, or as we may also say, the sensory world. Just as in the latter, too, infinity and indeterminability extend in all directions, so that we find ourselves everywhere in the middle, between an infinitely small and an infinitely great — indeed, just as here too neither a beginning-point nor an endpoint can be given, and we can only manage what lies in our proximity — so also is it in the spiritual world, that is, in the world of our consciousness. Its beginning-point lies for us in an utterly unfathomable distance and we have no concept of it, and the endpoint — which would be the complete cognition of a supreme ground of all existence, a perfected God-consciousness — is likewise absolutely unattainable and indeterminable. The entirely unlimited approximation toward both sides is, on the contrary, entirely freely given, but the actual attainment is conceivable at neither of these goal-points. Only what lies in the middle — the consideration of the reflections of the natural world in the spiritual, and the discussion of all the inner processes of spirit itself — this alone will forever remain the task of its closer and proper cognition. Precisely for this reason it is also not possible to draw an immediate comparison between the state of unconsciousness and that of consciousness. All the

[p. 98] less, however, it is not only not forbidden to consciousness to investigate the conditions under which, in an unconscious being, the wonder of consciousness discloses itself, but the very possibility that the conscious soul might become truly clear about itself is so bound to the observation and investigation of those unconscious states of soul — which, admittedly, can only ever be recognized by their signs, and never immediately — that in this respect too we had to begin all our reflections with the words: "The key to the cognition of the conscious life of the soul lies in the region of the unconscious."

### a. Vom ersten Hervorbilden des Bewußtseins aus dem Unbewußtsein. be — a. Of the First Emergence of Consciousness from Unconsciousness. be

One would think that every human being, because he lives and experiences within himself the transformation from a formerly wholly unconscious state into a conscious one, must already know so much about this process of his own accord that scarcely any scientific consideration of this development would be needed. But this is not so: that strange law, already named and discussed by me in several places under the title "law of the mystery," ordains that precisely some of the most important processes of our life remain shrouded in an impenetrable darkness. We may, with ever so attentive a spirit, try thinking back and recalling the earliest sensations of our childhood — the manner in which consciousness first manifests itself in its earliest expressions — I do not even speak of the very first, altogether, as already noted, utterly inexplicable awakening of it — will always entirely elude our representation. The soul of the child, when in its development it is still near that first awakening, is still too immature to engage in any reflections and endeavours of this kind.

Quite as if it should absolutely turn away from that wondrous inner process, the reflections of the external world draw it wholly to themselves, and it so loses itself in this external that for this first kind of consciousness the word "world-consciousness" has been coined, and it has been maintained that actual consciousness develops only after world-consciousness. Strictly speaking, however, this word is not entirely justified in this sense. "Consciousness" always expresses a "knowing," and a knowing presupposes a knowing subject, and a knowing subject that knew nothing of itself, the knowing one, would be an impossibility — would be a shining thing in which there were no light.

[p. 99] Before we go further, therefore, we shall have to arrive at complete clarity regarding these conceptual determinations, which are very important for the doctrine of the emergence of the conscious from the unconscious. Let the following, then, be considered. That something is affected by the external world, that it reacts to this stimulation — this can occur perfectly well without consciousness and does not in the least presuppose it. Everything and anything in the world — one planet to another, one stone to another, and so forth — already stands in reciprocal interaction in this way; everything acts upon everything else, is under certain conditions determined by influences to counter-effects, thereby shows itself to be an integral part of an infinite cosmic organism, and can yet in and of itself be something wholly unconscious. The possibility of any kind of consciousness rests therefore first of all on this: that between the reception of the influence and the emergence of the counter-effect, a middle term should manifest itself, one immediately representing the idea of the individual, and on both sides — partly receiving the influence, partly determining the counter-effect — which we then call feeling — common feeling, and

[p. 100] in its higher degree, self-feeling. Only through this feeling of oneself does any knowing become possible, and indeed a now darker, now brighter knowing, and such a knowing or conscious life of the soul will then manifest itself in the series of beings at very different levels, to be sure. There is here a level that still stands very close to unconsciousness, where the clarity of representations is slight, where they merge nebulously into one another, and where consequently the representation of a subject and that of objects are only slightly distinguished from each other. Here one may say the soul knows only generally of a world; it knows of itself along with it, as a part of this world, without yet sharply distinguishing and separating itself from this world, and at this level, therefore, its consciousness appears as world-consciousness. Now, the idea that attains this world-consciousness by having succeeded in fulfilling the organic condition — soon to be discussed more closely — without which no representation, and hence neither representation of the world nor of one's own self, becomes its lasting possession — such an idea alone do we designate as soul; if, on the other hand, it develops itself to the clear consciousness of its own existence, then, insofar as it attains self-consciousness, it receives the name of spirit. Both, soul and spirit, may therefore by no means, as has often been presented in psychology, be called two beings existing side by side; rather, just as the soul is the more highly developed idea, so spirit is merely the more highly developed soul as such. From all this it clearly follows that, insofar as we do not define the word "knowing" with the strictness that it must always involve self-consciousness, insofar as we rather also acknowledge a knowing of the world in general, the knowing subject included, we may quite well designate that dark general knowing by the name of world-consciousness, which has indeed been so frequently used for this purpose hitherto.

[p. 101] Let us therefore not oppose world-consciousness to consciousness in general, but rather regard it merely as the first level of any kind of consciousness, as the level which from now on gives the idea the name of soul, and from which the higher developments — namely those of self-consciousness and ultimately of God-consciousness — become possible; then we need fear no contradiction in the name of world-consciousness, and can rather, with sufficient justification, set forth the proposition:

"The first attestation of the consciousness of the soul, immediately following the merely unconscious state, appears as world-consciousness."

Of this earlier state of our soul-life, too — namely of the mere world-consciousness as it is present in the newborn child — we, as beings developed to self-consciousness, that is, as thinking beings, have absolutely no immediate experience any longer, and no definite cognition. Only from analogy, from the sharpest possible apprehension of individual states occurring here and there within us that sink back toward unconsciousness, from observation of the child, and from tracing the states of soul in the animal world as they manifest themselves in outward signs, can we obtain, to some extent within our own self-conscious thinking life, a picture of that state which is indeed conscious, but only generally conscious — of that form of life in which, after all, and indeed at the very first, our own consciousness once documented itself.

As we now gather all this together and endeavour to unite it into a concrete presentation of the becoming-conscious, we shall nevertheless first be compelled to enter more closely into the consideration of whatever conditions can be ascertained, to which, in the domains of life accessible to us, the emergence of any consciousness, and hence first of all of world-consciousness, is indispensably bound. But if we compare the various living beings, if we attend to the developmental history of our own bodily structure, we must above all recognize that the idea can arrive at consciousness within itself only under the condition of a certain formation developed through the agency of the still unconscious idea, only given a certain arrangement of the inner bodily structure of an organic being. There must namely appear a structure at which the finest polarizations of the inner fundamental thought of the whole can continually be active — a structure which, as a purely psychical one, like the original organic primordial substance, remains thoroughly impressionable from the idea, without however being transformed by these impressions into heterogeneous formations; and such a structure is, as the earlier considerations of the various systems showed, only the nervous system. Here it must indeed be acknowledged that, if in this system the first condition of consciousness is given, the primitive idea must already be thought of as being of that energy which, in its manifesting itself through substance, has demanded the development of such a system, a nervous system, and thus the very first ground for the possibility of a consciousness always lies essentially in the idea itself; nevertheless it is certain that, so far as organisms are known to us, before this very development of the nervous system has attained a certain maturity, no consciousness whatsoever, no knowing of one's own state, can possibly develop. The reason for this lies in the following: only where a nervous system has actually come into being as a special primitive

[p. 102] [p. 103] manifestation of the idea, only where all the stirrings which the more heterogeneous non-nervous structures of the organism undergo through their own unconscious life or through the influences of the external world resonate within it and, so to speak, concentrate themselves there — only then is one of those conditions given which must be presupposed if the wonder of consciousness is to be fulfilled. Indeed, it can be followed with the greatest precision, step by step, that the more perfectly the concept of concentration has been represented within the nervous system through the idea's own Promethean unconscious agency, the more perfectly is the first condition for the unfolding of a higher consciousness given. However, in order that the concept of concentration may really come to full representation within the nervous system, a special opposition is required within it as well — namely the development of the distinction between a substance remaining entirely in the primitive semi-fluid cellular state, and a linear or fibre-substance establishing the conducting connection among all these infinite cells or monads. Only through this formation can it become possible that, just as the nervous system is the centre of the higher organism, so now within the nervous system itself a centre of the nerve-mass may be established. The nerve-structure which then presents the most perfect concentration of the primitive semi-fluid cell-structures in contrast to the fibre-structures spreading throughout the whole organism receives, as is well known, the name brain, and this in turn repeats within itself, in the most manifold ways and in the most diverse arrangements, the contrast of fibre and primitive cellular substance. Thus also, insofar as the unconscious life unfolds the structures that make consciousness possible at all, one may say the unconscious — that is, the unconscious as something positive, the unconscious idea — is itself the first condition of the conscious.

[p. 104] The second indispensable condition for the development of a consciousness in the soul is the existence and action of an external world upon the organism. A living being that is perfectly, indeed even to a certain degree, isolated will never attain consciousness. Only by the fact that something external affects the organism, that within it some kind of re-attunement, some stimulation takes place, by which also the innermost, most thoroughly psychical structure — the nervous system — is affected, receives impressions, and immediately recognizes therein that these impressions did not arise from its own innermost life — only thereby does the immediate and first sensation arise that something external is present, that a certain opposition exists between outer and inner; and hence one may say that consciousness finds itself, as it were, first at the external, and for that reason unfolds itself initially as a still indeterminate knowing of the world, the individual included, before it learns to know of itself. If, therefore, we observe the child as its self-consciousness develops, we find that it arrives at the concept of the "I" only late, and that the personification of external appearances is already fairly familiar to it before it rises to the apprehension of its own personality. Children therefore initially still speak of themselves mostly in the third person.

A third essential condition, under which alone consciousness can develop, is in a certain sense the reverse of the first. Namely, just as the Promethean unconscious pre-formation of the nervous system was the first ground of the possibility of consciousness, so upon the Epimethean retention of all

[p. 105] stimulations of the life of the soul — that is, upon memory — rests all possibility of the higher development of consciousness. If the sensations of the manifold influences of the external world did not remain fixed in the soul, if it were not thereby made possible to contemplate simultaneously the diversity of these influences and thereby to arrive at a comparison of them, in order from this comparison then to make possible the knowing of a world in contrast to the individual — then there could never be a consciousness, not even as mere world-consciousness. In order rightly to understand this retention, this memory, and to grasp it in its necessary ground, one must therefore always keep present what was said earlier — that every living being, by virtue of the idea manifesting itself in it, insofar as this idea is something divine, must also partake of the essence of eternity, and that for this very reason its past and future must belong to it in a constant general present. Now, how all progression and relation of formation in the unconscious life is explained from this, we have already noted above. But it will furthermore only become clear from this that, when a truly psychical structure, a nervous system, has developed — when this structure, as the actual sensorium, is moved, or to use a more apt expression, polarized, by the stirrings of the external world in the same manner as by the stirring of its own psyche — changed in the tension-relations of what we call innervation — that, I say, these polarizations will not be thoroughly transient, but must to a certain extent enduringly persist, the past ones always present. No longer is it then merely proper to the unconscious life that the earlier, indeed the earliest state remains unforgotten to it (as we said above, for example, that the plant on its long develop-

[p. 106] mental course from seed to germ, to stem, and to blossom, does not let go of the formation of the first seed-grain from unconscious inner retention, and that only for this reason does it reproduce the seed at the height of its life again in quite the same form from which it set out); rather, what in this manner becomes lasting in the truly psychical system also thereby becomes an inalienable possession of the psyche and now belongs to consciousness — indeed, becomes one of the most important conditions for all consciousness. To be sure, it presupposes a deeper engagement with the doctrines of physiology if one is to comprehend how, to a most fine polarization of a particle of the brain-substance — one perhaps only microscopically discernible to us — some constant presence of a long-past excitation can attach itself — that is, precisely what we now call an enduring representation; but one need only make clear to oneself what the relation of sense-sensations to the excitations of the sensory nerves in the sense-organs amounts to, and one will be better able to understand, better able to grasp that process as well. What infinitely fine oscillation is it, for example, in the auditory nerves, which in organic terms is for us the first condition of the perception of, say, a symphony, indeed of all speech and all sound; what infinitely fine affections does light excite on the inner surface of the retina, after the manner of the processes which in the Daguerreotype process are produced by light on the iodized silver plate, and yet it is precisely this most delicate modification that is the immediate condition for all the manifold perceptions of the eye. Anyone who has not himself investigated such matters is only to be advised to cut open the eye of an animal and attentively examine the seemingly simple, uniformly soft retina, which to the unaided eye appears almost as mere grey mucus.

Let him then tell himself that to certain infinitely fine polarizations and modifications of this substance all the wonders of the world of the eye are attached as an indispensable condition — that even here, images of the visual field can remain and be retained for a certain time — and let him learn thereby to make comprehensible to himself how, to certain still finer physical tension-changes of the mass of those primordial structures of the brain-substance, the arising and becoming-permanent of representations may quite well attach itself psychically. At the very least, it is clear that one may set it forth as a perfectly simple proportional statement: as the sense-sensations relate to the most fine re-attunements in the nerve-structures of the sense-organs, which are no longer demonstrable to our observation, so does what we call an inner representation of the conscious life of the soul relate to certain most fine re-attunements in the primordial structures of the central mass of the nervous system, that is, of the brain; and I merely note in addition that one must not overlook the fact that these re-attunements in the central mass are essentially conditioned by the conduction of the altered innervation-tension of the fibres of the nerves from the sense-organs onward. Since indeed these structures and their innervation-tension persist throughout a healthy normal life, those re-attunements of them also exist — that is, those representations — in a constant present, although by no means always as conscious representations, but rather always periodically becoming unconscious again; and they now give the psyche, precisely as the lasting possession of the psyche, occasion enough: first, for the comparison of them; secondly, thereby again for abstraction among several; and ultimately, when at last an inner, original, divine element appears as judge among different series of representations, for judgment upon the representations, and finally for the cognition of this representing inner self. From all this one may accordingly gather that without that Epimethean faculty of the soul, without what we call "inner retention" [Innerung] in the unconscious and "recollection" [Erinnerung] in the conscious, consciousness would be an impossibility, and that herein, therefore, there truly lies an important third condition of the becoming-conscious.

[p. 107] [p. 108] Upon closer consideration, however, we are compelled to add immediately a fourth condition to the three previously discussed. Namely, the comparison of different forms of soul-life shows us, and our own judgment also tells us, that not merely the presence of multiple representations in lasting persistence in general can be regarded as a condition of consciousness, but that this manifold of representations must necessarily reach a certain measure, a certain scope — must be a larger and richer one — if it is to be possible for the wonder of consciousness to manifest itself. A definite measured degree cannot of course be established here, but that such is required admits of absolutely no doubt. In the child, consciousness always develops only after a certain material has been accumulated in a certain scope, and in the idiot with a stunted, small, atrophied brain-formation — that is, where that mass of the primordial structures of the brain-substance, recognized as the organic condition of a representation, had from the outset arisen in too small a quantity — a sufficient manifold of representations is never possible; and though even there a general world-consciousness is not absent, yet the higher form of self-consciousness will in such a case not come to manifestation. Just as little will this be possible in animals generally, and especially in the lower ones, where the development of the central masses of the nervous system shows so limited a development and on the lowest levels is still entirely absent, inasmuch as a certain plurality of individual nerve-masses distributed through the organism still takes their place. It is precisely because this is so that the importance of the diversity of brain-formation among different animals and different human beings rests. Physiology, and particularly what one might call the physics of the nervous system, demonstrates with certainty that a large mass — but, mark well! — of normally formed primordial substance of the brain is necessarily presupposed if a large mass of representations is to become the lasting possession of the psyche; and just as little as someone with a narrowly formed chest and small lungs is capable of as vigorous a respiration as another with a wide, vaulted chest and strongly developed lungs, just as little is a person with a small skull and meagre brain able to accumulate such a wealth of representations as one with a wide, regularly vaulted skull and strongly developed brain.

[p. 109] Here too, when physiology had occasionally already earlier suggested similar relations, people complained about the dependence of spirit upon body; such results were therefore regarded with a certain anxiety; people spoke of materialism and truly believed the highest interests of humanity to be endangered by such cognitions. Yet on the one hand, all such disadvantages and dangers, as they were called, would still have to be endured so long as one could not demonstrate that the cognitions themselves were erroneous; on the other hand, however, such skewed views will always be merely the consequence of holding a wrong representation of the psyche — that is, of the fundamental idea of our existence — of postulating for a so-called vital force a special alien something that supposedly builds the body and prepares it for the soul as a dwelling not always exactly fitting, and of refusing to comprehend that everything we call body is nothing but the manifestation and appearance of an unconscious psyche that has come to realization in substance — that is, of the fundamental idea of our existence, which at first operates only unconsciously. Once one has made it clear to oneself that it is this idea, this divine element itself, from which it proceeds how and in what manner the organism in general, and its nervous system and brain in particular, shall be formed, and that it therefore depends entirely and essentially upon the higher or lower energy of this idea whether the mass of the primordial structures of the brain shall manifest itself so richly and significantly that a great life of representations can unfold upon it — then one will recognize that it is always only the idea itself whose admittedly at first purely unconscious agency governs the form of the consciousness that only later unfolds. It is not the soul of the idiot, insofar as it has become only a weak conscious being, that bears the blame for its low level of spirit, but neither is it the body and the poorly developed brain of the same, as something somehow independent and governing consciousness; rather, the originally slight or forcibly externally restricted energy of this life-idea, and the slight primitive significance of this idea unfolding into actual existence, are themselves the ultimate ground of such imperfect development. Just so in the reverse case. The originally powerful idea unconsciously, Prometheanly, forms a powerful normal brain of an organism, powerful enough to grasp a multitude of the most diverse tension-relations as the physical expression of psychical representations, and from the comparison of all these — a past persisting in a present — there now necessarily develops the apprehension of a higher present, a higher self-consciousness, indeed God-consciousness, which is not attained through merit of the soul as a conscious being, but which can nevertheless sometimes be obscured and corrupted through a bad conscious administration of the psyche in its own realm. But of all these relations, important in very many respects, more detailed discussion must follow later; here it was only necessary first to make clear that a certain quantitative and qualitative development of the organic must be called an indispensable condition for the soul's becoming-conscious.

[p. 110] [p. 111] So much for the conditions of consciousness. Let us now consider the history of its gradual awakening.

Here it would admittedly likewise be the case that everyone would need only to look into his own inner being to behold the entire picture of this process, if only the eye could see itself and the spirit could behold its own becoming. But we are directed only to the observation of the phenomenon in others and to analogy, and these show us the following.

We can trace the individual moments of the development of the conscious life of the soul along two great sequences: namely, in the formation of the soul in the series of animals, and in the formation of the soul and of the spirit in the child and in the adult.

#### 1. Von Heranbildung der Seele in den Thieren — 1. Of the Formation of the Soul in Animals

Let us first cast a glance at the totality of the epitelluric creatures, how their multiform appearance arises, and indeed arises through a wholly unconscious agency of infinite ideas; we must at once become aware how important the distinction is which we pointed out earlier, when the subject was the manner in which the idea in general comes to manifest itself as a manifold, as an organism. For it was already shown at that time that a twofold direction in this agency is unmistakable: first, the striving to repeat the simplest — the primal form, the monad of all formation — the vesicular microscopic hollow sphere — innumerable times, always to see itself innumerable times again as unity, and thereby to manifest an indeterminable number of unities, or, as one may also express it, to create the greatest possible multiplicity of appearance; and second, the striving, through all this manifold, to hold fast the higher image of a totality and to present in this manifold a wholeness. I said, then, that attention to this twofold distinction is important, because in it lies the reason that the totality of all organisms developing within the range of our cognition is determined either by the predominance of the one or the other direction. From that one dark realm of those strange simplest creatures, which I have designated by the name of protorganisms as the zone of indifference between the plant kingdom and the animal kingdom,

[p. 112] and whose structures are themselves nothing but monads, simple cells often only variously articulated and formed, those two opposing radiations diverge entirely according to the two aforementioned oppositions. The plant kingdom rests through and through, as in every individual plant so also in the manifold of its forms, essentially upon the endless repetition of a fundamental form; it is through and through cellular construction, repeating itself into the infinite, and therefore from each individual cell always possibly reproducing the whole once again — [p. 113] in short,

and precisely for this reason the concept of totality never perfectly concluding. Even the layperson, without being aware of the higher ground, therefore separates parts of the plant with different representations and feelings than with an animal; he will, as it were, always take the former for a piece-work and the latter always for a whole; to pluck a leaf, a flower, is done with pleasure; to detach a limb of a living animal will always be painful to him. The plant, for that reason, has no viscera and no organs differing in the sense in which the animal's do — hence neither can such a primordial structure as the nervous system remain in contrast to essentially heterogeneous organs; it remains essentially always only a multiplicity of unities; it lacks such an inner centre as the animal possesses; and, although it too cannot be without a certain totality, the concept thereof is never as fully enclosed as in the animal kingdom. From this it follows, on the one hand, that the concept of higher and lower organization, which in the animal kingdom so clearly manifests itself, always expresses itself very imperfectly in the plant kingdom (it will always remain disputed which plants should be regarded as the highest); on the other hand, it follows that, since the plant lacks a truly central system and thereby a perfect bond of unity and wholeness, there can here by no means yet be any question of any kind of consciousness. If, therefore, we are accustomed to designate by the name of soul only that idea in which some consciousness has actually developed, then it clearly follows from the above that it cannot be said of the plant that a soul is given to it. In this too, the natural sense has guided the peoples quite rightly, and neither in our language nor in others has there ever been any talk of a soul of plants, although that a distinctive

[p. 114] divine agency creates and forms in every plant organism has by no means gone unrecognized; rather, already by the Greeks, quite like the agency in springs, lakes, breezes, and clouds, it was brought nearer to the form of intuition in its own divine figures — whereby it must be well noted that, where the idea itself unfolds into soul as in the animal, those peoples very perceptively never used to invent a special divine figure for this.

As for the animal kingdom, it is truly remarkable to trace closely how consciousness, and with it the concept of the soul, emerges so very gradually. Even more than in the gradation of the animal kingdom, this is to be observed when we follow the developmental history of the individual animal. Take the egg of the bird: it cannot be denied that it is the earliest, most imperfect form of the bird itself; it is alive, even if often for a long time in a state of latent life; but all the elements are already united in it which, so to speak, only await the action of external warmth, come whence it may, in order to manifest themselves forthwith in the entire organization of the animal. Nevertheless, no one will ascribe a soul to the egg as such, as certainly as the unconscious idea is already at work in it. As soon, however, as this idea begins to manifest its distinctive character in the articulation of the embryonic organism — as soon as the conditions which we have set down as indispensable for the development of a consciousness have thereby been fulfilled, namely and especially the formation of a nervous system and of the means of its intercourse with the external world — the idea will also forthwith develop within itself into a soul. To the egg developed into a bird we now ascribe a soul, whereas of the egg we were not yet permitted to say that in it there was a soul.

[p. 115] It must hereby become clear that, as definitely as the distinction between the idea in itself and the idea unfolded into soul can be expressed in the abstract [in abstracto], yet in the concrete [in concreto] it is absolutely impossible to fix a definite point of time in the developmental history of the individual creature, and hence equally impossible to fix a sharp division in the series of animals, at which the essential nature of the soul suddenly emerges in the idea. In approximately the same way, we can say with certainty in the history of vegetation that the concept of the seed-grain is a different one from that of the plant itself, and yet if we were then to specify quite precisely where, in the germination of the seed, the concept of the seed ceases and that of the plant begins, we would be attempting an impossibility.

All this taken together does indeed make us recognize that, if we wish to undertake investigations into the formation of the soul in animals, we may not so much believe ourselves able to find the starting-point of the manifestations of consciousness itself, but rather that we must seek out the facts of an undoubtedly already existing consciousness, and compare them according to their diversity, in order to obtain a clear picture of how diverse the appearances of a soul-life can be before, through a higher concentration within it, the manifestation of a self-consciousness, and with it the disclosure of spirit within the life of the soul, becomes fact. It must, however, also be noted here in general that the emergence of self-consciousness from world-consciousness is related just as the emergence of the latter from unconsciousness — namely, that to fix quite precisely the starting-point of this development is impossible. Although, therefore, it can be stated with certainty that the self-conscious spirit is first given only to the human being, yet the most diverse self-feelings, which often border very closely on self-consciousness, already appear in animals, and in the human being too the juncture at which the spirit is born within the soul is absolutely impossible to fix with precision.

[p. 116] The question therefore arises first of all quite in general: in what manner does the idea that has become soul, and within it some kind of world-consciousness, manifest itself in the animal?

The answer is: "By the fact that the three rays of all soul-life, upon whose unfolding the possibility of all consciousness also rests — namely cognition, feeling, and will — appear here for the first time in their separation." For everything that has any determinate existence, everything in which under any form an idea manifests itself, indeed every piece of one of these forms, where not even the remotest trace of consciousness surfaces, has, as was already mentioned earlier, a certain relation to other existing things — that is, it is in a certain way affected by others, and in turn affects others — but then also entirely without a trace of spontaneity. The stone is carried along by the stone and strikes something else; it can be warmed and warm something else, be illuminated and illuminate, and so forth — but always influence and counter-effect are inseparable. Just so does every individual behave in whose appearance a special idea is to complete its distinctive life-circle, but in which as yet absolutely no kind of consciousness has disclosed itself. The reciprocal interactions with others can be very manifold; in very diverse ways it can be claimed by the external and claim something else, but all only according to the unconditional necessity of a higher general life. Thus the egg is affected by external

[p. 117] warmth, and with necessity, as soon as the right degree of warmth acts upon it — be it from the mother-animal or from the incubator — the cells in the germinal disc crystallize, dissolutions and new formations arise, the fluids circulate, the heart-muscles twitch, and gradually the entire still soulless creature forms — still obeying necessity alone. Only when the formation has now reached a certain height, when the nervous system has consolidated itself more, and when the shell has been burst which hindered intercourse with a larger circle of the external world, does something new appear between influence and counter-effect — we call it feeling, in its first most indeterminate form common feeling; and as soon as this third, middle element, between a receiving from without and a reacting against something external, has developed, the unconditional necessity of all life-phenomena ceases, and it is now in the region primarily determined by the nervous system where for the first time a certain independence asserts itself, where now an inner principle appears with a certain self-sufficiency, against which, as it were, it is measured whether the influence shall have the counter-effect as its consequence or not. From now on it is no longer unconditionally necessary that the counter-effect should perfectly correspond to the influence; a small influence can have a strong counter-effect as its consequence, and vice versa; a middle determining element has appeared — the idea of this existence has, as it were, now first gained a representation, a certain immediate validity in external appearance. This starting-point of consciousness, this point of the first immediate manifestation of the soul — it is this which must be observed with the greatest attentiveness if the unfolding of the life of the soul is to become clear to us; here lies the primal phenomenon of all these processes, and the

[p. 118] although we can gain the experience of this starting-point itself neither in ourselves nor in other creatures, yet that it must exist, and how it must exist, is perfectly accessible to mature cognition.

Strictly speaking, one could accordingly say: the progression in the formation of the life of the soul rests upon altered numerical relations; for if the idea in itself is the one, the monad, then its first manifestation in actuality — but still as something unconscious — will appear as a duality, which presents itself through receptivity and counter-effect, whereas only its higher manifestation in the conscious individual becomes a triplicity, which indeed also lends itself to the reception of an influence and the execution of a counter-effect, but which between the two contains yet a third — namely a darker or brighter knowing of one's own state, a feeling of one's own existence, and indeed as something that increasingly and ever more decisively governs influence and counter-effect.

But it must be highlighted here, precisely where this articulation first becomes clear to us, as a most remarkable moment in the distinctive character of this third element — namely that this third and actually highest element, that in which the idea reveals itself most immediately and for the first time as something capable of freedom, can nevertheless never manifest and clarify itself entirely in and of itself, but always only in and through the two other moments — only in and through the receiving and cognizing of the influence, and the executing and willing of the counter-effect. For every kind of self-feeling, every dark knowing of one's own state, indeed every clearer self-consciousness, is always conditioned partly by a multiplicity of representations — that is, received sense-impressions that have become lasting and are remembered in the soul, which now form, as it were, the letters that the soul needs in order to write the word "I" with them or to read it therein — and partly by a multiplicity of likewise remembered acts of will, whereby in the course of desire and love, or in the endeavour of repulsion and hatred, the soul expresses its relation to the external world. We may persist in any feeling, in any sensation of our own state — that we are in this state can always become clear to us only by the fact that all representations, remembered and newly entering ones, assume a special colouring, a distinctive mood, and that the soul finds itself at this moment in a distinctive relation of attraction and repulsion. In short, that we behold and love ourselves presupposes an already prior seeing and desiring directed outward; the knowing of the self becomes possible only because there is for us a language in which we are at all able to think.

[p. 119] We see from this that in this way, and in pursuing these reflections, we arrive by another path once again at the understanding of the proposition set forth earlier — namely that without a system which, like the nervous system, mediates the becoming-permanent of representations, the development of any consciousness whatsoever remains absolutely impossible. Beyond this, it will of course also follow that the higher the energy of this middle element of self-feeling is, the more the receiving, the cognizing, is developed to greater sharpness, and the counter-acting, the willing, to true freedom.

But let this now rest for the present and be carried further later; at the moment it was first of all indispensable, in order to be able to purposefully trace the different levels of soul-life and especially of consciousness in the animal world, to send ahead the above regarding the manner in which consciousness comes about at all and where its starting-point lies.

[p. 120] This cannot be the place to attempt a detailed comparative psychology or psychic zoology; rather, from the enormous quantity and manifold of the animal world, we select individual great and significant facts and lay them side by side, so that it may become clear therefrom that much from this soul-life is indeed repeated in the human being, but that in him, from that which is, as it were, preparatory, far higher developments then emerge and can be attained.

The most significant division of the animal kingdom, also in relation to its soul-life, is that of aquatic and aerial animals. All aquatic animals, those that are entirely and for life consigned to this denser embryonic element, show comparatively far lesser developments of the life of the soul than the aerial animals. The lowest of that series are those where a radial nervous system uniting the entire body into a unity is still absent, even though individual masses in the substance undoubtedly already have the significance of nerve-mass; to this class belong the acalephs. In them that middle element especially manifesting the idea — feeling — is still at its darkest, and the unconscious soul-life prevails throughout. Properly speaking, only what I have designated above and in my System of Physiology as sensing [Erfühlung] — perception [Perceptio] — and what we may regard as the preparation for actual feeling, extends here over the entire organic mass; the feeling of inner organic need, and namely first of all nutrition, and then every external incidental influence, alone determine the creature's counter-effect. Such a counter-effect can therefore never yet receive the name of action; rather, it appears as the result of the unconscious driving or pressing of the idea — that is, of the drive — insofar as it is not merely simple reaction; both being forms of activity that occur also in higher animals, indeed in the human being, insofar as the primitively unconscious region of their soul-life extends. The highest doing of the lower aquatic animals is therefore either to be compared, in higher creatures, to the muscular contraction upon galvanic or similar stimulation, or it stands on a level with their activities arising immediately from the drive toward reproduction or toward nutrition. All doing on this level therefore still appears as something determined with the same necessity as, for instance, that in the egg when in its interior, through external incubation warmth, that inner reciprocal action of substances finds itself occasioned to the most diverse attractions and repulsions and to the articulation of the cellular structure; and one might say that in this respect too these creatures deserve the name of egg-animals, as in many another respect besides. The stimulus from without excites the contraction; the approach of something suitable for nourishment excites the movement of the catching-organs which convey it to the mouth; and therewith all expression of the still so dark soul-life is all the more concluded, as the species often, as in Hydra, is maintained and multiplied only through an immediate bud-like outgrowth of the old organism into new young organisms, and thus the reproductive drive also expresses itself only as growth.

[p. 121] Slightly more does a soul-life develop in the molluscs of the water, at least in their lower orders — the ascidians, bivalves, and snails — and everywhere the realm of unconsciousness is here far greater than that of a dawning world-consciousness. The most important phenomenon in the external sphere is the emergence of separate sex, and the union of the separated. Different individuals must here first feel that they mutually need each other for life, that only in certain intimate approaches can they reach the summit of their existence, the highest self-sensation. Here for the first time series of desires, intentional movements, and new sensations are set in motion, in which the inner realm of soul-life expands and enriches itself; and although the world is still reflected only dimly and darkly in these souls, yet it is here already another individual of the same species in which the first finds, as it were, and admittedly only momentarily and in no lasting life-relation, a substitute for the external world — so that here, on the lowest level and only as an organic foreshadowing, there emerges what can later be repeated in the highest psychical sense in the human being, when in another soul the substitute for the entire external world dawns upon him. In general, however, the cognition of the external also remains imperfect, feeling dark, and action still essentially either reaction or drive.

[p. 122] Somewhat more freely does the dignity of the soul manifest itself in another respect in the highest of the molluscs — the cuttlefish species — and then in the fishes. The world is reflected more manifoldly in them through more strongly developed sense-organs; the manifold of representations must become larger, the relations between the individual and the external world more numerous; yet on the other hand, the sexual relationship recedes more, for a true copulation is still proper to these creatures only in a few genera, and precisely thereby they lack the organically necessary reciprocal relationship with another creature of the same kind, which, as noted, can be in a high degree developmental and fostering for the innermost life of the soul. In place of this immediate relation of two creatures to one another, as it is expressed in the sexual relationship, there appears instead in several genera a certain herding together, which is to be regarded as the further development of that still actual organic connection such as we see in many bivalves, ascidians, and so many polyps growing together in masses, and in which we at the same time find the prototype of what we recognize in higher creatures as the social drive. On the whole, however, herding together is less conducive to the formation of world-consciousness than the paired sexual relationship, as it occurs especially in certain molluscs (the snails), since already here manifold relations have less formative effect than simpler but more intimate ones. In any case, in these deficiencies lies the reason that a hint of higher soul-life is not at all found at this level — no artistic drive, no attachment, no trace of any special understanding. The highest is that in the fishes a certain magnetic moment of striving toward definite regions of the world manifests itself in their remarkable migrations, and secondly, that by the fact that they become capable of habituation (for example, gathering at certain times at the sound of a bell) they reveal the most decisive signs of memory.

[p. 123] Higher still rises the life of the soul in the realm of aerial animals, and everywhere this elevation is documented chiefly by the fact that the middle element — the feeling of one's own existence, self-feeling — emerges more distinctly and places itself, separated, in the middle between the sensing and ultimately the understanding of the external world; the latter, however, is still always a wholly necessary one — a drive and not a free will. Here too we have in this necessity a good measure for gauging the increasing unfolding of the life of the soul, and it is clear that it always indicates a higher level of the latter when even

only in a single expression of the creature a greater inner spontaneity betrays itself, than when some remarkable but wholly unconscious, more purely organic activity — for instance, the building of some work of art, an artificial cell- or web- or nest-construction — is necessarily always practised in the same manner. The latter will mostly occur quite organically, or, as one often incorrectly expresses it, "mechanically." Thus the larvae of many aquatic insects build themselves an artificial tube out of sand or bits of wood — a kind of exoskeleton — in which they await their maturity and metamorphosis; and this formation occurs with the same necessity with which, in worms such as the Serpula, a calcareous tube grows involuntarily around the animal from secreted calcareous fluid, or the exoskeleton of crustaceans renews itself as it grows, or a lost limb is replaced in the animal. One could say that in those artificial little constructions only a semblance of consciousness is at work; and indeed it is often difficult to draw the boundary where growth, the purely organic forming, ceases and where artificial forming, the artistic drive, begins. The two characteristic moments indicated above for all unconscious soul-life, still so greatly predominant here — the necessity, and the immediate capacity proper to the unconscious life of the soul, without practice and without habituation — may, as stated, serve us quite well here as guiding thread and criterion. Moreover, as the animal kingdom rises from the water, sense-perception becomes freer and more manifold, and thereby too the inner life of the soul must become richer and consciousness must develop higher. Of special furthering effect is it then that here, almost without exception, the sexes freely confront each other and mutually need each other, indeed that here for the first time a higher sociality of many of the same kind, fie

[p. 125] and in a true communal life founded upon the fulfilment of common purposes, provides the prototype of the association of many into a state, which first truly comes into being only in the human being. Everything, however, still vacillates so greatly back and forth between unconditional necessity and slight willfulness, even in the life-circle of the seemingly most intelligent creatures of the lower classes of this series (one need only think of the state of ants and bees), that scarcely any intimations of a special psychical spontaneity can be attributed to the individual. Yet the phenomena of communal life at this level are psychologically very instructive, since from them one can recognize with special clarity that a very definite, and in many respects freer, individuality of the idea can manifest itself very decidedly in a multiplicity of beings, while all the particular members of this multiplicity betray a far weaker psychical development. The idea of the species, of which mention was already made earlier, asserts itself nowhere more than in such occurrences as an organic whole, over against the idea of the individual. Nowhere better than here can one therefore also come to an understanding of how it is by no means a condition that an idea must manifest itself solely and alone through the single thing which we commonly call an organism — a real organism — but rather that very definite cases, and in abundance, occur where an idea can manifest itself only through a totality — an ideal organism — consisting of many subordinate organisms. An observation that deserves much attention in order to grasp and understand the higher ideal organism of humanity that encompasses all individual human beings. One need only study the history of a bee-state, the wisdom of the arrangements prevailing therein, the rearing of the queen, the killing of the drones, the regular departures of new generations, and so forth, and certainly the thought of a deliberating, foreknowing, and remembering soul of the whole will present itself to the most unbiased overview as indispensable to assume, while each individual bee, taken by itself, will reveal only very dark traces of intelligence and willfulness, and least of all deliberation and mastery over the others.

[p. 126] It is now very interesting to trace how, in the higher classes of aerial animals — particularly in birds and mammals — from necessity and immediate capacity, the voluntary and the possibility of learning gradually emerge as signs of a conscious life rising higher. It is clear that the slightest capacity to learn something and to apply the learned with some freedom to even the simplest task will be a sign of a far higher soul-development than the unconsciously innate ability to accomplish something ever so artful but with unconditional necessity. In this respect, far too little distinction has hitherto been made; the so-called artistic drives, by which bees build their cells, ants their chambers, birds their nests, and beavers their timber-works, have been far too much regarded as signs of a special individual intelligence and compared far too much with the works of intention and freedom as they can be executed by self-conscious individuals. Everything, however, that appears with necessity, that is immediately known without being learned, and that always essentially repeats itself in one and the same manner, bears precisely thereby the mark of the unconscious life of the soul and accordingly belongs to an entirely different category. The occasion by which one has no doubt been quite especially led to ascribe so often a higher significance to all this doing is:
