that in the execution of the same, a kind of deliberation, a certain choice, a certain skill in overcoming present difficulties frequently becomes visible. It is as if the unconscious original capacity sometimes took on the appearance of wanting to raise itself to consciousness — much as in the exercise of a conscious willing (as was shown earlier) it is indeed the highest when this capacity again becomes wholly unconscious. Cases of such emerging semblance of consciousness are, for instance, when the spider is already careful to seek out the place for her web with circumspection: she stretches the threads this way or that, according as the given locality demands it; indeed, if the wheel lacks tension downward, she attaches, as Weber once observed, a little stone to a hanging thread. Just so do bees, wasps, ants, and birds choose the places for laying their combs and nests, and in doing so circumvent with apparent skill many an opposing difficulty. Nevertheless, there is in this no more evidence of a spirit already become free than in a hundred similar traits in the life of plants. Let one examine an ivy climbing up a wall; let one notice how the fine rootlets of the tendrils seemingly carefully seek out every little roughness and use it for their hold; let one observe how a sprouting plant-germ, seemingly intentionally bending, avoids a stone, how it forces apart a piece of rotten wood, squeezing through a crack it has found; let one follow the direction of roots, how they seem intentionally to seek moisture, extending only where water is to be found in the soil; indeed, let one attend to many traits of our own unconscious life, when its distinctive activity heals wounds, absorbs extravasations, and so forth — and in all of this one will be able to convince oneself that here a wisdom, an apparent deliberation, manifests itself which we might often find deeper and more remarkable than everything of the kind that reveals itself in the conscious spirit, and which nevertheless one may not equate with the higher conscious life of the soul. Already at the outset of all these reflections we had therefore regarded it as an important task to make clear the wisdom, the Epi- and Promethean quality, the divine element even of the unconscious idea, and one should therefore not regard it as a disparagement when we deny to those stirrings of the animal soul the relation to an independent spiritual being; but the distinction itself is nonetheless to be sharply emphasized. It should therefore not surprise us if in the stirrings of the animal soul that we call artistic drives, beyond the marvellous and the sagacious that is proper to them in any case, special traits of apparent freedom and intelligence are perceived emerging according to circumstances; these traits are, for all that, no more to be attributed to a soul-life truly developed to self-consciousness and freedom than those mentioned phenomena of seemingly special free choice in plant-life, or in the activity of the unconscious part of our own soul, as it asserts itself in the restoration of the healthy integrity of our existence.

[p. 127] [p. 128] We have now, however, noted above that just as it is impossible to determine quite precisely the moment at which the essential nature of the soul develops out of that of the mere idea, so it remains equally impossible to indicate quite precisely where the distinctive character of spirit — that is, of the self-conscious soul — emerges from the soul that has only attained world-consciousness. If we attend to the life of the higher animals, many indications of a cer-

[p. 129] tain spiritual development already emerge darkly, and more clearly in the highest animals some such indications, the closer determination of which we must now attempt to set forth. In the spiritual life, too, however, there is much qualitative diversity and higher and lower degrees thereof. If we study this gradation attentively, a very remarkable moment presents itself to us, one which admittedly can receive closer discussion only in the coming sections, but of which we must not omit to mention the essentials in advance already here. For we discover, first of all, in the history of the developing spirit a certain repetition of the history of the whole organism — a gradual growing and increasing, and indeed particularly a gradual formation upward from a childhood to a puberty and ultimately to the producing of new ideas. We find, therefore, that at first the spirit merely consolidates, as it were, its own existence, that it first only orders the relations of its world of representations, regulates the relations of the individual representations among one another — in short, that it first learns to understand their relation to its own individuality, and we call it at this level the understanding. At a higher level, the soul begins to operate independently with the understood representations, to combine them in its own distinctive way and to generate from them new representations such as were never offered to it from without; and this is, as it were, the driving youth-period of the spirit, in which precisely that takes place within it which in the real organism we call the development of puberty. At this level we give the spirit, insofar as it operates and creates in such a new-generating manner, the name of imagination. Finally, however, the spirit perceives, in a way that we can only make clear later, the secret of unity in multiplicity; it perceives under this activity more and more of its own divine Carus, Psyche.

[p. 130] nature and of that which, as something divine, lies at the ground of all being — in short, it arrives at the perceiving of the idea, that is, at reason [Vernunft]; and from now on it will behold ever more the eternal, and ideas will emerge before it ever more clearly, and as it first designates these more sharply in the world of representations and at last learns to give them life through science and art, there arises in the spirit what we call the higher productivity of the soul.

Let this be indicated only provisionally and quite in general concerning the development of spirit! Now we return again to the consideration of the souls of animals, to see from where and how far a development of spirit can already here assert itself.

Already the most superficial consideration of the higher animal soul according to the above presuppositions must, however, yield that only the lowest level of a self-conscious spirit — the understanding — can emerge here and actually does emerge, for nowhere in the animal world do we yet encounter traces of what we may designate as imagination or even as reason. But what immediately presents itself as of great importance for the sharper insight into the life of the soul in general is that, in exactly the same proportion as the first level of spirit appears, those remarkable manifestations of an unconscious life revealing itself with special wisdom and art beyond the organization — as drives — immediately recede, and that therefore those striking facts of artistic drives, migratory drives, and so forth, which we encountered in the lower classes, disappear the more, the more the first expressions of an intelligent soul are noticed.

[p. 131] The first intimations of spirit, as we find them in birds in the capacity to understand the human voice, to distinguish different persons, to imitate foreign sounds, to learn some movements not natural to them, do not yet exclude all those artistic drives demanded with necessity and signs of a certain, so to speak, unconscious intelligence. It is otherwise, however, in the higher, more thoroughly developed orders of mammals — the pachyderms (elephant and horse), the ruminants (llama and sheep), the beasts of prey (dog), and the quadrumana (ape). Here the signs of those higher manifestations of the unconscious life tend to disappear entirely, and instead the signs of a beginning self-conscious life emerge — in the often so distinct deliberating and choosing between two objects, in the so remarkable formability of their soul-life by the human being, in the so distinct memory, in the definite attachment to another individual, and in the so important marks of a specially pronounced individuality of each single animal of the same species.

For all that, the animal, even the most perfect, remains in the spiritual a child; it never reaches what I called above the spiritual puberty, let alone that there should be any question of spiritual productivity. Here, however, a remarkable compensation must be noted. The animal would evidently, given this slight development of spirit, be in many respects endangered in the attainment of its life-purposes, did not precisely the predominance of the unconscious in it provide an essential substitute. For we noted above that there lies in the unconscious a certain universality of existence; it is pervaded by a thousand stirrings of the world that escape the conscious spirit. Precisely insofar as the higher development of the spirit is denied to the animal and the unconscious predominates in it, it can now also do very much immediately, and knows and beholds in a dreamlike manner a multitude of relations and connections that are no longer possible in quite the same way for the awakened higher consciousness of the human being. Already Cuvier therefore once used the expression (without, however, properly justifying it according to the full development of its grounds): "the animal, if one rightly studies its remarkable instincts and drives, is to be regarded as a kind of somnambulist." Thus, just as on the lowest level of the animal kingdom and with the weakest degree of mere world-consciousness all determination of life, including outward life, proceeds for the most part only from the unconscious, so too even into the higher regions of animal life — because even there the development of self-consciousness always comes about only imperfectly — the unconscious side of the soul, through its — I would say — immediacy, is of essential influence in facilitating the attainment of the animal's life-purposes and in supporting and elevating what we have here called the childhood of its spirit.

[p. 132] It must now, moreover, be considered that even what is attained in the animal in the way of childhood of the spirit must still necessarily be regarded as essentially different from the childhood of the spirit as it presents itself in the human being, and I must at the same time draw attention here, provisionally, to a truth by no means yet properly acknowledged, about which we shall have to speak more extensively when discussing the human spirit. It is, namely, to be known and to be set forth as a second anticipatory remark: that everything we call understanding, imagination, and reason, and designate as different levels of spiritual development, is by no means one single unalterable something, but is thoroughly in-

[p. 133] dividual and most diversely various. We have quite wrongly accustomed ourselves to regarding the special human faculties — understanding — reason — by means of which we attempt to appropriate certain truths that are admittedly in themselves eternal and unalterable, themselves as something eternally self-same and unalterable, when in fact they are related as, say, the eye, which in every human being and in every animal beholds that one remarkable tension-relation — called light — each time in a qualitatively different and distinctive way. For as much as, in the large and essential, almost all modes of seeing by eyes agree, yet strictly speaking everyone sees not merely, as is well known, his own rainbow, but altogether his own light- and colour-world. In the same way I shall later draw attention to how infinitely diverse that can be, and according to the infinite diversity of human individualities must be, which we call understanding and reason, and how false is the representation that every faculty of this kind is one single self-same thing, of which merely a greater quantity has been allotted to one person and a smaller to another, and whereby the thousandfold qualitative nuances which faculties of this kind possess in different persons remain entirely unnoticed. As said, all this must come to closer discussion later; here the point is only to call attention to what influence the cognition of this truth has for the understanding of animal life and particularly of the animal soul. Whoever in the psychology of the animal cannot free himself from the notion that

In the third volume of my System of Physiology I have shown that the cases where a person sees no blue, for instance, or another cannot distinguish red and green, are only the maximum of this diversity, but that actually no human being sees in quite the same way as another, because he himself is another, and no eye is perfectly identical to another.

[p. 134] the spirit, insofar as it begins to develop in the animal, is something essentially, and indeed qualitatively, different from that of the human being — whoever thinks here only of a plus and minus will never arrive at a closer understanding. It is here absolutely necessary to proceed from the premise that the animal has its own particular understanding, just as it has a qualitatively definite distinctive soul, or more correctly said — is one. Indeed, not only is the understanding, or the first level of spirit, in the animal something distinctive over against the faculty of the same name in the human being, but in each animal in particular this level receives a distinctive colouring, and in the end, where within the species of the animal itself a considerable diversity of individuality becomes noticeable, it varies even according to the individual. The understanding of the dog must therefore be regarded not only as qualitatively different from that of the horse, but the understanding of the hunting dog is different from that of the poodle, and again here, among different hunting dogs and different poodles, there is an important and, from many signs, noticeable difference. From this standpoint we obtain of the spiritual states of the more highly developed animal an essentially different concept than if we wished to regard them merely as a minus of human states, and this view alone grants the observer himself the spiritual freedom that makes him capable of gaining at least some concept of all the manifold nuances that occur in the animal soul, although in the end it must always remain impossible to obtain the full and wholly adequate representation of the states of an animal soul, since it is often difficult enough for us to see even into the soul of another human being with any clarity.

Moreover, it is a necessary consequence that, as soon as the animal soul reaches that level of development

at which a certain clearer understanding of the external world and its relation to the individual becomes possible — in short, where what we designate as understanding emerges — the doing, the counter-effect against the external world, must also become freer, and the feeling of one's own state more distinct. In the higher animals, therefore, not only does the feeling of one's own state already manifest itself more distinctly — what we call affects, dispositions — sadness, joy, exuberance, fear, anxiety, courage, anger — can be distinguished; indeed the animal can be brought, by means of certain representations aroused in it — for example, by the representation of the will of its master, made known to it often in the mere apprehension of a glance — to govern these dispositions with a certain willfulness, to let fear pass into courage, anger into calm or into fear. Just so do the actions, the doings, from here on betray throughout that no longer does an external influence, a stimulus, alone immediately call them forth as necessary reaction, or only a common feeling condition them — in the form of drive — (although both occur here as well as later, even in the human being, whenever it is not the somehow conscious but the unconscious sphere of life that is in question); rather, they are from now on determined by dispositions, memories, and a certain comparison and assessment of received representations. In this way, actions come about here which can already in many respects approximate those of the mature human being. The animal avenges mistreatment received, is grateful to one who has shown it kindness, allows itself (which is especially of exceptional importance) to be educated, and thereby changes and ennobles not only its world-conscious soul-life, but at the same time, as a consequence of the influence of the conscious upon the unconscious, its entire physical constitution and its outward form — yet all this only within a certain circle, only within the limits drawn for it from the beginning. Just as in the series of the higher mammalian genera it is formally expressed that even those most closely approaching the human form, in the ape, rise only to a caricature of the human being, so also in the spiritual sphere the animal, however manifoldly its life of representations may be shaped, however much it may combine these representations, however much may develop in it through education, will always remain within spiritual childhood, will never be able to attain spiritual puberty, productivity, imagination, and reason.

[p. 136] These phenomena, too, have all too often been judged incorrectly hitherto. A very precise parallel was long recognized between this more imperfect spiritual development and the brain-structure still so distant in many respects from the human, and it was concluded therefrom: "the brain-formation, and properly that of the entire organism, has not, in consequence of a lesser vital force, developed in these creatures to the same degree as in the human being; the soul therefore did not find the organs that were necessary for a freer and finer activity, and that is why weaker powers of spirit appear and why self-consciousness is not attained" — as if the soul were always the same and merely could not express itself so well here because of the imperfect brain-formation, much like a man, say, who had been forced into a tight cuirass. This manner of reasoning we must declare absolutely inadmissible and reject, since it leads completely into the absurd. No, in the infinite manifold of ideas there are some of the most diverse energy. The divine thought that conditioned the actualization of an animality is and had to be in itself a different and lower one than that of humanity, and the lower idea of the animal manifests itself unconsciously in the less noble formation and development of the organism in general and of the brain in particular, just as it manifests itself more meagrely as soul, consciously, when that unconscious agency has reached the goal set for it. Only if we thus perceive and acknowledge the essential unity of that unconscious and this conscious, and if we have gained the conviction of the infinite manifold of ideas and the qualitative diversity of each one from every other thereby necessarily posited, can a satisfying and clear view of the world dawn upon us. Here, moreover, it must once more be stated with certainty what is actually self-evident — namely that when we use the expression "higher or lower" of divine ideas and "more perfect or more imperfect" of organisms, this is a thoroughly human and quite subjective mode of designation. In the infinite circle of divine universal life, everything can only be regarded as something perfect in itself, and in truth it is to be said in this sense that the human being is no more perfect than the infusorium, and that the seemingly lowest is, in God and for the totality of the world, just as significant and necessary as the seemingly highest — but not so from the standpoint of the human being, and therefore those designations are justified.

[p. 137] After all the foregoing, we can accordingly state the result: the highest to which the animal soul develops is the more definite expression of a spiritual individuality — that is, of a distinctive quality which, in So Dante says very beautifully:

"It was then clear to me how everywhere in Heaven is Paradise, even if the grace of the supreme good does not rain down upon it in one way." [Chiaro mi fu allor, com' ogni dove / In Cielo è Paradiso, etsi la grazia / Del sommo ben d'un modo non vi piove.]

[p. 138] psychical terms, belongs not merely to the species but distinguishes individual from individual; but the most essential thing that always remains unattainable to it is the spiritual puberty — in a word, the presentation of the person. An animal can also be spiritually an individual, have its own particular way of cognizing the external world, of feeling itself, and of acting against what is external; but it can never become what we call a personality, a character. Here lies the approximation, and here the enormous dividing wall in the relation to the human being. For this reason, too, only the totality of all animals attains the concept of a certain ideal organism, of a certain personality — and that is what we comprehend under the designation of animality. No single one of these creatures, however — not even the totality of all individuals of a species or of an order — can rise to this kind of personality. Language itself would find it nonsensical if there were talk of horse-ness, dog-ness, bird-ness. Only the concept of animality and the person of the human being can be placed opposite the concept of humanity.

It is, moreover, very remarkable to observe how the animal, which internally remains so far from the concept of personality, and in which on the lowest levels even individuality becomes doubtful (one will indeed find it hard to settle what, in a Gorgonia or Pennatula, is actually the individual), also externally has such an entirely different horizon of the world. The weaker the individuality, the less of world-apprehension is possible for the animal. It leads to the most remarkable reflections when one makes clear to oneself what "world" means for the shellfish buried in the mud, or for the worm in the earth! With increasing world-consciousness and first intimations of spirit, the concept of the animal's world also increases in a remarkable way. The greatest horizon and the widest world for the animal may be the world of the bird. No animal, however, extends its world beyond the earth, and the greatest celestial bodies, whose effects it senses, belong, according to its representation (as in the childhood of peoples), always only to the earth's nature. This entire gradation, too, of ever-increasing expansion of the world with increasing development of spirit, repeats itself in the growing child; for us too the world gradually becomes ever wider.

[p. 139] Let us now turn again to the soul-history of the animal. As already remarked, it cannot here be made the task to undertake a psychology of animals going into particulars; but — since the previous attempts to deliver something of the kind have remained so very insufficient — it still seems important to highlight the points that would especially need to be considered if something more complete and better grounded were to be furnished in this regard. I hope to be able to bring them into better overview in the following enumeration. For the sharp and adequate portrayal of these so diverse forms of soul-life, it would namely be indispensable:

1. The presentation of the kind of unconscious soul-life peculiar to each major division, insofar as it manifests itself through the development of the organic bodily structure and life. For the situation here is quite as Goethe says in relation to light: "actually we undertake in vain to express the essence of a thing. We become aware of effects, and a complete history of these effects would well encompass the essence of that thing. In vain do we endeavour to portray the character of a human being; let one instead put together his actions, his deeds, and an image of the character will emerge before us." Just so do we endeavour in vain to represent the unconscious idea in itself as it conditions the existence of an animal creature; but if all its manifestations in formation, transformation, and life become intelligible to us, then thereby the idea itself will in the end have become perceptible to our spirit. In this way one may say that zoology, zootomy, and zoophysiology are actually throughout only parts of zoopsychology, since in all the bodily formation and in all the bodily life of the animal, it is ultimately only the distinctive character of precisely this original life-idea that manifests itself.

[p. 140] 2. There would belong here the presentation of the diverse manifestations of the unconscious soul-life occurring in different genera that have already developed to a world-consciousness — manifestations taking the form of activities that indeed go beyond the boundary of the inner organic life but are still carried out with the same necessity as the unconscious forming itself. These activities fall into three kinds of drives. They are:

a, partly such as stimulate to actions by which the inner formative, transformative, reproductive life is necessarily conditioned, indeed actually first made possible. To this belong the so-called natural drives of animals — the nutritive drive, the respiratory drive, the reproductive drive — from which a multitude of actions of the animal, exercised with necessity, proceed.

b, partly such as appear as continuations of the inner formative and transformative life and, as it were, only extend the organization of the creature further and make it go beyond the boundaries of its own bodily life in certain artificial formations. To this belong the so-called artistic drives of animals — for example, the creating of artificial tubular structures around the surface of many insect larvae, whereby an exoskeleton is replaced; the forming of webs, whereby catching-arms are replaced; the building of cells or of artificial brooding-places for eggs and young, by which, as it were, an inner organic site of formation for the fruit — for example, a uterus — is replaced; and so forth.

[p. 141] c, partly, finally, such in which the relation of the individual animal life to the telluric life emerges; and to this belongs in particular the so-called migratory drive of animals, from which the remarkable migrations of insects, fishes, birds, and a few mammals proceed.

Note. Already here attention must be drawn to the fact that such separations as those of the drives individually enumerated here (natural drives, artistic drives, migratory drives) should on no account lead one to think of an actual division of the soul and to localize the sections of the soul in any way. This so frequently recurring endeavour, appearing in so many psychologies and in so crude a fashion in so-called phrenology, is only a sign of how difficult it is, in the purely spiritual, to keep free from all intrusion of a corporeal and spatial relation. Involuntarily, whenever different directions of the life of the soul are spoken of, a kind of palpable reality is so often ascribed to this diversity, and at one stroke all true representation is destroyed. If anywhere, then here everything depends upon rising, in spiritual cognition — in the perceiving of the idea — to the point where one learns firmly to behold the possibility and reality of a multiplicity within an undivided unity — an original monad.

3. And finally, it would be the task of a true zoopsychology to give a definite presentation of the conscious soul-life emerging in such diverse ways from the unconscious soul-life in different genera and manifesting itself spiritually. Here it would particularly need to be highlighted that this conscious soul-life manifests itself partly in a negative way, by more or less entirely cancelling or at least significantly altering the necessity of the unconscious soul-life; and partly in a positive way, in that a more independent understanding and will and a distinct, manifesting self-feeling gradually really emerge in the animal.

[p. 142] The former — the negative behaviour — is especially remarkable; hitherto, however, it has been less noticed as such, although it must be very evident that precisely by the fact that something formerly unalterable, something formerly indispensably demanded, can now at least partially also be cancelled, a ray of freedom decidedly asserts itself, one that must be essentially fit to herald the first dawn of the actual realm of spirit. Admittedly, these negations then occur only on very high levels, and if one wishes really to feel the power of the unconscious that is here negated, one need only make it quite clear to oneself that if, for instance, it were possible for a bee to take it into its head one day to build round cells instead of hexagonal ones, or for a spider that must weave round webs woven from a spiral to weave a web of square form, like a fisherman's net — these would be phenomena that would have to surprise and dismay us no less than if we saw the moon rise in the west instead of in the east.

In a more detailed consideration of this negative influence of the awakening consciousness, there would especially need to be considered how very differently such negations and cancellations can express themselves as they relate to the various directions of the unconscious soul-life.

In any case, this will occur least with respect to that unconscious agency of the idea by which the bodily organism arises and is maintained. To be sure, under certain conditions in the highest classes, this agency too is refined and ennobled, and indeed in approximately the same way as such a change was discussed with regard to human formation — which is decidedly refined and beautified by nobler, higher soul-life, and worsened and degraded by lower, common soul-life. Here too, in the animal, I say, such occurs through the influence of the conscious upon the unconscious — but the impulse for this does not come from the animality, not from the self-sufficient spiritual development and self-liberation of the creature, but always from humanity; and in a most remarkable and consequential way we then observe that such a given liberation from the yoke of the necessity of ordinarily customary formation (what is commonly understood by the expression "artificial ennoblement of races") is effected only through the subjection of the animal to the dominion of the human being. This emergence of greater freedom, in opposition to the law of formation, from subjection and discipline — a liberation that develops from service and obedience — we shall also recognize in our consideration of the remaining form of the negation of the unconscious by the conscious, and shall find it again in another form in the history of the human soul itself.

[p. 143] The other series of different directions of the unconscious soul-life, which presents itself in the natural drives, the artistic drives, and the migratory drives, can likewise be negated, or at least modified, by the awakened world-consciousness only to a slight degree; and insofar as it is, this happens almost again solely as a consequence of subjection to the dominion of the human being, and through education — only rarely because the feeling of one drive negates the other. Thus, in the first case, the hunting dog learns to master the nutritive drive by standing still before the game that is by nature destined to be its prey, or by refusing to take food itself when it is not offered in the manner it has been trained to accept, and so forth; but of course all this is not a true liberation, having emerged in the very light of freedom, and therefore it also bears no further fruits for the animal's own soul-life; rather, as it was learned through the force of mastery, so it is also practised only as the service of a slave, and the words of Faust about the poodle always hold:

[p. 144] "I find not the trace / of a spirit, and all is mere training!" ["Ich finde nicht die Spur / von einem Geist, und alles ist Dressur!"]

In the other case, the animal finds itself, when through the reproductive drive it masters the nutritive drive, or carries to its brood food that could belong to itself, and so forth.

After all these negative expressions of awakening consciousness, the positive ones would then come under consideration, whose diversity must necessarily be essentially determined by the fact that they manifest themselves either in the sphere of cognition, or in that of feeling, or in that of will. Of special importance here are the expressions of soul-life that reveal a cognizing — not merely an unconscious being-drawn-to or being-repelled-by something, but a taking-up of the representation of something external for its own sake. These traits in the life of the higher animal world especially deserve the psychologist's study and are discoverable only through the fine eye of the researcher. The earliest of this kind perhaps betrays itself in what one calls the curiosity

[p. 145] of birds. The way especially some of the altogether more spiritually developed songbirds approach an appearance unfamiliar to them — one standing in no relation to any of their natural or artistic drives — the remarkable look of their eye that one can then become aware of, the attentiveness and the return they devote to such an object — they provide a prototype of what develops in the human being into the spiritual, cognizing, intelligent perception. Even the distinction of persons emerges in birds — especially tame parrots — very distinctly; and what is very noteworthy, with the emerging cognition there also immediately appears, as an inseparable companion, error — the erring, the being-deceived — which the absolutely unconscious naturally excludes. Animals of this kind are already often deceived — a tame cockatoo was often startled by a flying feather or something of the sort, and its anxious look betrayed that it took it for an enemy — perhaps a bird of prey. But the possibility of erring is the first step toward the cognition of truth; we too learn to know the world only through much erring.

All cognition, moreover, compares freshly supplied representations with ones longer already tested, and thus presupposes memory. So, then, the more highly developed mammals, placed closer to the human being, cognize and distinguish objects and persons still more definitely; they note and recognize localities — for example, paths — with great precision; they learn to understand words and retain them; and if birds already repeat words, learn melodies, and the like, yet precisely in this more of the unconscious is at work — an unconscious which, because it points more to a direct transference of auditory sensation to the vocal organs, does not presuppose and cannot presuppose so clear an understanding of what is received

[p. 146] as, for example, in the dog, the way in which it shows understanding and compliance to every quiet word addressed to it, indeed often to every expression on its master's face. On the other hand, it is of course not to be forgotten that even the highest cognition as it occurs in animals always remains only a very subjective and egoistic one, and never rises to attaining a true objectivity — that is, to taking delight in cognition as such. The simple and necessary reason for this is precisely that the animal never reaches the puberty of the spirit, never becomes capable of the productivity of the idea; therefore the perception of the idea in the phenomenon must remain closed to it, for all delight in truly objective cognition becomes possible only insofar as the spirit learns to perceive the idea of the phenomenon. Thus, because in the animal the idea of beauty cannot enter consciousness, the animal will never become aware of the beauty of the world. An animal stands, for example, before the most beautiful landscape, the purest work of art, utterly without feeling, and grasps the beauty of the human being as little as the kind of beauty that may embody itself in it, the animal, itself.

Together with cognition, self-feeling develops, and precisely thereby does what we call emotion — affect — first become possible on higher levels of the animal kingdom. Of an insect, a mollusc, a fish, we cannot yet say they are joyful or sad, and so forth; at most the affect of anger appears very early on. In the higher animals, however, almost the entire series of affects is distinctly distinguishable, insofar as they do not — as, say, love and admiration do — presuppose a perceiving of the idea. Joy, sorrow, aversion, attraction, anger, fear, rage, and so forth make themselves very definitely known. Naturally, here too we must never surrender to the thought that these affects in the animal are quite the same as in the self-conscious

[p. 147] human being. The higher development, the awakened productivity, the perceiving of the idea in the human soul, gives it through and through a different colouring, recreates it through and through into a different one, so that even the unconscious in it is qualitatively different from that in the animal, let alone what belongs to the region of world-consciousness. (Let one recall here what was said further above about the distinctive character of the understanding in every animal and human being as something particular.) For this reason, earlier observers usually carried far too much of their own soul-life into the history of the soul-life of animals, and thus often gave the portrayal of affects in them too human a colouring; and indeed it is difficult, in such matters, to abstract from oneself to such a degree, and the transposing into an utterly different mode of sensation is always only approximately achievable.

A kind of advantage, yet actually a loss and proof of the low station of animality, is moreover the fact that passion in the feeling-world of the animal is as yet entirely impossible. Here too the animal's insufficient objectivity is the cause and the explanation of this distinctive quality; for passion presupposes that a vivid apprehension, a certain enthusiasm of the soul for the idea of another individual or for a certain doing in regard to individuals, be possible, and for this the animal lacks partly the development of spirit in general, partly in particular the possibility of perceiving a foreign idea.

Even more than the sphere of feeling, the will is most intimately connected with cognition. As it can be demonstrated in the nervous system that there are two different directions in the flow of innervation — a centrifugal and a centripetal one, which in a healthy condition of the organism perfectly balance each other — so too shall willing — that is,

[p. 148] the relating of the inner to the external — and cognition, the relating of the external to the inner, mutually correspond and condition each other. Nevertheless, the energy of the two is not always equal. There is sometimes found a very vigorous willing calling forth strong reaction with a moderate energy of cognition, and conversely; and in general one may say that, in relation to humanity, the former is the case in animality. What we call, as the highest development of the will, the freedom of the will, is properly the being-free from all other determinations to action than those which the cognition developed to the perceiving of an idea presents as the most fitting. Every will that is immediately determined either by a newly received representation or by a representation newly stimulated inwardly according to the later-to-be-discussed circulation of representations, without first being measured against the standard of an idea, is to be called unfree; and since the animal is without the cognition of the idea, its will must necessarily differ entirely from the will of the mature human being by virtue of its unfreedom. The highest, therefore, to which the animal can attain in this respect, and where once again a certain gradation among the different genera very definitely emerges, is "willfulness" [Willkür]. By willfulness we understand the quality of the soul by virtue of which, in a given case, the inner impulse to any doing is not immediately determined by the external stimulus or the inner organic need, but where the decision to act is measured and given, if not against an apprehended idea, yet against one's own self-feeling and a certain cognition of whether one or another doing could afford this self-feeling greater pleasure. Thus, under certain circumstances, the animal spurns one food and chooses another; thus the bird chooses among several opportunities for nest-building one that seems to it more suitable; thus the dog does not follow the call of one less familiar person and follows most exactly the call of another, and so forth. As said, however, this willfulness likewise disappears more and more the deeper we descend in the series of animals. On the lowest levels, the momentary stimulus always immediately decides the doing; and just as one plant opens its blossom when sunlight falls upon it while another closes in sunlight, or just as in ourselves the muscle twitches upon the galvanic stimulus — just so definitely and involuntarily does the polyp contract upon touch, the mussel close its shell, the piddock bore its stone-cavity, the moth fly toward the light, and so forth.

[p. 149] It requires a very fine observation to trace also this side of psychical development in animality in detail and in all its manifold nuances; yet we believe that for the time being the given indications may well suffice to obtain a general picture of how that development of the conscious life of the soul of which the animal is altogether capable gradually completes itself out of the unconscious. It is therefore now time to pass on to other considerations.

#### 2. Von Heranbildung der Seele und des Geistes im Kinde — 2. Of the Formation of the Soul and of the Spirit in the Child

As here and there in the morphological developmental history of the animal kingdom and of the human being, the error had been indulged that the form of the higher animals and of the human being itself, in its gradual development, truly passes through all the different formative stages of the lower animals — that it begins as an infusorium, then becomes a mollusc, then a worm and a fish, and so forth — so too one has at times, in considering the psychical development of the human being, pursued the view that the first soul-life of the foetus or newborn child is really a merely animal one, and that it only gradually develops into human distinctiveness. The one is as false as the other. Whoever could really think such a thing has never grasped the essence of the idea; he has not understood the great word:

[p. 150] "According to the law by which you entered, / So must you be; you cannot flee yourself!" ["Nach dem Gesetz, wonach du angetreten, / So musst du sein, du kannst dir nicht entfliehn!"]

Admittedly, the earliest and first phases of the development of a being destined by higher energy of the idea for a richer existence must always be to some extent similar to the later phases of a being destined by lesser energy of the idea for a poorer existence; but these can always be only similarities, never identities. As regards form and formation, observation thoroughly refutes this, and the first precise investigation of developing organisms demonstrates the diversity; only one thing is true and deserves a somewhat closer consideration here — namely that the very first visible form of existence of the human individual as the first microscopic germ-cell can hardly, or not at all, be distinguished from the form of existence in which the lowest animal individual appears at this stage. On the first plate of the fifth fascicle of my Explanatory Tables of Comparative Anatomy, I have assembled the earliest egg-vesicles of very different animals, and when one considers these and finds those of the highest and lowest creatures so very uniformly formed, this gives a certain justification for thinking that really here, at the very beginning of formation, there is no essential diversity among the animals — which one could then admittedly once again regard, in a sense, as a ground for the original equality even of human and animal organization. But this is not so, and were the diversity really not present in act [actu], it would always have to be there in potency [potentia]. Certainly, the earliest human egg and the animal one — they are both of hardly visible smallness, both are spherical vesicles nested one within another in multiple layers — innermost the so-called germinal spot, around it the germinal vesicle, around that the yolk-sac, and around that the chorion-sac — and even to the armed eye it will be scarcely possible to distinguish the egg-cell in the ovary of a rabbit or a cow from the egg-cell in the ovary of the woman; but this lies merely in the fact that every organic formation must necessarily begin from the presentation of the hollow sphere. This ideal hollow sphere is, however, as we already remarked earlier, in relation to the infinitely diverse further developments of the organism, as it were the centre-point from which infinitely many radii can be thought toward the periphery. Just as each of these radii then, the nearer to the centre, appears also the nearer to the beginnings of the other radii, so too is that sequence of development, the further back, the more approximated to the hollow sphere; and just as, necessarily considered, the beginning of each radius at the centre coincides with the beginnings of all other possible radii and yet, because it belongs to precisely this radius, is itself already to be thought of as a different one even at the centre — so too is the very first germ-cell of the egg admittedly everywhere necessarily the same, but again, precisely because for each different creature it belongs to the beginning of another and special developmental series, it is also in itself each time to be thought of as a different one.

[p. 151] Thus, then, that consideration of the originally seeming equality of the earliest human and animal germ can absolutely be no ground for asserting a real first and perfect equality of the human and animal organism.

[p. 152] In precisely the same manner, the truth soon becomes apparent that the human soul does not first behave merely as an animal soul and only gradually become a human one; rather, in its earliest activity it will at once unmistakably reveal itself as something distinctive and higher. How else would the organism of the developing child become so entirely different, how else would so distinctive and more beautiful a proportion of brain and sense organs and overall articulation develop, if the still absolutely unconscious yet so entirely different and higher idea did not manifest itself in it in just this way! How much more, then, must its other essence make itself known when the idea elevates itself to world-consciousness! What an entirely different character, therefore, already marks the first dim expressions of psychic life in the child, as compared with those of the animal! How different is the first gaze of the eye directed at the mother, how different the first smile playing about the child's lips! In everything it is announced that here something shall be revealed which, in contrast to animality, is something utterly new — a being through which the life of the earth first truly receives spiritual worth and spiritual significance — in a word, as it were the first spiritual thought of the planet, the true awakening of the earth, without which all its phenomena would remain caught in a dull, heavy, indifferent slumber.

Once the idea of the human being is thus first of all recognized as a different and entirely new one, we may now turn to the consideration of the manner in which it gradually emerges and the way in which it attains the highest revelation of its essence.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> One could well compare human individuality to the number one, and all other individuality of the earth to zero. The latter alone, however much it is heaped up, always remains zero, while through the one each zero as a decimal place receives a determinate and always a particular significance.

[p. 153] As earlier with animality, three stages are to be distinguished here:

1, the revelation of the entirely unconscious idea in the organism; 2, the revelation of the idea that has attained world-consciousness as soul, but still without freedom, continuing the organism itself, as it were, merely with necessity in drive;

3, the revelation of the soul through developed self-consciousness in the spirit. Here too the higher and later will always encompass and include the lower and earlier.

As for the revelation of the idea in the organism, it is properly this whose history encompasses the entire domain of human morphology and physiology. It was an old error that, instead of treating physiology in this sense as a part of psychology, one was accustomed to presenting psychology as a mere appendix to physiology. Herein lies in truth the fundamental error that obstructed all deeper understanding even in physiology, and to which I must here especially draw attention. This error is so deeply entrenched in the contemplation of life that, although the elaboration of my system of physiology was already entirely founded upon showing in every single moment of human life the development of the idea of the human being in the organism, even there that so universally prevalent error still prevented me from proceeding, already in the composition of that work, straightforwardly in the sense that physiology must be presented as a part of psychology. Perhaps at some later time the opportunity will arise to order the physical [Physis] just as sharply from this standpoint, just as the psyche is ordered here. For the time being, one must therefore refer to the study of the morphology and physiology hitherto available, and I remind the reader once more of Goethe's simile of light cited above: every closer cognition of organic life as it reveals itself through the unconscious working of the idea will necessarily react to promote a closer cognition and better understanding of the idea itself; only, all these formations must continually be regarded and, as it were, read as distinctive hieroglyphs in which a divine word expresses itself. How significant, then, becomes especially the history of growth and the gradual development of the human being in this sense! To observe how, on the one hand, from the countless repetition of the monad of the primordial vesicle or cell the mass of the organism arises; on the other hand, how this mass then through fusion or splitting, transformation or dehiscence continually develops further; and how, in general, through the ever-creative articulation of the individual structures a distinctively divine idea writes down its invisible essence, as it were in visible letters, and proclaims it spatially — all this is not merely for the physiologist but equally, indeed actually far more for the psychologist, an object of ever-renewed admiration and ever-renewed study.

[p. 154] The second stage was the development of the idea into a preparatory general consciousness, a world-consciousness. We must here first recall the conditions to which all consciousness was bound. The first is: the development of a homogeneous substance continually impressionable by the idea, and indeed without this substance being stimulated by these impressions to heterogeneous organic further developments — that is, the development and maturation of a nervous system. The second condition was the action of an external world upon the organ-

[p. 155] ism and the possibility that that substance, continually impressionable by the inner idea, should also be so for the ideas of the external world. The third, that these impressions of the nervous system should not immediately vanish as they came, but should become permanent and capable of being compared. The fourth, that in order to make a higher consciousness possible, a certain larger quantity of these impressions of the nervous mass — or, as we may now briefly say, representations — should remain present in the soul. Of these conditions, the first is fulfilled by original formative processes; the second enters only with birth, and from that point onward the third too can first assert itself. The fourth will be realized still later, yet the conditioning possibility for it must in every case be given in the development of the brain mass. If we now call the idea "soul" only when at least some kind of consciousness has awakened in it, then we recognize that in every case only after the birth of the child may it be designated by this name, for only with this act — which, moreover, includes a complete metamorphosis of the creature, inasmuch as thereby the fetal human being enclosed in membranes casts off these membranes and is transformed into a true human being — are the essential conditions of a consciousness and first of all of world-consciousness fulfilled. Precisely because, however, no true self-consciousness is possible immediately after birth, what first becomes clearly apparent in the newborn is that, just as in the organism so also beyond the organism, the idea is still active with necessity, and this then always gives its first actions the form of drive.

Of all the drives of the animal world, however, only those develop in the human being (precisely because his higher destiny for self-consciousness continually resists determination in the form of necessity) which we have called natural drives, and first of all in the newborn the nutritive drive — that is, the continued working of the idea beyond the organism, in order by means of general world-consciousness to find, and by means of a willing driven by this finding to take in, what serves the further development of the organism. In the nutritive drive the soul works in the child as unconsciously as it worked when it formed the organs of nutrition themselves; only later does a sensation that comes more to consciousness develop also in the striving to take in nourishment.

[p. 156] The third stage, finally, is marked by the development of spirit in the emergence of self-consciousness, and only at this stage does the great threefold division of the higher life of the soul — as cognition, feeling, and will — once again emerge with distinctness. The development of the soul into spirit occurs, however, no more suddenly and in a single moment than anything else, but gradually, and in proportion to the richly accumulating representations and by means of their comparison and their assessment against the touchstone of the energy of the inborn idea. It is here that, in consequence of a mirroring of the I — that is, of one's own innermost idea — in the representations of an external world, the miracle of a becoming-self-conscious first arises: this miracle which, in and of itself, like the existence of a world at all, can only be acknowledged but admits of no further explanation. Yet even though we must concede this, it is still equally important, with regard to self-consciousness and the emergence of spirit, to observe under what conditions this miracle takes place and becomes real, just as earlier, in considering consciousness in general, we had to observe that from the investigation of the conditions of its manifestation so much clearer a light was shed upon the essence of this consciousness itself. It is, moreover, certainly to be expected that those former conditions can only be heightened versions of the conditions found for the development of consciousness in general, and thus we recognize here in particular two such conditions.

[p. 157] The first, namely, from which the possibility of self-conscious spirit arises, is a heightening of what was already listed as the fourth condition among the conditions of consciousness in general — that is, that a formation and health of the nervous system and especially of its central mass be present which makes possible the retention of a sufficient quantity of mutually comparable representations.

It could therefore never come to self-consciousness in us — that is, to the development of a spirit — if only a stunted, unhealthy brain formation had developed, and herein also lies the reason that a particularly vigorous cultivation of the spirit will always be essentially co-conditioned by a certain more vigorous development of the nervous system and especially of the brain. It must, however, be expressly recalled here once more that this condition is absolutely never to be thought of as an external one, as one foreign to the idea in itself, but always as one thoroughly grounded in the idea itself, since it is always only the earlier unconscious working of the idea itself that creates this formation.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> If one makes this relation quite clear to oneself, it must be striking how unclearly the reproach of materialism was conceived that has so often been raised against facts of the above kind. The thought of materialism always first introduced something foreign into the matter; according to it, some foreign something had created a material structure of which one was supposed to imagine that it had nothing to do with the spirit, even though one had to perceive at every moment how much the spirit was conditioned by it. The more bravely one believed, despite this, that the spirit was entirely independent of the material structures, the more meritorious it was considered; but one sees what a

[p. 158] The other great condition for the development of the spirit is, as for consciousness in general, the presence and action of an external world — but not merely of an external world in and of itself, but of the conscious spirit also manifesting itself in an external world. We must acknowledge it as a fact that a human individual alone, without the spirit of humanity, without the spirit of other individuals acting upon him, absolutely does not develop as such. The child left entirely to itself either perishes or attains in its consciousness only a development that makes it resemble the animal, indeed perhaps sink below the animal. Upon the well and especially with respect to its nervous system and brain well-organized child (for even a child born without arms and legs, for example, can attain the clearest consciousness), there must accordingly act the spiritual influence of other human beings. Spirit must kindle itself upon spirit, and by no means is this always and straightforwardly to be regarded as a communication; rather, often, indeed most frequently, this influence makes itself felt by evoking a counter-effect, a decided reaction, and this reaction, by strengthening the spirit, forms and furthers the spirit. A judicious education of the child will always place great weight upon this relationship and always derive special benefit from it.

In this most remarkable, irrefutable condition of the development of the spirit in the child — only under the given condition of the influencing spirit of humanity — there is thus repeated at a higher power the relationship of lower animals, where many individuals either live outright only in a fixed organic connection, as in polyp-

chaos of false conceptions lies before us here.

[p. 159] colonies, or where they are conditioned in their existence and their distinctive higher psychic expressions only through a close social bond, as for example with bees. We find indeed that in the human being, too, the fact that his spiritual development can always occur only within humanity already indicates that the cultivation of the bond of humanity — wherein, again, the highest concept of the state is given — should after all always be regarded as the highest and greatest task of the spirit itself.

These conditions, then, are those under which the miracle of the spirit unfolds in the soul of the child. It is not something new that is given therein, but it is the grasping of a present, in the soul that until then had revealed itself only in the inseparable flow of past and future; it is a re-concentrating of the idea that had become manifest in the appearance of the organism — one might say, laid out; and just as the rainbow is immediately there and visible as soon as only the rain-curtain and the sun are given, without one being able to say how it gradually came into being, and just as this rainbow is no new light but only the very same everywhere-diffused sunlight which here merely concentrates itself in innumerable drops and thus produces in the total effect the beautiful image of colors — so the thought of the I is at once there, as soon as the unconscious working of the idea has established the remarkable living structure of the organism and especially of the brain, and as soon as in this life an external world and especially a spiritually animated external world — that is, one or several other personalities — are mirrored, so that thereby henceforth in the stream of the ever half-past, half-future becoming, the holding fast of a present, and in it a guarantee of eternity, can be attained.

[p. 160] From the moment, then, that in this way the first thought is born in the idea unfolded as soul, from the moment that the spirit is thus disclosed in the soul — there begins a development which, precisely because the soul no longer merely drifts along unconsciously as half-past, half-future, but because it has become capable of grasping the present and thereby, in holding fast the moment, of overcoming time, must also no longer be merely a finite but an infinite one, since only in such a development can and will the idea fully realize its originally eternal, divine essence.

Already where the souls of animals were discussed, it was indicated, on the one hand, that the development of the spirit in its positive advance repeats in a certain sense the history of the organism, inasmuch as it first moves toward a spiritual puberty in order then to realize itself more and more in a higher productivity; and on the other hand, that it must relate negatively to certain earlier manifestations of the life of the soul. In the latter regard it was already mentioned that precisely on account of the higher development of spirit in the human being, the emergence of particular drives prompted by inner necessity must be restricted to a high degree, and that therefore there can no longer be any talk of art-drives and migratory drives; later on, however, further reflections must follow on how what we have called the natural drives, in the same way as the unconscious further development of the organism itself, can be influenced and elevated in a distinctive way by the higher development of the spirit.

As for the development of the spirit in and of itself at present, we already earlier distinguished three periods of it: the first, that of the understanding; the second, that of imagination; the third, that of reason. Here it is where sharp self-observation, a lively recalling of earlier states already lived through with self-consciousness, and exact observation of the manner of thinking and acting in childhood can help us to a very complete concept of this most remarkable of all histories. If one therefore looks about in the science of the soul and perceives here too so much that is imperfect and abstruse, one cannot fail to recognize that a certain misuse of the distinguishing, separating understanding itself has here caused manifold harm. To be sure, the difficulty of regarding something manifold and very various always within a unity, and never as something really existing outside one another, is very great — but it is so more through a scholastic habituation than in and of itself. For the healthy, simple sense it is easier than one might think to acknowledge multiplicity within unity, and it is only from the habituation of the understanding that that mechanically separating tendency arises which has scarcely anywhere caused more confusion than precisely in the doctrine of the soul. Roughly in the same way that, after the more widely disseminated knowledge of anatomy and the insight that so many individual parts can be distinguished in the living body, the view gradually became fixed in the minds of many physiologists that the body is to be regarded as a composite, and the thought was almost lost that all this multiplicity had arisen only within a unity and can only be comprehended as an ever-further articulation and division of a single whole — so too in the doctrine of the soul. As one came to distinguish several particular directions in the soul developed as spirit as truly different in kind, one gradually separated them more and more as so-called special faculties, and had then, before one knew it, a multitude of special beings that could no longer be brought together into a single one.

[p. 161] [p. 162] We therefore justly regard it as the first condition and most essential task in the further pursuit of the development of the spirit that it be always held fast how the soul in itself, as a God-thought, as a divine idea, is only a whole and indivisible; how every particular new development of it always comprehends and encompasses the preceding one within itself; and how, although the most manifold metamorphoses and the most diverse directions belong to the revelation of its essence — precisely because it is itself something divine and infinite and because for this reason it can never do justice to its essence sufficiently in any one direction — it nevertheless remains through and through a single, indivisible whole.

This task is in itself difficult enough to fulfill, and when van Helmont relates that, prompted by a dream, he spent twenty-three years in longing to behold his soul, until at last in a moment he was granted the happiness of glimpsing it as a luminous ethereal form enclosed in a strange envelope, one may ultimately consign this vision of his to the category of hallucinations, but nevertheless find in this longing a simile for how long and persistently the spirit must gaze into its own world in certain directions before the full light of certain cognitions can dawn upon it.

Of everyone, then, to whom it shall also become clear that in the unity of the more highly unfolded soul a multiplicity must at the same time be present, it is indispensably required that he devote himself to such gazing, and only with full concentration of the spirit will all obscurity here, as elsewhere, dissipate. The first and most indispensable thing, however, that must now especially be grasped by the spirit is that that summit of the soul at which it becomes spirit by no means excludes what we earlier designated in the very same one as idea and as soul: the idea is not yet soul and the soul is not yet spirit, but the spirit exists only within the soul and the soul only within the idea, and these three are only one in all their difference, and only as existing in a single unity can they be understood by the spirit. The same must we then say of that sequence in the development of the spirit which we have called understanding, imagination, and reason, with regard to the separations of the three members of this series in our representation. The complete negation of all finitude, all limitation, all inner division which belongs to the essence of the spirit precisely because the idea can attain the real experience of a present and the complete realization of its originally eternal essence only by overcoming finitude — this negation must also abolish all such divisions. As long, of course, as the spirit at the stage of the understanding still lingers chiefly in the contemplation of finitude such as the world of the senses presents to us, it appears to it as a miracle that something should be most manifoldly divided and yet be a single whole; as, however, it arrives more and more at the true apprehension [Vernehmen] of its own essence — that is, precisely at reason [Vernunft] and true self-consciousness — this ceases to be a miracle for it, and it feels itself in the limitless, undivided-one, eternal, thoroughly and solely in its true element.

[p. 163] In the right consideration of these truths we have at the same time the measure by which to gauge the developmental history of the spirit in the child. The first awakening of self-consciousness, of the spiritualized soul, of thinking, in the thought of the I — it is, as it were, the gaining of a first foothold in the flight of existence, and from this moment onward, everything that in time ceaselessly transforms itself passes before the one that holds fast, and is now first able to mirror itself as something changing in this one abiding element. Only this, then, is what further leads and shall lead to infinite distinctions of infinite representations — representations which now, since there is something abiding in the soul, themselves also remain continually present, so that only from this moment onward can that earlier-mentioned unconscious Epimethean principle realize itself as conscious Epimetheus, as memory. It is in such distinguishing of abiding representations, then, that the essence of the understanding is especially expressed and given, and it is very remarkable to trace precisely how this inner beholding of difference in the soul of the child gradually develops, without any consciousness of unity and eternity having previously occurred or become possible. The child begins to distinguish a multitude of representations; memory is the freshest and most willing; the delight in the ever-new reception of representations is the very greatest; but everything that points to a higher unity of phenomena, indeed of the world in general, is still far from the child, and although an understanding is now developed, it is for precisely this reason still so much lesser a one than that of the mature man. In this relationship we can immediately find confirmed yet another great law which, as already in the outer organic world, holds good also in the realm of the soul: namely, that whenever in any individual a series of developmental states becomes the task of existence, the emergence of the higher always at once negates and destroys something of the preceding lower, but also, insofar as that lower remains present, elevates and ennobles it. For it will immediately become perfectly clear, when from the standpoint of higher spiritual development we consider and reflect upon our own earlier merely understanding-based formation of the spirit, or that of the child in general, that what is here called understanding is something quite different from what we, in the perfectly matured soul with higher rational intuition, designate as understanding. The understanding of the child and the understanding of the higher, rational spirit that has attained the beholding of the eternal are two very different things, and we are here once again reminded of what I already mentioned in considering the animal soul, and what we shall soon have to discuss in another connection — namely, that the understanding, like all rays of the spirit, is by no means everywhere one and the same, but something most manifold and everywhere individual.

[p. 164] [p. 165] Thus develops, then, the distinguishing and understanding of more and more representations in the child. As we earlier observed that the so remarkable structure of the organism comes about only through an innumerable, ever-repeated positing of a unity — that is, of the microscopic cell — so we experience in ourselves that the development of the organism of the spirit, which points toward infinity, likewise advances and completes itself more and more only through innumerable, ever-repeated positing of representations. (One must always bear in mind here that what we call a representation is really only the sum of the relation of the whole soul to some object, and that we must accordingly regard a series of representations as a repeated seeing of the respective state expressed in a relation of the soul to the object of the representation; whereupon this process can indeed be perfectly compared to the innumerable self-seeing of the idea in the primordial cells of the organism.) The more the mass of representations grows, the more manifold become the combinations, divisions, differences, similarities, distinctions among them and the actual I, and here too the comparison can be carried through very strictly with those primordial particles of the bodily organism which, the more their mass grows, combine in ever more diverse ways, develop further — here into nerve fibers, there into muscle fibers, here into bone cells, there into membranes and glands — and as they thus continue to grow, give room for new dehiscences, unions, or articulations.

[p. 166] Before long, moreover, just as the mass of representations increases, as in them the image of individuality is more distinctly mirrored and the representation of the I is distinguished from the remaining representations — that is, as self-consciousness dawns — there stirs in the interior of this new world of the spirit the development of representation from representation. Not merely do new representations from without enter in addition, but what we may call the productivity of the spirit, and which, when it continually realizes itself, indicates the puberty of the spirit, begins to manifest itself. We call this capacity for further development within the spirit imagination [Phantasie], and remarkably enough, such a spiritual puberty does indeed emerge more distinctly and most powerfully only when the child grows up and approaches bodily puberty. Through imagination the realm of the spirit now grows ever larger and ever more powerful, although here again, as with the understanding, it must be noted that imagination, when it emerges in the growing child, is still very far removed from the grandly creative imagination of the most mature period. At the same time, in relation to the previous stage of the spirit, there appears, on the one hand, a certain negating, and on the other, an unmistakable heightening. Imagination, the autonomous production of representations, becomes the occasion for these to mingle with the original ones, indeed for them even to be confused at times, and in this way especially there arises that which negates understanding — that is, error. The period of imagination is therefore quite especially the period of error, and often on this stage errors arise that give the entire later development a particular coloring, a disturbed direction. Nevertheless, it is also precisely imagination which, by heightening the manifoldness and inner richness of the spirit, opens a wide field for the exercise and development of the understanding. The most important thing, however, that is given by this distinctive productivity for the higher unfolding of the spirit — for the apprehension [Vernehmen] of the idea of unity — for reason [Vernunft], lies in this: that it completes, as it were, the individual — who in itself must always appear thoroughly inadequate over against the universe. For indeed, in order to arrive in the highest sense at the apprehension of the unity of the All, the impossible would have to be presupposed — namely, that the representations of all manifoldness should be grasped in the spirit and the common element of all drawn together into a unity, just as the glass lens draws infinite rays of the sun together into a focal point. That lacunariness, then, that inadequacy of the individual over against the All — this is what the spirit is elevated above by becoming capable, within itself, of the production of infinite representations; in this way it can become aware of something infinite, an All within itself; the most remote and most opposed things in reality can be connected through new and ever new productions; and thus what could never be achieved by the mere reception and distinguishing of representations from without is prepared — namely, to arrive at the grasp of unity. In this way it actually happens, then, that imagination becomes the preparation for the highest stage of the spirit, for reason; from here it is to be explained that a great, creative imagination, but one also regulated by the understanding and preserved from error, has always been recognized as the first condition of a higher reason and a truly great spirit; and therefore the merely rational activity of the spirit alone is never capable of arriving at the cognition of the higher unity of the world. These considerations, if we pursue them somewhat further, yield the most remarkable insights into the development of the various views that have unfolded in humanity concerning the highest unity of all ideas — concerning God. For one perceives in manifold ways that by virtue of a certain leaping over the regular progression in the activity of the spirit, what actually, as will presently be more closely discussed, can only be an object of the fully matured development of reason, is sometimes already reached, or at least should be striven for, through imagination alone — in other words, through imagination not yet fully arrived at reason in the proper sense. In this way the idea of the divine — the representation of God — is brought to cognition, as it were, prematurely, and there arise the — I would say — monstrosities of the divine: the fetishes, idols, gods, all created according to the measure of imagination and of the understanding and of the more brightly or more darkly dawning reason of still spiritually immature individuals. One could without doubt trace from this standpoint a ladder of religions of its own; for when in the stone lump that the savage worships as God, the enormous gap that exists here over against the totality — a gap which a lesser understanding does not recognize as such — is somehow filled in and completed by a childish imagination (whereby, to be sure, only a monstrous image of God can arise), nevertheless the human form and the kind of humanly personal consciousness that so many monotheists attribute to their deity is no less to be put to the account of a premature, phantastic filling in of an on the whole very insufficient cognition and lacking cultivation of the highest reason. And this effect of imagination makes itself felt not only in matters of religion; it makes itself equally felt in matters of science, and can bring forth monstrosities here as there, when without the sufficient material it alone fills in the gaps and it alone, without waiting for the maturity of reason, would complete the edifice of the scientific work of art. Therefore, as certainly as without imagination there is no religion and no whole of science, just as certainly do innumerable miscarriages in both come about through a falsely applied phantastic productivity of the spirit.
