[p. 167] [p. 168] [p. 169] It is a different matter when, with richly accumulated material of representations ordered in the understanding, the full puberty of the spirit arrives; when what remains impossible for the inadequacy of human comprehension over against the All is completed through a higher productivity of the spirit; when the newly pressing-forth representations are grand and beautiful and in the sense of the divine thoughts of the world; indeed, when these thoughts then complete themselves with such clarity that we recognize in them something that in reality can never be given in this perfection — in this abstraction; then, at this creative power and under the continual exercise of it, the highest developmental stage of the spirit — reason — also ripens.

It has already been indicated that the word reason [Vernunft] in our language is more meaningfully than in any other to be derived from "apprehending" [Vernehmen] — that is, from the apprehension of the idea. The concept, of which the spirit is capable already at the stage of the formation of the understanding, is inadequate when the idea is in question; for an idea is something original, eternal, divine, and as such can never be completely encompassed or grasped [begriffen] by the representational life of the soul, but can only be seized [erfaßt] or apprehended [vernommen]. The soul must, however, already have developed in a powerful way if it is to be capable of apprehending the idea; the animal soul never can. The human soul too, although by its inner eternal essence it is, even in the undeveloped state, already a different and higher one than the animal soul, becomes capable of this "apprehending" (we are accustomed here to call it also a "beholding") only after it has passed through the stages of the understanding and of the imagination. Only at this higher stage does it dawn upon the soul that the idea is not only just as certainly, indeed far more certainly, a truth than an immediate sense-representation or the concept abstracted from it, and precisely for this reason it is only at this stage that the soul can apprehend the idea of its own being in the higher self-consciousness, and that of a supreme source of all ideas in God-consciousness. This apprehension, this rational beholding, emerges also as a miracle in the spirit, just as the spirit itself appears as a miracle in the life of the soul, and we are often able to specify with considerable precision the moment in the development of our spirit at which the idea first appeared to us in its eternity and truth as something objective. Indeed, we would even be able to express ourselves about this moment with greater precision, were it not that, precisely because of the higher energy of his soul, this possession belongs to the human being so entirely and originally and distinctively that even when he is long not yet capable of grasping it with perfect clarity, the possibility of this possession and this beholding nevertheless always hovered about him like a half-veiled secret. [p. 170] It is with this as with the sublimity of the human

[p. 171] soul in general, which already from the very outset asserts itself as something other and thoroughly distinct from the animal soul — in the beauty of human form, in the first gaze of the child's eye — long before it has yet attained true consciousness. Just so the idea, as something higher, already hovers about the childlike understanding; so it already announces its presence in the awakening imagination and urges it to create forms that express symbols of ideas; and it is precisely for this reason that it becomes difficult, indeed almost impossible, to distinguish with perfect sharpness the gradually ever more distinct presentiment of the idea from the final beholding and thus the truly genuine awakening of reason, and to fix it as a period of life — the most important one. But this period of life is only to be called such insofar as it must be distinguished from the earlier period of the understanding or of imagination; by no means, however, as though it were one complete in itself, for it has rather entirely the direction toward something infinite, never-to-be-exhausted — eternal. For this reason, too, there is not a moment of standstill in it, but a continued development and unfolding.

In considering this developmental history of the spirit, it can become perfectly clear why that stage of the spirit which we call the understanding can contain and furnish no proofs for any true idea. The highest idea, the idea of all ideas — God — will never be able to be proved in its necessity by the understanding, and that sophist was quite right when he said, after having been rewarded for a well-conducted proof of the existence of God, that for double the reward he was at once prepared to prove the non-existence of God as well. But precisely because here everything depends on listening in the finest and purest way to the inner essence of the spirit, because only the full concentration of spiritual activity here leads to true apprehension [Vernehmen], and because the human being so seldom truly approaches this state and yet never entirely and perfectly reaches it, the utterances of human beings have scarcely ever been so multifarious about anything as about precisely the truly highest tasks of life. In all this, it is once again a beautiful trait of the aptness of our language that it actually regards and acknowledges the highest rational knowledge, the knowledge of the idea, as knowledge pure and simple [das Wissen schlechthin], by forming from this knowledge the word wise-being — wisdom [Weise-sein — Weisheit], and that it calls the one who, enlightened by the apprehension of the idea, now beholds and evaluates all things of the world in the light of the idea — a sage [einen Weisen], thereby designating the truly highest development of the human being, the development with which his earthly existence must properly conclude, since for further development in this present form of existence no possibility is given at all.

[p. 172] At this height of life, then, the most nearly perfect possible contrast has been reached to that state in which the whole of human existence, as in the fetal period, still persisted in absolute and general unconsciousness. While in the latter all consciousness is absent and each and every movement of life was guided only by the dark but in and of itself divine striving of the inborn idea, we cannot quite say in equal measure of the former that now everything in the human being has come to full and clear consciousness, since (as the next section shall demonstrate in detail) even here so much of the unconscious still remains that consciousness itself is continually conditioned by it; yet for all that, it is very remarkable how far, at this final height of life, consciousness can cast its rays and how far it can illuminate the unconscious! The soul that has become perfectly clear about itself not only henceforth directs its own spiritual movement according to the pure light of higher cognition, but the whole natural existence of the human being raises itself to a higher level of measure and beauty. Through consciousness, a certain order and regularity is brought into the functions that for the most part belong to the unconscious sphere — an order that is so easily disturbed when it is no longer the unconscious ruling everywhere according to inner irrefutable laws, but a consciousness, yet one not fully illuminated by the light of reason, that prevails; indeed, it has already been touched upon how decidedly a higher soul-character, filled with ordered and beautiful representing, reacts even upon the entirely unconscious forming and must necessarily impress upon the organism a more beautiful and nobler type. Thus it must indicate not only that the free act of the spirit at the height of wisdom is so entirely different from what it was at the stage of the understanding or of the imagination, but that the entire life — from sleeping and waking to the taking in of nourishment, the manner of muscular movement, the manner of breathing and secretion, and so forth — in short, the entire stirring of the organism, assumes a nobler and freer character, signifying that the human being has here reached a height at which a still further development on the whole cannot readily be conceived at present. Let us therefore leave these considerations for the time being and now turn to what is to be said.

[p. 173]

### b. Von dem fortwährenden Bedingtsein des bewußten durch das unbewußte Seelenleben — b. On the continual conditioning of conscious soul-life by the unconscious

During the whole of our continued existence as beings that have attained self-consciousness, what we earlier called the absolutely unconscious continues to work silently within us, not as something universal, but certainly as something partial, and is still always the first condition of our entire phenomenon of life. The earlier considerations have shown us how everything we call nourishment, growth, and further development are only particular rays in which that unconscious realizes itself, and if it now follows of itself that those formations which we recognized as especially the condition of the awakening of consciousness — that is, the nervous system and brain — are, like everything else, maintained in their integrity only through this unconscious working of the idea, it follows already from this how very much everything we may call conscious soul-life is to be regarded as quite essentially conditioned by the unconscious.

[p. 174] But now the task is actually set for science not merely to acknowledge that condition in general, but to demonstrate sharply in particular through what processes on the unconscious side of life the particular processes of consciousness find themselves conditioned — or in other words, what physical processes underlie those psychical radiations, just as we are able to demonstrate, for example, of visual sensation that it is only the most delicate retunings in the state of innervation of a small part of the retina of the eye whereby the representation of seeing is conditioned. To these demands, however, science can at present respond only to a certain limited degree, and it is very much the question whether a perfection as complete as is indeed possible in other branches of physiology will ever be achieved here. That law of mystery already mentioned earlier, applying precisely to the highest tasks of life, makes itself particularly felt in this domain; what has so far been established with greater certainty may be the following.

[p. 175] Although we may regard the entire organism outright as the bodily expression, as the phenomenon of the fundamental idea of our existence, yet, as we have earlier demonstrated, insofar as this idea comes to consciousness, only the properly psychical structure of this organism — the structure in which the semi-fluid, primordial substance, thoroughly impressionable by the idea, has been most purely preserved — that is, the nervous system — is to be called its preeminent phenomenon, its first sensible manifestation, and thereby also the first condition of its revelation. Of this great, much-branched structure, however, not all parts are in equal measure related to the highest manifestations of the life of the soul. A multitude of outer branches of this system — all the nerves of the limbs, even nerves of the great sense organs — can become obliterated; indeed, even the trunk from which most of the nerves of the body originate and into which their fibers return — the spinal cord — can be destroyed progressively from below (whereby, to be sure, life itself is soon abolished), without the representational life and the consciousness arising from it being destroyed; and all this proves irrefutably that in all that outer part the organically spatial condition of consciousness is not given. As soon, however, as we in any way impair, compress, or injure the great central mass of the nervous system, the brain, in which the greatest accumulation of the truly primitive nerve substance (that is, the so-called investing mass, which consists of semi-fluid primordial cells) is found, the representational life is immediately impaired, and at any higher degree of injury, or at affliction through abnormal life, consciousness is disturbed, the sequence of the representations continually appearing in it is thrown into disorder and obstructed, and finally consciousness is completely abolished. From this one can immediately state with the greatest decisiveness that the primordial substance of the brain, thoroughly impressionable by the idea, is that spatial manifestation of the organism which must be regarded as the nearest condition of consciousness, or, as one may also say, as the organ of the higher revelation of the soul; for which reason the diversity of souls, just as through different bodily organization in general, must reveal itself especially through different organization precisely of this structure. It is now indeed very remarkable that in this organ the formation, transformation, and further development of the elementary parts is continually realized and maintained only through the unconscious idea, while at the same time upon this very structure the highest conscious development of the idea mirrors itself in the stream of representations; yet again, in order to grasp the possibility of this union, we must first recall the earlier remark that absolutely no real separation exists between idea, soul, and spirit — although the latter are higher developments of the former — but that these three are always to be thought of as one; and secondly, we must recall the sensory nerves and indeed the double life of the entire nervous system, where on the one hand the continual further development and transformation of the nerve fiber takes place, while simultaneously, as a second vital expression, the nerve fiber is steadily pervaded by the current of innervation, in whose infinite modifications alone both sensation and the incitement to movement are grounded. Such a twofoldness-in-one repeats itself, after all, even in artificial experiment! For when we bring certain metals into contact with fluids so that a transformation of the same (a chemical process) takes place, there are at the same moment also given phenomena of a dynamic current (galvanic current). One will find that here, too, the latter manifests itself in a quite different manner from the change in composition, and thus here at the galvanic pile, just as in those nerve structures, two acts in one continually present themselves.

[p. 176] [p. 177] This latter example is certainly the most suitable for making comprehensible how, given the distinctive organization of the nervous system, precisely the interaction of the actual nerve substance with the blood streaming through its capillary vessels — and partly emerging from these vessels by exosmosis as formative or vital fluid — conditions what we call in physiology the innervation current. In this innervation current we find a vital phenomenon which really has very much in common with electrical or galvanic current, which indeed, up to a certain degree, acts upon the galvanometer, and is itself the cause of animal electricity. We distinguish in this current, as necessarily, a centrifugal and a centripetal direction: the former is the condition of all perception of external impressions or internal excitations, the other of all reaction producing movement (motor), and indeed of all reaction upon other systems; but both by no means presuppose an already developed consciousness, whereas they themselves are the conditions of every conscious life of the soul, as has already been noted earlier. Already in the fetal state, and therefore long before a world-consciousness, let alone a self-consciousness, is developed, centrifugal and centripetal currents in the nerve accordingly realize themselves most decidedly, for one observes that already the fetus, in response to external stimuli which without

[p. 178] consciousness cannot yet be apprehended as true sensations but only as dim feelings [Erfuhlungen] (perceptions), allows movements to follow. From this one can quite clearly understand that between centripetal and centrifugal innervation currents in their relation to conscious sensations and acts of will, there always remains approximately the same difference as between the unconscious idea and the soul that has become conscious — a difference that does not signify a truly being-other, but merely indicates and expresses a being more lowly or more highly developed in itself, of one and the same thing.

Although it is now anatomically impossible, owing to the infinite delicacy of the objects, to demonstrate directly an originating from and returning to the brain of all the millions of primitive fibers of the nervous system, we nevertheless have in the fact that we nowhere discover a free end of a primitive fiber, but that the outermost parts of them everywhere bend around in loop-like fashion, and that we perceive the same loop-like bendings also in the brain, a sufficient ground for regarding every primitive fiber of the nervous system and brain as an often very elongated ellipse, one bending of which lies at the periphery and the other amid the cell-form primordial substance of the brain. If we therefore now further examine what can be said about the particular significance of the individual parts of the brain's structure in relation to particular rays of the psychical conscious life, we can add as a still closer<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> The anatomical and logical grounds for the claim that really all primitive fibers of the nervous system behave in this way I have set forth in the third volume of my System of Physiology, and that this view, although it has been variously contested, is nonetheless the only correct one, has been confirmed in a very remarkable way especially by the finding of Lonsdale, who saw that in a malformation where the brain presented only a membranous bladder, the nerve roots, which could still be traced for a stretch into this bladder wall, there everywhere clearly bent into loops.

[p. 179] determination of the above proposition — according to which the brain in general was to be regarded as the organ of the higher revelation of the soul — the following: 1, within the brain itself there exists again a distinction between the cell-form primordial substance and the fiber substance, which partly enters and exits from the rest of the nervous system and partly establishes the connection between different brain structures. 2, since the current of innervation proceeds only along the fiber substance and there, as centripetal, determines the sensations, and as centrifugal, the reactions, it is to the fiber substance of the brain entering from and exiting to the nervous system alone that, on the one hand, the conveyance of new representations to consciousness and, on the other, the issuing of any reactions from consciousness outward is to be ascribed as a condition. Were we to think this fiber substance removed, it would be impossible for the spirit to receive new representations or to make known any inner excitation outwardly, and the possibility of the spirit's own further cultivation would thereby be immediately and entirely abolished. Indeed, one can pursue this proposition with all the rigor of a physical experiment: for example, some injury destroys both optic nerves, and from that moment onward the conscious soul-life receives no new representation relating to the effect of light; on the other hand, nerves are perhaps destroyed that branch out to muscles or to the organs of voice, and from that moment onward the conscious soul no longer has the power to realize itself through reactions toward these sides of the organism, and so forth. 3, the primordial substance of the nervous system and of the brain is everywhere that of the primordial vesicles, primordial cells — in the small embryo there is as yet no kind of primitive fiber of the nervous system; everything is only vesicle or cell formation. This substance, then, is also that in which what we call the distinctive function of the nervous system — the innervation —

ori[p. 180] ginally generates itself, and from where its current through the lines of the fiber substance must begin and to where it must return; this substance itself is at rest within itself, and to it therefore attaches what is abiding in the inner revelations of the life of the soul — that is, the persistence of representations. This proposition too can be pursued with full experimental rigor. For we observed in the above example that from the moment the optic nerves are destroyed, no new visual representations enter the soul; on the other hand, it is an equally established fact that even when no new visual representations enter, the earlier ones are nevertheless preserved, since such persons still have and retain the clearest visual representations within them for a long time, and especially can dream themselves as seeing. Indeed, this retention goes further! Persons whose limbs have been amputated have so vivid a representation of these limbs in their consciousness that they believe they feel pain in them. Since it is now impossible that this persistence of the representation in the conscious soul should be tied to the fiber substance — inasmuch as the above propositions demonstrate that the fiber substance only mediates the current of innervation and the conveyance of new representations, as well as the stimulation of certain reactions — we can now further specify the earlier statement — that the brain is the spatial organ of the higher revelation of the life of the soul — as follows: in the brain, the primordial cell substance with the innervation tension developed upon it is just as much the organic condition of the possibility of persisting representations as the fiber substance, insofar as it is to be regarded as a continuation of the spinal cord and nervous system, is the organic condition of conveyed sensations and issuing reactions; and the diversity of representational life in different souls will and must therefore be found conditioned especially in the different arrangement and mass of these substances of the brain. It must also be acknowledged, however, that not only fiber substance exists in the brain that is a continuation of external nerve radiation, but that there is also fiber substance, and indeed considerable amounts of it, in the fully developed brain that belongs to the brain itself, establishing in the most manifold radiations the connections [Commissurae] between the most diverse accumulations of the primitive cell substance. No experiment has yet been able to demonstrate that currents of innervation are present in these fibers as well; but if it is definitively established what the psychical significance of the vesicle mass is, and what that of the fiber mass as continuation of the nerves is, then there can really be absolutely no doubt about it: namely, that in the fiber mass of the commissures the psychical significance is given only as the organic condition of the thousandfold relations and connections of existing representations among themselves.

[p. 181] With this, then, the psychical significance of the three essential substances of the brain would at least be demonstrated with decided certainty!

Thus, by the fact that as a result of the unconscious working of the idea the vesicular primordial substance of the brain and its fiber substance arise; by the fact that continually at these structures, through their interaction with the blood ceaselessly coursing through them and the formative fluids secreted from it, that mysterious potency develops which we call innervation and innervation current — by this the higher conscious life of the soul is continually conditioned, as by something unconscious, and not one of these conditions can be abolished without disturbing the integrity of the conscious life of the soul.

[p. 182] A different question is whether one can demonstrate that certain divisions of the primordial substance of the brain are determined for certain classes of representations or certain sides, or rays, of conscious soul-life in general. Here the investigation becomes ever more difficult, and what can be demonstrated with certainty alone is approximately the following:

Although we know that the living vesicular mass of the brain, pervaded by innervation, presents the organic condition of the abiding representations of all conscious soul-life just as determinately as, say, the nerve mass of the retina of the eye contains the organic condition for visual representation, yet any particular local relationships and connections between certain representations and certain deposits of vesicle substance can be demonstrated just as little as one is able to demonstrate in the retina particular microscopic portions for red, others for blue, and so forth. We really have here no measure, no rule, no analogy that could guide us to discover such relationships except that very relationship of sense-perception to the soft, semi-fluid spread of the nervous substance of the sensory nerves. So enormous, then, is the gulf which, according to our cognition, lies between them, when we are to connect the vast world of visual perceptions that we experience in our eye at every hour with the small, grayish-soft spot of the nerve membrane of the eye at which all these infinite mirrorings take place; just as great is the gulf which for us lies between them when we are to bring the still vaster world of our spirit into connection with the soft, curiously shaped nerve substance of the brain. That it is there we must acknowledge; as it is there, it does not directly permit us any further cognition, because here cognition itself has its ground, and it is impossible that the eye should see itself.

[p. 183] We must therefore consider whether the morphological development of the brain still offers us a foothold for demonstrating any other local relationship here. If we take this into account, the following further emerges: We see that in the lower animals, in which only a general world-consciousness develops and there is as yet no talk of spiritual cognition and freedom of will, the brain is always represented only by a single spherical mass from which essentially only the pair of optic nerves emerges. In the higher animals, from the fishes onward — that is, in the series to which at its summit human formation directly connects, and where finally the spirit dawns in the soul, and with cognition the will too becomes freer, while there always still remains a broad dark region of unconscious or merely world-conscious soul-life — there is throughout, alongside a pervading division of breadth into two symmetrical halves, an original threefold articulation of the brain running along the dimension of length, and of the skull structures enclosing it. Everywhere, and always most distinctly in the first formation, one distinguishes in the latter regard three essentially different brain masses: a forebrain, a midbrain, and a hindbrain. The midbrain, by the fact that the optic nerves still emerge from it, clearly reveals that it is the same mass that in the lower animals still constitutes the entire brain. The forebrain is that from which the olfactory nerves emerge, and in it we observe most definitely that, as the significance of the animal increases and its soul-life develops more toward the conscious, toward the spirit — and therefore most of all in the human organism — it increases in mass by far the most; that its lateral halves most definitely separate into two distinct lateral brain masses connected only by commissures; and that this double mass, united into a unity, becomes more and more the true and highest center of all nerve structures. The hindbrain is that from which the auditory nerves emerge — that is, the nerves destined for the sense of the finest, one might say most spiritualized movement — and which likewise separates into two lateral halves that, however, remain more intimately connected; but it is also that which lies nearest to the spinal cord — that is, to the structure that mediates especially the nerve conduction to the organs of our reaction outward, the musculature of the limbs. The hindbrain too receives an ever greater development with the heightening of the animal's significance and with the more energetic and varied reaction, while the midbrain, also divided into two lateral halves, never develops to a higher degree; on the contrary, the higher the general type, the more it conceals itself between forebrain and hindbrain. If we take all this into consideration, we absolutely cannot deny that the significance of these three brains, or three divisions of the brain in general, must each be a certain different and distinctive one. That the mass which through the unconscious working of the idea is posited as the center of all nerves — that is, of all properly psychical structures — is formed not, as in the more unconscious creatures, as a simple one, but perfectly analogous to the triad here developing in the soul — of self-conscious cognition and willing, alongside the feeling that still proceeds from the unconscious — likewise as a threefold, or properly twofold-threefold one (because in all three structures two equal halves are present), is in any case a moment from which important insights about the determination of these individual parts must emerge. In addition, one must bear in mind that the earliest formation of the brain shows as yet absolutely no conducting fiber substance at all, but only uniform, self-resting cell substance, and that thus each of these three parts can originally be far less connected with the others than later; so that the more we consider the developmental history of the brain in the series of animal creatures, the less we may doubt that in this tripartition the threefold radiation of the soul unfolding itself in higher significance — that is, the distinctions of unconscious feeling, of conscious higher cognition, and of conscious willing and acting — is organically indicated, just as in the lateral bipartition (one could say into three right and three left brain masses) the general symmetry of the higher bodily structures repeats itself, and provision is thereby made that if one side is damaged or destroyed, the other may take its place.

[p. 184] [p. 185] If, then, this different significance — this earliest symbolism of the spiritual in the corporeal — emerges so clearly in the first formation, where the connecting, conducting fiber mass is still absent, then even in the full development of the organism something of this local relationship must continually remain, although unfailingly the further the inner development advances, the more thoroughly all parts, including the two lateral brains, are connected, most intimately fused and interwoven through a thousandfold radiation of fiber bundles, and the less can relationships bound to locality between individual regions of the spiritual and the corporeal be distinguished. For this reason it is therefore to be explained why, on the other hand, in animals — in which the higher human unity is never yet fully achieved — even the physiological experiment still most definitely indicates the relation of the forebrain to cognition and that of the hindbrain to movement and every kind of reaction. Animals from which the hemispheres (both halves of the forebrain) are removed are as though in continual sleep, recognizing nothing more; animals from which the hindbrain (the cerebellum) is removed are utterly incapable of any regulated movement. Even in the human being, however, the same relationships remain clearly recognizable. Pressure, injury, and disease of the hemispheres still produce preferentially disturbance of cognition, and pressure, injury, and disease of the cerebellum still produce preferentially disturbance of volitional movements, although here — and indeed the more maturely the organism is developed, the more thousandfold the inner relationships between the brain masses become — the less, therefore, any more definite localization of the three rays of the life of the soul upon the three masses of the brain can make itself noticeable. It is the latter especially whereby Gall's organ-doctrine of the brain presents itself as a complete absurdity, and herein lies at the same time the great difference between what I have set forth as a scientific doctrine of the brain and skull<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> [p. 186] Herein also lies the refutation of the view of that English physiologist who attributes to the lateral duplicity of the brain masses such a significance as if thereby the existence of two different brains and at the same time of a true dualism of the soul were demonstrated, so that each brain could think for itself and carry on a kind of dialogue with the other. It is indeed important to note that the duplicity which expresses itself in all higher human organs, so that each individual in this regard is set against the world as a twofold-equipped being, also presents itself in the brain; however, just as seeing is only one faculty, although expressing itself in two eyes, so too is thinking only one, although the representations are twofold present (already as a consequence of the duplicity of the higher sense organs receiving them), and although it can certainly thereby become clear that when through disease of one lateral division of the brain the representations grounded there are also altered, this may often occasion a disturbance of the congruence of thinking, just as the incongruence of both eyes can produce a disturbing double vision.

[p. 187] and the craniology of the Viennese physiologist.

It is evident, namely, that if originally — that is, at a time when no conducting substance at all was yet developed in the brain — the threefold articulation of the brain must be called the organic expression of the threefold radiation of the soul destined for higher unfolding, then, I say, in thousandfold qualitative and quantitative modifications of the relationship between these three brain masses — even if not measurable for our acuity of sense — the diversity of different human beings must be indicated. Necessarily, in the more or less of the one against the other, and of all again in relation to the total organism, the kind and energy of each psyche in general and of its individual radiations in particular will be depicted and expressed. The particular modality in which, accordingly, in such a configuration the first disposition for each individual is given will of course then also develop further through the later unfolding of the brain, and therefore even at completed maturity a thousandfold different individual relationship of brain formation must occur among different human beings — a relationship in which now the one, now the other brain mass appears particularly developed, and which then will quite definitely always correspond exactly with a particular psychical individuality. It has indeed already been observed how in the mature state the individual rays of the psyche by no means appear bound only to this or that brain mass, but how the higher synthesis of the brain's structure has to a great extent leveled out these separations — yet in such a way that the predominance of the original significance must still always make itself recognizable in injuries and diseases of the individual brain masses. This synthesis is, however, achieved at a higher stage of development by the fact that we find the tissue of that remarkable structure we call the brain thousandfold and again thousandfold pervaded and connected everywhere by the radiations of fiber bundles of those most delicate, semi-fluid, crystal-clear primitive fibers. Each and every deposit of primitive vesicle substance is manifoldly linked by conduction arcs with most of the rest, and just as in the more highly formed spirit all feeling, cognition, and willing increasingly and more completely becomes only a unity, so too the organic image of this archetype — the very image which in itself at the same time again becomes the condition of the archetype's revelation in self-consciousness — appears more and more only as a single whole: a whole whose injuries therefore at every point must always affect all rays of psychical life simultaneously, and only under certain circumstances affect one or another predominantly. Thus that apparent contradiction is completely resolved which seemed to be given in the fact that, on the one hand, a local mirroring of the three fundamental directions of the soul and of their respective individual relationship was irrefutably present, and yet, with the completed development of soul and brain, the established higher synthetic unity once again almost abolishes all local relationship. Whoever rightly grasps this mystery can thereby certainly have the most complete possible elucidation of the way in which, even in these highest regions of our life, the conscious is everywhere conditioned by an unconscious.

[p. 188] However, so that here, where it could not be avoided to mention the doctrines as well as the false doctrines of cranioscopy and phrenology, some insights in this regard too may be found, I will add further what the relation of the skull formation to the brain is. In general one must bear in mind that the skeleton, or more precisely the nerve-skeleton, arises in consequence of the necessarily demanded contrast of a most perfectly solidified, consolidated mass to one that most perfectly persists in that semi-fluid primordial state of all that is organic. This solidified element is therefore everywhere in exact reciprocal relation with the central masses of the nervous system, in which precisely that elementary state of the organic is maintained with remarkable purity. As many divisions of the spinal cord, expressed in the number of nerve pairs, so many vertebrae of the spine; as many essential divisions of the brain, marked by the essential nerve pairs of the great sense organs, so many essential vertebrae of the skull are there. Of the entire vertebral column of the cranial skeleton, then, which consists of six vertebrae and several intervertebral elements, the most essential and largest — the three proper cranial vertebrae — correspond exactly to the three principal masses of the brain. Here too, in the first presentation of this formation in the embryo or in lower animals, this correspondence is the spatially most exact, so that precisely each brain mass is contained in one cranial vertebra; in higher animals and in later periods of human development — that is, precisely where the synthesis of the brain's structure through the development of conducting fibers increasingly establishes the higher unity of the whole — not only do the three vertebrae fuse ever more completely into a spherical bony cavity of the skull, but it is also no longer exclusively each vertebra filled only by the brain mass assigned to it; rather, the forebrain (the hemispheres) in particular, in accordance with the ever more and more heightening significance of cognition, grows to such an extent that it first, besides the anterior cranial vertebra, also fills that of the middle cranium, and finally even partly that of the posterior cranium, although the hindbrain always still essentially remains in the occiput and the midbrain in the mid-cranium. In all this, however, the remarkable structure of the skeleton faithfully preserves the original articulation of the enclosed, exalted nerve structure, and the original more equally divided triplicity of the brain is continually represented in the fairly equally large three vertebrae of the skull — except that the latter grow together into the single cranial cavity, just as the brain masses, through the unifying, connecting fiber formation, increasingly fuse synthetically into a unity. But just as in the relationships of the three brain masses to one another infinite variations and deviations are posited from the very beginning in individual cases, so too already in higher animals of one genus, and most of all in the human being among several individuals, the relationship of the three cranial vertebrae to each other and to the whole organism varies infinitely, and thereby likewise points to the infinite varieties of which the human personality is capable and must be capable, so that only in all these differences can the high significance of the microcosm of humanity fully live itself out.

[p. 189] [p. 190] Each of these three bony vertebrae must therefore necessarily become for us a symbol at which the particularity of the brain mass originally enclosed by it alone is always betrayed, and indirectly thus also a symbol for the ray of the life of the soul that was originally organically represented precisely in this brain structure. If we take thousands of human skulls, the formation of all these individual relationships of the vertebrae will never and nowhere be perfectly alike; rather, the three great vertebrae will always appear somewhat differently constituted in relation to one another, just as each spiritual individuality must essentially differ from every other. To be sure, these differences of the symbol, like those of the essence, are here often very fine, and when we attempt to express these deviations of the cranial vertebrae in measurements, it can always be only a matter of a rough esti-

[p. 191] mation, and only the especially conspicuous will present itself as measurable. On the other hand, besides what is actually measurable, the entire manner of formation of these vertebrae will also be characteristic — the manner of their vaulting, the lines of their circumference; in short, nothing will be without significance, but much will be written in signs that can only ever be partly intelligible to us. Nevertheless, already by grasping the measurable, the larger proportions, much will be gained for the knowledge of the original dispositions of the human being, and for this reason cranioscopy deserves all possible cultivation as a special branch of knowledge, and as important in many respects, especially for the study of psychology.

Of it only so much could be mentioned here as to make clear the manner in which it has really and indeed now gained a scientific foundation. For the rest I must refer to other writings of mine.

If now, however, a concept has been gained (and through the foregoing it will indeed have been possible to gain it) of the manner in which the unconscious in the formation of the nervous system and in the development of innervation must be called the first condition of all conscious soul-life, then the question arises in what other ways the unconscious can still become a further condition of consciousness.

First, attention must be drawn here to the fact that the nervous system, which we have hitherto had to regard as the first and principal condition of consciousness, and through whose primitive fibers in particular an essential condition of newly received representations to be conveyed to consciousness was given — that this nervous system, I say, is by no means in and of itself suited alone and without the cooperation of something unconscious in itself to receive new sense-representations. The exposed or severed nerve is affected by every external stimulation only in a pathological manner, and the representation that we thereby obtain has nothing objective about it any longer, but is purely subjective; it is a mere change of state — and is felt as pain.

[p. 192] In order, then, for a sense-representation to come about, besides the nervous system there is still needed an in itself unconscious intermediary, a middle link, into which in some way the external world vitally penetrates, as it were lives its way in. This thereby retuned middle element is then first grasped and understood by the nerve-life, and only the alteration of innervation tension thereby occasioned and conveyed to the central organ through the conduction of the primitive fibers is then mirrored in consciousness as a sense-representation. If, for example, a taste sensation is to arise, a particular action of something tasteable must have taken place upon the epithelium of the tongue; it must have retuned the in itself unconscious life of that epithelium; and only then will the gustatory nerve, whose finest ultimate bending ends at the epithelium, peripherally take up the altered vital tuning of this epithelium in a modification of its innervation tension and in the same moment mirror it centrally in the brain, so that it thereby comes to consciousness as a gustatory representation. The exposed ends of the gustatory nerve would from every external contact feel only pain and would occasion no gustatory representation. The same holds, however, for all the other sense organs: for smell, for hearing, for the daguerreotypic process on the retina, and so forth.

In this way we must therefore recognize that every enrichment of conscious life through sense-representation depends not only immediately on the conscious nerve-life, but at the same time on a cooperating unconscious region of life.

Yet not only the actual sense-representations, through which an external world first properly comes into existence for us, but also all the innumerable sensations and feelings — of the various states and moods, of pleasure and displeasure of our entire existence — arise in the manner that states of the unconscious are taken up by the nervous system and thereby first brought to a certain consciousness. Thus arises that by which all our conscious life is so infinitely modified — what we call feelings, movements of the disposition [Gemuthsbewegungen], and ultimately passions: stirrings without which the life of the soul would, so to speak, lack color, the inner principle of movement. All this is quite essentially conditioned by the unconscious. For we have already noted earlier that the human being is a microcosm not only in that, through an immeasurable seeing and repeating of the first monad, the first egg-cell, he appears as in this respect an immeasurable cellular structure, but also in that he articulates himself into a certain number of subordinate life-circles, organic systems, in each of which a distinctive, subordinate idea of life again realizes itself — thus the life-circle of nutrition, of respiration, of secretion, of sexuality, and so forth. All these are in themselves unconscious, but have nonetheless their distinctive psychical side, their psychic signature, and it is this which, in its various moods, enters into the life of the soul and even into consciousness thereby that that properly psychical system, the nervous system, pervades them with its conducting fibers and thus introduces their particular life into the highest life-circle — that is, into the conscious cognition of the individual. Every particular mood of one of these subordinate spheres will thus in this way resonate in consciousness: the excited state of the nutritive systems in a different way from that of the sphere

[p. 193] [p. 194] of sexuality, this again in a different way from the state of the blood circulation and respiration, and so forth. Language has already taken care that we distinctly differentiate these stirrings, which come to the conscious soul in this way, from what is conveyed to it from the sense organs.

For while through the latter what we call representations is conditioned, on the other hand, as has already been mentioned, what is conveyed to consciousness from these various life-circles of the unconscious conditions those states of the soul designated by the name of feelings; and one can now well be convinced that here too only the quite sharply pursued genetic, physiological-psychological path leads to establishing a clear and truly adequate distinction between what should be called feelings and what representations. Thus it is only now to be understood in what respect the domain of feelings must in a certain regard be far more limited than that of representations, for in the latter the whole infinite external world with all its ever-changing phenomena is mirrored, while in the former only the mood of the various inner spheres of the finite organism is imaged. To be sure, the feelings in turn have a distinctive infinity of their own, and in particular, because infinite degrees of lesser or greater vividness and depth, and infinite combinations of different feelings, can occur, they have a scope that once again equals that of the world of representations; yet the scale to be traversed up and down always remains on the whole the same, while the world of representations can grow in ever new directions. The difference between both kinds of stirrings of the soul really cannot be better compared than with the difference between forms and colors, and in particular this simile also makes perfectly intelligible their simultaneous presence, their partly mutual conditioning (for example, so that the representation of dying, of separation, of being ill is most connected with somber, melancholy feelings), and yet also, for the greater part, their complete independence from one another. That in which both kinds of soul-stirrings most closely approach each other appears especially in this: that both kinds, once present, also share in the eternal essence of the soul in an abiding way — that is, they both remain present in memory and can partly be deliberately re-aroused, and partly recur of their own according to a certain cycle. Deliberately, however, the feelings cannot then be re-aroused immediately, but only by means of the representations homogeneous with them. In truth all these relationships are very remarkable, and in a later section the history of these stirrings must occupy us further in detail. At present the point was only to show how the world of unconscious life within us enters into conscious life and determines it; whereby, moreover, it is by no means excluded that conscious life in turn also most manifoldly determines the unconscious, of which more detailed treatment now follows.

[p. 195]

### c. Von der Art und Weise wie das bewußte Seelenleben auf das unbewußte einwirkt — c. On the manner in which conscious soul-life acts upon the unconscious

The whole history of the healthy psychical development of the human being shows a continual growth of the conscious region of his soul-life and an increase in the determination of the unconscious by the conscious. The realm of the unconscious, which earlier encompassed all life, diminishes from stage to stage, and what still remains experiences more and more the influence of consciousness. This progression is especially most remarkable because it points to a goal that cannot be reached in this existence — we cannot attain a thoroughly conscious life in this form of life; and it is evidently chiefly from this demand that the belief in a purely spiritual existence after what we call death has entered the thought of all peoples in a now more, now less enlightened way. What science has to say about this must be considered later; at present we have only to follow the course in which the influence of the conscious upon the unconscious expresses itself.

[p. 196] In this regard we must first distinguish between voluntary and involuntary influence. To the latter belongs especially everything that, as was already mentioned earlier, the formative life receives in the way of determinations from the conscious life. The manner in which consciousness, the spirit, develops in individual persons — whether in a beautiful and noble or in an ugly and base way — powerfully reacts upon the shaping of the human being's appearance, lending now nobler, now ignobler forms, which we call by these very names because it has long been recognized that these appearances are thoroughly and indeed symbolic. But not only does the conscious, which always also partly returns to a relative unconsciousness, thus act upon the unconscious in general in this way; particular and local currents of this kind also continually permeate the organism, and the conductor and bearer of them can naturally be none other than the nervous system. For what we said above about the particular world of feelings — that they arise because the distinctive inner mood of certain individual life-circles of our microcosm is conducted through the psychical system of the nerves into consciousness — this also reverses itself, in that certain series of representations in the spirit directly arouse the feeling homogeneous with them in the soul, and this feeling in turn, through nerve conduction, affects those particular life-circles corresponding to it. Thus, for example, when in the spirit the representations of death and separation recur, the corresponding feeling of grief, of sorrow, will likewise awaken, and the conducting primitive fibers from the brain will now condition an innervation current toward those organs which, precisely through their distinctive and externally occasioned disturbances, could formerly evoke the feeling of sorrow in the soul; thus not only, for example, the blood metamorphosis in the bile-producing organ is immediately disturbed, but similar secretory organs in the head are stimulated; the tear glands immediately secrete more strongly; weeping, the physical symbol of the most inward sorrow, makes itself known. Here one thus recognizes quite clearly how various processes in such a case had to be set in motion at once: the arousal of the representations and thereby the re-sounding of the feeling was the first. The attendant alteration in the tension of the innervation of the brain mass then establishes, as the second, a reacting centrifugal current in the nerves distributed to the liver and tear glands, and thereby, as a third, the altered blood-life and the altered secretion is immediately produced there — and indeed produced in exactly the same way as, in other centrifugal innervation currents in the muscles, the contraction and movement is there produced. Just as, therefore, with regard to the representational life one could say that nerve conduction is so precisely the condition of newly conveyed representations,

[p. 197] [p. 198] that one can immediately make this conveyance impossible by destroying the nerve conduction, so one may say that that involuntary reaction of feeling upon the unconscious sphere would immediately be impeded as soon as one could abolish the nerve conduction going thither. Thus, for example, in the above case, despite the aroused feeling of sorrow in the soul, the change in tears and bile would not follow if one could sever or ligate the nerves running to these secretory organs. Something of the kind we can actually observe when, in afflictions of the lower region of the spinal cord, the innervation current to the sexual organs is impeded. If in such a case a voluptuous feeling is aroused in the soul through representations relating to sexuality, the excitation of the sexual organs that otherwise immediately follows upon the arousal of that feeling will unconditionally fail to occur — and indeed only because that nerve conduction is interrupted.

In all these matters the way of conceiving had hitherto been made very difficult by the fact that one always spoke only of the inexplicable effects of soul upon body and conversely, and never of the fact that here only the action of one sphere of the psyche upon another — the action of the conscious upon the unconscious, and conversely — may properly be spoken of.

From the foregoing, everything can now actually become clear that is to be noted about the involuntary influence of the conscious psyche upon the unconscious: all those manifold radiations whereby, quite involuntarily and in manifold ways, the appearance of our bodily life becomes a symbol of our world of representations and feelings — from the blushing of shame to the pallor of fright, and from the smile of the mouth at cheerful representations to the bristling of the hair in fear — all this involuntary activity is fully explained upon further reflection by the above indications. As for the voluntary influence of the conscious upon the unconscious, however, it must be noted that it will be partly a direct and partly an indirect one. From the knowledge of the inner relationship and the various conditions of conscious soul-life one can easily gather that, precisely because this sphere has wholly raised and elevated itself out of the domain of the unconscious, the entirely immediate influences of the former upon that domain are possible only to a very limited extent. No one can merely through his will make himself digest better, make his blood flow differently, make his secretion and respiration proceed otherwise. On the other hand, the conscious soul-life — thought — can perfectly well investigate and pursue how and in what way the conditions are constituted under which it is possible to alter the phenomena of unconscious life, and thereby it can then indeed also succeed in deliberately either bringing about or not bringing about these conditions. In this way we act, for example, when we support or also weaken the further development and growth of the body by either supplying more nourishment to the organism or withholding it. In this way we proceed when we wish to stimulate more rapid blood flow and increased innervation current by bringing ourselves into a warmer temperature and enjoying stimulating beverages — wine, for example. Upon these rules, finally, rests the whole retuning of unconscious life of which we are capable through medicines. Indeed, it prompts the most remarkable reflections when we convince ourselves that in this way the conscious psyche, through deliberate action upon the unconscious, can even react back upon itself, upon its own representational and feeling life, and daily and hourly does essentially react upon it — now depressingly,

[p. 199] [p. 200] now upliftingly, now arousing this or that particular feeling. Thus, in the above example, the elevated temperature and the wine will not only condition more rapid blood flow and livelier innervation, but these changes in the unconscious will also immediately produce a different, heightened, more vivid feeling in the conscious soul and condition a more rapid stream of more vivid representations, and so forth. It is only from here, therefore, that the true task of that art becomes truly comprehensible which we call the art of living [Lebenskunst], and whose purpose can be none other than precisely to develop the conscious world of the spirit, both in the richness of its representational life and in the beauty and depth of its feeling-life, to the most perfect degree possible; for one sees how, on the one hand, the conscious spirit can work toward this end merely through itself and within itself to a certain extent, but how, on the other hand, an essential power of this art of living is indeed given only in that the unconscious, by means of a kind of education and guidance from the conscious, continually reflects back upon the conscious all those radiations whereby the conscious itself is maintained and furthered in sufficient fullness and harmony of inner existence. Only under this condition will the spirit itself then attain that height and clarity which it is indeed capable of reaching in a genuinely human existence, and which it will never reach if the unconscious of our bodily formative life does not realize itself in beautiful, harmonious health and support its unfolding. Naturally, one must here again be reminded of what we already mentioned at the beginning of our consideration of the unconscious life of the soul — namely, that "every ability first truly becomes art when all doing, insofar as it is to serve a certain purpose of the will, is again in and of itself carried out unconsciously, so that precisely thereby the highest ease of production is favored." The art of living, therefore, must not remain a mere calculating and deliberate reflecting; rather, it too must in part again become unconscious if it is truly to deserve the name of art and truly to yield the highest results.

[p. 201] As for the possibility of the direct influence of the conscious upon the unconscious, it is in essence limited to the possibility of violently opposing the life-course of the unconscious, and with it life altogether, obstructing it, indeed destroying it. That the conscious can, however, to this extent withdraw itself from the power of the unconscious, can to this extent place itself in direct opposition to it — for this the development of the full freedom of self-consciousness is absolutely required, and therefore the human being who has matured to self-consciousness is alone and uniquely capable of suicide. There lies herein an extraordinarily remarkable relationship. Not at all as though suicide should really and necessarily be practiced, but that the possibility should be there — that life, this creation at first only of the unconscious, could be abolished when sufficient grounds for it are present — thereby alone is that necessity, that compulsion, which is properly the mark and domain of the unconscious, broken and entirely abolished, and thereby alone is the unconditional freedom of consciousness fully established. This is what is pointed to in those great words of Shakespeare:<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> It is a complete misunderstanding when it has been said of some animals that they too are capable of suicide; when, for example, the tortured rattlesnake bites about itself and bites itself and dies of its own venom, this is naturally not to be compared with the deliberate self-killing of the human being.

[p. 202] "Wherein, ye gods, ye make the weak most strong; Wherein, ye gods, ye tyrants do defeat; Nor stony tower, nor walls of beaten brass, Nor airless dungeon, nor strong links of iron, Can be retentive to the strength of spirit; But life, being weary of these worldly bars, Never lacks power to dismiss itself."

Even though, therefore, the cases in which the conscious soul-life is justified in destroying the unconscious and thus its own world of representations may be reduced to the very fewest, indeed even though in each individual such case it may always remain almost impossible to demonstrate the absolute necessity of suicide — precisely on account of the mysteriousness and ultimately unfathomable nature of all human life, in which even the seemingly clearest cognition usually cannot prove with unconditional necessity that really all other ways out are barred and will remain barred — nevertheless, the fact that this possibility is given would always have to be called an inestimable good and, in the proper sense, the pinnacle of the complete freedom of a complete self-consciousness. Whoever will rightly consider these relationships can be made clear thereby why in the history of humanity, on the one hand, individual cases of suicide by significant persons are held in very high esteem, and why, on the other hand, the great mass of ordinary suicides are found exposed only to pity (insofar as they are occasioned by morbid states of the soul) or to contempt (insofar as in them a worthless life sunk to the uttermost runs out). Every not perfectly justifiable not merely destroying but in general harming of the unconscious in us must, however, appear all the more as a sacrilege, the more we have learned in the foregoing to revere in it the originally divine element of the idea.

[p. 203]

### d. Vom Rückkehren des bewußten Seelenlebens ins unbewußte — d. On the return of conscious soul-life into the unconscious

Right from the outset we must here distinguish the return of individual representations and feelings into the unconscious realm of the soul while consciousness in general remains present, and the periodic return of all consciousness into unconsciousness. Let the former first be taken into consideration.

It has already been noted at the beginning of this work that we must daily and hourly become aware in ourselves of the remarkable process whereby a constant alternation of representations and (though at a slower pace) of feelings takes place in and before our consciousness. Such an alternation is possible only because individual representations constantly become unconscious to us again, while others, hitherto unconscious yet present, step before consciousness. This cycle is partly a perfectly involuntary one, partly one subject to volition. As for the involuntary cycle, I was the first to speak of it in the third part of my System of Physiology and showed that we have indeed reason to bring it into a certain connection with the circulation of the blood. For I have demonstrated that we may well assume that the development of nerve-life which we call innervation — and whose current has much similarity with that of magnetism or galvanism — stands in a relationship to the blood-life, and especially to the continual endosmosis and exosmosis in the capillary system of the brain, quite similar to the way in which the development of the galvanic current stands in the most exact relationship to the chemical process of the salt solution brought into contact with the metal plates. Precisely for this reason it is found, then, that abolished or too weak a blood current disturbs or likewise abolishes innervation just as immediately as does an excessively violent one, and we ourselves can feel that our thinking and entire spiritual life in its integrity is conditioned only by the fact that in a pure, regulated course the millionfold-divided blood stream pervades our brain. A single disturbance therein and our thoughts become confused; a more violent disturbance and consciousness vanishes.

[p. 204] For one who is quite in the dark about the fact that that unconscious life of the blood rests upon the same divine ground by which the conscious spiritual life is conditioned, truths of this kind must always appear as a fearful dependence of the spiritual upon that unknown something which he calls vital force or simply the corporeal — precisely because he then has to think of the highest and richest thing within himself as bound to the lowest and most material. If, however, it has become clear to us that the unconscious in us, just as the conscious, rests upon one and the same divine ground, upon one and the same idea, and is conditioned only thereby, then those relationships present themselves to us in quite a different way in the spirit; we feel ourselves everywhere upon a higher, divine ground, and see ourselves in every respect elevated and reassured.

If, however, we follow further the relationships of the blood-life to the innervation of the brain, we soon find that, just as all organic processes are bound to a certain rhythm, this rhythm also As I already once noted, in this regard, down to the most recent times, a strange enough procedure has been followed with the unconscious in us! Quite failing to recognize its distinctive inner wisdom and beauty, it has been set up under the name of the corporeal as something outright pernicious — arch-evil — over against the conscious as the sole purely divine; and while it becomes the task of a genuine asceticism to see to it that the unconscious as well as the conscious should live itself out only in a beautiful manner, it has often become the task of false ascetic endeavors to corrupt and make wretched the unconscious, whereby, of course, the conscious too necessarily had to suffer damage.

[p. 205] operates in the currents of the blood mass in manifold ways; indeed, from here it even gives occasion for certain regulated movements of the whole brain mass and also of the fluids contained in its cavities. A rising and sinking of the entire brain, conditioned by the rhythmically corresponding — with breathing — greater or lesser accumulation of blood in the veins of the brain, continues uninterruptedly throughout the whole of life, and can already be felt in the small child as a slow pulsation on the crown of the head at the great fontanelle. In short, in all this a certain rhythmic behavior of the blood mass toward the brain substance is unmistakably to be observed, and if it is now established that the generation and maintenance of the innervation at the primitive brain substance is conditioned by the blood-life, and if it is equally certain that this innervation in its thousandfold diverse tensions in turn conditions or itself is what we call the representational and feeling life, then obviously a path is given from here to arrive at the understanding of why the tension of innervation in the brain continually changes in a certain rhythmic way — with which the involuntary alternation of representations and feelings is necessarily in some way connected.

It will never be possible for physiology and psychology to demonstrate in complete detail why in this process the representations press themselves to consciousness and return into the unconscious precisely in this or another sequence, since we have already shown earlier how obscure the relationship of the brain substance to the representational life remains to us everywhere; but science can rest content at having advanced this far and at having shown that there does indeed enter into the life of the brain substance a rhythmic principle grounded in a certain moment of its organic life. Let us therefore now hold to the pro-

[p. 206] cess in the life of the soul itself. First of all, the fact is irrefutable that in our representational life a constant alternation prevails, that involuntarily representation after representation presses itself to consciousness and others are thereby always vanishing from us again.

If we then pursue the manner in which representations follow one upon another, it seems to be especially the simultaneity of their reception and their similarity that gives the determination here. This has been brought under the name of "association of ideas," and that the particular conditions of the innervation tensions of the brain mass also have a decided share in this is all the less to be doubted since special processes in the sensory nerve distributions also speak for it — where, for example, a decided color involuntarily calls forth the color tone polarically opposed to it, certain visual images linger longer hovering before the eyes, or one tone demands another. Moreover, cases of brain diseases or brain injuries have also amply demonstrated that with certain disturbances in the brain certain series of representations are obscured or disappear entirely. Nevertheless, it will always remain impossible to give a quite exact account of how it happens in these cases that with the increased stimulation of one innervation tension a certain other one is always co-stimulated; the fact, however, must be accepted notwithstanding.

As, moreover, the involuntary awakening, so too the equally involuntary vanishing, and yet remaining-present, of a representation is a most remarkable process. Representations of persons, things, places, and so forth can sometimes seem to have long entirely vanished from us, as can certain distinctive feelings, and suddenly they awaken again in full vividness and thereby attest that they were never really lost. Indeed, certain

[p. 207] strange observations have been made in which it seemed as though a brightness of consciousness were suddenly preparing itself over a whole domain of the representational life. Such an experience was once made known by an English opium-eater, to whom, before the onset of the full narcotic effect of the stupefying substance, it seemed as though everything he had ever taken into consciousness lay spread out before him all at once, like a sun-lit landscape. In the same way it is told of a young girl who, upon falling into the water, had the same thing happen before the loss of consciousness. It cannot be denied that in such instances too the conditioning of the representational life by the innervation tension of the brain substance clearly emerges. When a rare, sudden radiation of it at once pervades all brain substance, the entire representational life necessarily mirrors itself in the spirit all at once — almost in the same way as, in the sphere of reaction, sudden excitation of the centrifugal innervation current in all muscular nerves produces a general violent tetanic spasm. The excitation of the innervation which here, acting from the brain outward, produces the general spasm, can in another direction, radiating inward toward the representational life of the brain, produce the flare-up of a momentary general consciousness.

To form a clearer concept of what the existence of a representation is like of which we are not conscious and which is yet not lost to us will never be possible; we can, however, recall in this connection what we are accustomed in physiology to call latent life. For there too we encounter states in which all perceptible vital processes, all phenomena of life, are abolished and yet life is not extinguished (one need only think of the seed grain lying for a millennium, whose capacity for germination nevertheless

[p. 208] realizes itself at the first access of moisture and warmth), and in this way we are compelled to think of this vanishing of a representation too as one where every appearance of the representation is abolished and yet the representation itself remained undestroyedly present; a gentle stimulus, and it presents itself again. But the question is whether representations and feelings that have in this way returned into the unconscious can gradually be completely lost. We call a representation that we are unable to draw back from unconsciousness into consciousness "forgotten," but we recognize at the same time that a relative relationship exists here, for often representations that we thought entirely forgotten suddenly surface again — especially in certain unusual states — diseases of the nerve-life have been noted in this regard — so that we thus never have proof of absolute forgetting. Without doubt, the most important thing here is that we distinguish what accrues to the inherently eternal essence of the soul from the representations, and what is conditioned by the organization of the brain and its distinctive innervation tension. If one could say that every representing and feeling belonged entirely and solely to the idea in and of itself, only to the soul itself, or to the soul developed into spirit, then necessarily every such representing would also share in the predicate of being eternal, and it would then also not be thinkable that even something of it could temporally vanish from consciousness. But this is decidedly not the case. All the above considerations had to lead us to the conviction that it is quite indubitable that just as sense-perception is conditioned by a distinctive peripheral modification of the innervation tension in the distribution of the sensory nerves, so the representation is conditioned by a distinctive central modification of the innervation tension in the brain. Between the possible duration of the same organization and the preservation of the same modification of innervation tension, and the possible duration of a representation, there must therefore necessarily exist a certain connection, a parallelism, and if it is undeniable that once the idea of the individual has lived itself out through the formation of the organism, this type must essentially be preserved for the duration of life — though continually in internal fluctuation and in dissolution and reconstitution — then this at once also completely decides the question of being able or unable to forget. The most conclusive experiences in this regard are also furnished by the cases already mentioned above of the loss of individual sense organs. Whoever loses sight as a tender child, when the brain mass is internally less developed and in rapid transformation, will, as the earlier substance of the brain is transformed, also so completely forget all visual representations that they will no longer appear to him even in dreams; whoever, on the other hand, already grown, loses sight, never entirely forgets the representations of this sense in his whole life and at least still often dreams himself as seeing, although even here after a series of years a fading of these representations and a rarer surfacing of them will always be unmistakable. Indeed, even the general experience that as adults we can as a rule remember absolutely nothing from the first three years of life belongs here, and many will scarcely be able to think back even to the fifth year. If we add to this that again in advanced years of life, when a pathological softening of the brain (the cerebral softening of the aged) takes hold, general forgetfulness is the unavoidable consequence, then it must thereby be proved to the most complete evidence that

[p. 209] [p. 210] the complete loss of representations is possible only insofar as the organic condition of their existence is completely abolished; where this condition is not abolished, a representation may persist for however long in unconsciousness and yet will suddenly press itself forth again.

If now, to be sure, the result just found of our considerations allows us to recognize with decisiveness that all individual representations and feelings in and of themselves, as particular stirrings of the soul conditioned by the organic, cannot outlast the organism — that they therefore as such do not share in the eternal essence of the soul — yet it is by no means to be concluded from this that the representational and feeling life in general does not have an influence upon the fundamental idea of our existence, and we shall make it a special task, when the growth of the life of the soul and what in the soul is perishable and what eternal are to be treated, to show that science is quite well able to shed essential light on this as well.

Finally, we must in this regard also note as an important circumstance that, although to a certain degree — especially by making use of the association of representations — we are able deliberately to call representations from the unconscious state into consciousness, we are absolutely unable to deliberately and directly sink conscious representations into the unconscious or to forget. An art of memory or mnemonics can therefore exist, but no art of forgetting. The reason for this is near enough at hand; for volition and intention can occur only under the condition of consciousness; the calling into consciousness — the positive — is therefore a work of the spirit; forgetting, the negative, the passing over into

[p. 211] unconscious life, can also be mediated only by the unconscious and hence only by the involuntary. That we can indirectly do something for this as well follows from what was noted above about the possible determination of the unconscious by the conscious.

Let this now be enough said for the present about the return of individual representations into unconsciousness, and let us now turn to the consideration of the periodic return of consciousness as a whole into the unconscious.

What is actually repeated here in the whole is only what we previously considered in the particular. Everywhere unconsciousness is the primitive state of every particular idea and of everything idea-like.
