Through its inborn inner power and under the influence of the ideas of the world, consciousness gradually develops within it, and representations are then mirrored in it. We have found, however, how much of the relatively unconscious always still remains in the soul when it lives itself out in a human manner, how late higher consciousness first develops, and how narrowly even this development is bound to the constant condition of the unconscious. Hence it is to be understood why, even at the full height of this manner of living-itself-out, the state of clear consciousness of the soul cannot be an unceasingly enduring one, but only one emerging alternately and rhythmically. We call the clear emergence of consciousness awakening — being awake; the re-entering into the state of unconsciousness — falling asleep — sleeping; and it is quite clear that this rhythmically alternating state once again only repeats the great periods in the eternal being of the divine idea itself, which we call living and dying. We may therefore say: as in the eternal being of the divine idea the period of living and dying is related, so in the temporal living-itself-out of the idea as soul are the periods of waking and sleeping related, and so too again, in the periodic state of waking, the consciously having-present and the merely unconsciously possessing of the individual representation or of the individual feeling are related. The one periodicity necessarily conditions the other, and they explain each other reciprocally.

[p. 212] If we now consider more closely the state of general unconsciousness of the soul which, in contrast to waking, we call sleep, attention must first be drawn to the fact that in human existence too, an entirely unconscious state, which we must call the prototype of sleep, presents itself as the original one. It is this state that embraces the inborn idea in its entire embryonic existence as the abiding one, whereas waking begins only after birth, when all the above-expounded conditions for the onset of consciousness can be fulfilled. The organism, however, even after birth, still for the most part caught up in relatively unconscious existence, must, as it were, take a special upswing, apply a special force, in order to arrive at waking; and in the natural proportion, insofar as it is something planetary, it requires for this the sensation of a higher influence from the solar — that is, the brightness of daylight. Of this exertion it is therefore capable only for a certain time, and in the normal proportion it returns, with the disappearance of light, to a state similar to the originally general unconsciousness, and this is sleep. The difference of sleep from the absolutely unconscious state before birth lies in the fact that, just as waking still always involves an unconscious, so sleep continually involves a preceding conscious, and precisely thereby becomes capable of continually receiving intimations, influences from the conscious enclosed within it, just as waking continually receives intimations, influences from the unconscious lying within it. Only thereby, then, does sleep truly become not merely a contrast but a complete inversion of waking.

[p. 213] For the history of sleep, the most essential insights once again lie in the special attention to the processes of brain-life occurring therein. The earlier considerations have shown us that among the three brain masses, the middle one in particular represents the center of unconscious life, for it is the repetition of the simple brain knot which, in creatures not yet attained to self-consciousness, represents all that of the brain which develops, and it therefore appears in the higher animals and in the human being always the less developed the more significantly the other two brain masses develop. Now it is very important that precisely the pair of optic nerves proceeds from this midbrain, for precisely therein lies one of the most important reasons why it comes to an awakening at all in the nerve-life. That precisely the most powerful solar relationship — light — affects the peripheral innervation tension on a nerve pair through which the self-resting nerve-life must be moved and shaken precisely where it is destined to be the center for the entire unconscious region of the psyche, explains especially why nothing so powerfully holds consciousness together and nothing so strongly counteracts the sinking into the unconscious (sleep) as light. Therefore, even though with the more and more developing unity of brain-life all other excitations of peripheral innervation tension also bear upon the awakening and maintenance of waking, light will nonetheless always act most powerfully and most naturally, precisely because its effect especially and first of all reverberates and excites where the self-absorption of the unconscious, withdrawn most

[p. 214] deeply into itself, otherwise broods. Only when one makes these relationships quite clear to oneself does one recognize and understand the important relation of the sense of sight and of the opening and closing of the eyes to the disappearance as well as the return of sleep. The ray of light, which already through the eyelid excites the retina, unconsciously prompts the opening of the eye, and only then does a stronger peripheral modification of the innervation tension of the optic nerve result, which mirrors itself in the midbrain and which, as it were, illuminates the night of unconscious life, indeed which occasions that henceforth cognition and willing receive their higher determination only from the focal point of self-consciousness — the human being is awake!

But it has already been shown earlier that only the unconscious has the property of being seized by neither fatigue nor of requiring practice, whereas everything that rises to consciousness must, after a certain time, experience in its activity a relaxation that we designate as fatigue. Thus, then, that higher degree of innervation tension which we designate as waking also becomes fatigued, and thereby further all the reaction that, in waking, extends over the entire posture and movement becomes fatigued; the tension abates, and again, inversely, it is first in the eye that the transition to sleep now announces itself; despite the influence of light, the sensation of light grows dim; the eyelids close; the midbrain is again free from aroused higher innervation tension; unconsciousness once more enters into its former rights — the human being falls asleep.

Certainly, if anything makes vivid the distinctive kind of independence that the conscious and the unconscious realm of the life of the soul maintain over against one another in us, once the development of the soul has progressed to the unfolding of conscious spirit and the creation of a particular world of representations, it is the comparison of sleep and waking. Here, the encompassing of all that is unconscious by consciousness, which pervades all processes of the unconscious side and strives to take them up into itself, just as involuntarily in it the unconscious continually makes itself known in dark feelings; there, the entering of all that is conscious into the sphere of the unconscious, so that even within it the continued flow of a world of representations and feelings, but without spontaneity of cognition and of will, still realizes itself. This reciprocal relationship is very remarkable, and whoever can rightly think it through will thereby decipher all the mysteries of the life of sleep. In it are repeated not only the constantly alternating states of the planet between day and night, but also a constantly alternating state in the most essential vital fluids of our organism — in the blood — of night-side and day-side, of illuminated (reddened through pulmonary respiration) and darkened (carbonized through interaction with the substance of the body) blood.

[p. 215] That through the representations and feelings that continue to flow and rhythmically surface even during this immersion in the unconscious, the world of dreams is conditioned, is already clear from the foregoing; yet precisely for this reason the question must first occupy us: is there a sleep without dream? The common opinion is that a deep, sound sleep excludes dreaming; but we must here think back to what was said about forgetting — namely, that we may be unable to recall a representation for however long and yet it will be present so long as its organic conditions are not abolished. Likewise, the rhythmic stimulation of present representations and feelings will not be absent so long as the innervation tension of the brain substance, constantly and changefully maintained by the blood coursing through it, is not abolished; but on the one hand, the deeper the sleep, the more sharply sleep and waking are demarcated from each other, and the less will an immediate memory of what was beheld during sleep as a particular series of representations in the soul carry over into waking; and on the other hand, a difference in the brightness of the representational life at different times and according to different moods of the organism is quite unmistakably present, both in waking and in sleeping. If, therefore, the boundary and contrast between sleep and waking is less sharply marked and the representations are of great brightness, we shall remember the dreams as very vivid; if the contrast is very sharply pronounced and the energy of the representational life is slight, nothing of dreams will remain in memory after sleep. Moreover, the mode of the spirit's activity in dreams is very remarkable. If we go deeper, we find that of the three stages of the development of the soul — unconsciousness, world-consciousness, self-consciousness — only the second, the consciousness of the continued being-affected by a real world, and therewith naturally also all reaction against a real world, is abolished by this encompassing of the conscious by the unconscious, and that we are left only with the representations of this world, which, however, now, since they lack the firm support of the real world, waver back and forth in the most arbitrary manner. Self-consciousness the soul cannot lose again, once it has attained it, as long as it maintains the conditions of consciousness at all, and thus it possesses it also in the dream; but of the three stages in which the self-conscious spirit develops — understanding, imagination, and reason — really only the first two come into activity here, since in order for the human spirit to be capable of the highest manner of bearing — that is, precisely as reason — an absolutely undivided, simultaneous working in all directions is indispensable, and thus also the presence of a real knowledge of the world and likewise of the beautifully living-itself-out basis of an unconscious. Precisely because, then, in sleep the soul works only imperfectly, the most rational person often dreams things that in the light of reason appear absurd, and therefore even judgment in the dream is so imperfect that something — a speech we gave, for example, or a plan we devised while dreaming — can appear to us in the dream as quite extraordinary, whereas, as soon as we remember all this while waking, we are only able to declare it insignificant. It is otherwise with the imagination, which, just as in the young person before the ripening of reason it is more powerful — just as it there fills the mightiest gaps of the representational life at a stroke and ever anew creates the boldest forms — so too in the dream it realizes itself in the most unbounded manner, and indulges itself utterly without measure both in the splendid and magnificent — rarely, and only with special endowment, in the purely beautiful (because its grasp presupposes reason) — and in the grotesque. From this side, now, the distinctive poetry of the dream-world is most clearly to be understood. For here no series of representations can be called up deliberately, since with the abolition of the possibility of a will-empowered activity directed outward and with the abolition of the highest activity of the spirit altogether, a deliberate drawing of representations that have become unconscious into consciousness is likewise abolished. The series of representations which even in the deepest sleep pass through consciousness can therefore be determined in only two ways: either through the inner associations,

which the representations themselves connect among one another — hence we often dream in a certain way onward from what last occupied us in waking: if we have heard stories of robbers, we may well dream of robbers, and so forth — or the feelings that arise from our external circumstances or from the mood of our inner life — that is, of our unconscious life — and from the particular relations in which the various provinces of our organism happen at this time to stand toward one another draw along certain series of representations as well; thus, when the soul is full of care or grief, we see in dreams series of representations corresponding to this feeling — corpses and graves and the like — or in morbid states, images in which these states themselves assume a certain form: in breathing difficulties, a monster pressing upon our chest; in fevers, a fire, and so forth. In that the soul thus draws along those representations that correspond to these feelings, it proceeds indeed entirely like the waking poet, who also calls up the images and seeks to bring to the greatest distinctness those which are most adequate to the feelings that most inwardly move him. In this way we may easily see how a part of dream-interpretation, which relates to bodily ailments and their premonition, is entirely conditioned by this kind of poetry. A disproportion developing between systems of the organism, a principle of disease asserting itself among them, excites a particular feeling (one may recall what the distinction was between the coming-to-consciousness of feelings and of representations), and this feeling now determines a certain series, a certain kind of representations, whose images can then be regarded as poetic symbols of precisely these feelings, and thus of these disproportions, these morbid states. Thus I knew a man who regularly, before peculiar attacks of chest spasms tormented him, dreamt of being bitten by cats; another to whom, always before a certain kind of headache, heavily plodding or charging bulls appeared in dreams, and so forth. This kind of dream-poetry must therefore in this way be called perfectly intelligible.

[p. 219] There is, however, yet another kind of poetry, indeed of prophetic art of the dream, for whose understanding the foregoing by no means suffices, but for which an earlier cognition already gained must be recalled. When we discussed the various properties of the unconscious in relation to the conscious, it also had to be mentioned that the unconscious is something more universal; that while the conscious of the organism first lets individuality and ultimately personality and freedom appear, the unconscious of the organism on the other hand binds it more closely to the universal life of the world, as it were universalizes it, and that therefore, as something unconscious, it is actually pervaded by and participates in all the stirrings of the world; indeed, that in it not only the distant and the near and spatial things in general, but also past and future and temporal things in general, interpenetrate and meet. If we now know that sleep represents a distinctive captivity of consciousness in the unconscious, with the abolition of the knowledge of a real world and of activity against such a world, we can also comprehend how in this wondrous state the soul, precisely on account of its deeper immersion in the unconscious, must participate more than in its free conscious state in that being-interwoven in the universal and in the being-pervaded by everything spatial and temporal, as belongs to the unconscious in general. From here it then becomes intelligible to us how, for the conscious caught in the unconscious, much may now in sleep or dream become accessible that in waking will never be attainable. Since the most ancient times, therefore, a host of experiences have accumulated which prove in the most indubitable way the reality and truthfulness of such dream visions, and once one has placed oneself at the right standpoint, nothing can occur herein that should surprise us or appear, as one is accustomed to say, "miraculous." After all, all the events of humanity, indeed of the world, hang together as a great, immeasurable whole, the greatest as well as the smallest, and it is perfectly natural and necessary that, just as in our own organism the most remarkable sympathies often show themselves between various organs, so too in this great outer organism the invisible feelers of our inner being embrace certain sides more, others less, so that the more closely embraced must then resonate in our unconscious with complete distinctness, even without being perceived by our awakened spirit. It is these, then, that can be beheld when the spirit rests embraced in the unconscious, and it is only especially to be mentioned that here too a certain poetry of the dream can assert itself, so that some distant thing in time or space is really grasped as what it is, while other things are beheld not immediately but, through exchange with some somehow associated representation, only in the form of a symbol.

[p. 220] From here one can actually make perfectly clear to oneself everything that, in the way of genuine facts of dream-life — whether in ordinary sleep or in so-called magnetic sleep — has become known, and it must become clear that a certain "far-seeing" under these circumstances will be for the human being just as much something thoroughly natural and necessary as this farseeing is natural for the pigeon carried many miles away in a box, which, once released, always flies with certainty in the direction where its nesting place is located. Precisely for this reason we had to cite earlier the expression of Cuvier, who compared the animals in their remarkable presentiments and instincts with somnambulists.

[p. 221] Indeed, on this occasion it still seems indispensable to append some remarks on the aforementioned artificially induced magnetic state, which, after all that has preceded, will now be more easily understood. For it has been shown above how sleep ensues as soon as a higher degree of fatigue is present, and how in the healthy state fatigue is produced by the fact that, in the stronger and sustained currents of innervation directed toward the purpose of conscious representation and voluntary reaction, this streaming itself gradually exhausts itself, and thus the unconscious once again absorbs and submerges consciousness within itself. This momentary exhaustion of innervation can occur by natural means through vigorous and prolonged reaction, particularly muscular movement, and through sustained and overwhelming absorption of sensory representations; on the other hand, to produce it by artificial means is possible only by exerting some direct influence upon the nervous life that causes this innervation to be more rapidly absorbed by the non-nervous structures. For this purpose the manipulation called "magnetising" is serviceable, that is, especially the stroking applied from the head downward (thus in the direction of the centrifugal — outflowing innervation), and often also the mere laying on of hands. That in this process a certain immediate mutual interaction of the nervous systems of the magnetiser and the magnetised person must take place, in order to bring about that increased streaming of innervation in the magnetised person, is clear; one could therefore call it a kind of marriage of two nervous lives, and in this respect the magnetic relationship does indeed have something in common with sexual love, which likewise in its highest moods submerges and sinks the conscious into the unconscious. Now, since magnetising powerfully directs the currents of innervation toward the periphery and allows them to exhaust themselves there in other structures (for precisely this reason convulsions or pains caused by relatively excessive innervation are thereby calmed, and for precisely this reason peripheral secretions, especially perspiration, result), the conscious life of the soul thereby becomes fatigued and the unconscious reasserts its primitive rights — the person falls asleep. It is now remarkable to observe, yet also clearly to perceive and understand, that a sleep produced in this manner will in many respects differ essentially from sleep produced merely by natural fatigue. I cannot enter too deeply into the particulars here, but need only refer to the history of vital magnetism itself; what I do wish to emphasise, however, is that magnetic sleep decidedly renders one more suited for the aforementioned clairvoyance than natural sleep, and the reason for this is as follows: As was shown, the sleep of the somnambulist and the absorption of innervation that conditions this sleep arise in consequence of a preceding, so to speak, marriage of two nervous lives. The nervous life of the magnetised person is, through the influence of another stronger one, in a sense forcibly drawn out of its individuality and pressed from its isolated being toward an external element, so that the universalisation of existence which we attributed to the unconscious soul and to sleep in general is here, where a stronger nervous life in a sense overpowers and draws to itself a weaker one, necessarily heightened and augmented in the one so overpowered. Such an evoking of the unconscious, then, precisely because it does not arise through ordinary fatigue but is forcibly induced in the somnambulist through a powerful drawing of her innervation current outward and the consequently sudden diminution of the individual freedom of consciousness within, appears for this very reason not as an ordinary but as a potentiated, heightened sleep, and precisely for this reason the universalisation of the soul must here increase, and the presentiments and sympathetic feelings toward distant things must here necessarily make themselves more perceptible than in those naturally asleep — which accordingly furnishes a sufficient essential ground for all somnambulistic clairvoyance. It follows clearly, moreover, from the foregoing, what one is to think of the declamations that present the somnambulistic state, precisely on account of this heightened, far-extended sphere of its sensibility, as the highest human, or

[p. 222] [p. 223] as one is wont to say — supramundane state. For if we have recognised that precisely the highest development of the spirit is that of reason, and that it depends solely upon a clear rational beholding if the noblest blossoms of humanity — science, art, and the free noble deed — are to become reality, and that this clear rational beholding and doing presupposes a pure healthy consequence of total development and continually requires this, as well as a free and many-sided interaction with the world and humanity as its condition, then it follows from this alone that certainly not in such a state essentially caught up in the unconscious can the true summit of human life be found; indeed this will be even less the case here, because this sleep is always connected with certain morbid dispositions of the organism. Given all this, however, it follows in particular from what we have said about the peculiarly divine nature of the unconscious, from its unwitting wisdom, perfection, and immediacy, that such a peculiar submersion of self-consciousness in the unconscious, when it manifests in a pure soul, is quite well capable of displaying a peculiar beauty, perfection, and immediacy. Although such a life of the soul may therefore never pass directly into deed (which explains why from these states nothing great for science, art, and the active life of humanity has ever emerged or ever will emerge), it will nevertheless represent a remarkable summit of human life. One might say that if in the life-active wisdom the summit of the healthy life appears, then in clairvoyant sleep-waking there presents itself in truth the summit of the sick life; and I will merely add as a further remarkable point, partly that precisely for this reason — and because through all illness a continual striving toward health proceeds — the highest tasks of clairvoyant sleep-waking have at all times had to be called the recovery of health, and partly that precisely because the antithesis of the unconscious and the conscious is repeated in the structure of humanity through the antithesis of female and male personality, every higher degree of clairvoyant somnambulism belongs essentially to the female sex, and occurs in the male sex only exceptionally in boys.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> This attraction of the innervation currents of an individual by the stronger innervation of another toward the outside is very remarkable. It is explained by the sensible atmospheres which innervation spreads around the spatially bounded nervous systems, as well as by the special organs of the hand nerves (the Pacinian corpuscles, condensers of innervation, as it were). This being-drawn of the weaker nervous life by the stronger is moreover reminiscent of the ancient name for amber, which on account of its electrical attraction of paper flakes was called harpaga [harpaga] — robber.

[p. 224] [p. 225] But let this suffice to indicate that by which alone a complete understanding of the seemingly so inexplicable states of dream-life can succeed; at present there remains for us yet another aspect in the psychological history of sleep to discuss, and this is its restorative, renewing, invigorating peculiarity. This has commonly been explained merely by "resting," without considering that there still remains a great difference between resting and sleeping. We can keep just as still, lie just as comfortably, and yet, if sleep eludes us, we will not experience the same invigoration and restoration that often a quite brief period of sound sleep affords us. It is therefore once again to be recalled what we have said of the unconscious life in general, namely that it, as such, does not know what we call fatigue, just as little as it requires preparation and practice in order to perform an activity. Herein lies it principally that from sleep, precisely because here the conscious temporarily sinks into the unconscious, we experience that peculiar refreshment which makes it an indispensable condition of our entire existence. But it is not merely a passing refreshment that we are to find in sleep; rather, we can, partly through this, and partly because the unconscious life also most decisively negates illness (about which the details have likewise been discussed earlier), find healing from morbid states here sooner than in waking, and precisely because this is so, that dreamlike cognition which has curiously been called "clairvoyance" — since it is after all not a perfectly clear, that is, thoroughly conscious seeing — directs itself, as soon as it develops with a certain purity, quite essentially toward making clear the means for one's own recovery.

[p. 226] Natural sleep and artificial, magnetic sleep are, moreover, by no means the only states in which the totality of conscious soul-life is again encompassed by the sphere of unconsciousness; rather, in many and various other ways such benightments can occur. For all of them it is essential that the heightened innervation currents be diverted from the central organs — the physical conditions of consciousness — and either conducted to the peripheral regions of the nervous system, or momentarily exhausted altogether. The latter is the case in the deep fainting spells that occur with loss of blood, or that set in through momentary abolition of all innervation current, for example through a lightning bolt or through special poisons — opium, prussic acid, alcohol, and so forth; and in all these cases the faint can easily pass into actual death. Similar things will furthermore occur with violent emotional disturbances, which either through excessive excitation (unexpected joy) or most powerful depression (great fright) momentarily abolish the innervation currents. The same will further happen where an external mechanical pressure upon the brain (especially upon both halves of the forebrain) paralyses the innervation there, or where a similar pressure through significant accumulation of blood in the brain, or through haemorrhage over the brain (as in congestions and apoplexies) produces a paralysis that here too can easily pass into death. Likewise, strong diversions of the innervation currents toward the periphery of the nervous system can produce temporary paralysis in the central sites and thereby momentarily abolish consciousness. In this way, for example, a violent pain, but equally an excess of ecstasy, for instance in the sexual system, can produce fainting, and so forth. Finally it must also be noted that in a similar manner sometimes also spe-

[p. 227] cial important local processes, such as critical movements in illnesses, metastases, suddenly appearing secretions, and so forth, can become occasions for fainting spells; and so we see in how many and various ways it happens that the conscious life of the soul can be driven back into the unconscious.

### e. Vom Wachsthume des Seelenlebens durch Lebensinnerung und Lebensäußerung — e. On the Growth of the Life of the Soul through Life-Innerisation and Life-Externalisation

The preceding considerations will have made clear by means of which processes the wondrous formations and currents of nervous life arouse representations and feelings in the conscious soul, how thereby acts of will are in turn occasioned, and how in all of these, constantly shifting and in motion, the inner divine idea of this our existence reveals itself. We cannot think at all more deeply upon this inner world of movement without asking ourselves to what extent through all of this something can be changed, and really is changed, in the innermost being-in-itself of the idea?

This question is one of the most important but also one of the most difficult to answer. Strictly speaking, it decides the value of life in general; it decides whether the idea of our being can, through its living itself out, gain a result for its own essential nature or not. It connects at the same time precisely with the question to be discussed later concerning what in the soul is transient and what in it is eternal. When we come to the discussion of this latter question, we shall convince ourselves — as already follows from the reflections on the conditioning of the conscious by the unconscious — that all that which is aroused in consciousness solely through modifications of bodily structures and of the innervation current in particular, that is, everything we call particular representations and feelings, belongs to the realm of transience, and that only to the idea in its being-in-itself can the predicate of eternal being be attributed. — To be sure, this cognition, together with the recognition of the peculiarity and particularity of each divine archetype, could lead one to entertain the thought that in such an archetype, because it, as something particular, can never relinquish its distinctive being, the temporal existence and passing away of however many representations, feelings, and acts of will could not manage to change anything, and that, just as a clear water-mirror always restores the same purity when the wave-motion of its surface has passed, so in the deepest ground of the soul the same stillness of the same divine must always return as soon as the life-movement that had for a time been mirrored upon this ground were over — the same stillness that was also present before such a life-movement began.

[p. 228] The answer to that question can, however, rest upon two kinds of grounds in particular: negative and positive. The negative ground is this: the entire history of temporal life, if it contained no result, no certain balance for the eternal fundamental idea of our existence, if every life merely ran back unsuccessfully into itself to the same starting point from which it departed, would lack every rational ground; indeed it would be in itself actually a lie, because everything in us points to and leads us to expect with certainty that the thousandfold revelations and actualisations of the idea must truly have a certain consequence for the eternal core of this existence, and because then the truth of this indication would never and in no way be fulfilled. As for the positive grounds, which can indeed prove that an increase or decrease in the energy of this our innermost idea of life is really to be assumed and exists as a consequence of life, one must first appeal to the immediate consciousness of the spirit developed to a certain height. A deeply inward knowledge in us gives us the testimony that in a certain later period of this particular temporal existence, the inner power and freedom of our true essence — that is, of the idea existing in itself — is greater and more significant than in a

given earlier period. Secondly, and especially, one must here recall the remarkable rebirth that the inherently higher idea experiences at the moment it attains self-consciousness. The idea which, as we showed, thereby steps at once out of the realm of necessity into that of freedom, must from this moment on — although it can never lose the true essence of its being, in a word, itself — be free to the extent that it need no longer prove itself absolutely and in all respects as one and the same (such compulsion prevails only over those ideas to which the possibility of a knowledge of themselves was never assigned), but that from now on it will be capable of increase or decrease and of an altered relation to others. Indeed, precisely for this reason the latter consideration also gives the conviction that only from the moment of the rebirth of the soul in the achieved self-consciousness of the spirit can there be any talk of a growth or a sinking of the being-in-itself of this soul, and it has never occurred to anyone to expect from what we have designated by the name of the universal absolute unconscious, which Promethean-wise develops our organisation, any increase or decrease of the fundamental idea of our existence.

May we, indeed must we, after these discussions, truly regard it as an inescapable fact that [p. 230] the innermost idea of our existence — that which alone

can be eternal in us — must necessarily experience through this particular temporal living-itself-out a certain modification, a real re-attunement, an increase or decrease, then first of all what kinds of modifications are possible is to be set forth more fully in general terms.

It is difficult here to follow with the element of language, which moves entirely within the temporal, that which stands not merely above all that is temporal but even above the properly spiritual, and just as Dante already had to create his own new words to express to some degree the penetration of the spirit into God, the deeply inward appropriation of the idea (hence the words: "inluiare," "inmiare" [inluiarsi, inmiarsi]), so here too, when we venture to unlock such deep mysteries, the reader must with his own deeper spirit pursue further the intimations that we are able to give here only in limited measure. God,

Attentive deeper contemplation of these relations must, however, first show us: every particular idea, and thus also the higher idea of a human individuality developed to self-consciousness, finds itself on the one hand facing the supreme divine mystery and on the other hand facing the infinite multiplicity of those ideas to which it itself belongs and whose existence establishes what we designate by the name of the All — the world. According to these two directions, then, a possibility will already emerge that the innermost essence of an idea reborn to free self-determination may develop more or less, and a twofold manner of approaching or withdrawing from other being will already result from this. The direction of the idea toward the supreme mystery we may designate as God-inwardness [Gottinnigkeit]; the turning away from it as godlessness; the greater being-drawn toward the world in its ideal foundation will express itself as world-inwardness [Weltinnigkeit], and the turning away from its ideas and being-drawn toward its merely temporal manifestations will be expressible as secularisation [Verweltlichung]. But to these twofold opposed directions a third is added; for inasmuch as the soul has in general been reborn through self-consciousness to free self-determination, it has found in itself a certain measure of energy which can be now greater, now lesser, and which likewise in the further development of life can now increase, now decrease; we can call a heightening in the former sense self-inwardness [Selbstinnigkeit], a diminishing in the latter sense self-nullity [Selbstnichtigkeit]. According to all this, it will therefore be clear without further ado that insofar as the fundamental idea of the soul can modify itself in highly manifold ways through its conscious living-itself-out during a temporal life, it will be elevated in the direction of God-inwardness, self-inwardness, and world-inwardness, but that it will sink in the direction of godlessness, self-nullity, and secularisation — directions which moreover appear among themselves in highly entangled relationships, and which must therefore also leave behind a very different result of a human life for the eternal essence of the soul.

[p. 231] Once one has therefore first brought these highest aims and outcomes of a temporal life of the soul for its eternal essence to complete clarity and objectivity, it may further be undertaken to elucidate and make vivid in detail by what path and in what manner that through which the soul is active in life at all — that is, the most deeply inward element by which all its representations, feelings, and acts of will are conditioned — can reach any result of that kind, one pointing upward or one pointing downward.

[p. 232] It soon becomes decidedly clear in this connection that the three great radiations of all soul-life — knowing, feeling, and willing — stand in the most precise relation to the named three directions of possible modifications of the eternal essence of the soul in general. In general one may say that the richness of cognition, the absorbing — the assimilating, as it were, of ever new ideas through increasing intelligence — nourishes and heightens in particular the power, the independence, the freedom of one's own idea — thus self-inwardness — just as conversely the lack of cognition, ignorance, promotes a certain decline of the idea, a sinking of its freedom and self-sufficiency — in a word, its self-nullity. Furthermore, the richness of feeling, the warmth and height of its development, and especially the development of the highest feeling — of what we shall later call the primordial feeling, that is, love — will through ever more decisive turning toward the highest become especially conducive to God-inwardness, just as conversely losing oneself in base and unworthy feelings, for example in love of the unworthy, or in hatred, must bring about the godlessness of the soul's essence. But not only cognition and feeling promote and ripen the essence of the soul; of quite special power for this is the exercise of will and the execution of deed. These stirrings of the soul are those by which, in the higher sense, what we called world-inwardness ripens, while a losing of oneself in action without higher purpose, or with base aspirations, brings about that sinking of the soul which we have named secularisation.

It would, however, be very one-sided if we gave room to the thought that only and solely one of the three essential radiations of soul-life always caused the essence of the soul to grow or decline in only one of the three designated directions; on the contrary, we are speaking here only of a predominant influence, for — manifold and necessarily, as all soul-life can in its ground only be a single and indivisible one — here too everything mutually promotes or retards. Thus a deeper cognition cannot but be elevating and conducive to God-inwardness, just as true world-inwardness, the right working upon other ideas, is unthinkable without higher cognition. No less is it hardly possible to say how powerfully the right exercise of life promotes self-inwardness and thereby also God-inwardness: the consciousness of a great deed carried through, of the worthy completion of a significant scientific or artistic work, ripens something in the being-in-itself of the human idea that in turn cannot be ripened merely and solely through cognition or through the depth of feelings. Finally, it may equally be stated that greatness and warmth of feeling alone are what, through the loving apprehension of the ideas revealing themselves alongside us in the world, also give world-inwardness a higher meaning, just as self-inwardness too can receive its true consecration only from this source. It need not be added that in the reversed directions pulling the soul downward, these mutual influences must make themselves felt in exactly the same manner: lovelessness and base feeling in general will promote secularisation and self-nullity just as ignorance promotes secularisation and godlessness, and just as false and base action decidedly brings about godlessness and self-nullity. Thus the most diverse relationships emerge here, and to everyone who follows the course of development of various human lives with a keen soul-searching gaze, guided by these principles, not only will the most important relationships reveal themselves, but from here a measure will at the same time be given him to gauge the height or the baseness of the true core of a human individuality, the rising or falling of the true essence of a soul.

[p. 233] [p. 234] As for the further considerations to be pursued here, it seems necessary that they should extend more closely over three subjects, namely: the diversity of the soul's growth as it manifests in different life-periods, the diversity of this growth as it is appropriate to the two sexes, and the summits and depths of the innermost psychic existence as they can be reached as the endpoints of these transformations.

As regards the growth of the soul according to the various periods of life, it has already been stated earlier that the child begins with the development of understanding. Cognition, then, is the form of soul-life which, just as it continually nourishes the innermost energy of the idea throughout this entire existence, so especially at first develops and promotes the conscious. This absorbing, assimilating (one might also use the Dantean word — inmiare [inmiarsi]) of representations and ideas nourishes and strengthens the power of one's own idea just as the assimilation of elements strengthens the formation in which the unconscious lives itself out, and even though the individual representations as such can no more be a permanently abiding possession of the idea than the absorbed aethereal elements are a permanent possession of the aethereal body, nevertheless a lasting after-effect upon the primitive divine in us is especially and necessarily present from them. The boundless need of the young soul to absorb and appropriate, as it were, in ever new representations the very material of the spiritual world of thought, proves the necessity of heightening oneself through cognition in self-inwardness and thereby more and more increasing the energy of the idea in general — for the spirit demands first, like Archimedes, that it be given where it may stand, where it may find ground in itself — and then it will move the external world. Although therefore the soul requires the advancement through cognition all the way to the summit of life, according to the saying of Solon:

[p. 235] "Learning without cease I advance in old age," yet this need is especially powerful and altogether imperative at the beginning of life.

One stage higher in the development of the spirit — at the stage where the unconscious brings the formation closer to the maturity of puberty — there where, as was already said earlier, imagination becomes powerful over understanding, feeling also emerges with force and works powerfully upon the growth of the soul. The peculiar divine creative power of the unconscious — of the unconscious which makes itself felt in the spirit precisely through feeling — shows itself as truly the nourishing, primordially conditioning principle, as that from which all spiritual warmth of the life of the soul must proceed. In the feeling of admiration, of love, God-inwardness ripens, as self-inwardness ripens through cognition — and the highest form of consciousness, God-consciousness, would not come to development without feeling.

Yet one stage further, when the full maturity of life is reached, the need for deed makes itself felt more and more, and here it is that through the transfer of inner productivity to the outer world, through creating and accomplishing, the world-inwardness of one's own idea elevates and grows. It is indeed remarkable how this life-externalisation, through which the representations created in the spirit, indeed the ideas arising in the idea, realise themselves through artistic creation and active life in a manner similar to how divine ideas realise themselves through natural formation — is just as indispensable for inner growth as was that life-innerisation through which, by means of absorbed representations and perceived foreign ideas, the innermost of the soul grows and ripens. It is therefore the period of manhood that is predominantly destined for life-externalisation, as that of early youth is for life-innerisation; and whoever will consider all this in such sequence cannot fail to see the complete picture of the soul's growth. Naturally, one must here also bear in mind how such separations are never absolute, how all radiations of the soul operate at all periods, only now one less, now one more; and then it can also become clear what is actually the task of the last life-periods for the growth of the soul, namely that of equalisation, calming, purification, and completion in the union of all radiations of the soul together.

[p. 236] In this history of the soul's growth in different periods of life one can at the same time find the key to understanding in what manner, according to the individual periods, a sinking, a diminishing of the energy of the being-in-itself of the soul can especially take place. For such a result must emerge especially when in any life-period precisely that radiation of the soul remains undeveloped which should have especially unfolded in it. Now it will therefore become understandable why in the soul from which in childhood the spiritual nourishment of cognition was withheld, in that which in later youth was not in the right way warmed through by the world of feelings, and in that which at the mature height of life did not prove itself in vigorous action, in the vitality of deed, there will most take place a regression and sinking toward self-nullity, godlessness, and secularisation.

[p. 237] At the same time one can now grasp why, the more truly through childhood, youth, and maturity of life a beautiful growth of the idea was achieved in fully fitting measure and the more its being-in-itself had already been heightened, the less a decline of it can again take place; the impossibility grows with the height. This is a very remarkable fact for the history of the soul, and it explains itself through the heightening of the peculiar divinity of the idea, which in the same measure as it approaches the primal source of the divine must be ever more attracted by it, ever more stimulated to inner growth, ever more freely and lovingly turned toward the world, and to which therefore nothing can any longer permanently attach that could move it in the opposite direction. As easy as it therefore is to grasp, and as manifold as experience confirms, that a weaker individuality sinks from stage to stage in the manifold encounters of life, so difficult would it be merely to think that the matured individuality of a Plato, a Goethe, a Spinoza could sink to baseness of being. Humanity has also received very early on weighty conceptions on this matter, and it is especially remarkable how through the philosophy and religion of ancient India the thought of a growth of the soul toward the divine, and of the manifold temptations — which in higher natures always ultimately prove unsuccessful — to turn it back from this path, runs throughout.

Conversely, of course, it will also follow from the foregoing that if there is an elevation of the energy of the idea from which a sinking belongs among the impossibilities, there can also occur, and really does occur, a sinking of this energy from which, within this existence, a re-ascent belongs among the impossibilities. This will be the case when through complete brutalisation of cognition, through coarseness and blunting of feeling, and through repugnance of deed, the inner sovereign completeness of the being-in-itself of the idea has been lost to such a degree that of renewed growth the question can no longer arise at all. The divine light of the soul can indeed be awakened and strengthened in the carefully nurtured child of the African, but no longer in the lost soul of the aged cannibal sunk in the deepest brutishness. In this sense, then, the threatening word is to be interpreted: "There is no redemption from the underworld" [Nulla est redemtio ex infernis].

[p. 238] The next task is to make clear in what manner a difference obtains in the growth of the souls of the different sexes. It has been shown above upon what remarkable law it rests that the revelation of humanity continually diverges into the difference of the ideas of both sexes and continually generates itself anew from the synthesis of this antithesis. Precisely upon this rests the original contrast that distinguishes the soul of the man from that of the woman, and thus necessarily the manner in which in both the being-in-itself of the idea increases or decreases during life is essentially different. The growth of both must certainly begin with cognition; for since self-consciousness develops only through the combining of the very first cognitions, and since through further cognition the inner energy of the idea, self-inwardness, is first of all destined to be raised, the ground for an advancement of the idea in general can everywhere be laid only thereby. On the other hand, with respect to later further development, when it proceeds in a true and beautiful course, decidedly different paths are assigned to the male spirit than to the female — to the former the steady predominance of cognition and deed, to the latter especially the predominance of feeling. If one pursues this law further in detail, it can lead to the most remarkable observations — in particular how, on the one hand, feeling, by occasioning growth in God-inwardness, precisely because the objectively highest direction is here immediately stimulated, is able to compensate for the other two life-directions to such a high degree, and yet on the other hand the true power and height of feeling can only be completed by maturing through intelligence and by becoming, through the feeling-productions of poetry and art, actual deed once more. We therefore find, in the first respect, how in certain cases solely and alone the feeling of the woman turning toward God-inwardness can already grant her that immediate blessedness and beautiful self-purification whereby the deeper male insight and the vigorous male deed can, to a certain extent, be compensated for and rendered dispensable, and there is no question that the often manifested peculiar beauty of the female soul uplifted solely by love and faith becomes intelligible only in this way, while a male soul that seeks to heighten the energy of its being-in-itself merely and solely through cognition and deed is always only capable of achieving an imperfect growth. In another respect, however, we must also concede that a male feeling matured through cognition and deed is in itself more powerful and will ripen God-inwardness in a higher manner than anything of this kind that can be developed in the soul of the woman. Above all, however, it would most endanger a lifelong growth of the idea in the woman (less by nature, however, than because of the mostly lesser intellectual cultivation devoted to her) if in later years a deficient cognition makes itself felt in her and thereby the progression is hindered. But occasion to speak of this will arise later still.

[p. 239] [p. 240] Finally, we had the task of taking into closer consideration the extreme summit and nadir of the soul's development, or of showing by what seal and what sign the most highly heightened or the lowest being-in-itself of the idea reveals itself in the life of the soul. Already above it was said: "the consciousness of the thinking spirit does indeed allow one to perceive that in a later and happily attained more mature period of this particular temporal existence, the inner power and freedom of our essence has truly become greater and more significant," and this was adduced as an important argument that the life-history of the soul does indeed leave behind a result for the being-in-itself of the idea.

Now, after we have already examined in detail the history of these elevations or sinkings, we can also undertake to designate more closely the state itself attained through growth — its height or its depth — as it reveals itself in the idea. The remarkable and in many respects so profound peculiarity of our language comes wonderfully to our aid here, and indicates more than would be possible in any other language the path that our reflections must take. For since the soul, or rather the idea conditioning its being, is something divine, every heightening of its being must all the more attest the divinity of its essence; the revelation of the idea as soul must be all the more soul, and thus there arises for us the concept of blessedness [Seeligkeit] as the only state truly and fully befitting the soul, but at the same time as that in which it possesses within itself that highest repose, truth, and clarity, that highest happiness of being, on account of which the attainment of this state is to be called the highest task of every further development of the soul. — In this connection, moreover, two things are especially remarkable to mention: firstly,

[p. 241] that the soul, at the various earlier stages of its growth, already knows continually (precisely according to its inner eternal being, in which times are one) of this blessedness, but not clearly and distinctly — rather darkly and more by way of presentiment — and that it therefore experiences in its particular states many things that it already takes from afar for that blessedness itself, but of which, having reached them, it always perceives that they were only semblances of it. (This is something about which Dante already set down very remarkable observations in his Convito, comparing the course of development of the inner human being to a pilgrim who believes he sees the goal of his journey now in this, now in that, but always, upon arriving there, perceives that the actual goal had not yet been reached.) What thousandfold remarkable applications this admits to the history of the soul can already be recognised here in general and will become clearer still many times in the course of these reflections. The second thing is that the soul in its deepest interior knows nothing so certainly as the direction toward this highest, truly soul-like state, and that it therefore in its deepest ground continually has a perception — now darker, now brighter — of whether it moves in a straight direction toward this goal, or whether it deviates from it and becomes estranged from this direction. Here too an excellent word is at hand in our language that designates quite precisely this original knowing, this certain knowing — namely, we call this property of the soul conscience [Gewissen], and it is this property of our soul that therefore always instructs us, insofar as we are disposed to hearken to the deepest essence of our spirit, whether we truly find ourselves on the path to blessedness or not.

From what has now just been stated, it follows further immediately how a state opposite to blessedness

[p. 242] is to be conceived — namely as the state most unfitting to the soul — most unhappy — in a word, as that of troubled-soulness or unsoulness [Unseeligkeit]. If the magnetic needle had consciousness, the state in which its north pole is turned to the east or south would be what we have called unsoulness for the human soul, of which we can strictly speaking say that it is the only true misfortune, just as blessedness is actually the only true happiness. Here too one could completely reverse what we have said about the semblances of happiness and apply it to semblances of misfortune, since so much of what people commonly take for misfortune is in itself none — at least not the true one; and indeed these semblances of happiness and misfortune are the very will-o'-the-wisps of the soul, which manifoldly disturb and hinder it from recognising the one or the other in truth! Indeed it may be maintained that precisely because the soul, by virtue of its being everywhere pervaded by unconsciousness, finds an absolute consciousness and therefore also a perfectly complete cognition impossible during its temporal living-itself-out, the unconditional removal of all these semblances cannot be achieved in life and therefore also should not be. It is therefore also a fine peculiarity of our language that, since unconditional blessedness and unsoulness lie outside the range of this existence, it possesses for what takes their place in life a middle word — felicity [Glückseeligkeit] and infelicity [Unglückseeligkeit] — whereby the summit and nadir of the soul's growth during this temporality may be considered sufficiently designated.

Now the soul grows and diminishes in its being-in-itself not merely as something general,

[p. 243] but always as something peculiar and quite particular, and so further we must speak:

### f. Von der Heranbildung der Seele zur Persönlichkeit und zum Charakter, und von der Verschiedenheit der Seelen — f. On the Formation of the Soul into Personality and Character, and on the Diversity of Souls

Already at the beginning of these reflections it once came up in passing what the word "person" actually means, and how it is meant to designate a being that is not merely animated — "inspired" — by the breath of the divine, but through which and out of which the peculiar inner divinity of its particular nature now also sounds forth [personare] clearly and audibly, though not always in a manner befitting the truly divine, and makes itself known. Hence there are indeed infinite living and infinite animate beings, but as persons we know only human beings developed with self-conscious souls.

Thus not even the human being as such is a person; the embryo, the newborn child — they fall under the concept of the human being, but not under that of the person. What first makes the human being a person, what develops personality in the soul, is only the awakening of the self-conscious spirit grasping itself in its particularity. It is therefore at present not so much, as in the preceding section, the growth of the being-in-itself of the idea in general, but rather the manner in which the soul develops precisely as this particular one, that is to be considered. Here, from the outset, as already indicated above, two different views would be possible: the one, which would proceed from making the diversity of the emerging personality appear entirely dependent upon the temporal de-

velopment of the human being, which, given the infinite variety and restless change of the world's appearance, can never be conditioned by exactly the same relationship, and would therefore each time necessarily become a different one even if, in its first being-in-itself of the idea, it might originally have been truly everywhere the same; the other, which proceeds from thinking the diversity of the emerging personality as fundamentally already grounded in the first being-in-itself of the idea, and letting temporal development under ever different circumstances operate only as reinforcing and modifying. — As for the first view, it could be justified all the less, the more decided it was that, although the appearance and revelation of what we call personality, and still more of what we call character, falls quite essentially into the region of conscious soul-life, yet the inner conditions of the same rest chiefly upon that part of its essence which we have called the absolute unconscious. But it is precisely the unconscious region, because it does not stand in as extensive a commerce with the outer world as the conscious, that will also be far less influenced by the external, and it will therefore in its workings chiefly hold fast to the inborn peculiarity and particularity of its idea, and will not fail then to transfer this particularity also to the conscious region. Even if it did not already contradict the concept of the necessary infinite variety and the never unconditionally uniform repetition of all divine revelation that infinite ideas of particular individuals within the idea of humanity should all be absolutely identical to one another in and for themselves, it would be sufficiently proved by the infinite diversity of the still unconscious working of these ideas — upon which it depends that in detail no human formation ever appears perfectly identical to another — that the fundamental ideas themselves must here be thought of as originally different.

[p. 245] If we therefore hold fast to the thought that within the one idea of humanity infinitely manifold individual ideas are grounded, but that only the actual living-itself-out of these is capable of bringing their individuality to that maturity where we can apply to it the expression of personality and character, then it must now become a special task to trace this course of development in detail, to indicate more closely the different phases of it, and to designate more sharply the essential diversity of the individual classes of matured personalities and characters.

But a remarkable reflection in this regard is first of all occasioned when we must observe that, with respect to the gradual formation of the person and the character as a spiritual being, exactly the same law prevails that we recognise in the organic bodily formation of the figure: namely, the progression from the indeterminate to the determinate, from the soft to the firm, indeed to the rigid, on the one hand, and on the other, the continual transformation in the process of forming and growing, the constant destroying and perishing and the constant reproducing anew. This perfect identity of the course of development in precisely the most essential moments explains itself at once when we consider that both proceed from one source, that is, from the same essential nature of the idea, which in the organically corporeal operates as something absolutely unconscious, while in the spiritual it governs as something self-conscious. In general, one cannot insist enough that only to the person who rightly grasps and vividly comprehends this peculiar relationship between the conscious and the unconscious can a proper understanding of the world open up, and at the same time the barrier disappear that older conceptions have erected as an absolute and insurmountable one between the corporeal and the spiritual. Only through this concept does the inner accord — I would say the truth of the world — confront us in its full significance, and we perceive why the study of the sensibly apprehensible processes of nature provides so definite a parable of the processes in spiritual life, and why precisely the erring and straying in the consideration of the latter is all the less to be feared the more the researcher's own spirit has been educated through the more easily and securely pursued study of nature.

[p. 246] Those two laws now admit of the most remarkable applications to the history of the development of a personality — a character. Not only in general does it press upon us that the spirit of the child remains more indeterminate and precisely for that reason so much more easily determinable than that of the man, or the spirit of the old man which easily hardens to the point of obstinacy, but we also now understand why a certain softness, a certain determinability of the soul is an indispensable condition if it is to remain capable of longer continued growth and deeper cultivation. It is very important for the psychologist to notice how exceedingly different in different individuals the extent of spiritual development is, how certain spirits very early cease to develop further, very early arrive at a certain rigidity where further unfolding, new development, living assimilation of the foreign is no longer possible, where only what was once accustomed and attained remains valid and effective and the desire to try oneself in new regions ceases entirely. On the other hand, there are other individualities whose spirit continually retains a certain softness, never closes itself off, is never finished, and therefore, though never entirely secured against error and vacillation, is nevertheless restlessly driven forward, hastens toward ever new metamorphoses, and can thus ultimately reach a breadth and greatness to which, when paired with inner truth and beauty, we have always owed the most extraordinary achievements for all of humanity. Among human beings much discord and strife has always arisen between these different natures, and all the more so, the more fundamentally essential the difference is that prevails here, and the less it therefore depends upon any accidentally operating external circumstances, but the more upon original inner constitution. A parable of this latter relationship is afforded when in the plant and animal world we behold individual creatures that at a certain point decidedly conclude their growth, are then finished and never from that point on reach further development, whereas others, the longer their life lasts, the more they enlarge, spread, and continue to develop; the former cannot by anything in the world be brought beyond the summits destined for them, and in the latter no power can entirely arrest their constant further development so long as their life is not to be destroyed altogether. So it is also in the souls, in the spirits of human beings. Those who close themselves off early, who have become fixed, boast of their consistency, contentment, and security, while the mobile and constantly developing ones charge them with hardness, one-sidedness, and falling behind in the progress of humanity. Conversely, the latter rejoice in their receptivity and their metamorphoses, but precisely because of their constant transformation never arrive at a certain contentment with their fate, live more between the anguish of relinquishing and the delight of absorbing and becoming in a perpetually agitated state, and the fixed ones therefore commonly reproach them with softness, inconstancy, fickleness, discontent, and faithlessness. — Relationships between which, naturally, a complete reconciliation and befriending is never possible. The secret of the contrast that Goethe depicted so movingly in his Tasso and Antonio rests entirely upon the perception of this original diversity of spirits within the circle of humanity.

[p. 247] [p. 248] Equally, a great diversity presents itself according to whether the determinateness emerges from the indeterminateness of the spirit earlier or later. One might well expect that the earlier the personality, the determinate individuality of the spirit asserts itself, the sooner its growth must cease and that becoming-fixed, that virtual rigidifying of the spirit, must set in; however, it must here be taken into consideration that again, the more pregnant the energy of the spirit is in general, the earlier it will also announce itself in its peculiarity, and from this there usually emerges precisely the reverse relationship, precisely because the significant individuality has the need to extend its growth further than the insignificant one. For this reason, in natures that are the expression of an energetic idea, there already appears for the most part in quite young years something distinctive, a very definite predisposition to a sharply marked personality, and nevertheless they continue to grow spiritually with power and endurance into late epochs of life. Whether the really sharply marked personality, what we call character, emerges earlier or later usually depends upon the particular direction of life, that is, upon whether spiritual development advances more in the regions of cognition, more in those of disposition, or more in those of will and deed. Already above it was discussed how cognition, feeling, and the stirring of will work differently even upon the being-in-itself of the idea; here it must be noted that especially with respect to the timing of the sharpness of character, the different direction has an essential effect. Goethe's significant word:

[p. 249] "A talent forms itself in stillness, a character in the tumult of the world."

finds here its complete application. For it admits of no doubt that, just as strong exercise of the musculature develops the contours of the figure more decidedly than exercise in thinking and in the life of the senses, so too the direction toward doing — the direction toward what we above called world-inwardness — develops the contour of the spiritual figure — character — more decidedly than the direction toward cognition and feeling. Indeed, in what we call character the direction toward the world, toward active life, remains continually predominant, and we therefore designate the various characters especially according to this diversity. We distinguish a stern, a gentle, a vehement, a suspicious and reserved, or an open and honest character, and so forth, and in all of this the manner in which the individual actively confronts the world is always especially emphasised. Thus it is also remarkable to observe how a personality whose direction on the whole is turned more toward cognition and toward the life of feeling can attain a very high development without, however, cultivating itself through to a quite decided character — much in the history of distinguished poets and scholars is first fully explained from this standpoint. But if one surveys once more everything we have adduced here concerning the nature of the spirit's development up to character, there remains as a definite result that the complete formation of a character will seldom occur without a certain rigidifying of the spirit, indeed that this would be far more unavoidably the case were it not that, against the advancement of cognition — that is, the utmost sharpness of the conscious spirit — feeling with its elevations and fluctuations continually pushes the soul back into the unconscious, and precisely thereby brings forth again the soft, ever malleable element. Only in this way does it happen that in the most extreme masculinity the childlike still resounds, and that this peculiarity is to be recognised precisely as an especially essential one for higher development of the soul, and has indeed already been early recognised, is actually expressed by the old saying: "unless you become as children, you cannot enter the kingdom of heaven." Moreover, we must not conceal from ourselves that a very special energy of the being-in-itself of the idea will always be needed if, with the sharpness of character perfectly achieved, that vital livingness of the soul is still to persist which can be capable of a development continuing ever further within itself. All of this provides the richest opportunity for application to the study and comparison of great characters established in history or created in poetry.

[p. 250] We now turn to the consideration of the second law of formation for the development of the spirit into personality and character, according to which a constant perishing and destroying on the one hand, and a constant absorbing and newly forming on the other, was demanded as inseparable from this growth of the spirit. Here too series of the most remarkable phenomena present themselves. Whoever will only look back attentively in his own life, or whoever is in general accustomed to following the course of his life with self-consciousness and quiet self-contemplation, will make singular observations of this kind. Like drifting cloud formations in constant transformation, so the inner states of the human being! New impressions press in, new feelings are aroused, other and new activities are demanded; on the other hand, a multitude of representations not only continually return temporarily to the unconscious, but are also gradually recalled into consciousness more rarely and finally not at all, are forgotten, and all spiritual formation becomes a different one. Necessarily, whole series of feelings are also lost in this process, many activities come to be practised little or no longer, and thus the spiritual self-conscious individual, while always resting upon the same basis of the idea, gradually becomes something differently appearing through constant transformation — just as the unconscious formative life too achieves everything we call bodily preservation and bodily growth only through a constant destroying and re-creating. Here too, moreover, a continual influence of the unconscious upon the conscious manifests itself. We have demonstrated above how the various stirrings of the unconscious life of our organism, the various states of individual systems and organs, make themselves known in consciousness not in and for themselves, but through re-attunement of feeling, through stirrings in the disposition, and we know that precisely these kinds of re-attunements, precisely because they proceed from the unconscious — from the realm of necessity — also for that very reason exercise such a decided power in conscious life. Since, then, the regions of the unconscious are so entirely subject to the inborn laws and the relations to the totality of the world that their inward-turned life revolves with necessity like the spiral movements of celestial bodies, and since it lies precisely therein that in them, with the progression of life, under unceasing destroying and rebuilding, gradually everything becomes something different, it is easily comprehensible why already from this source it is determined that in the further development of the spirit, continually pervaded by feelings, this perishing and emerging and ever-becoming-different remains altogether indispensable. But what herein the inner ground of resting upon the ever mobile and ever moving unconscious does not yet sufficiently explain, the constant alteration of external circumstances explains — the constant revolution of the life of humanity and the world, which are partly mirrored in consciousness and necessarily partake of its inner becoming-different.

[p. 251] [p. 252] It is therefore not enough that the psychologist can never cease to be occupied by the infinite variety and diversity of human souls and characters; there emerges yet an intensification of this variety through the fact that in each individual soul, in each individual character, such essentially different stages make themselves noticeable. In this respect too the human being thus becomes a microcosm, since in him as a species the entire infinite variety in the character of the most diverse beings of the world repeats itself and will always repeat itself. Hence that so often recurring surprise in the history of humanity, that when we believe the possibility of still newer, still more singular characters and spirits must surely at last be exhausted, yet again the unexpected, the hitherto unheard-of can emerge — something unexpected through which suddenly, often by means of a single individuality, an entirely new turn has been given to history. Precisely for this reason, too, when the attempt is made to give a general overview and classification of the diversity of characters, one can always proceed only in broad groupings, and interstices must always be left open to incorporate new unexpected encounters. Here too the diversity presents itself most easily and most decidedly insofar as it rests upon the unconscious and is thus indicated by the formation of the organism itself. The diversity of female and male character and the diversity of characters of the essentially different ages of life is therefore the first and most clearly presenting itself. To this would be joined the diversities of the characters of the races of humanity and that diversity which is stamped upon one and the same race when it distributes itself into very different climates. Other diversities, which work more upon the conscious region, then emerge when we trace the influence exercised by way of life and external circumstances; through all these influences, however, the power of the innermost peculiarity grounded in the being-in-itself of the idea presses through, and what is determined from here can always be brought less and less under any definite divisions, and indeed precisely because it is there that the diversity most extends into the infinite. — In what follows, it shall by no means be the task to trace all these diversities in detail, especially since it is beyond doubt that for the science of the soul it is not so much the multiplicity of objects that can be of importance, but rather all the richer results will emerge the more powerful the spiritual individuality it takes up for study. One may state that the more careful consideration of all the various phases through which a great distinguished human being educates himself up to his height yields a hundredfold more for science than the comparison and psychological study of all African tribes, of all wild eastern and western twilight-peoples, or of however many crude, spirit-poor subjects of our own race. In this too the great significant human being proves himself — I would say doubly and triply as a microcosm — that he in one [p. 253] — can unite what otherwise many perfectly — represent.

and often only im- Before we therefore make the transition to the collective characters that manifest themselves especially in the diversity of sex and age, it shall occupy us to consider somewhat more fully those rarer characters that rise above the ordinary measure of human peculiarity, of whom the ones might be called geniuses or "primal spirits," the others one-sidedly great talents or "particular spirits." To interpret both rightly, we must seek to make the collective idea of humanity comprehensible and intelligible to ourselves.
