Only when we behold in the historically living-itself-out of the idea of humanity the development of a great ideal organism can it become clear to us that, just as in any bodily organism individual organs appear of higher significance and greater self-sufficiency, so too in humanity certain individualities must represent the particular bearers of great turning points of its history and its life. In a word, the idea of humanity in general concentrates itself more in certain individualities, less in others (just as in the organism one organ has more, another less, the significance of the whole), and only those that bear the most such significance heaped upon themselves have always been those whom one designated as primal spirits; indeed, the expression "the Son of Man" for that lofty primal-spiritual individuality that was destined first to proclaim the gospel of love to its humanity has always seemed to me especially profound in this regard. We recognise from this at once that, if there is any particular seal whereby these true primal spirits were recognisable, it can be no other than that of a higher universality. The true genius, the real primal spirit, is never a merely one-sided being; in him a special divine power proves itself that everywhere, wherever he turns, presents him as mighty, as true, as creative,

[p. 255] as vivifying, and it often depends seemingly on only slight circumstances whether more the one or more the other side of his activity shall come to the fore. Even if his activity in life is to pursue essentially only one direction, the seal of universality will infallibly be stamped upon this single direction too; he will also, where he grasps a particular thing, always understand how to let the universe mirror itself in it. — Precisely the opposite of this are the particular spirits or the great talents. Here it is the highest one-sidedness in which it becomes possible that by no means the collective idea of humanity, but for that all the more powerfully a single quite special direction of it, comes to presentation in a remarkable manner. — The primal spirits will therefore betray a certain general, to all of them common, peculiar manner, and will always meet one another in a certain way, while the particular spirits, in whom, so to speak, all particular life-externalisations and life-innerisations embody themselves with a life of their own, diverge in innumerable different rays. These singular phenomena, in which often true atrophy of all other spiritual gifts except the one of which they are the bearers manifests itself — these souls that are now mere calculating machines, now nothing but virtuosos, now nothing but memory-books, now nothing but gymnasts or mechan-<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> A similar thought has been expressed by Carlyle: "The Hero can after the shape of the world he finds himself born into, be Poet, Prophet, King, Priest, or what you will. I confess I have no notion of a truly great man who could not be all sorts of men. He stands in primal intercourse with the Universe, though all others play with it. He first and above all possesses the virtue of Sincerity. He is the Revealer of what we are to do, what we are to love: both domains go into one another and cannot be separated." "The grand fundamental character is always that the Man be great. The great heart, the clear deep-seeing eye — therein lies it, whoever he be and wherever he stand." On Heroes, Hero-Worship, and the Heroic in History by Carlyle.

[p. 256] ics, and so forth — always also have a certain significance for humanity, but a decidedly lesser one than the primal spirits. Through the confusion of the two a dreadful muddling of concepts has often been created. Whoever heeds the marks given above will be secured against such things. Those great talents serve essentially only to show the remarkable, often almost incredible perfectibility of human qualities, and through them much is accomplished that could otherwise not be carried out at all.

Passing now to what may be called collective characters, as providing the general expression for the possibility of an immeasurable series of different individual characters, we highlight in particular the first and most important contrast, namely that between male and female characters. In order to grasp to some extent this relationship that everywhere penetrates and animates the entire history of humanity, one must begin at once by letting it stand as something irrational, never entirely resolvable; for that which in both sexes, already within the domain of the absolutely unconscious soul, is alike and yet different, appears in itself so extraordinarily intertwined and manifold that precisely thereby each sex appears to the other as a mystery that only rarely, and at the very most only in the most perfect love, becomes comprehensible — indeed, that in this especially a great part of the power of mutual influence, attraction, and being-drawn toward the other sex is given.

However deeply the man may therefore penetrate in order to make clear to himself the peculiar world of the female life of the soul, however much he may assert the power of his cognition to find the spiritual principle by which all feeling, thinking, and willing of the woman is conditioned, and however much may also truly become

[p. 257] intelligible to him in this — at last there remains something incommensurable, to be resolved only by another incommensurable, that is, only by the mystery of love. It will usually be no different for the woman in her understanding of the man, and only insofar as one may assume that in her in general the disposition determined by the unconscious predominates more, and cognition proper does not become the task of her whole life to the same degree as in the man, does perhaps a certain more immediate perceiving of the mysteries of the male soul emerge — a certain magnetic sensing — which, even without that highest incommensurable, will in many respects bring the life of the soul of the man closer to the woman than is usually the case in the reverse relationship.

It is indeed for precisely this reason that poets have always succeeded more perfectly in the depiction of individual characters sprung entirely from their own imagination — in presenting the contrast of the masculine and the feminine with remarkable clarity — than psychologists and philosophers have managed in scientific deductions. For the poet here stands to the scientist also as a feminine to a masculine, and precisely because he grasps the mystery as such, that is, more unconsciously, he often comes nearer to it than the latter, when the latter proceeds everywhere from the principle of wanting to draw everything and anything into clear consciousness. To be sure, as far as we are concerned, we are of the opinion that this latter view is altogether erroneous, and that precisely the higher cognition must do justice to each: present the conscious as such with the greatest clarity, and acknowledge and accept the unconscious in its darkness and mysteriousness — just as a pictorial representation, for instance, cannot merely let light count as light, but achieves true distinctness only through the combination and artful arrangement of

[p. 258] light and darkness. That this has hitherto been less perceived and applied is obviously owing to the fact that people had not brought themselves to the conviction of the important proposition that we place at the head of all our reflections, and to which we must always return, namely: "that the key to the cognition of the essence of conscious soul-life lies in the region of unconsciousness."

If we now proceed further along this path of reflection, there soon discloses itself something more about the diversity of male and female character; it becomes clear that the woman, precisely by virtue of a certain predominance of unconscious life, also adheres more firmly and immediately to that divine which we, precisely because it can never be fully measured by cognition, revere as a mystery and as the primal ground and highest condition of all that exists, while the man, in his task of penetrating through to the perfect deed of the self-conscious spirit, can easily remove himself too far from adherence to this mystery. To be sure, the man on the one hand, if he is capable of continually including the region of the unconscious in his reckoning, will become capable of a development of soul and spirit higher in every respect than the woman; but on the other hand, he will infallibly, if he wishes to give up the unconscious entirely for the conscious and allows his spiritual life to reach a certain rigidity in a one-sided egoistic or merely worldly direction, fall far behind the vitality and malleability of the woman. Among women there therefore remains in the majority, precisely because at the very highest point an unconscious something always rests in the depths of phenomena, a certain unconscious sensing of the innermost mysterious essence of nature and spirit, and they thereby retain a peculiar fund of vitality and

[p. 259] malleability which in the majority of men easily dissolves in a certain dry one-sidedness that likes and commonly develops precisely where the region of the unconscious is permanently deprived of its rights. What we therefore call a pedant, and, when it expresses itself without any spiritual energy in a meagre constriction of life, a philistine — and what in the society of men repeats itself most frequently in quite various forms — is for this reason entirely foreign to women in this way. On the other hand, their character for its part usually keeps more to a narrower and traditional track; it is rarely possible for them to step out of the ordinary, to prescribe for themselves with decision their own course of life; the actual concentrating of life upon particular purposes recognised as especially worthy is, with few exceptions, almost always denied to this sex, and never has a great invention, through which new paths would have opened for the genius of humanity, sprung from their spirit. — Of the male spirit in turn one can say that if the woman only rarely succeeds in penetrating through to the higher consciousness, to the deed of the free self-conscious spirit, then it marks the summit of the sex when, in free clear self-consciousness, he fully encompasses the mystery of the unconscious as well. Just as in the science of calculation those kinds are the highest which, like algebra and the infinitesimal calculus, deal with unknown quantities (with x) just as with known ones, so that male spirit governs most powerfully and bears within himself the highest character of the sex who, with a consciousness clarified in the highest sense, and a cognising, feeling, and accomplishing elevated by significant individuality, is entirely pervaded by the power of the unconscious. It is through this especially that what we called genius

[p. 260] certifies itself, for in a remarkable manner such a more highly endowed spirit is distinguished precisely by the fact that, with all the freedom and clarity of his living himself out, he is everywhere pressed and determined by the unconscious, the mysterious God within him — that insights arise for him he knows not whence, that he is driven to work and create he knows not whither, and that an urge of becoming and developing masters him he knows not yet to what end.

Already from what has here been said about the contrast of the characters of the sexes, it further explains itself that again within each individual sex the diversity of characters must be very unequal. It lies in the greater sharpness of conscious life in the masculine that here a far greater variety in this respect will occur than in the feminine. If we recall the different growth of the being-in-itself of the soul, partly in the direction of God-inwardness, partly of self-inwardness or world-inwardness, then the two latter directions fall predominantly to the male spirit, while to the female predominantly the former pertains, and hereby too the greater variety of characters and the sharper delineation of the personality of the spirit in the male nature is explained. It is, after all, the immediate consequence of higher God-inwardness to give up all that is self-concerned ever more, to merge ever more with all particularity into something higher; whereas both the development of self-inwardness and that of world-inwardness demand the sharper emergence of individuality and especially mediate it by the fact that they decidedly occasion active influence upon the world — a kind of activity which, as has already been remarked, especially develops what we call character, and wherefore also the various designations of character (a strong, a weak, an honest, a false character, and so forth)

[p. 261] are always essentially taken from diversities of deed. In general one could therefore well say: the character of the man develops more through doing, that of the woman more through suffering, and truly the peculiar strength and beauty of the female character usually emerges with a special power where the depth of the life of disposition has been tested through manifold sufferings.

Finally, one also cannot leave unnoticed that it is not equally easy for both sexes, owing to these differences, to maintain the peculiarity of their character in its height through to the end of life. For it has been shown earlier how essentially the growth of the soul is bound to the increase of cognition, and when we now perceive how precisely for the female individuality this direction is less natural than for the male, there results from this, in conjunction with the diversion of women from opportunities for the advancement of cognition that is grounded in their hitherto prevailing position, why so seldom individualities and characters are found in very aged women that attest to a beautiful unhindered progression in psychic growth, and why so much more frequently in this regard the individuality of the very aged man can be called satisfying. But there will still be occasion to add many a further trait to the characterisation of the sexes and persons when we now pass on to the history of the particular radiations of psychic life.

### g. Von den verschiedenen Strahlungen des Seelenlebens — g. On the Various Radiations of the Life of the Soul

Scarcely has the science of the soul been more harmed by anything than by the dividing of the soul into a multitude of forces, drives, and properties; for not only did that which should absolutely and at all times be regarded as a unity thereby imperceptibly appear in representation as something internally cleft and manifold, but there ultimately arose in psychology the concept of a being-composed of the soul out of such diverse members — quite exactly as in the doctrine of the bodily organism the notion of a composition of parts and of a mass of forces has wrought the most manifold confusion. One must find these aberrations all the more strange since, quite apart from the higher beholding of reason, even the simple healthy sense from the outset does not think of making such separations. Whoever contemplates a crystal and observes that it is at once heavy, and that it is electrical, that it even emits light, that it has a certain temperature, that when placed in a solution of its own kind it strives to continue forming, and so forth — it does not occur to him to separate these properties, these manifestations of activity, and to think that they could exist as different and distinct things in the crystal; it is entirely natural to him to think all of this as one. Only with a certain luxuriating of the understanding does the effect arise that dissolves this unity, or, if it thinks it as a unity, conceives it as a composition of diverse elements. — It is exactly so with what Plato calls the "formless, colourless, existing in thought" [gestalt- und farblos, gedankenhaft Seiend] of the idea and the essence of the soul and spirit emerging in it. Here there is not and cannot be any question of divisions and partitions, and already in the consideration of the formation<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> [p. 262] To what absurdities such things have led in physiology, the following passage by a Dr. G∗∗∗∗∗ can serve as an example: "Two forces are chiefly active in the healthy human body: the one is the force producing the desire for solid nourishment, and the other is that producing the need for fluid nourishment (!); both are also active together in mediating the business of digestion, and together with the remaining forces (!) form the digestion (!)"

[p. 263] of the higher consciousness from the unconscious, we had to take strict care that everything always be held as existing in one. Only a radiation in different directions, a doing, a working toward different ends, a positing of ever more different relationships, will emerge all the more clearly the more the soul develops toward individuality, toward the sharp impress of personality, and indeed necessarily so, because in the same measure the relationship that that one has to other unities also becomes all the more varied. Such different relationships, then, such different modes of living oneself out, such different radiations of one single and indivisible being, are what we now subject to a special consideration, and in any case it must first be ascertained with sufficient clarity how many and which radiations should be posited here at the highest level.

The first and most essential relations of the soul that has become conscious of itself are, however, without doubt those to the unconscious realm of its own existence; the other and likewise essential ones are those to the world of the ideas living themselves out around and beside it. Everything that in the night of unconsciousness our soul builds, creates, does, suffers, presses, and broods in us; everything that stirs there, making itself known not merely directly at our own organism, but equally what is stirred by the influences of other souls and of the entire outer world — all of which now more forcefully, now more gently also pervades our inner unconscious life — all of this resounds in a certain manner from this night of unconsciousness up into the light of conscious soul-life, and this resonance, this wondrous communication of the unconscious to the conscious, we call feeling. Feeling, this quite peculiar colouring of the conscious soul, which, like the truly conscious representations, lives itself into the self-conscious spirit and lives on there, but which shares everything we have stated of the unconscious soul — all that immediacy, that necessity, that completedness in itself, that not requiring practice and habituation, that inexhaustibility, that utter involuntariness, and all that unfathomableness — with the absolutely unconscious: it is unconditionally the first ray, the first great peculiar direction of the soul that here presents itself for consideration. Another great and powerful realm, indeed the actual hearth and centre of conscious soul-life, is cognition — the world of clear, definite, named representations, the realm of thought, which, articulating itself infinitely within itself, strictly and entirely according to the Cartesian proposition, "I think, therefore I am" [Cogito, ergo sum], first posits and guarantees the existence of the self-conscious spirit. While the world of feeling rests upon the entirely concrete and holds itself entirely within the subjective, the world of cognition is entirely founded upon abstraction, and in it the subject everywhere strives toward objectivity and finally toward the wholly universal. But were the soul that has come to consciousness nothing but feeling and cognition, it would have to sink into itself, giving itself up and losing itself, for no kind of determination of the outer and inner, no resolution and no deed, would ever emerge as a life-result, and precisely thereby all self-development — all growth of the being-in-itself of the idea — would become impossible. That, then, which in the unconscious life of the soul appears as one and indivisible within its world of creating, further-forming, suffering, and doing — this continual reaction to every perception, this constant doing inseparable from life, this wherein the soul now turns again from the universal gained by abstraction back to the entirely particular — it must now also be distinguished as something special that confronts feeling and cognition as will and deed, and although continually determined and stimulated by those, nevertheless presents itself thoroughly as a peculiar radiation of the life of the soul. This will, which first announces itself as arbitrariness and at its highest grows into freedom of deed, operates least in the domain of feeling, which, as said, carries over from the night of the absolutely unconscious the necessary and involuntary; on the other hand, it pervades and determines the sphere of cognition through and through. If we therefore consider that all thinking is conditioned only by the fact that the series of representations are voluntarily and with freedom summoned to the light of the self-conscious spirit and determined in their sequence, one may indeed designate the will and its freedom in this respect as the most essential condition of all cognition, just as cognition in turn must provide the determining factor to make choice in the will possible. But enough of these distinctions among the radiations in general! The history of the soul now has the task of pursuing each individual one in particular.

[p. 264] [p. 265] Just as there are certain delicate structures of our bodily organism that can be made visible to the eye only in the quite fresh state and only with the most careful treatment, while every attempt to preserve them longer as dead tissue fails and every rougher, crushing treatment instantly destroys them, so approximately it is in the life of the soul with the world of feelings, whose peculiarity likewise can be brought to a pure result for the eye of science only with a certain immedi<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> Among these structures belong, for example, especially those finest primitive fibres in the spinal cord and brain, which can be made visible under the microscope only through the most careful treatment and only a few hours after death, and which therefore, after people had occupied themselves with anatomy for centuries, were discovered only in the most recent times.

[p. 266] acy and freshness, and only with fine and gentle treatment. Harsh distinctions, manifold schematic divisions and subdivisions, nowhere more easily displace the actual standpoint and the appropriate apprehension than with these fine, always somewhat nebulous objects emerging from the unconscious!

If we therefore seek, always keeping to the whole and the living, first only a certain moment of orientation — if we strive to grasp the cardinal points of this world — we find here too a south and a north, a sunrise and a sunset; we call them joy and sorrow, and love and hatred. In the most wondrous oscillations and deflections the magnetic needle of feeling moves between these four essential cardinal points on the horizon of the psychic world, and just as from comparatively so very few letters an infinity of different words can be formed, so the number of moods of our disposition is immeasurable that can arise from the combinations of these four fundamental stirrings with the peculiar world of various named and conscious representations. In any case, it is therefore the first task of a history of the world of feelings to take into closer consideration those four fundamentally essential feelings in their genesis, further development, their periodicity, their relation to others and to the whole human being, in their morbid deviations, and in their final extinction and passing away, but lastly to consider those intermediate states which, similar to the zenith and nadir, keep themselves at the middle height or depth of the sky, equally distant from all four cardinal points of the horizon of a stirred disposition, and yet preserve in greater or lesser degree the receptivity for each of them.

[p. 267]

#### 1. Die Geschichte der freude — 1. The History of Joy

How powerful the unconscious stirring of the soul in the formative processes of the organism is for the genesis of this feeling is shown by its decided predominance in the years of most vital development and youth. Not only does the rosy Eos precede the hot, often thunderstorm-laden day, but also a hot, stormy, as well as a busy, tedious later life is preceded by the rosy time of joy of youth, while in maturer years even a Goethe had to say in his versus memoriales:

"Jubilate is a rare occasion."

Yet like all the other primitive feelings, joy too arises from a twofold root — once entirely from the night of the unconscious and another time from the day-side of conscious representational life. The more refreshed the health, the more favourable the organism's relations to the outer world, the more rapid and normal all life-functions, the more favourable is the disposition for the development of the feeling of joy from this side, and all this works all the more powerfully the less the soul has yet developed as self-conscious spirit. The more, on the other hand, consciousness is developed, the more perfectly the soul is active in thinking, the more will the feeling of joy also rise only from these sources and communicate itself proportionally more powerfully to the unconscious side than the excitation of the unconscious alone has power to resound in consciousness. Hence the so infinite diversities of joyful excitement in different ages and in different individualities. The child, the young girl — not quite so much the boy — can be filled with joy without knowing why; their features are joyfully transfigured, a cheerful laugh plays about the mouth, the eyes shine more than usual, and all inquiry after a cause would be in vain, or the causes given — insofar as they can be consciously recognised — would scarcely make it credible to us that they could really have excited this joy. In truth, the physiognomy of joy more grounded in the unconscious is also a very different one from that essentially grounded in consciousness. To the practised eye of the judge of human nature these differences, difficult to designate in words, will not escape, and perhaps he will most indicate the distinction by designating the physiognomy of the latter as the more spiritual. It is remarkable, on the other hand, how precisely this joy arising essentially from conscious representations can break through radiantly even amid genuine sufferings of the unconscious, amid sickness and infirmity of the constitution, and how much its motives increase the higher the being-in-itself of the idea has progressed and the clearer the cognition has become. Especially this mentioned increase of motives gives occasion here for extensive reflections. The highest is obviously when in the spirit the full perception has dawned — the apprehending of the idea — and when this apprehending kindles the joyfulness of the soul. The joy of the researcher when he may exclaim: "I have found it!" [Eureka!] — the joy of the poet, of the artist, when he may apply the Goethean expression: "an idea has come to me" — the joy of the lover when the deepest inner idea of the beloved being becomes perceptible to him — they proceed entirely from the conscious soul, but over the unconscious life too they spread a wondrous shimmer, altering the features of the countenance and the radiance of the eye according to peculiar, far from yet unveiled laws. In short, already in these reflections a very manifold genesis of joy discloses itself to us, and we perceive in what a multitude of different forms this feeling can appear. Precisely thereby the so diverse

[p. 268] [p. 269] further development of it is also conditioned; for while in individual cases, and especially where it had its genesis merely in the unconscious life, the feeling resembles a quickly extinguishing glow and does not reveal particular inner intensifications and developments, in other cases, and indeed the more it rests upon consciousness, it will be capable of a peculiar intensification and expansion, and can ultimately be cultivated to the point where it spreads the peculiar colour-shimmer that we call "joyfulness" over an entire life — a goal which, precisely because it can be reached only with a certain inner enlightenment, assurance, and purification of the soul, must be counted among the most beautiful tasks of what one may designate by the name of the art of living.

What we have now expressed about the varying duration of the feeling of joy gives us occasion also to mention the periodicity that we observe everywhere in this as in other feelings. Just as at the very highest level everything in this existence is tied to a certain rhythm, to a rising and falling and rising again, so too the world of feelings. As regards joy, its periodicity is decidedly the more powerful the more it is conditioned by the unconscious life. In this respect too one need only observe children to become convinced how often after a brief duration joy extinguishes — and without any particular external cause — indeed turns into its opposite, and often just as suddenly kindles again. A general law of this periodicity can never be demonstrated, because the conditions are too individual, but in the individual case it will indeed often be possible to find processes in the unconscious life that prove themselves as exciting factors of the feeling. Many a significant hint for the art of conducting life in a higher and finer manner can be drawn from these reflections; one can grasp why all attempts to extend joy into the immoderate lead so much to the opposite, why at the right time and in the right place the pleasant excitation of the unconscious sphere will heighten a joy grounded in conscious representations, but why all attempts to ground joy merely on excitation of the unconscious life must yield such imperfect and quickly vanishing results, and so forth.

[p. 270] Furthermore, the relationship of joy to other feelings and to the whole personality of the human being is to be considered. In relation to other feelings it is partly excluding, partly readily associating. Not only its opposite, sorrow, does it naturally exclude, but hatred too is just as incompatible with joy as, in contrast, a loving disposition is readily brought about by it. — A certain relation to the active side of life is also not to be overlooked. Productivity is easier, the daily tasks of life are more quickly dispatched, and even though the higher productions of the soul properly belong to the calm intermediate state of the feeling-life, joy is especially suited to mastering with ease the lesser things — I would say the material of life — at which the soul, owing to its mass, often threatens to flag. Remarkable, then, is the retroaction of joyful representations upon the unconscious and especially upon certain regions of it. Most decidedly affected by the conscious experience of happiness is the sphere of vascular life and of respiration, which stands in such close relation to it. More lively heartbeat, freer flowing of the blood in its finest pathways, and easier, more rapid breathing — just as, having arisen in the unconscious, they attune the conscious to joy — are also excited in return when the conscious grasps joyful repre-

[p. 271] sentations; indeed, one must call those excitations outright the unconscious joy of the organism itself, just as one says metaphorically of a plant: it greens and blooms joyfully. — A special mention, moreover, deserves that accelerated breathing movement that is quite particularly characteristic of joy — laughter. Laughter is nothing other than an accelerated, convulsive breathing, and it is acknowledged that it is inseparable from the feeling of lively joy, although it always remains remarkable that the more the joy is of a spiritual kind, the more it positively ceases to be a modification of actual breathing, and the more it restricts itself merely to an intimation of laughter appearing in the facial features —

the smile — indicating that a certain spiritualisation of joy no longer requires those more palpable processes. From its reflection in the unconscious there result, moreover, many healing effects of this feeling, but also, under certain circumstances, when it enters suddenly into weak and sick organisms, dangerous over-excitations and over-effusions. — Finally, with respect to the whole human being, the communicative nature of joy must also be mentioned. Joy, laughter, like sorrow, has something contagious, and that humanity is in itself again a whole, an ideal organism, may indeed be recognised in much that is great and communal, but also in these and similar communications — in the quite involuntary, one may say organic, passing on and transference of cognitions, feelings, and stirrings of will from soul to soul — that unity proves itself most distinctly.

As for the morbid deviations of the feeling of joy, they appear everywhere where it emerges with vehemence and at the same time devoid of ideas. In every case of this sort we feel such expressions immediately as unbeautiful, and instead of the pure unconscious joy of the child,

[p. 272] in whom the idea is not yet demanded, or the most lively but idea-pervaded joy of the mature person, working upon us itself joyfully and communicatively, that wild and coarse joy will appear to us thoroughly repulsive and disagreeable. Language also makes a fine nuance here, for while the pure feeling of joy can also be designated by the expression pleasure [Lust], and while in pleasure the element is expressed, as it were, in which the soul filled with cheerful representations and happy stirrings of the unconscious life floats, the derived word "merrymaking" [Lustigkeit] immediately gives a concept that indicates an excess tipping into the absurd or the morbid, and forms the perfect contrast to the beautiful concept of "joyfulness" [Freudigkeit] that we had to recognise above as a true elevation of joy.

Finally, with respect to the extinction, the cessation of joy, we must observe that it, like its genesis, can proceed from two different sides, that is, from the unconscious or the conscious. Even in the brightest soul, arrived at the fullest consciousness, special processes occur in this regard. Without our being conscious of any reason, there often overspreads the spirit a peculiar gloomy mood under which, as sunshine behind a dense fog at the firmament, all joyfulness vanishes from our soul, and we then recognise later either that some disturbing, obstructing influence in the unconscious life had already set in, which perhaps soon breaks out into actual illness, or the unconscious of our existence, because it is at the same time the universalising element, magnetically senses certain distant events unfortunate for us, and since ancient times such sudden involuntary cloudings have therefore often been regarded as full of presentiment. Now, as the unconscious here works over into the conscious, so again saddening, sorrow-arousing recognised representations work into the unconscious, and the extinction of joy announces itself in the slowing of pulse and breathing, the streams of blood withdraw from the finest vascular networks of the surface and the person turns pale, the eye becomes dull — in short, everything turns to the opposite. The joy is over.

[p. 273]

#### 2. Die Geschichte der Trauer — 2. The History of Sorrow

Between joy and sorrow there exists a most peculiar opposition; it becomes evident, namely, that the two by no means stand on entirely equal footing, but rather the latter sinks back toward a certain less worthy condition of the soul, and this goes so far that, just as in our language "traurig" [sad] is also used in the sense of stunted or contemptible, in Italian "tristo" is often taken as synonymous with malicious. If one traces the ground of this conceptual kinship, one arrives at the insight that in sorrow there lies concealed a certain opposition and dissatisfaction with respect to the necessary organic course of the world-order, and that therefore, in the same degree as it is esteemed great and significant when what we have called "joyfulness" takes possession of the entire soul and under no circumstances whatsoever — neither in adverse events and painful experiences, nor in bodily illnesses — departs from us, so it appears lesser and less worthy when, through such occasions, sorrow or sadness becomes a predominant mood in the soul.

Turning to the history of the origin of sorrow, we find here too that it emerges from two different regions, the conscious and the unconscious, and indeed that, like joy, it arises from both regions all the more easily

[p. 274] the weaker the energy and the stronger the sensibility of the individual, yet we recognise, on the contrary, that while higher joyfulness is attained all the more easily and remains all the more enduring the mightier the individuality, sorrow and enduring sadness under such conditions can all the less become the dominant mood. What we earlier called apparent misfortune plays, namely, a most essential role in the genesis of sorrow, insofar as it proceeds from conscious life, and although it cannot be denied that apparent fortune likewise has a very substantial part in the genesis of joy, it is precisely the true task of the growth of the soul to attain truth in general, and thus also true happiness, to thoroughly remove true misfortune from itself, but to recognise apparent misfortune as such — whereby the latter is then deprived of the power to arouse in the soul what we call sorrow. As was likewise expressed earlier, however, that in this temporal existence we can never and should never become entirely free of the semblances of fortune and misfortune, so too sorrow may never be entirely absent from us, and the greater maturity of the human being, and thus also the higher worthiness of sorrow, will be indicated by the fact that only worthy and significant representations, namely those that point to a true misfortune, arouse and strengthen the feeling of sorrow. When, therefore, the contemplation of a hindered, prematurely arrested growth in the being-in-itself of an idea, or the sinking of a soul toward true misfortune — unsoulfulness — or when the separation of the united life with a soul that forms a true complement of our existence arouses the feeling of sorrow, there also lies therein a certain power and worthiness, and in this temporal existence the human being can and should not withdraw from this sorrow. Whoever, on the other hand, is plunged into sorrow by the loss of insignificant fleeting externalities — this is indeed counted against them as a sign of meagre development of the soul.

[p. 275] It must, however, be especially emphasised that the actual feeling of sorrow by no means arises merely through the cognition of what excites sorrow in us, but rather that at the same time the penetration of the unconscious sphere is always required in order for the state to pass from cognition to feeling. If we ask, however, where in particular the share that the unconscious takes in sorrow is expressed, we find it above all in that governance of the unconscious soul which determines the life of the blood, and not without a deep sense of truth have peoples from time immemorial regarded the heart as essentially involved in both joy and sorrow. To be sure, so long as one regarded the bodily organism as something soulless in itself, as it were like a mechanism in which only the soul placed within it pulled certain strings to stimulate it into movements, and so long as the doctrine of the unconscious life of the soul was itself an unknown chapter in the history of the soul, one could not conceive the thought that in the formation and flow of the blood itself an unconscious soul-life was to be revered. But whoever has made himself sufficiently familiar with this, to whom these cognitions have dawned — to such a one the circulation of blood and the heartbeat become unconscious thinking, and the world of feelings appears to him for the most part only as a reverberation of the various moods of these organic circles in the self-conscious spirit. Thus it is wrong to say: sorrow effects a slower heartbeat, a blanching of the skin through withdrawal of the blood-flow from the finest networks of the surface, a less well-prepared blood, a slower sobbing respiration, and so forth — rather it should be said: sorrow is in part precisely all

[p. 276] this itself, and through the fact that these unconscious processes reverberate in the self-conscious spirit in a peculiar way, there arises, in conjunction with and through simultaneous representations of misfortune, what we call sorrow. Only in this way do we fully understand how, even when in our world of representations definite occasions for sorrow are lacking, a disease coming entirely from without, which places the organs of blood-transformation — in particular the organ of decomposition of all blood-corpuscles — the liver, in a suffering condition, already of itself not merely comprises a sad, melancholic mood, but in part actually is one; we understand how the deficient blood-formation of chlorosis must resound in consciousness as a gloomy, dejected mood, and again how a misfortune suddenly breaking into the life of representations can directly cause paralysis of the heart and stagnation of the blood-flow, and so forth. A particular phenomenon, however, which marks the higher degrees of sorrow upon the organism just as unavoidably as laughter marks joy, is weeping, and here again the physiological significance is most remarkable. The life of the blood, that unconscious hearth of the joyful and sorrowful world of feeling, has namely two poles: respiration, in which it steadily renews and forms itself, and secretion, in which it steadily perishes and decomposes. Just as laughter, as an accelerated respiratory movement promoting livelier blood-life, belongs to joy, so weeping, as a peculiar secretion, becomes the symbol of sorrow; for in a mysterious way the processes of the rest of the body are repeated in the head, and just as in the organ of smell a repetition of the thoracic respiration appears, so in the eye certain processes of the digestive region come to renewed expression, and thus disturbances of the latter show themselves in altered condition of the pupil and conjunctiva of the eye. Hence also the secret relation between the lacrimal gland and the liver, of which naturally the former, in its more spiritual region, more swiftly, though just as unconsciously, senses the retuning that the unconscious experiences in general, and so it happens that we know no bodily symbol of the feeling of sorrow which appears more eloquent in its silent speech than the tear.

[p. 277] As for periodicity, such conditions are unmistakable in sorrow as well, and precisely because a rhythmic movement continually runs through everything unconscious, gloomier moods alternate with brighter ones in our largely unconscious soul, even with the most vigorous development of the self-conscious spirit, but far more so, indeed, the less vigorously consciousness is developed. The influences which periodically arouse sorrow from the unconscious can naturally be most manifold. The changes of the atmosphere already have a significant effect in this direction. Overcast, foggy days and cold dampness decidedly produce such moods; certain climates are more conducive to them, others less so; indeed it is remarkable how many inner processes of development in the human being exert their influence in this peculiar way. The melancholic tendency that sometimes seizes a person in the midst of blooming youth, the despondency that attaches itself to certain evolutions in female life — all of these point to such a connection. One cannot better express this involuntary, often premonitory onset of sorrow, this sudden overcast of the soul with clouds, than Shakespeare does — to whom the world of feelings was so deeply disclosed — when he has Antonio say: "In sooth, I know not why I am so sad: It wearies me; you say it wearies you; But how I caught it, found it, or came by it, What stuff 'tis made of, whereof it is born, I am to learn."

[p. 278] From the foregoing it becomes quite clear, moreover, what the relation of this feeling to the whole human being must be. Brooding over sorrowful sensations paralyses activity in general and destroys the fullness of formative life; the transitions of such states into dangerous illnesses are therefore something the physician encounters often enough. Among other feelings, hatred is on the whole more closely related to sorrow than love, and easily there combines, especially in smaller souls, with the gloom of the disposition also the bitter, misanthropic mood, while in greater souls sorrow turns instead toward the feeling of the sublime, and then produces what the Greeks designated by the name of pathos, and regarded as the highest task of tragedy. This latter peculiarity of sorrow is at the same time that through which the remarkable relation of pain and sorrow to poetic productivity becomes clear to the psychologist. One may indeed regard it as a well-known experience that in persons who otherwise display little imagination and no inclination whatsoever toward poetic or artistic productions, often all at once and with a certain violence an endeavour emerges to express and make known the innermost of their essence in some poetic work, be it in tone or word or image, when a great pain has come upon them and a true and deep sorrow holds them in its sway. It is true of other feelings as well that they awaken such productivity of the conscious spirit, but with this intensity only of love, and even of love partly for the reason that love, by its very nature, is pervaded and filled with a never fully satisfiable and therefore always somewhat painful longing. Among many peoples one can, precisely from that cause, demonstrate conclusively that the first beginnings of poetry, the first

[p. 279] myths, the first songs and chants possess a peculiarly melancholic, dark, gloomy character, and if one wished even in modern times to separate the works of art freely arising from inner poetic necessity into those that spring from overflowing pleasure and joyfulness and those that arise from inner pain and as rescue from sombre moods, the great majority would decidedly fall on the side of the latter. The explanation of this phenomenon lies undoubtedly in the words expressed in the foregoing: namely, the soul caught in affliction, in its inner drive toward blessedness, seeks a rescue from the condition unsuited to its inner essence, and it summons for this purpose the productive power concealed within it — the imagination — partly in order to create for itself from representations a world over whose blissful essence the pain of its present existence can be forgotten, and partly also merely in order to make its own sombre and unhappy condition fully objective to itself. In this becoming-objective, however, there lies so great a pacification because it works as a mirroring, and thereby grants that which we further above already had to call so significant for the growth of the soul — cognition. All cognising, namely, all genuine knowing, is something so sublime that, precisely because in it the ultimate and highest of the development of the soul is attained, it must also work powerfully and peculiarly in a liberating and gladdening way upon the soul, and thus already in the fact that the spirit, through a peculiar productivity of the imagination, is able to create something by which it can become clear to itself, there lies a particular happiness and a remarkable rescue from the darkness of the condition of sorrow.

Already in this way, then, this making-objective-to-oneself grants a certain rescue from the condition of pain, but it accomplishes still more, and indeed for the reason that in the light of cognition it must also become ever clearer what merely from semblances of misfortune had aroused sorrow in the soul, and what therefore does not truly deserve genuine sorrow.

[p. 280] There remain for us still to consider the pathological aberrations and the extinction of sorrow. The former will also here, as with joy, only result when, where consciousness is required, feeling reigns measurelessly and devoid of ideas. The less, therefore, sorrow is merely an expression of the unconscious mood of the organism, and the more it is, insofar as it is grounded in representations, excited by truly insubstantial semblances of misfortune, the more it is to be regarded as pathological and unworthy of a higher development. That sorrow, when significant derangements of the organism prevail and especially when the life of the blood is oppressed to a high degree, when deformations of the liver develop and in turn weigh upon the nervous life, can emerge in such excess that it appears as mental illness — in which then, darkly turned inward, amid weeping and deep melancholy, sometimes an entire existence miserably drags along — is confirmed by medical experience only too often; indeed, from such moods there often springs that pathological suicide of which mention was already made earlier.

As for the extinction of sorrow, this too is given half from the unconscious, half from the conscious. It is remarkable to observe how certain retunements in the unconscious, when consciousness does not too decidedly resist, are immediately able to annihilate sorrow! "And the vines are breakers of care!" it is said of a noble wine not without good reason, for wonderfully do careworn thoughts and sombre moods often disperse even without a change of external circumstances, when one succeeds in giving the life of the blood a fresher impetus. How exercise, especially exercise in fresh, free, sunny air, decidedly works to disperse grief is a well-known experience; indeed, that the now more cheerful, now more sombre character of entire nations is co-determined by similar influences of climate admits of no doubt, and thus a change of place of residence can indeed contribute substantially to displacing a lasting sombre mood. If one wishes to make clear to oneself in detail the processes under which sorrow vanishes when cheerful conscious representations and happy events bring joy back again, one must always bear in mind how all the life of representations is inescapably bound in a peculiarly mysterious way to certain immeasurable changes in the innervation-tension of the brain; one must make clear to oneself how millions of microscopic primitive fibres spread from the brain, as just so many conductors of innervation, throughout the entire body, how therefore the altered tension of the brain-innervation must at every moment also manifest itself peripherally, by means of these conductors, everywhere where the quality of the central tension had particularly directed it, and how therefore representations that engage the cheerful side of feeling, precisely because the cheerfulness of the unconscious can express itself in nothing other than a freer, fresher life of the blood, livelier formation, and vigorous respiration, must immediately call forth these manifestations, since the central change of innervation also determines the peripheral one.

[p. 281] To anyone, then, to whom this has once become clear, it will also not be difficult to comprehend how altered representational life abolishes the bodily phenomena of sorrow, and how conversely a joyful retuning of the unconscious can help to disperse the representations of sorrow in consciousness.

[p. 282]

#### 3. Die Geschichte der Liebe — 3. The History of Love

When one can in a certain respect designate joy and sorrow as passive and subjective feelings, inasmuch as they rest more within themselves and appear without definite desires or rejections directed outward, love and hatred emerge, by contrast, as active and at the same time more objective feelings, since their innermost essence consists in decidedly going out of oneself, decidedly attracting the beloved and being attracted by it, and just as decidedly repelling the hated and letting oneself be repelled by it. Both active feelings thereby always acquire a certain violence, and can in individual cases intensify to a vehemence under which the whole existence of the human being collapses; indeed, on account of the pain connected with it and the suffering bound to it, we then no longer call love and hatred feelings, but passions. Joy and sorrow cannot become passion, and even of the two active feelings, the positive is so much more powerful, but also so much more suited to the transition into passion, than the negative.

Let us now undertake to penetrate more deeply into the history of the mightiest of all feelings — that of love. Above all things it must be vividly grasped that, like every feeling, this one too, and especially this one, rests only half upon consciousness and for the other half upon the unconscious. When we examine something in consciousness according to its qualities, and, because we recognise these as excellent, take pleasure in it, and perhaps also, precisely on account of this excellence, wish to remain near this object and to have it permanently about us, the unconscious in us is thereby entirely uninvolved; but precisely for that reason there is then absolutely no question of love. Love therefore necessarily presupposes a deeper seizure simultaneously of the unconscious of our soul, and herein lies the mystery that, in the case of love, has been felt a thousandfold, grasped and portrayed by true poets in its full power, yet almost never taken into consideration by researchers when the cognition of the essence of love was at issue.

[p. 283] Above all, however, it is necessary that we observe how varied the object of love can be: the universality and power of this feeling expresses itself in this too — a universality and power of which the other objective feeling, hatred, is never capable. Through unconscious attraction and conscious cognition, love can in truth embrace the most humble and again the most exalted; from attachment to the soil and the dwelling-place, to stone and metal, from the love of plants and animals, it turns, as to the actual centre of its existence, to the love of the human being — the love of oneself, of friends, parents, siblings, children, and above all to the love of the other sex — and finally elevates itself to the love of God. According to these different objects it itself assumes infinitely varied nuances, and displays a richness and a manifoldness of states that it becomes entirely impossible to describe and explain exhaustively. Let us therefore first exclusively and closely fix our spiritual eye upon what we may call the true centre of this feeling — I should like to say the primal feeling altogether — and what language too often exclusively designates by the name of love: that is, the love of the sexes toward one another, and we shall, precisely because this is so, be able to grasp the essence of this feeling most vividly therein, so that it will then become easier to spread adequate cognition over the lesser branches as over the highest blossom of the same with a few intimations. No one, however, will be able to follow even that deepest and most intimate feeling adequately in contemplation without starting from the consideration of that mightiest opposition which a mysterious unconscious governance of the idea of humanity has Prometheanly shaped forth in the duality of the sexes, and whose great significance for the continued development of the species has been developed further above. For to the fact that in every individual realisation humanity must diverge toward two organically opposed poles, as male and female, in order precisely from this separation and in the reunification thereof to perpetually generate itself anew, is bound the essential mystery upon which and out of which the blossom of love of which we now speak can alone emerge. This remarkable opposition one must above all make as clear to oneself as possible, so that it becomes intuitive how, as a consequence of a deep unconscious governance, two forms of existence of humanity are perpetually shaped forth anew, which precisely through their difference within a certain likeness (and herein lies the fundamental essence of all sympathy) attract each other with the greatest power, seek each other with the liveliest longing, and at the highest point, in their finding one another and passing into one another, reach the satisfaction of their existence. Already the history of the other organisms, living themselves out entirely unconsciously or only with dim world-consciousness, is most significant for these processes. The highest formation of the plant world, the blossom, divides into polar organs which we already here call male and female; finally these oppositions unite in the dispersal of the anthers, and the death of the blossom immediately ensues. Likewise for the entire animal kingdom the union of the sexes is the summit of existence, and millions of creatures live only until this peak of their existence, and in their mutual dissolution into one another they attain what, remarkably enough, in an ancient religion of humanity that has gradually become overgrown with the greatest oddities — Buddhism — is also presented to the human being as the highest blessedness:<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> [p. 284] This longing, this powerful effect of the desired object upon the one who longs, has found its most beautiful depiction in the Phaedrus of Plato. How profoundly the unconscious governance of the soul is grasped therein is also evident from the fact that the effect of beholding the beloved upon the development and arousal of the soul is variously compared to the sprouting of plumage — that is, precisely to an unconscious formative process. Strangely, however, at that time the divinely ordained love of the sexes had not yet been grasped in its higher significance, and, perhaps on account of a too slight spiritual development of the female sex in general, the more vehement love still revealed itself solely in the relation of male to male nature. [p. 285] annihilation. the

Also in humanity something similar now emerges; for when we said that humanity perpetually regenerates itself anew in the union of opposed individuals, it follows directly therefrom that these individuals necessarily find in precisely this union also the first ground of their own decline, since every regeneration presupposes annihilation, just as the continued existence of our own organism is bound only to the ceaselessly recurring new formation, which in turn first becomes possible through ceaseless self-destruction. Now it must further be considered that humanity distinguishes itself from all other circles of living beings otherwise known to us through the infinitely varied expression of individuality, that therefore the original opposition of humanity in the sexes must express itself in immeasurably varied forms, and that consequently (as could be quite rigorously demonstrated) truly every individual of one sex can actually find only a single individual of the other sex that is perfectly opposed to it in likeness. (Hence the old half-humorous myth of Plato concerning the primordial human beings torn asunder, whose halves now everywhere

[p. 286] longingly sought each other.) In these circumstances it follows, then, that long before anything of an actual feeling of love comes to consciousness, relations of the individual to others exist, that a drive, a longing, a seeking is present which necessarily roots deep in the unconscious region of the soul, which at first in all its indeterminacy lets itself be only dimly presentiment, and which yet points toward reaching the individual, appropriating it, dissolving into it — the one which not only in its general validity formed its organic opposite, but which alone could quite specifically grant it the fulfilment of its existence. Already here an important moment for the definite history of love emerges, for it soon becomes apparent that the more individual and refined the organisation of an individuality is, the less it can find the satisfaction of its deeply inborn desire and longing merely in the species, merely in the apprehension and appropriation of the sexually opposite in general, but the more it must seek and strive with all its power toward precisely the individuality opposed to it alone. Indeed, in the striving after this satisfaction there will often recur what was already said above about the striving after blessedness, namely that in this seeking too the most manifold errors occur, that several semblances of the actual sole appropriate opposite meet the seeker, which are temporarily taken for the real goal, and which always pale again when it is granted and destined that the object truly filling the whole existence is finally actually reached. This seeking, this striving, this longing presents, moreover, considerable differences in the two sexes. One might perhaps say that the man, whose stronger intelligence and will-power somewhat veils and conceals the feeling, experiments more in his seeking and is thereby, and because he trusts the senses and cognition more, subject to more frequent errors, while the woman (as already mentioned above), in her predominating unconscious, wanders through life with the divining rod of feeling — more like a rhabdomancer — and therefore perhaps more often than the man experiences that her innermost self is only there, and often before her clear cognition, completely seized and kindled with love, where the actual archetype confronts her. [p. 287] It

From all this it follows, then, that in order to comprehend the essence of love we must always begin with unconscious necessity, and precisely therein, in this unconscious, lies the great power, the entire immediacy, the high reason, the actual right, and on the other hand the weakness, the lack of understanding and law, and altogether the constant contradiction, as it will always exist between the unconscious and the conscious. From this one also comprehends the peculiar observation which has hitherto been more expressed by poets than grasped by psychologists, namely that with this feeling there occurs what occurs with no other to the same degree — a certain astonishment, indeed a startling of the conscious spirit at the emergence of this feeling from the unconscious. Dante in his Vita Nuova here as always unites the poet and the philosopher when, in depicting the first flaming up of love, he says: "the spirit of life, which dwells in the most secret chamber of the heart, began to tremble so violently that it manifested itself terrifyingly in the smallest pulses, and trembling it spoke the words: 'Behold, a god stronger than I, who comes to rule over me' [Ecce deus fortior me veniens dominabitur mihi]." Indeed, every very violent flooding of the conscious spirit by a special stirring of the unconscious life, or of the unconscious soul, must give a certain alarm and astonishment, because to it — that is, to the soul that has attained greater independence — something is communicated, indeed obtruded, which is actually foreign to its innermost essence; but in no case can this work so powerfully as in love, which not only overwhelms the conscious spirit generally with the unconscious, but also sweeps the entire individuality toward that of a foreign soul, and thus doubly threatens the existence of the conscious, independent spirit. Nevertheless, for this alarming and threatening element its complete pacification is also prepared, when at last in the conscious spirit the cognition dawns that the actual fulfilment of the entire life of the soul, and thus a peculiar blessedness, can really be given only in the finding and grasping, indeed the penetration of another soul — a soul in which the true complement of one's own existence has come to appearance. To be sure, that this satisfaction actually come about presupposes that it be no mere semblance of what is truly opposed in likeness which has excited the feeling, and that in the individual itself the full vitality of existence still be present which conditions such a longing for the finding of this opposite. How manifold, therefore, are the movements of the human soul which occur when a conflict of the conscious against the unconscious is occasioned by the conscious spirit's having to recognise that it was more a semblance than the archetype of the truly appropriate object by which, as by a deception, the feeling of love had been excited, and that the deception had arisen perhaps through the general opposition of sex being taken for the truly individual one — of this the inner history of all those people who have lived through many phases of developmental states of their entire being can give manifold testimony. In general, attention must here at once be drawn to the fact that through the powerful excitations which the feeling of love calls forth in the whole being of the human person, and through the manifold conflict that thereby occurs, as well as through the wavering of the soul between the happiness of satisfaction and the unhappiness of non-satisfaction, the most remarkable contribution must be and actually is made to the development of the soul, and that for this reason alone the study of these states will always remain one of the most important tasks for the history of the soul.

[p. 288] [p. 289] As for the other condition under which the blessedness of satisfaction is reached, we had to locate it in the still persisting full vitality of existence; for if in general the whole world of feelings begins to fade with diminished vitality of the organism, then especially the feeling of love uniting the opposition of the sexes, as such, is absolutely unthinkable without this vitality, and it is very remarkable to observe how upon the seemingly low something very high is here necessarily co-founded. Just as little as the sexually stunted man, therefore, is the sexless, hence only apparent, woman capable of this feeling, and it has always given me occasion for peculiar reflections when I have read the original letters of Abelard and Heloise, to observe how in him, the violently destroyed man — in him who earlier, through his songs passing from mouth to mouth and the whole peculiar love-inspiration in his history, had decidedly exercised precisely this feeling of love with great vehemence — only a certain empty formalism still expresses itself in the later written letters; while in hers, written beneath the nun's veil, the true sensation of love, in the full understanding of the necessity

[p. 290] of her complete dissolution into the beloved being, manifests itself. Precisely for this reason, too, this feeling is more than any other bound to a definite period of life, and just as little as the child can be capable of it, so too a far-advanced higher age; indeed precisely because in general the woman dies sexually earlier than the man, in the latter the feeling of love will often still emerge in full power in the latest period of life, while in the aged woman its continuation belongs to the very rarest exceptions.

Moreover, in the history of love two further moments remain very characteristic and remarkable: the one is the peculiar exclusive nature of this feeling, the other the cycle of its activation, how, proceeding from the unconscious, it finds its completion only in the return to the unconscious. In the first respect we find not only that love is exclusive in its object, and indeed so much so that it would itself reject the equal love of others toward the same object, that it can direct itself in true vitality only toward one object and demands no less the same unity from it, but it is also exclusive within one's own soul, and pushes away and out all other feelings, endeavours, and cognitions; it wills only itself, only the one thing that shall fill every single stirring of the soul, and only in this sole dominion will it prove itself in its full power and only thus reveal that peculiar blessedness which, as a wondrous phenomenon, can transfigure a certain period of life by granting to the individual existence its complete satisfaction through another being. This exclusive character of love decidedly distinguishes it from all other feelings and gives it, moreover, something through which a transition to monomania is always facilitated in the highest degree. As for, secondly, the return of the conscious to the unconscious, it is expressed in the inner necessity given through this feeling to strive for the union of the sexes, and despite everything that is offered in the conscious soul in cognition of the love-relation as happiness, yet absolutely, as the highest satisfaction of an inborn desire, to demand the mystery of organic union, whose highest happiness is experienced precisely as a surrender, indeed a dissolution of consciousness in unconsciousness. This cycle becomes all the more significant when we consider that really only to it is the continued development of humanity bound, and only upon it should it always depend that from a peculiar formation of the female organism ever new, once more independent, individual human organisms develop. Were it indeed conceivable that all continued development of humanity were bound only to this cycle — that is, through a feeling that has come to truly high and beautiful consciousness, completing itself in the unconsciousness of the love-mystery and ever again surfacing in consciousness — then indeed a different blossom of humanity would populate the earth than is now the case, now when by far the greatest part of humanity in its continued development is bound to conditions which are only too far removed from the higher significance of the feeling of love! To the observer of humanity, in any case, much in the now happier, now unhappier individuality of the members of different generations is explained from these conditions.

[p. 291] The periodic element in the feeling of love also asserts itself to a high degree, now less, now more, according to the various, now deeper, now shallower natures. The more shallowness prevails, the more the awakening of the feeling of love depends upon so-called accidental stirrings of the unconscious, and is thereby

[p. 292] also entirely subject to its periodicity; the more the essence of the human being is a more profound one and the more completely the worthiness of the feeling is also recognised in consciousness, the more it will enduringly fill the soul. With all of this, a rising and falling, a certain rhythmic growing stronger and weaker and rising again, is inseparable from this feeling, and deeply grounded precisely in the rhythm of all life. Here too, moreover, the law of the spiral fully proves itself — the law to which I already drew attention in my Physiology as essential for all life, and which I there illustrated by means of a diagram. Several revolutions infallibly occur in this respect too in the life of every human being, but only one will be the highest, and yet even this, given the whole peculiarity of our organisation, can endure in full splendour and vitality until death only if this existence is altogether destroyed earlier; by contrast, with declining general life-energy it cannot maintain its full force into a more advanced age.

Very much that is remarkable is further offered by the feeling of love in its relation to other feelings and to the human being in general. As for the former, it immediately becomes clear that from it, precisely insofar as we have called love in general the most powerful feeling and the love of the sexes in particular the primal feeling, the most violent arousal of all other feelings must be expected. In the disposition seized by intense love, neither what we have called the zenith nor what we have called the nadir of the world of feeling — neither the free, vital equilibrium of the soul nor its apathy — will occur; rather, between joy and sorrow, between love extending in other directions as well and hatred, the magnetic needle

[p. 293] of the life of the soul will swing in constant oscillations around the entire compass-rose of the world of feeling, now filled with torment, now with the highest joyfulness — for which reason the ancients already devised the most manifold images and similes for this condition, and especially repeated the butterfly tormented by Amor with his torch as a frequent symbol of such states. From this very reason springs furthermore the powerful significance of love for art and poetry, for the life of feeling is altogether what conditions all production of this kind, inasmuch as the soul can for the most part only attain the weight of these stirrings by being compelled through an inner necessity to make them objective — as it were, to bring them to intuition under symbols — and so to transpose them into the domain of full consciousness, into the domain of cognition, whereupon immediately, as was already mentioned in the history of sorrow, the storminess of their movements is broken and a greater equilibrium of the world of the disposition gradually restores itself.

As for the relations of the feeling of love to the whole human being, they are of great power in unconscious as in conscious life. In conscious life, representations ceaselessly press forward which make the object of love again and again the centre, and which show themselves animated in the most peculiar way. They stimulate related representations; the enduring ones develop themselves to the greatest objectivity, and thereby too the realisation of these representations through action is facilitated and the productivity of the spirit is essentially promoted. Likewise, more or less all endeavours of the will find themselves not only quite essentially stimulated in this direction of representational life, but often also so heightened in their energy that activities come to light such as otherwise never used to occur in precisely this individuality, and that active power appears with a force and endurance such as it attains on no other occasion.

[p. 294] Powerful, finally, are the transformations which are stimulated in the unconscious life — that is, there where the root of all feeling of love lies — through that same feeling. First, the world of half-unconscious representations which we call presentiments, dreams, and effects at a distance is nowhere so animated as in the soul moved by love. The generalisation in the unconscious, of which I spoke above — by virtue of which everything immersed in the power of unconsciousness is held and bound more in the one great circle of universal natural life, while everything that has come to consciousness maintains itself more independently and in isolation — is for precisely these presentiments, sensations at a distance and from a distance, for the manifold attractions, repulsions, and nervous overflowings as they occur in the feeling-world of the lover, the sole and sufficient explanation. From precisely this vantage point alone one understands that, as with the totality of the world, so and still far more with the beloved object, the lover exists in unconscious united life, so that precisely for this reason even the most wondrous overflowings at a distance can never here be unexpected. But also the quite unconsciously occurring retunements in the bodily life of the lovers are strong and significant; for not only do the ceaselessly alternating stirrings of joy and sorrow live themselves out in all the various changes of flow of the blood-system and all the fluids that characterise these feelings, but there result, partly in the manifold currents of innervation and partly in the organs which quite properly represent the sexual life, the most important retunements through this feeling. Magnetically, even in the finest and purest organisation, the proximity, indeed often the mere thought and still far more even the lightest touch between lovers, works in these directions, and however modestly the conscious spirit may veil itself before the mystery of the most vital excitation of the sexual spheres, it must nevertheless quietly acknowledge that only then, when it itself momentarily sinks entirely and loses itself again in such unconscious excitations (as language itself calls such states "a being-beside-oneself") is the cycle of this feeling from the unconscious through the conscious back to the unconscious fully closed, and the actual satisfaction and completion of the feeling of love reached. The most peculiar and most profound relations here meet the student of the soul who enters with reverence: it becomes clear to him how much richer and mightier this happiness of being-beside-oneself, pouring forth from the excitation of the unconscious, must become the higher consciousness had previously developed, and he further senses that precisely herein a remarkable necessity is given to surrender all selfhood as much as possible before the beloved and thus to humble oneself to the profoundest degree. Both demand yet a somewhat closer consideration. In the first lies already the reason, partly why, as in general so also on this point in particular, the nobler human stock and the more finely developed person stands higher than the more savage, ignoble stock and the coarser individuality, but partly also why in general the completion of the need for love cannot be bound to the earliest developmental period of the human being, and why it only later, indeed sometimes very late, and with more advanced spiritual maturity, appears to the human being in its full abundance. In the raw and savage condition of humanity, therefore, there is only a wild stimulus and intoxication, what for the more highly developed, finer soul becomes a tender and wondrous beautification and transfiguration of human existence, and in the incompletely developed human being an excitation must become the most ruinous perversion which in the matured state of life appears as a true, health-promoting happiness and satisfaction.

[p. 295] [p. 296] As for the second point, first this direction of the feeling of love itself must be made quite clear, and it seems to me that this can scarcely be achieved more completely than by communicating a passage from the already mentioned remarkable letters of Heloise to Abelard. It reads thus: "Nothing have I ever, God knows, sought in you but yourself alone — desiring purely you and not what is yours. Not the bond of marriage, not other marital endowments have I expected; not my own will and pleasure, but yours to fulfil have I striven, as you yourself know. And if the name of wife appears holier and more worthy, sweeter to me was it always to be called your beloved, or, if you would not be angered, your concubine or courtesan; so that the deeper I abased myself for you, the greater grace and favour I might find with you, and the less I might offend the glory of your eminence."
