Truly in this passage the entire unconditional devotion, sacrifice, and consequently also self-humiliation is perfectly expressed just as it follows from the indicated essence of the feeling here considered; indeed this self-humiliation of this love is all the more significant, since it, which here overcomes the shame of the conscious spirit in its complete surrender partly to the unconscious within itself and partly to the other soul, at the same time in this self-renunciation provides a peculiar preparation for still higher forms of love. For to every higher human nature, in the sinking and complete dissolution into another soul, there will most clearly disclose itself the under-<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> Abelard and Heloise, their Letters and the History of their Sufferings. By M. Carriere. Giessen 1844. P. 67.

[p. 297] standing of the universal and of all that is divine. One may say that such a love is the first redemption from individual being and the first step toward the return into the All, and from this renunciation of all selfhood there most easily proceeds the unconditional devotion to a divine hovering above all consciousness — in a word, to the love of God. It is therefore not unimportant to observe how unmistakably, precisely for this reason, souls to whom it has been granted to live through the feeling of love between the sexes in all its phases are often thereby at the same time especially matured, in later periods of life, partly — when the flame of the sexual feeling of love has burnt out — to become capable of perfect spiritual love, and partly to feel and experience with complete inwardness the love of God.

This consideration is, however, at the same time what can prepare us for the cognition of the most important relation and significance of love in general for the whole human being. For if we express it in general terms, all higher development that the soul of the human being can attain at all rests primordially upon love. Not without reason does the Apostle say: "Though I speak with the tongues of men and of angels, and have not love, I am become as sounding brass or a tinkling cymbal"; for in its deepest essence love is actually nothing other than a powerful drive, penetrating and moving both the conscious and the unconscious, toward the completion of our existence, toward the highest and most blessed perfection of our very own being. Precisely, then, because it must first be acknowledged that the sexes in their individual life and being-for-themselves are something incomplete, the love of the sexes emerges with this power and necessity surpassing all other earthly forms of love, and in the natural course of development becomes the condition for every higher cultivation. And yet it is not this form of love alone that is formative and developmental; rather, equally so is the love of all world-phenomena, of the soil on which we grew and which nourished us, of the star that illuminated us, of the air we breathe, of the plant and the tree, of animals, and especially of the multiform appearances of the human being: only through love of all this does there arise, partly, the desire to investigate the essence of the phenomenon, and thus cognition is nourished; partly, the desire to represent anew in mirror-images of this phenomenon its inner essence itself, and in this way poetry and art are developed through love. Where there is no love, where the world is not inwardly embraced in some direction with the impulse of love, there the soul will also remain barren and will not attain to art and knowledge, not to higher development; it can only be as "sounding brass and a tinkling cymbal." Where, on the other hand, the soul has unfolded in the loving cognition of the world and in the aspiration pervaded by love toward art, and where it has matured in the love of the sexes, there the impulse toward the highest divine primal ground of all life and all appearance will manifest itself more and more distinctly; there it will become clear to the soul that in everything it previously believed it loved and indeed truly loved as something particular, it had actually unconsciously loved essentially the highest, what lay at the ground of all this, and with this clearer cognition it will now also become conscious within itself of the highest love — the love of God. Herein, however, one must especially recall that, just as we already had to observe in the previously considered developmental history of the soul, the higher stage does not exclude or annihilate the preceding lower one, but rather includes it, encompasses it, takes it up into itself, and thereby also immediately perfects and ennobles it in its essence — so too this highest form of love, the love of God, can by no means have the significance of excluding or annihilating the earlier forms of love, but rather it will include them, embrace them, and itself complete and elevate them in their essence. To deeper contemplation it thus becomes clear that the genuine development of the human being is bound to a cultivation through a multiplicity of forms of love, and that, just as the love of the world in general, so in particular the love of the sexes, are great and significant links in this chain which is destined to bind the deepest to the highest, and which alone is fully able to lead what is initially least soulful upward to the perfectly soulful — to blessedness.

[p. 298] [p. 299] If now from this standpoint alone the question concerning the significance of love, and especially also of the love of the sexes, can be truly decided, then at the same time from here too what remains to be said about the pathological aberrations of love will receive its sole and complete resolution and decision. For if in the foregoing we had truly convinced ourselves that, just as the love of the sexes may rightly be called the heart and centre of all feeling of love, so love in general is also the heart of the feelings, or the primal feeling itself, then thereby at the same time the history of the sick, the burdensome, and the abnormal — as it were the night-side of the feeling of love — must inevitably acquire an important significance. Language itself points with definiteness to this importance, in that for all love appearing in feverish intensity, and therefore commonly regarded without distinction as pathological, it has created a special word: the word passion [Leidenschaft] (passio). A certain confusion in the use of this word is, however, not to be denied, for sometimes something higher was truly understood by it — a fervent glow of the soul for knowledge, for art, or for a beloved soul that perfectly completes an in itself only half and single existence, indeed even for the divine itself — and sometimes again a certain letting-oneself-fall of the soul, a complete self-abandonment, something unworthy and base was meant to be designated by it. This contradiction was too great not to be noticed, and only inadequately did people help themselves by customarily calling the former states and aspirations the higher, the latter the lower passions. To be sure, thereby the question of what was actually pathological in both forms of the feeling of love was never adequately decided, and one would in any case do better to designate the so-called lower passions straightforwardly by another and special word, fortunately already offered by the language, and thereby to separate them, as the actually sick, entirely from what is merely painful, merely suffering. For if we examine the distinction between these states more closely, we soon recognise: what in the higher sense is called passion is actually only the flame of love in and for itself, which (according to its already earlier mentioned exclusive nature), even in its purest and most worthy direction, can never free itself from a certain deep, indelible pain of the soul — a pain which must be inseparable from all this love precisely because no kind of this striving can ever be granted a true and perfect satisfaction, and because everywhere some thorn of unsatisfied, tormenting longing remains. Let one therefore consider this passion in the higher sense, or passion simply, or also what we call genuine passion, as it appears in the exclusive glow of a soul for science and art, without which a great, extraordinary achievement in either is unthinkable, or in the intense glow of a perfect feeling of love between two mutually indispensable and only reciprocally completing human natures, or even in the fiery ardour of the love of God that conditions the inspiration of the saint — in none of these flames of love will it fail that at the same time the impossibility of full and unconditional satisfaction, the insufficiency of our whole existence, and the incompleteness of human circumstances, add a deep, painful trait, a trait of earnest sorrow, to all the joyfulness and blessedness which can indeed dawn and truly dawns upon the soul in such higher love-striving. From this cause there therefore indeed arises a suffering, that pathos which has earned for all intense and precisely the noblest love-striving the name of passion.

[p. 300] [p. 301] In this suffering now, in this passion, there is nothing truly sick; just as little as a mere deep pain that we feel, a pressure that weighs upon us, a sting that wounds us, can be called a sickness. This impulse of love-glow, then, for art or religion, or for the soul truly to be loved — for no semblance — this impulse which directs all the powers of the soul toward one point and which yet we can never fully satisfy, never completely appease: it can indeed not be without pain or suffering, it can even have something that destroys earthly existence (just as not merely a disease but also an injury, a painful wound, can kill a person), but it is compatible with everything noble and great, it is healthy and worthy in itself, and in this sense passion becomes akin to, or is itself, that pathos (pathema animi) which already among the ancients constituted the actual element of their tragedy.

[p. 302] As for the so-called lower passions, on the other hand — the intense impulse of love that can seize the soul for something wholly unworthy of its actual divine essence, for earthly possessions, for external splendour and fame, and for merely bodily pleasures — here there combines with the individual happiness, or rather the mere semblance of happiness, which strivings of this kind can alone ever attain, inevitably a certain dark consciousness of inner degradation, an inner torment of the soul of actual sickness, a state of suffering which differs very much from the pathos of genuine passion, and which usually contributes just as much to the sinking and regression of the soul as that pathos can contribute to the rising and growth of the soul. In this sense, then, we do not hesitate to set forth the so-called passion of avarice, of gluttony, of loveless sexual desire, of gambling, of the chase after external distinction, as a truly pathological aberration of love bringing danger to the life of the soul, just as a severe, life-endangering bodily disease brings danger to bodily life; and if we now seek to find for such aberrations a special name distinguished from that of passions, we can take up no better one than that of addictions [Suchten], a name by which bodily destructive, life-consuming diseases — dropsy, consumption, and so forth — are customarily designated. Greed [Geldsucht], ambition [Ehrsucht], gambling addiction [Spielsucht], alcoholism [Trunksucht], and so forth, are accordingly in any case better names than passion for money, honour, gambling, and so forth.

These addictions have in common with the true passions that they too are entirely exclusive in nature; indeed they are so in a certain respect to a far higher degree, and for the reason that with the sinking of the soul in its captivity to lower ends, the conscious, cognition, must always essentially sink as well, and error, indeed even to the point of complete mental illness, can increasingly intrude, while a higher passion in many respects matures and advances the soul and often becomes the most important lever for promoting the spiritual development of some individuality. The more inferior, the more unworthy the goal to which the one caught in one of these addictions aspires, the more degrading and destructive will its effect upon the life of the soul be. Thus alcoholism, gambling addiction, are far more destructive than, say, ambition, in which active power at least always tends to be stimulated in a peculiar way, while in the former direction feeling, will, and cognition gradually and irrecoverably perish, and what earlier might perhaps still be called merely an inclination toward enjoyment must later founder in a veritable spell.

[p. 303] Now insofar as this relationship has become clear, there is still needed, with regard to what we have in the higher sense called passion, a special discussion. For if we consider these states more closely, we must soon perceive that here too the goal alone cannot entirely determine the health of love. The most striking example of this kind will at once be furnished by the highest form of love — the love of God. In the sanctified disposition, which through and through glows only in love for the highest divine mystery, and which therefore fills the torments of existence and the insufficiency of all its human endeavours alongside the blessedness of that sensation with sustained pain, the entire beauty of that pathos will indeed emerge with true perfection; but to what caricature, to what aberration, to what utter mental illness can this love not also lead, and has it not often actually led! If we trace more deeply the cause of such a pathological love of God, we find that this love, which in the true and healthy sense always presupposes a certain higher maturity of the soul, so that it may be possible to think the highest mystery in a truly worthy manner, falls into aberration and sickness especially when it ignites with vehemence in a soul that has not itself previously attained a higher maturity and grasped some worthy cognition of the divine. A vehement love of the divine in such an immature state may then quite well be compared to the emergence of the striving for sexual love without the actual maturity of sex having been attained. Just as the latter under such circumstances can lead to nothing but perversion and sickness, so too will the former under such unsuitable conditions bring the spirit only to wild fanaticism, theosophistic nonsense, and complete madness; indeed it is still especially to be mentioned concerning it that, while the pure and genuine love of God spreads an infinite mildness and tolerance over the one who so loves, this pathological love on the contrary must produce a veritable hatred and discord against everything not directly relating to God, and must engender intolerance, indeed a desire for extermination, against anyone thinking differently.

[p. 304] It can now by no means be the intention here to go further into the depiction of those aberrations which so sick a love of the divine exhibits; precisely of such detailed depictions there is no lack either in previous psychologies or in the annals of the asylums — but it was only a matter of making it clear that it is not enough that one love fervently, and that a high goal be loved, but rather that it is necessary that one also love with higher cognition and in a beautiful manner, if this love is to appear as a healthy and genuine love, becoming passion only through its exclusive character and through the pain of finite insufficiency. Once one has gained this true conviction from this example, one will be able to apply it to all other love and passion as well. There is no form of love that could not, through benighted cognition and when pursued in an unbeautiful manner, appear equally sick and, as one very aptly says, senseless. Practised in this way, the love of parents for children becomes a doting love; practised in this way, the love for the beloved becomes a monomania and frenzy; and in this way even the love of science and art can become outright folly. In all these cases we have the right to declare the passion of love as sick, and we see therefore that not the lower, reprehensible goal alone makes love appear abnormal and pathological, but equally the immature condition of the subject and the deficiency of cognition, as well as the unbeautifulness of its striving. Whoever wishes to look about in life will very soon recognise in these circumstances the reason why what is here called genuine and higher love occurs so rarely in humanity.

[p. 305] It must now, however, further be remarked that a doubly severe sickening of the capacity for love can additionally be caused by both forms being combined — by an unworthy goal being pursued in an unbeautiful manner and with severely benighted cognition. These cases are the most severe, and where the aforementioned addictions or lower passions quickly lead to destruction and to delusion or imbecility, there is usually found such a sad twofold cooperation.

The last thing that may finally still be set forth concerning the sickening of

[p. 306] passionate love-striving in general is the effect of disturbing external influences. The history of every bodily disease teaches that we do not fall ill merely because gradually introduced inner derangements finally take shape, as it were, in a particular form of disease, but that quite a number of diseases also arise almost solely through external influences capable of impeding in some significant way the course of ordinary life-events. A similar relationship obtains also with the passion of love. The above considerations have pointed especially to that kind of love-sickness where it arose from an inner deficiency of the soul or an aberration in the nature of its goal; yet it is quite well to be considered that here too, very frequently, external influences make themselves felt, and that in particular hindrances and impediments that confront a vehement, internally healthy, and consciously justified passionate striving can often drive the feeling of pain of the soul — already constantly kept suffering and tormented in a certain way by the insufficiency of our existence — to such a pitch that either general derangement and sickness of the soul set in, or, in a soul not sufficiently strengthened within itself, the previously healthy and genuine passion can now pass over into sick and unbeautiful passion — driven, as it were, by a kind of despair.

After all these general considerations of the pathological feeling of love, it will now be appropriate to the foregoing to treat especially the pathological derangements of the love of the sexes. We recall, however, that the essence of such healthy love was to be sought precisely in that longing, imprinted from the very origin of the idea of our existence, for the completion and perfection of our soul, and that this longing, as it is originally always necessarily an unconscious one, also has the significance of penetrating both the conscious and the unconscious with equal power in all its stirrings. Accordingly, the highest health of the love of one sex for the other is to feel the longing and to strive for its satisfaction which grants to the man in the woman and to the woman in the man the completion of their existence, in such a way that thereby the growth of these souls is made possible and truly promoted, and indeed so that the same fine oscillation between the unconscious and the conscious upon which every genuinely human existence must rest also remains valid through and through in this relationship. Among the various pathological aberrations of such passion of love there must therefore be named, on the one hand, every misdirection of the longing first toward inappropriate objects incapable of granting the completion of the soul, and on the other hand every suspension of the beautiful, properly human equilibrium between the conscious and the unconscious, in that this primordially vital relationship is either merely reflected upward into a thing of mere thought, or drawn down to a merely animal impulse in which ultimately the worth of individuality is entirely lost and only sex in and for itself is pursued. Now while in these directions, which everyone will easily work out in detail with examples following the given indications, the actual false forms of love are quite particularly expressed, attention must yet also be drawn to how often true states of sickness of the soul proceed not merely in the course of individual life from that false love-striving, but how these states of sickness also proceed from the true and healthy love-striving and love-need, when combinations of life-circumstances decidedly impede and disturb the fulfilment and satisfaction of that

[p. 307] [p. 308] striving. Here, then, a wide field is given for the contemplation of the most entangled conditions of the human being, and from the slightest derangement and clouding of the disposition to the most violent manifestation of madness and frenzy, the most manifold occurrences develop solely from these causes. Here too, however, attention must again be drawn to the fact that, in much the same way as we earlier remarked that the higher regions of organic life are proportionally far less subject to diseases than the lower, or as we found that there exists a certain height of the life of the soul and of soul-growth from which a sinking back and falling away appears utterly impossible, so too in love, the higher the key struck therein and the higher the development of the soul seized by love-striving, the far less will the external circumstances of life possess any power that could impair its essence or threaten the danger of sickness.

One thing, however, must here above all else still be especially discussed in its effects upon the life of the soul before we proceed further, and this is the history of what is particularly called "unhappy love" — that is, a love which, by being one-sided, by finding no reciprocation from the other side, will, more than most other disturbing influences of life, often enough bring about a pathological state of the soul. If, however, we have already demonstrated above that actually among all possible more highly developed human individualities there are always only two conceivable that, in the opposition of sex, as Eros and Anteros, perfectly require each other and perfectly dissolve into one another, then it also follows that, strictly speaking, every love-relation that remains without mutual reciprocation will not be a perfectly normal one, but rather a relation to a semblance, while, on the other hand, an encounter of natures truly and perfectly corresponding can never remain without mutual love, and here the love-relation must emerge just as inevitably as the attraction between sun and planet or planet and moon. If one therefore thinks this relationship through more carefully, one will become convinced that what is commonly called an "unhappy" unrequited love must more correctly be reckoned as love for semblances, that it is therefore always to be regarded as a certain aberration, and precisely for this reason, as such, it can also more easily, when violently excited by somehow disturbing harmful influences, lead to actual sickness of the soul, than would be the case with a love founded upon truly mutual necessity that only experiences external impediments. In the latter case, namely, a disturbing life-circumstance will, even if it causes much pain and much sorrow, yet be unable to bring about either a true sickening of love or a sickening of the soul through it; rather, the becoming-certain of the encounter with the other — the one idea, among all conceivable, alone perfectly corresponding to the essence of the soul — will develop a spiritual growth against which every external disturbance can in the end only prove powerless. It will be otherwise, however, in the case of "unhappy" love for a being not truly adequate to the soul; here disturbing, impeding circumstances will partly excite the intrinsically erroneous love and antagonistically only make it more intense and obstinate, perhaps developing it to the point of monomania, and partly they can also throw the love in itself now too much into the unconscious — merely bodily — and now too much into the conscious — sublimely spiritual — region, and may well also cast the entire life of the soul, precisely because it here lacks the inner support of what is thoroughly appropriate and true, into a truly sick mood. In short, there lie here before the observer of the soul a multitude of important processes which will only now become possible to survey and distinguish more clearly, and precisely because of the important influence of such conditions on human life it was necessary to grant these discussions at least this much broader scope.

[p. 309] [p. 310] Finally, with regard to the feeling of love, we must also consider those processes in the soul which condition the cessation, the extinction of this feeling. It will herein immediately become clear that there are certain forms of love of which a necessary cessation, an absolute extinction in the primal ground of the soul, must not be thought of as necessary, while on the other hand there are others of so transient a nature in themselves that not only necessarily with this temporal existence, but often already in a very short time, their cessation must occur. The former holds for those forms of love which lie in the direction of the growth of the soul itself. Just as we have found, therefore, that an inner progression of the idea during its living-itself-out as conscious spirit is conceivable and actually given, once in self-inwardness, and this through the right love of oneself, another time in world-inwardness, and this through the right love of other ideas living themselves out simultaneously with one's own idea, and most of all of the one that most completely completes one's own essence, and finally in God-inwardness, and this through the right love of God, so it is also clear that these strivings, precisely because they are of the same essence as the idea itself, must also be thought of as independent of the particular temporal existence of the idea. Forms of love of this kind will therefore always activate themselves anew as soon as the conscious life of the soul develops, and a temporary resting or, as it were, being-turned-inward will only take place insofar as the conscious spirit returns again to an unconscious being; and it follows accordingly that in these cases an actual extinction only takes place when in general a sinking of the soul, a regression of the being-in-itself of the idea, occurs. As for the transient forms of love, however, all those must necessarily belong here which are directed not toward a being-in-itself of the idea but only toward something in itself transient, a mere form of appearance. In these cases, either some newly awakening pleasure tends to displace an earlier one, and thus also an earlier love, or the further growth of the soul causes such strivings in itself to pale and vanish. Every development of a soul shows a multitude of such often very quickly vanishing forms of love, from the child's love of its toy to the love of external splendour and of many a semblance in later periods. A special consideration may then here too be devoted to the extinction of the love of the sexes, as one of the most powerful forms of love.

[p. 311] In this regard, however, it was already intimated earlier how the love of the sexes, precisely because of its essential co-foundation in the unconscious, and hence also its essential subjection to the periodic transformations of life, cannot as such continue into the final regions of life, but is indeed destined to cease in this form at a certain height of life. If, however, love in general, as the sole active and positive feeling, as that which should properly be the lever and mover of our entire higher life of the soul and the heartbeat of our world of thought, may also in the whole and in general never be separated from life — from life, to which living, as love to the body [Liebe to Leibe], according to the suggestiveness of our language, stands only as a

[p. 312] transformed form of existence — then with regard to the love of the sexes and its extinction, in accordance with the law set forth above, it will always only depend upon how much therein is love for the opposed idea in itself and how much is a love for a merely transient form of appearance. For if in genuine feeling of love there is really no question of an actual cessation, of a complete extinction, but rather, as in our whole life and striving, only of transformations, then that form of love too in which an idea forming the complement of one's own self is grasped with the most perfect inwardness will never and nevermore experience a genuine cessation, but only a continual transformation. Just as, therefore, the form of appearance of the idea is everywhere an infinitely manifold one, so too love must perpetually become manifest in infinitely manifold phases, and it can therefore only appear to cease when the transition into another phase is required. The love of the sexes will accordingly indeed not be able to persist for all time in its full relation to the unconscious sphere of life for the continued development of the species; yet, insofar as it was a genuine love, it will indeed be quite specifically destined to endure perpetually, in relation to the mutual complementing and the ever deeper understanding of two souls or ideas assigned and reciprocally completing each other. These circumstances become still clearer, moreover, when we recall the law which we already earlier had to acknowledge concerning the continued growth of the idea, namely, that this growth is not to be assumed in such a way that the higher annihilates and entirely abolishes the lower, the conscious the unconscious, but that the higher takes the lower up into itself, transfigures it within itself, and preserves it encompassed within itself. Now we do indeed recognise that it is only the material of appearance, only the actual revelation of the higher idea in the aether, which continually fades and is extinguished, while in this idea itself the capacity to reveal itself in a conscious manner will by no means abolish and destroy the capacity of ever-new revelation in an unconscious manner, but rather will preserve, elevate, and maintain all of this within itself in the idea. If we therefore apply these results to make clearer to ourselves what the doctrine of the soul is able to set forth concerning the duration of love between man and woman, we shall be able to say that, as soon as a higher form of love, truly completing the individual, was at issue here — such that both ideas became capable of the actual continued growth of their essence only in their reciprocal interaction — then, I say, in them themselves absolutely no necessity of the cessation of their most loving and intimate relationship in time may be conceived, and that, even if both will immerse themselves ever more deeply in the mysteries of higher forms of love, and especially ever more in the love of God — indeed must do so — yet with all this continued growth it remains indispensable, as an originally required reciprocal relationship, that the inner encompassing of their most essential being must increase all the more, the further they themselves are developed. Though this penetration and encompassing may later indeed express itself in ever different forms, and pass more and more from the region of the unconscious, with increasing growth of the souls, into the region of the conscious, or, as we are accustomed to say, the purely spiritual, yet, as has now been amply demonstrated, even the purest spiritual, as something individual, can always be conceived only in part as conscious, since it must perpetually rest with its other unconscious half in the universal divine; and thus also in relation to that mutual relationship of both ideas a constant oscillation between the conscious and the unconscious will in any case be indispensable and remain so for all eternity. It is otherwise, to be sure, with the love-relation to a semblance, in which case it is always necessarily required that with the continued growth of the souls it dissolve itself entirely and perish in advancing development. That most of the relationships of ordinary human existence belong only here may indeed, as yet again one of the manifold imperfections of humanity as it is destined to live itself out on this planet, by no means be denied.

[p. 313] [p. 314] May it then have succeeded, through the history of that feeling which is often exclusively designated as love, as through the most important example, to have brought the psychic processes of the feeling of love in general to a clearer cognition. If one wished to go through and trace all the individual forms of this feeling — from the love of material possessions, which always rests only upon an error, up to the love of nature in general, the love of the fatherland, of children and parents, of humanity, and finally to the love of God — according to all their phases, then indeed essential enrichments of psychology could result in all directions, yet such thoroughness would exceed too much the scope of the present task. We therefore now turn to the much more briefly to be treated history of the in every respect last feeling, to the<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> It is very remarkable to observe how in this way, precisely in more highly developing natures, the existence of another soul can be quite indispensably the constant condition of their continued growth, even when this other existence no longer falls within the circle of their own temporal life. The way in which Dante's further spiritual development was entirely bound to the thought of Beatrice can serve as an approximate example of such a relationship.

[p. 315]

#### 4. Geschichte des Hasses — 4. History of Hatred

When the preceding considerations have led us to acknowledge the feeling of love as "the inborn longing of the idea for completion and perfection," we can now just as decidedly designate the feeling of hatred as the inborn resistance of the idea against everything decidedly inappropriate and opposed to that longing for perfection. We therefore called love a positively active feeling; hatred we must designate as a negatively active one, and indeed as negative in a twofold respect — once insofar as it decidedly negates this inappropriate and hindering element, and again insofar as even its object is a negative one. Not, as in love — which strives only toward the solely positive, the divine, and indeed in all its various, now rightly, now wrongly recognised forms — is what is striven for here a positive, but even what is negated is itself a negating, namely something now truly, now falsely assumed to negate the divine. So long as one proceeded so childishly in knowledge as to oppose to the positive principle of the divine an equally positive principle of the diabolical — just as in the childhood of physics one could still treat a special principle of cold as opposed to the principle of warmth — one could have said of hatred that it must by nature be devoted to all forms of evil, and in the highest degree to the devil. Such nonsense, however, dissipates in the light of a higher philosophical cognition and therefore requires no further attention here; rather, it follows from the above and from all earlier considerations that hatred cannot really have any actual individual or in any way personal object, but that its appropriate object in truth may only be the absence of what promotes the soul, only the sinking back of the idea itself — a sinking back which somehow becomes manifest instead of its demanded constant forward-pressing, and wherein precisely the depth and darkness of its ungodly condition is most deeply recognised.

[p. 316] Like joy and sorrow, however, so too, along with love, hatred has its semblances in common, and it is therefore, in a multitude of cases, excited as a resisting, combating, destruction-seeking feeling by objects that are falsely taken for negations of the divine — and indeed always the more so, the more incomplete the growth of the soul and the darker the cognition still is. What has not, therefore, been hated by human beings! It is just as remarkable as what has been loved by human beings! But in both cases, through higher development of the soul, the human being is guided toward dispelling such phantasms, and in the most fortunate case led to the appropriate direction of hatred and love. It is really sometimes as though the soul were meant first to practise itself in developing and bringing about these feelings before it could be in a position to assign them their proper objects.

Let us now consider this feeling too according to the various aspects, as we did the others, and thus first according to its genesis and development. Hatred rests, however, like love, only in part in the conscious, and for the other part in the unconscious life of the soul. What is merely distinguished in rational cognition as incorrect, as erroneous, is not yet for that reason capable of exciting hatred; hatred arises only when a decided resistance has also been stirred in the unconscious against something. This mixture of the unconscious and the conscious is in hatred often as strange as in love. There are influences and conditions which are not at all acknowledged in the conscious as something particularly disturbing — hateful — and which excite a deep inner antipathy, and conversely. Here too there is often remarkable occasion to recognise, indeed to admire, the depth, the great wisdom, and infallibility of the unconscious of which we spoke earlier, and almost everyone will in their life be able to recall instances in which a deep inner antipathy, a vivid feeling of hostility and hatred, rightly indicated circumstances to them before cognition actually perceived them as such, or unhappy experiences had confirmed them.

[p. 317] But if the feeling of hatred is truly founded and arisen in both its roots, the conscious and the unconscious, then we perceive it in ourselves always as something bitter, unhappy, unblessed, and its kinship with the feeling of sorrow has therefore already been mentioned earlier. It is, however, also here as with sorrow to be noted that a great difference exists as to whether the hatred has really been excited by an archetypal relation (if one may be allowed to say so), or by a semblance. The latter, precisely because it already in and of itself rests upon a false, inappropriate, pathological direction of the soul, is always far more bitter, more unblessed, than the higher, more inwardly justified hatred; that former hatred will therefore more easily become a genuine state of suffering, and this hatred will therefore become passion so much sooner than the latter, which preserves within itself the healthier nature and will never reach the vehemence of the former, but rather, with higher development of the soul, always more completely vanishes. The same higher development, therefore, which makes us ever more inaccessible to sorrow must, for the same reason, ultimately make us inaccessible to hatred, since it comes to be understood that in the end the highest world-order does encompass all those imperfections and deficiencies, and all those development-hindering and disturbing conditions which in particular appear so troublesome and hateful,

[p. 318] as necessary conditions of the whole, and that it is a most unphilosophical proposition when it is said: "Offences must needs come, but woe to that man by whom the offence cometh!" Just as, therefore, before the higher physiological gaze upon the universe, despite all the striving of individuals, the concept of death cannot endure, but only a universal life is recognised, so too in the light of that cognition there vanishes not only evil as a special demonic principle, as Satan, but also, despite all the unfortunate deeds of individuals, the concept of a special sinfulness, of a special hateful negative — of sin — ceases. To the soul so enlightened, nowhere does a truly in itself hateful object any longer appear — and all hatred dissolves into compassionate love. The rise to such cognition seems in humanity to have dawned first with Christ, and from this quite particular great redemption — this redemption from the concept of evil and from hatred — much great result can still be expected in the time to come; for the more it is recognised how most of the misery of humanity, and everything that in particular receives the name of the sinful, depends partly on imperfect conditions of communal life and partly on the deterioration of cognition and feeling, and how necessarily, but also all the more lamentably, wretchedness had to emerge hitherto from those conditions, the more an approach to universal blessedness may be expected from the continued development of that cognition. Quite the opposite result, however, follows from an opposing view. Indeed, it is equally possible that, just as in an isolated contemplation of nature one must arrive at the dark thought of the sole power of death and universal dying — since the ever-new becoming also conditions the ever-recurring cessation of what has come into being —

[p. 319] so in the ethical domain one may arrive at the thought of universal sinfulness and universal evil, precisely because all, even the most beautiful revelations of individual ideas in life, measured against the perfection of the most supreme, will always appear as only something imperfect and merely striving. Where, then, this view prevails, indeed where the conscious spirit itself is regarded as originally fallen to evil through its being bound to the unconscious, and where the unconscious is viewed as thoroughly sinful — there lies the darkest, most pernicious view of life; there hostility and hatred penetrate deep into the soul, and the aberration then escalates even to the completely insane assumption of an evil divine — a Satan — whereby at last full dominion is granted to the misery of humanity in hatred and discord. Yet such a view cannot endure; for just as necessarily the insight of universal life abolishes absolute death, so universal love abolishes hatred. For it would be entirely impossible, despite everywhere becoming aware of death, to deny all life, or amid the most widespread hatred to abolish all love, since the love of God and toward God must under all circumstances still remain. Thus with increasing cognition it will also be demanded ever more decidedly that all hatred vanish, and that despite the always vividly maintained insight into infinite imperfection and disturbance, universal love be allowed to reign more and more. To be sure, even in the most fortunate case the human being passes here through infinitely many gradations; he can and should not attain that height all at once, and precisely for this reason the experience of a lively resistance and violent rejection and combat — in a word, hatred — against everything that can in any way set itself obstructively and impedingly and hinderingly against the progress of the soul, necessarily belongs to the developmental history of our spiritual life, so that we must be thoroughly convinced that the salutary development of the soul is just as little conceivable without alternating states of hatred and love as without those of joy and sorrow.

[p. 320] But if it has emerged that love consists not merely in the conscious acknowledgement of some excellence, but simultaneously in the permeation of the unconscious and the excitation and sympathetic attraction of the latter, and if it has also been acknowledged that the conscious recognition alone of a disturbance and of something disagreeable will produce only disapproval but no hatred — the latter arising only when at the same time the unconscious has been pervaded by the liveliest antipathy against that hindering and disturbing element, for only in this way does cognition become feeling at all — then the further question arises: which regions of the unconscious life of the soul are particularly called upon in the negation of hatred? Certainly not the generating, developing, attracting ones, but the killing, destroying, repelling ones. Everything that can stir in this direction in the dark unconscious realms of our inner organic life immediately resonates when the feeling we call hatred is truly kindled. The destructive here turns indirectly against the external, but actually immediately against itself, so that, while love — where precisely the opposite in organic life is to be called upon — proves itself, in the attainment of its object and full satisfaction, as promoting life and calling forth the bloom of health, hatred in all satisfaction and attainment and injury of its object causes only destruction of one's own life and often bitter states of illness.

It was, however, already mentioned earlier that the sphere of life in which the most important bodily fluid, the blood, perpetually destroys and decomposes itself, must be called that of the secretory organs in general, and in particular that of the hepatic circulation. In these regions, then, hatred also particularly expresses itself; one says figuratively "the blood begins to boil," and the expression is apt enough, but it boils in its own decomposition, it boils from the sharp juice of bile that completes the decomposition of ingested nourishment, it boils from the foam of the saliva, which in anger (that momentarily flaming hatred) becomes sharp even to the point of a poison of fury, and in this boiling it makes the self-nourishment of the elementary formations from the blood just as impossible as occurs during a fever — and so, through persistently nourished hatred, the entire formation of the body wastes away.

[p. 321] With this feeling too, moreover, one observes that now the conscious, now the unconscious side of life provides the first impulse for its development. Where no higher intelligence decidedly opposes it, a perhaps entirely externally conditioned stronger secretion of bile will spread a bitter, hateful feeling over the soul, just as surely as an occasion for hatred in conscious life excites the unconscious to more vigorous, irritated hepatic activity.

From the foregoing it may now also easily be inferred what must result regarding the relation of hatred to other feelings and to the whole life of the soul. Of its kinship with sorrow mention has already been made, and it is likewise clear how it excludes joy, or can stand close only to a very low variety of it — malicious joy [Schadenfreude]. A special consideration, however, is required by its relation to love. For however much one might anticipate that one of these two opposed feelings must exclude the other, yet this is not at all the case in truth, and intense love and intense hatred are therefore not infrequently found in the soul at the same time, only working in different directions; indeed it is remarkable how in

[p. 322] one and the same direction one of these feelings often suddenly leaps over into the other, how from hatred intense love and from ardent love no less ardent hatred can spring. Here too everything depends on the degree of growth of the soul and on the development of intelligence. It is easy to understand that a higher stage, which more and more excludes semblances of love and of hatred and tunes the soul more toward general joyfulness and love, will no longer be accessible to such alternation and discord; on the other hand, however, it will on the one part always remain necessary and natural that, when in any direction, and be it even a quite noble and appropriate one, vivid love develops, everything that persistently and obstructively opposes this love, and every lesser, disturbing, and inappropriate condition, must be apprehended and regarded with vigorous resistance, that is, with a certain decided aversion and hatred; on the other part, however, it will also become intelligible what sharply alternating oppositions can occur here, for instance when it is suddenly recognised that an object of love, which had been taken for the archetype, is only a semblance, so that the soul now decidedly negates it; or conversely when something that perhaps at first, through the power of its appearance in and of itself, invited opposition, is recognised as a real love-archetype and is now with all vehemence longed for and seized upon — cases for which life offers the most manifold examples.

The relation of hatred to the whole life of the soul can in a certain respect be called a promoting one, and in many others only a disturbing one. It is promoting in the earlier periods of development, in order to push back vigorously and decidedly reject much that is inappropriate, much that runs counter to the innermost essence of the soul. It is remarkable then how hatred, often in a measure similar to love, intensifies and develops the powers; one might say how it, as an exercise of the will, matures and strengthens the power of the spirit that is to be exercised. It has a disturbing effect because, as something in general inappropriate to the soul, it strikes back in a poisoning manner, as it were, upon one's own life of the soul, and indeed all the more so the more it becomes positive — that is, turns against individualities and semblances. Here it is, then, where we must connect the transition to the consideration of the pathological aberrations of hatred.

[p. 323] One might, however, first perhaps be tempted to regard hatred in general as a pathological phenomenon and not to acknowledge it as belonging to the history of healthy soul-development at all. In so doing, however, one would proceed about as erroneously as someone who would always declare certain organisational conditions to be pathological because they are so when they occur in this manner in the mature, fully developed organism. The hepatic structure, which especially corresponds to hatred, immediately furnishes the most complete example of this: we find it proportionally larger the younger the organism, and the newborn child still has a liver which is so much larger in proportion to the whole than in the adult that one would indeed have to regard a similar proportion in the adult as pathological. So too with hatred! In earlier developmental periods it is indispensable in order to free us from much that is inappropriate and to exercise the power of reaction. In higher development it should recede more and more and ultimately dissolve in universal love. Pathological, therefore, hatred will be when, in earlier periods, it still remains entirely without any stirring against what is recognised as bad, as low, and as base, so that the soul sinks into apathetic nothingness and sleepy general lukewarmness; and pathological it will also be when, where higher development should already have been reached and where in the light of higher cognition reconciliation with the world as a whole, alongside all recognition of deficiency and poverty in particular, should have taken hold, a hating disposition still overwhelmingly asserts itself and so poisons life. In the latter case, then, it only still requires that the error of an evil divine principle be present, that this principle be believed to be especially active in individual ideas, and the horrors of all utterly darkened states of the soul, and in particular the belief in witches and the devil and the persecution thereof, as well as the monomania of murder, can very easily develop.

[p. 324] In the foregoing there lies at the same time what may be said about the extinction, the cessation of hatred. Hatred, as something negative, should itself gradually be more and more negated, and it is therefore already in the order of developmental history that in the healthy course it must finally cease. The more perfected soul will always turn away from what is contrary to the higher, from the ugly, the base, the false, but it will no longer be in hatred — in hatred which otherwise also had to penetrate the unconscious region — but in compassionate love. We can therefore briefly say that the natural death of hatred is to perish in love, and that it has truly been extinguished will be recognisable in the fact that that simultaneous resonance of the unconscious, even where cognition must negate, no longer takes place.

#### 5. Von den gleichmäßigen Buständen des Gemüthes — 5. On the Balanced States of the Disposition

There exists, as has already been noted, a certain middle state of the world of the disposition in which neither the excitement of love nor of hatred, neither of joy nor of sorrow expresses itself. This middle state, however, is again a twofold one, inasmuch as the one maintains the full mobility of the disposition, preserves alive the full warmth that is the condition of all various feelings, and only at the height of conscious clarity, joyfulness, and love maintains that beautiful equilibrium which qualifies the soul for everything great; while the other has truly lost that mobility of the life of the soul, and solely in the weakness and dullness of the entire life-expressions contains the reason for not being occasioned to particular movements of the disposition. In both directions there are very different gradations, among which sharp demarcations are no longer possible. The summit of pure, beautiful equilibrium we may almost call divine, while the nadir of the lifeless or feelingless soul verges on the animal. By no means, moreover, may we believe these states to be distributed in such a way that each belongs to one soul alone; rather, alternating with often violent excitations of individual feelings, both prevail at different times and in different measure in the human soul and contribute to varying into infinity the colour-scale in whose vibrations the world of the disposition activates itself. The higher the individuality, the more mature the growth, the more the entire life of the soul inclines toward divine equanimity and clarity; the lower the individuality, the more decidedly the regression, the nearer lies the possibility of submersion in a feelingless, animal-like apathy.

[p. 325] It would, moreover, be a great error if one wished to think of even these heights or depths of the life of the soul as conditioned only by the conscious and grounded only in consciousness; the genuine higher equanimity as well as the dull and deep apathy always proceed from the root of all soul-life in such a way that they present themselves equally in the conscious and the unconscious: in the former through clarity of thinking and a certain stillness of the soul, and in the case of apathy through complete thoughtlessness; in the latter, with respect to higher equanimity, through ease and calm of inner life with full strength of health, and with respect to apathy, through sluggishness, stagnation, and weakness. People have also long felt and recognised the significance of these states for the conscious and the unconscious, though not demonstrated it scientifically; for quite rightly they separated from the true equanimity — grounded in the entire beautiful and healthy organisation also of the unconscious life of the soul — what they called the forced, merely reflected-upon equanimity, which latter always had to present itself as something artificial and imperfect. This distinction is in any case very important, for both the manner in which a soul hovering in genuine, higher equanimity also grounded through the unconscious manifests itself, and the manner in which it receives what is external, will always diverge entirely from the artificial kind of equanimity which is not so beautifully grounded in the unconscious. Shakespeare has intimated something of this when he contrasts with Brutus, who vividly feels the mightiest blows of fate yet bears them with higher equanimity, the vehement, irritable Cassius, of whom we then hear:

[p. 326] "I have as much of this in art as you, But yet my nature could not bear it so."

That genuine equanimity which lives a rich, full life in the deep soul, which is warmed through and through by higher joyfulness and comprehensive love — it is actually what indicates what we call greatness of the soul, and what for this reason especially deserves this name, because through it the human being so greatly enlarges himself inwardly that the vicissitudes of life are no longer able to shake him. This greatness is what intimates that there can be certain ideas which by the very energy of their being-in-themselves are mightier than all others, and this greatness is also what, when the being-in-itself of the soul had grown thereto, already appeared to the ancients as the highest result of a life, and which imparts to so many of their productions a peculiar power and beauty that can never be admired enough — wherein, however, one must not forget, first, that precisely this equanimity is also what imparts to these productions, for all their beauty, a certain cooler character, and secondly, that precisely because in antiquity that primal feeling of love, from which stronger excitations of the soul will always proceed, had not yet truly entered into the world, there too that higher equanimity had necessarily to appear more appropriate and easier to attain than in later times and our own. In that sense, then, one might think of a Sophocles, for whom only in the ripest age the highest tragedies succeed! Place beside him in thought the character of a Goethean Tasso, where with so much that is beautiful and profound the greatest irritability combines, and the picture of the different states we wished to depict will emerge all the more clearly.

[p. 327] The deep apathetic state, where no mobility of the disposition is possible because the vital condition for excitation in various directions is lacking, is often directly caused by partial dying-off of the unconscious life and paralyzing illness; rarely does it proceed from conscious life, and only previously enormous upheavals of the life of the disposition or excessive exertions in outward activity then bring it about.

Finally, it is to be noted that, if the particular and beautiful equanimity can only be reached at the full height of life — namely, when the restless inundations of unconscious life have already naturally levelled themselves — then it is thereby expressed that it can be less appropriate to the earlier periods, precisely because there the ongoing oscillations of the life of the disposition provide the most essential moments for promoting the development and continued growth of the soul.

[p. 328]

### β . Zur Geschichte der Erkenntniß — β. On the History of Cognition

The possibility of all cognition rests partly on an external, partly on an internal ground. The internal ground is the idea itself, the divine in us; for were we not in truth through God's grace, were not precisely in the normal human being a peculiar idea of God living itself out, then there could be no question of cognition at all, since cognising actually, as the Greeks long expressed it, is only a re-membering [Er-innern, literally a making-inward], that is, can only aim at the human being becoming inwardly ever more aware of himself; for the divine is indeed eternally essentially self-identical, and though revealing itself in still so many thousandfold ways, yet the possibility of everything actual is really contained in every divine monad, in every part-divine, in such a way that the more the idea becomes aware of its very own, eternally unfathomable essence in all its phases, the more it must also cognise and know of all divine revelation in general. The external ground of cognising is the conflict with other ideas. No idea, however, can in and of itself perceive another idea; they perceive each other only insofar as they somehow also externally, naturally live out their inner divine. The sides of the organism formed through the unconscious working of the idea, by means of which a conflict pressing toward consciousness with the reve-<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> Of the existence of an idea in and for itself, without any kind of revelation whatsoever, we can have absolutely no representation.

[p. 329] lations of other ideas becomes possible, we call the senses, and so it becomes clear that, more precisely defined, we may also say that the second ground of all cognition is given in the senses. It leads now to very remarkable considerations when we further reflect that, however, the actually sentient element in the sense-organ, that is, the sensory nerve, never directly perceives the external object, but rather (as physiology demonstrates with the greatest definiteness; see my System of Physiology, vol. 3, p. 146) that in the nerve we receive only a sensation of a certain change in the intermediary substance which lies between the terminal loops of the nerves and the external world. Everything we call the external world, that is, what excites our representation of such a world, is therefore, closely considered, only a part of our own organism, and it is only an inference, a conclusion, that something outside us must exist which excites the changes in that in-itself unconscious intermediary substance that come to sensation, whereby we receive the representation of a world, or of ideas revealing themselves outside us. So considered, one can even say: the ground of all cognition lies in ourselves, namely, once in the idea itself — that it is such a one as can potentially raise itself to consciousness — and another time in the unconscious revelation of the idea as organism, whose retunements can in and of themselves occasion in the idea the representation of an external world and indeed necessarily must do so.

The more powerful, therefore, the innate idea is, and the more clearly and sustainably the senses operate and convey to<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> Only in this sense does the Schopenhauerian paradox "The world is my representation" and indeed all idealism have perfect right. The guarantee for the existence of an external world and of other ideas beyond one's own lies solely in the above inference, and whoever will not accept this inference cannot be refuted apodictically.

[p. 330] the inborn idea the reciprocal interaction of more numerous and mightier ideas, the more perfectly are the conditions fulfilled which cognition presupposes, and the higher the level cognition can attain. We can therefore also briefly say: if feeling may be called the becoming-aware of the condition of one's own idea, then cognising is to be called the becoming-aware of the relation of the idea to other ideas and to the highest. Feeling therefore rests as such initially in the idea alone; cognition is unthinkable without the relation of one's own idea to others. A cognising of the idea merely as a cognising of itself is not conceivable, for the immediate becoming-aware of the idea is given solely through feeling, and whoever would therefore wish to understand the inscription of the temple at Delphi in that sense would precisely not understand it.

In the developmental history of the soul in the child it was traced in what manner the miracle of the spirit emerges, and how thereby, through the appearance of the first thought in the soul, the beginning is given for an infinite continued development. We also had to distinguish there between three stages of the development of the spirit, which we called understanding, imagination, and reason. To the history of cognition, however, essentially only the consideration of understanding and reason belongs; yet it must here once more be pointed out that whoever makes any absolute separations and divisions in the doctrine of the soul is immediately lost, and whoever does not also here strive toward the grasping and holding fast of the indivisible unity within

be known. A knowing, however, which is given in the truth-conscience of the soul and which can become audible to everyone who wills to hear it, tells us that the idea is in truth not alone in the All, but that within a highest idea there are infinite ideas.

[p. 331] amidst multiplicity can elevate itself. Thus understanding, imagination, and reason are at bottom indivisibly one. For if we characterize understanding simply as the cognition of appearance, reason as the cognition of essence, and imagination as the productive capacity of the soul made possible by the attainment of spiritual maturity, then it is immediately striking and unmistakable that the understanding would not even grasp the appearance if its own inborn idea did not already feel the unconscious attraction toward the idea existing simultaneously with it in God, and thus, like a Prometheus, did not have a presentiment of the idea outside itself in its appearance (thereby bringing about the formation of the sense organs) before it could yet apprehend it as idea; and on the other hand it is also clear in and of itself that a reason without simultaneous understanding is unthinkable, precisely because appearance is the revelation of the idea, and the grasping of this revelation in the understanding alone can prepare the apprehension of the idea through reason. Finally, however, both understanding and reason are in turn impossible without a cooperation of the soul in that measure where we call it imagination, for not only does it follow that every representation whatsoever is produced only through a peculiar productivity, since we learn nothing immediately of a world outside us, but we must also convince ourselves that every thought, insofar as it can only be presented and made objective, as it were pictorially, through certain symbols we call words, is a kind of work of art, and that as a work of art it can only be created through inner productivity. Thus an artistic creative principle operates even in the seemingly driest and sharpest construction of understanding and reason, just as conversely no work of art can be created and no expression of genuine imagination is conceivable that would not necessarily possess some cognition of appearances and their essence. When we therefore speak of understanding and reason as particulars and singulars, as they condition cognition at its highest, we must always bear in mind that only the predominant element in certain directions of the life of the soul is here brought into view, and that there can never be any question of something specifically different and entirely separate. Indeed, it may be added at once that, far from imagination being inseparable from cognition, the higher positive feelings of joy and love likewise cannot be separated from it. Plato says very beautifully that all philosophy (in the word itself, incidentally, the Greeks already included love, as love of wisdom) must begin with wonder, and thereby expresses that that joyful astonishment of the soul over the inborn loving attraction of our essence toward the deepest possible apprehension of other ideas becomes the essential source of all higher cognition.

[p. 332] If one now examines more closely in detail how cognition gradually develops in the spirit, one must above all bring into sharper focus an important fact, namely how all cognition presupposes that a certain numerus, a spiritual equivalent for both appearance and idea, has been found, through which a mediation and an understanding between these two, at first so disparate, objects could arise. This numerus, this equivalent, is language. Appearance and idea lie, although one conditions the other, seemingly so enormously far apart that we would be utterly incapable of comprehending both together in the spirit and thereby producing them, if the word did not step in between, the word that is formed from the sound, that is, from the deeply inward trembling of a thing, in which innermost secret movement the nature of the essence of this thing betrays itself, so that this sound, this word, can for precisely that reason be taken as the spiritual symbol of the thing itself. Precisely because this symbol is no longer a thing itself, it can furthermore also, just as it was at first only a symbol of appearance, become a symbol of the idea as well, and only when it has been elevated to that point does what is essentially the substance of all cognition become possible, namely, the grasping of the relation between idea and appearance. Without the word, without language, therefore, no cognition, no knowledge! It goes without saying that the word is not taken here merely as sound and as symbol of appearance — such a word even animals can learn to speak — but the word simultaneously as symbol of the idea. One can go even further and separate the word from the sound entirely by regarding it merely as a written sign, and even so it can still become a condition of cognition. To be sure, it would not arise merely as such a sign, and if there were no spoken language, no written language would have come into being either; but once the wondrous work of art of language has been created and has come into being, it can indeed also without sound, as gesture or sign, continue to operate among human beings and condition cognition, as is demonstrated in the most remarkable way in the instruction of the deaf-mute.

[p. 333] Thus — in a word — only that a medium, a middle term of comparison [tertium comparationis], be present, in which idea and appearance, these two otherwise seemingly forever irreconcilable yet through and through mutually conditioning factors, can be absorbed and expressed — only on this does it depend, and from this does it follow, that cognition comes about. The inner, deliberately willed movement of the soul within the domain of precisely this third element, this numerus, this algebra, this quite peculiar symbolic calculus

[p. 334] in the higher sense, we call thinking. In thinking we thus live neither wholly in appearance nor wholly in the idea, and thinking can neither grasp nor discern either one perfectly in and of itself; but precisely for that reason it is suited to make the relation of both intuitable. Therein, therefore — that feeling truly is alone that in which the state of the idea, and in this state thus also the idea itself, is most intimately and immediately grasped — lies a tremendous advantage of feeling over thinking, one that only now becomes entirely clear, and that has long been recognized in silence by every more richly developed soul. Thus it is true that no thought can grasp the idea of God, the idea of the soul, as deeply as immediate feeling; yet that must not be regarded as a reproach, but is only the necessary consequence of the difference between feeling and thought.

In general, this relation itself gives much further to think about. First, however, attention must be drawn to the fact that feeling too has its equivalents, its symbols, through which it can make itself known in its particularity and be grasped by another spirit.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> One may actually use the word "equivalent" in a double sense with respect to both cognition and feeling: first, in that language really does subjectively represent for the spirit, and the realm of tones really does subjectively represent for feeling, that which is to be intuited or felt, now as idea or determinate representation, and another time as feeling, so that through these signs, these symbols, cognition and feeling first truly attain consciousness; secondly, however, "equivalent" also has an objective meaning, in that through word or tone, through language or music, the soul can in turn make the cognition awakened within, as well as the feeling kindled within, objective, can make them known outwardly. It is most remarkable how in this case the soul, especially with respect to feeling, rescues itself through this making-known from the overwhelming power of this inclination! All art, even the art of speech, not merely music, is in this sense an equivalent of feeling, and the creation of art therefore calms the feeling. Yet cognition too can, in the flood of words, as it were expend itself, and leave behind a hollowness of the spirit. Hence the advantage of precision!

[p. 335] and that these equivalents stand in a very peculiar relation to the equivalents of the idea and of representation as the artistic domain of language offers them in the word. These equivalents of feeling are partly involuntary, and to this category belong the re-attunements of one's own unconscious life as it reveals itself outwardly, as they express themselves in the alteration of the features of the countenance, in the modulation of the voice, in the changed coloring and temperature of the skin, indeed in the involuntary bearing of the whole body; partly they are voluntary, and to this category belongs the artistic domain of tones, music, indeed art as a whole.

If one wishes to compare carefully these two artistic domains, that of language and that of music, in this respect, one will arrive at very interesting results. Much is common to both, much is also peculiar to each domain. Common to them is that both can express themselves in sounds as well as in written signs, and that both can serve now the greatest clarity and sublimity, now the greatest confusion and vulgarity; common to them is the remarkable artful structure that the human spirit has given to each; common to them also is this: that both attain their highest beauty only when they give expression in a truly organic, inwardly necessary sequence — here, to successive moods and modulations of feeling, there, to representations and ideas linking one to another. Peculiar, on the other hand, to each artistic domain is essentially the manner of its inner organic structure, inasmuch as for language the spirit was left free to choose now these, now those arbitrarily seized sounds and signs to serve as equivalents of representations, concepts, and ideas, whereas for music the choice of tones is prescribed by natural laws, and for the whole of humanity it must therefore just as certainly be everywhere one and the same, as with respect to language every branch of humanity was left free everywhere to form its own idiom. Whoever, then, has rightly recognized this so very different significance of language and music will find it not difficult therein to recognize completely the relation of cognition and feeling, and he will on the one hand indeed find that thinking in words, while it alone, as it first establishes the spirit in general, also alone makes the renewed spiritual construction of the world possible, it nevertheless lacks that immediacy of which feeling and its equivalent, music, are capable to so high a degree.

[p. 336] Yet it is equally undeniable that sense-representation in and of itself, and its reproduction in the imagination, possess, with respect to immediacy and objectivity, an enormous advantage over thinking, and that no thought as such can attain the force and vividness of an immediate sense-representation; nevertheless, precisely because all life is ultimately conditioned only by the constant manifestation of the idea in appearance, the thought, as the middle term of comparison [tertium comparationis], or the equivalent of both, will always remain something through which alone, in the higher sense, the reconstruction of the entire world in the spirit becomes possible, and through which then even the immediacy of feeling as well as of sense-representation can first receive its full value and significance. Thinking is thus a constant equalization, an ever-recurring synthesis, a perpetual reciprocal measuring of the idea against appearance and of appearance against the idea.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> One could raise the question: by what organic element is this constant measuring of the representation against the idea and of the idea against the representation represented? It turns out, however, that after we have recognized in the molecular substance of the brain the organic element of representation, and in the primitive fibers of the nerves the constant relation of the soul to the external world, for the relation of representations among

[p. 337] The more perfectly, therefore, appearance is traced back to the idea, the more it comes to consciousness how an entire chain of mutually conditioning appearances is ultimately conditioned by a common fundamental idea, the more is that given which we call explanation, and at its highest, satisfied cognition, and the attainment of which is a deeply inborn need one another and to the fundamental idea of one's own being, no other organic substrate can remain than those connective arcs that establish the synthesis of the brain's structure, through which all cerebral formations are connected and which collect in the commissures. Precisely because this is so, the enormous difference of the human brain from the animal brain shows itself less in its relative size than in the richness of synthetic fibers and the strength of its commissures. For precisely this reason, too, the commissures (the so-called fornix and corpus callosum) are strongest in the forebrain, since it is to this that the significance for intelligence especially belongs.

If we ask further, what is it that takes place in the life of the brain while we think? the answer is: an infinitely varied radiating back and forth of innervation along the peculiar conducting fibers of the brain. If a changed tension-relation of a nerve running from the brain to a muscle causes the muscle there to twitch, or if an excitation of the optic nerve originating from the brain awakens in the eye the subjective sensation of a development of light, then a heightened tension of a primitive fiber arc running from one region of the primitive brain substance to another excites the entry into consciousness (one could figuratively also say the becoming-luminous) of that representation which was bound to the affected site of the brain substance. Now, however, representations are not merely called into consciousness in such a deliberate manner; rather, a serial involuntary awakening of representations, which follows a certain hidden law and (as I have already demonstrated in the System of Psychology) is in any case connected with the perfusion of the brain by blood, takes place in addition, and this alone explains the thought-images that pass by unintentionally behind and between all deliberate thinking, like cloud formations. This involuntary series can indeed be drawn into deliberate thinking, yet the two stand, as it were, in an inverse relation. When deliberate thinking is pursued with great sharpness and exertion, the unintentional thought-images pass by more palely and, as it were, at a greater distance; when, on the other hand, voluntary thinking grows faint (for example, before falling asleep), the involuntarily passing series of representations press forward more distinctly and more closely — indeed, it is they which in sleep become almost the only ones and produce the confused, strange character of dreams. nim

Whoever now wishes to make truly clear to himself that, really almost just as the deliberate radiations of innervation in the motor nerves produce muscular twitching, so deliberate thinking is conditioned by means of voluntarily
