[p. 338] of the spirit, a need through whose satisfaction the spirit first truly comes into being and exists. As was already remarked earlier, therefore, the proposition of Descartes — "I think, therefore I am" [cogito ergo sum] — can only be justified in this sense; for indeed it appears almost absurd if one wished to accept thinking as the proof that something exists, since so much exists that

stimulated alternation of innervation-radiations in the brain's own fiber substance — will readily understand why the one, quite as much as the other, must become fatigued with prolonged continuation. Sustained radiation of innervation exhausts its production, and the substance requires rest in order, by means of the life of the blood, to generate new innervation. But just as these deliberate movements of cerebral innervation can produce exhaustion, so too are they subject to the laws of all that is conscious in the respect that they must be practiced and are perfected through practice. Hence the phenomenon that one unaccustomed to thinking cannot at all, or only with great difficulty, think through or comprehend something that a "thinking head" thinks through and comprehends almost in an instant. Here, then, an infinite perfectibility is reserved for the human spirit! The very same mental operations that on a lower level are carried out heavily and slowly, because the right modality in combining the innervation-radiations cannot yet be immediately attained, as it were, take place on a higher level with the most enormous speed and precision; indeed, it must be noted that this speed can become so enormous because the conductivity of innervation-tension is in and of itself entirely unlimited. After all, the speed of the galvano-magnetic telegraph already has almost no measure of time, and the speed of innervation-conduction along the primitive fiber of the nerves appears to be entirely timeless! If it is only possible, therefore, to develop the succession of conducting radiations of the primitive fibers of the brain in the most rapid possible alternation, then the speed of thinking will be extraordinarily great, since those radiations in and of themselves require no time at all. Diefe

These reflections can solve many a psychological riddle. If one observes, for example, a calculating talent elevated to the highest mastery operating, which completes in a few minutes the most difficult and intricate calculations and enumerations that take others many hours, this is by no means to be regarded as a kind of "divination," but only as the consequence of enormously rapid succession of the combinations of certain innervation-tensions of the brain, combinations that in and of themselves require no time at all. Almost the same case arises here as with the movements of the conjurer, which we no longer notice because of their great rapidity. In short, here lie hidden elucidations of the most varied kind that can completely illuminate the secret of the life of the brain in its relation to thinking, once one has grasped the essential point of the matter.

[p. 339] by no means thinks. If, on the other hand, one says that the spirit first comes into existence by thinking, or rather, that the soul first develops itself into spirit, and only insofar as it arrives at thoughts and attains cognition, then "I think, therefore I am" [cogito ergo sum] has a meaning, but it no longer means "because I think, I exist in general as something that is," but rather it means: "because I think, because my soul has now attained the power to grasp idea and appearance together in a third element, the thought, and to deal freely with this common element, I am now no longer merely idea, merely soul, but I am at the same time spirit."

I hope that it must become clear to everyone who in this sense duly observes his own inner course of development what the nature and development of cognition involves, and it will now be easier to follow, in part what attests to the lowliness and what to the height of cognition, in part what the relation of cognition to other directions of the psyche may be, in part what one may call pathological aberrations of cognition, and what relation exists between cognition and both the outward self-manifestation and the innermost idea of our essence.

As regards lower and higher cognition, it can easily be understood that the height is demonstrated all the more, the more purely and grandly that third element — the spiritual equalization between idea and appearance — that is, the world of thought that sublimates and unites idea and appearance through the symbol of language — has developed. The expression "world of thought" is very apt here, for in truth cognition has the task of gradually reconstructing the universe of the world in these its equivalents, of creating the world anew for itself, as it were, just as the soul brings itself through the first conscious thought to a

[p. 340] spiritual rebirth. Only when we no longer merely stare at appearances like the animal, but when we are able to build them up, to construct them for ourselves once more in the spiritual equivalents of words, may we say that we cognize them, just as we do not cognize ourselves until we can think about ourselves. There, therefore, where as much as possible of all that appears to the human being, and not merely the present but, in accordance with the eternal essence of the soul, the past and future as well insofar as is possible, has entered through the transfiguration of thought into the full consciousness of the spirit — there where the spirit has at the same time beheld itself and where it has beheld the divine — there that height of cognition is reached which, when it is united with purity of disposition and freedom of the will (and so perfect a cognition cannot be conceived without an equal development of disposition and will), constitutes the wisdom that has at all times been regarded as a sign of the highest development of the human soul.

As regards the low state of cognition, three very different relations are noteworthy. The first is the natural one, namely the weak cognition belonging to the first stage of the child's life. This indifference of feeling, willing, and cognizing, this simplicity of the soul, if I may so call it, is thoroughly beautiful in itself. Here the realm of thought is not yet supposed to assert itself, and a shrewd thought uttered by a small child would be something horrifying.

What is natural and beautiful here, however, becomes repugnant and morbid at a higher stage of life where the means of cognition have been developed. The case here is twofold: on the one hand, the world of thought is not the true unifying sublimate of sense-representation and idea insofar as the flat natural representation is already supposed to take the place of thought almost by itself; and on the other hand, representation and idea are indeed equally powerful in the world of thought, but they are not truly congruent, and not the homogeneous but only the heterogeneous comes together into thought. The first gives that form of lowliness of cognition which is called narrow-mindedness, stupidity, and in its highest degree imbecility; the other gives that which we designate by the name of erring, error, and in its highest degree insanity. In the first case, the spirit stares empty and devoid of ideas into the world of appearances; like the animal it is filled and governed only by whatever external thing appears to the senses at any given moment, and precisely because the deep original kernel of all appearance — the idea — is lacking to it, appearance itself remains unintelligible to it: it has nature and yet does not have it. In the other case, there does exist a train of thought, and often one vigorously stimulated; indeed, it is even presupposed that the realm of cognition has already developed to a certain height; but since ground and consequence, fundamental idea and appearance, do not truly correspond, the thought affords no satisfaction — it is not certain in itself and cannot satisfy the conscience for truth. What has been mentioned regarding error, however — that it always presupposes actual thinking — must also be noted with respect to stupidity: what does not think at all is just as little capable of stupidity as of insanity. Indeed, it is remarkable how a certain narrow-mindedness, a certain error in some direction, is scarcely ever entirely absent even in very highly developed cognition. One may indeed say that the manifold errors and the becoming-aware of ever more manifold limitations of the spirit are among the most powerful stimulants for continually increasing the growth of the spirit, just as the poet says:

[p. 341] [p. 342] "Error never leaves us, yet a higher need / Gently leads the striving spirit toward truth."

As regards the relation of cognition to feeling and to the will, it too gives occasion for very remarkable reflections. In general, people tend to regard thinking — cognition — as the enemy and destroyer of feeling, and to ascribe to the clear thinker a less lively feeling; but this is not entirely so. Cognition is indeed, as it were, the touchstone of all things, and thus also of feeling, and is in that respect very dangerous to everything we have called semblances of feeling and negative feelings. In the history of the feelings it was pointed out that grief and hatred are, in the highest sense, to be called irreconcilable with the perception of the world as the constant revelation of a divine being; and since it is precisely through thinking that cognition of the divine alone can dawn upon us, because only this third element helps us to understand the relation between idea and appearance, the deeper cognition penetrates into the essence of things, the more the ground for hatred and grief will vanish, and the more universally joyfulness and love will arise. Furthermore, it must be readily admitted that thinking itself, through its — if I may apply a Platonic expression here — colorless and substanceless being, through its in itself indifferent nature, must on the one hand somewhat temper the vehemence of particular feeling and press it more toward what we have called the zenith of the world of feeling, toward that higher reposing-in-itself of feeling; on the other hand, however, it is also undeniable that where cognition itself first discloses the greatness and beauty of that which moves the feeling, the power of feeling is itself in turn essentially furthered by cognition, just as the highest form of

[p. 343] love — love of God — can rise to the true holy glow only through the depth of cognition, and just so the highest joyfulness of one's own being emerges only from the deeper gaze into the innermost essence of the soul. Necessarily the reverse holds in the opposite case — that is, the lesser the cognition, the more easily semblances deceive the feeling and the more violent agitations of feeling they can occasion, so that then also undoubtedly the greater part of what in the world passes for grief and hatred, and often enough also what passes for joy and love, is conditioned only by a deficient cognition and therefore appears of a very transient and intrinsically slight nature. At the same time, however, it must by no means be overlooked that, precisely because the idea in itself truly lives and moves and has its being only in feeling (whereby, as was said above, feeling again stands above cognition), feeling does also possess a certain power independent of cognition, which sometimes before, sometimes after, and sometimes against all cognition determines its oscillations. There accordingly occur cases where cognition flatly asserts that feeling ought not to be stirred and — reasonably considered — actually cannot be stirred, and yet perhaps the waves of feeling surge high; and conversely, others where cognition enumerates all the reasons why an agitation of the life of feeling must here set in, and yet feeling remains perfectly cold. In short, here the strangest apparent contradictions exist in abundance. On the whole, one is always inclined to assume that feeling is in itself invariably the erring party; but although pathological aberrations of the world of feeling occur frequently enough, upon closer consideration it is beyond doubt that in such conflicts a quite considerable number of cases involve an error of cognition.

[p. 344] We consider thirdly the relation of cognition to the will, to the capacity for active counter-action.

All conscious deed is so immediately conditioned by cognition that one might become doubtful whether it is permissible to speak of a relation between will and cognition at all, or whether the two are entirely one. To convince oneself of the former, however, one need only observe, first, that there also exists an unconscious doing, a doing without cognition, and second, that the same cognition calls forth in different individuals a different energy of action. To be sure, the higher the development in which we observe the life of the soul, the more decidedly the oneness of all its radiations becomes evident, and it is absolutely impossible in our consciousness to draw any sharp boundary between cognizing, feeling, and willing; for in every moment of our conscious existence we deal deliberately — that is, through an act of will — with thoughts that carry the light of cognition within them, and these thoughts themselves are in turn always simultaneously pervaded by some coloring of feeling. That wherein, however, within the unity, the trinity of this higher life of the soul at the same time also becomes manifest, is the different directions, or if we prefer to say so, the different applications of this same life. The element in the soul that contains the condition for any cognition or any feeling becoming a deed — that is, for manifesting itself in some way through outward effect, whether as speech or as action, as an edifice of science or a work of art, or as any deed that alters the relations of human life — it is a particular and distinctive radiation of our inner being and is designated as will; while that pure immersion in the world of thoughts, every deeper inward

[p. 345] continuation of thinking without any outward effect and without any outward purpose, solely for the sake of a more complete understanding of the relation between idea and appearance, constitutes the realm of cognition; and conversely, every letting-oneself-flow-on upon the wave of the innermost changes of state of one's own idea, without determinate reflection and without action directed outward, encompasses the world of feeling. To be sure, in every direction the whole is always included, but each time in essentially altered proportions — just as in every sunbeam the full image of the sun is always contained, and yet it can appear here as white, there as blue or red light. It will now become possible, only in the history of the will, to set forth at great length how essentially this direction of the soul distinguishes itself as a particular one, and how very much it stands under the influence of cognition. Here we have above all to discuss the question whether the height of cognition is conducive to the energy of the will or not, and whether conversely the exercise of willpower can impair the height of cognition. In ordinary life, a certain antagonism between the two is often enough assumed: a very richly developed cognition is held to be incompatible with decided willpower, and conversely, of the active and practically capable person it is presupposed that a particular height of cognition could not well be attained by him.

In a certain sense this opposition may indeed be granted, but it is by no means unconditionally so, for otherwise one could oppose to it the no less and indeed unquestionably more deeply founded proposition that all higher willpower and every significant deed is possible only through great height of cognition, and conversely that truly rich cognition becomes possible only in one who is also capably vigorous in will and proves himself in deed.

[p. 346] Let us therefore see how these apparent contradictions resolve themselves! die

As regards the first point, concerning the incompatibility of higher cognition and powerful willing, one must recall a law similar to that concerning feeling — namely, that a very great height of cognition brings with it a certain reposing-in-itself, a higher indifference, a thorough and complete satisfaction of the soul from the unceasing beholding of the divine, and precisely thereby, just as it renders violent storms of feeling impossible, so too it scarcely any longer admits of a special drive to action or a special exertion of willpower for the deed that intervenes in life. This higher dissolution once again into the unconscious, or rather the attained full certainty of the equal divinity of the unconscious and the conscious, conditions a certain letting-oneself-flow-on in the universal sea of becoming and being, which, precisely because the All as totality is so divine, no longer gives the soul any compulsion to exercise individuality in particular. Herein, then, lies the higher ground of this incompatibility. There is, however, also a lower ground, drawn from action itself, which makes clear that a constant exercise of the will, determined more by external occasion, must engage the soul to such a degree that a development of higher cognition will not easily be achieved any longer. As regards now the other point — namely, the simultaneous and reciprocal heightening of will and cognition — it is in itself clear not only that the deed, the accomplishment, receives its value only through cognition, through the clear consciousness and judgment from which it is called forth; but beyond that, the higher cognition and the more complete concentration of the spirit also trains and promotes the decisiveness and power of the will. That which is commonly called presence of mind, and which everywhere, because it rests upon

[p. 347] the sharp and rapid assessment of prevailing circumstances, alone exercises true willpower, is without more mature cognition utterly impossible. On the other hand, it is also impossible for cognition to ripen truly without the spirit proving itself through outward effect. Strictly speaking, the whole of life can only receive its value for the growth of the idea insofar as it is the means by which the idea manifests itself in action; were it possible for an idea to ripen without such manifestation, then the whole of existence would be superfluous.

So much, then, for these relations! What one might call pathological aberrations of cognition is really already included in what has been mentioned, in considering lower cognition, as narrow-mindedness and error, and in their extreme degrees, as imbecility and madness. Special notice is required, however, of how powerfully in this regard the influence of the unconscious manifests itself in conscious life. It has been shown in many ways how the highest blossom of conscious spirit is always to be regarded only as the ideal center and summit of all that previously, and simultaneously, takes place in the realm of the unconsciousness of the idea, and how therefore continuously even the purest self-revelation of conscious spirit in cognition is conditioned by a pure attunement of the unconscious idea governing the existence of the organism. Just as the center of gravity of a body changes and shifts when one side of it is suddenly loaded more than the other, so too the pure ray of cognition — which in its own inner contentment and its inner joyfulness bears the seal of its truthfulness — can no longer be conceived as such when more significant disturbances have upset the equilibrium of unconscious organic life. Just as the eye in jaundice no longer receives the perception of the world in proper coloring, so is the cognition of human and divine things most variously altered and displaced by disrupted harmony in the regions of unconscious organic life. In this process, the strangest relations then arise, which have caused and will always cause infinite dispute over right-cognition in humanity. For it is entirely natural that to each person, precisely because cognition is always that highest ideal midpoint of his individual existence, his own cognition must at first appear as the especially and truly well-founded one, however much it may perhaps be displaced in and of itself; and only rarely, and especially only when feeling can at least divine, rather than clearly perceive, one's own abnormal state, does some doubt then come to the spirit — precisely because of the higher divine nature of the idea in and of itself — whether this cognition of his is the right one or not; and in this sense one may say that the ancient question "What is truth?" is answered only by the demand of that Delphic inscription: "Know thyself!"

[p. 348] It is certainly an important task of applied psychology to attend to the various forms in which these aberrations of the conscious spirit, resulting from disturbed equilibrium of the unconscious sphere, can and do occur; the discord and varied cognition in humanity can be understood only in this way, and from the dark outlook on life and beclouded cognition of the hypochondriac, from the frivolous grasp of human affairs and inconstant cognition of the sanguine youth, to the monomania of the sufferer tormented by disordered life of the portal vein system and cerebral congestions, and to<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> This will be discussed more fully in the doctrine of health and disease of the soul.

[p. 349] complete madness (a very apt word for the displaced spiritual center of gravity of conscious life) in cases of malforming or inflamed brain — from those hints the understanding might well be elucidated. To be sure, it is necessary in this connection to recall what I have noted here and elsewhere about the different psychic signature of the individual organic (unconscious) systems, for only thereby will one make clear to oneself how, in this or that disturbance of life, the spiritual center of gravity — that higher midpoint of the life of the soul resting in cognition — is displaced from its proper position and can be, as it were, shifted now in this or that direction, where cognition ceases to be genuine cognition. As I have said, however, ascertaining this cognition that no longer holds itself in its right and pure center, and its relation to the true and highest cognition — whether in other souls or in one's own — is always one of the most difficult tasks of the higher art of life, and seldom are tasks of this kind resolved without the most manifold contradictions and disputes. Attention to these contradictions and irresolvable disputes in the realm of cognition — the basis of the fact that the aforementioned question "What is truth?" will never be entirely and fully decided — is precisely for that reason so important, because it is capable of casting a particularly bright light on the nature of cognition itself. For if through cognition we became aware of the idea immediately (that is, without having to translate it into those equivalents of words), such a fluctuation would certainly be impossible; but precisely because cognizing through thoughts is only a combining of various such equivalents, because it is a kind of symbolic calculus in which the relation of ideas can be discovered, while in feeling the state of the idea alone is immediately intuited, the former moves in so wide and indefinable a circle. Who will demonstrate, through the workings of intelligence alone, the nullity of an absolute idealism? Who will find the certainty of God through mere syllogisms? Who will find, through cognizing thoughts alone, the truthfulness of one's own soul and its peculiar divine being? One must always remind oneself of such things in order to become convinced that true knowledge can only proceed from the whole of the soul.

[p. 350] There remains for us now the relation of cognition to the outward self-manifestation and the inner essence of the soul to consider. In the former respect, a twofold influence of cognition is to be considered: an unconscious and a conscious one. It is remarkable enough and seems almost a contradiction that the sphere of cognition can also operate unconsciously; and yet it is so, and it is explained by the fact that cognition itself is never entirely and universally illuminated by the light of consciousness, but rather, as was discussed earlier, even in the conscious spirit by far the greatest part constantly rests in the unconscious. To that extent, therefore, the sphere of cognition also has a side that perpetually passes over into that of the absolutely unconscious life of the soul, and in this respect it has accordingly been mentioned earlier that a quiet but unceasing influence of the state of cognition upon the unconscious formative life is undeniably present. Just as commonly, with the expression of a thinking face, a thoughtful brow, a certain peculiar modification of the organism is indicated that symbolically reveals the secret processes of the spirit, so it can soon become clear to the more keenly observing eye with what power the higher light of the soul shines even into the lower dark regions of life. For this reason, on the one hand, a certain fine shimmer and breath of a nobler existence spreads over the entire exterior of a more highly developed human being, which impresses upon the form and movement of his body a character that, remarkably, even those less developed in cognition immediately feel as a distinguished and particular one. And while one may on the one hand say that it is absolutely impossible for what we call higher beauty of the body ever to develop unless a higher key has been struck in the fundamental idea of this existence in general, it is on the other hand curious that, conversely, a plastically quite consummate beauty seems seldom to permit that exercise of cognition in a spiritual manner to an outstanding degree — perhaps precisely because cognition has, as it were, gone under in the crystallization of the unconscious, whereas in a less outstandingly beautifully articulated organization the living power of the soul and the spirit will be a less bound one, one that has never entirely gone under in the formal. The manner in which a certain development and direction of cognition manifests itself in this or that region of the organism is difficult to trace and will remain one of the most intricate tasks of physiology in the future as well. Here one must for the present hold to the fact that the unconscious formative life of the soul stands in the closest relation to that unconscious region into which periodically all cognizing spiritual life sinks back, and that when the blossom of the latter — the conscious spirit — is ennobled or dulled, the former must necessarily be essentially modified thereby as well. Moreover, the thought expressed above about the relation of bodily beauty to cognition explains not only why what is called plastically beautiful perfection of form may rather be called a spatial crystallization of an intrinsically significant idea, while for the free beautiful conduct of the idea with cognizing thought

[p. 351] [p. 352] that submersion in the plastically persisting beautiful is less appropriate; but it also gives a closer indication of why certain peculiarities, directed partly more toward reaction, partly more toward production, are utterly incompatible with a higher cognition. Everyone feels immediately why the figure of the Farnese Hercules is quite impossible to unite with a soul that has won the higher philosophical cognition in the spirit, and significant enough in this regard is already the myth of Heracles himself, who with all his rough might nevertheless remained the slave of a weak man and could only carry out assigned labors. The same applies to bodily productivity. It is extraordinary what the seer-art of the poets has already stated with certainty on this subject, without any closer scientific construction. One of the sharpest passages on this is the well-known one in Shakespeare's Julius Caesar, where Caesar says of Cassius:

"Let me have men about me that are fat, / Sleek-headed men, and such as sleep o' nights. / Yond Cassius has a lean and hungry look; / He thinks too much: such men are dangerous."

For who would believe at first glance that a stronger or weaker deposition of fat and cellular tissue could be related to lower or higher cognition? And yet it is so! Indeed, what else is the reason that the female soul is proportionally less suited to higher cognition than the male, other than that the female organism is more destined for unconscious bodily productivity than the male.

Even more decidedly and even more clearly than upon the formative processes of unconscious life, cognition acts upon those manifestations of life of the unconscious that issue in feelings and movements. A higher cognition ennobles and elevates; a lower cognition degrades and drives into the common everything that belongs to formative life.

One could almost say that cognition relates to these processes as the human being relates to the animals. Just as the human individuality, as I showed earlier, is able not only through its influence to refine and beautify the form of the animal, but also to awaken the idea in animal life itself and impress a higher seal upon the entire being and doing of these creatures, so approximately does a higher, purer intelligence act upon the movements of respiration (the sound of the voice, and so forth), as well as upon the processes of nutrition (eating and drinking) and upon the processes of sexual life — indeed, upon this last quite especially — in a manner just as elevating and refining, in a word consecrating, as a lower cognition acts in a degrading and deteriorating way.

[p. 353] Here, incidentally, is the point where the unconscious influence of cognition passes over into the conscious, and where alongside the entirely involuntary and quite spontaneous and unintentional overflowing of higher cognition into the lower bodily manifestations of the psyche, a deliberate governing and a kind of education of the lesser by the higher commences. Everything that in the higher sense may be called cosmetics — that is, the education of the body toward beauty; everything that is called hygiastics — that is, the education of the body toward health; and gymnastics, or the education toward beauty and strength of movement — all of this now falls entirely within the domain of the completely conscious and deliberate influence of the cognizing spirit upon the bodily condition of the human being. What extraordinary results the influence of these three directions of cognition can produce in the development of the higher human, and how wretched and pitiable human existence collapses when these rays are entirely withdrawn from it, the history of humanity teaches on every page. The Greeks are here again the first who with full, fresh

[p. 354] draughts drank from such springs, and it is a task of humanity, as it ever more highly educates itself toward the realm of truth, to reunite in later periods all these advantages with much that in that favored people had not yet been able to break through.

So much, then, for what we could comprise under the influence of cognition upon the outward self-manifestation of the idea. What can be said about the influence of cognition upon the essence, upon the being-in-itself of the idea, coincides with what was set forth earlier about the growth of the idea. In no case can it remain hidden from the knowledgeable person that cognition — that is, the becoming-aware of the relation of ideas among themselves and to appearance — is the first and most essential condition for an inner heightening, a higher growth of one's own idea to become possible. One need only recall the meaning given above to "I think, therefore I am" [cogito ergo sum], and consider that the development of the soul into spirit first takes place only in that moment when, through that conduct with the wondrous equivalents of the idea and its sensible revelation which we call "thinking," the soul is first put in a position to learn to apprehend other ideas as well as its own; and one cannot be in doubt as to what a powerful influence cognition must have upon the development of the being-in-itself of the soul. Neglected cognition cannot but diminish and weaken; heightened and ever-clarifying cognition cannot but elevate and strengthen the energy of the idea — the highest element of the human being. And so may all this suffice for now to have made clear the history of cognition.

[p. 355]

#### 8. Bur Geschichte des Willens — 8. On the History of the Will

A willing, an act of choice, and at its highest a free will, become thinkable in the human soul only because, through the unconscious workings of formation, bodily organs develop for it that make a determinate doing, an outward effect, possible. Only to a soul to which the possibility of a determinate doing is given can a determinate will belong; the strength of the will therefore increases first with the development and strengthening of these structures, and it diminishes with their weakness or atrophy. Physiology demonstrates that what must be regarded as the bearers of the active element in the organism are, primarily, the primitive fibers of the nerves conducting the centrifugal current of innervation, and secondarily the organs of movement and sense stimulated by these currents. So long as these structures — as we find in the lowest organisms and in every earliest embryonic state of the higher ones — still rest in an incompletely differentiated, indifferent cellular substance, there is no question of choice, much less of free will; rather, only with necessity does the immediate reaction follow upon an applied stimulus — a contraction or the like. Only where the differentiation of all substances is complete and in the development of the nervous elementary substance of the brain, which conditions consciousness, the actual independence of the organism is established, does cognition emerge between stimulus and counter-action, as was shown earlier, and from this moment onward the counter-action need no longer unconditionally follow the stimulus; and from now on, stimulated merely by cognition or by feeling, a willing can assert itself — in a word, only from here onward is there any question of choice, and at its highest, of a free will.

[p. 356] For this reason, as was already noted in the doctrine of cognition, the will has sometimes been held to be so united with cognition that one did not wish to declare it permissible to separate the two. To this it must be replied that the mere fact that one thing is the condition of the other can never prove that both must be one, and that here, in the relation of cognition to the will, a distinct being clearly enough emerges from the fact that both in their relative height and energy by no means condition each other as standing on equal footing; for willpower can decline greatly under the same cognition (one who is ill, one exhausted by blood loss, will no longer have the same power of will as before, although he is still the same cognizing being), and the energy of voluntary willing can be very great when cognition has quite declined (for example, in madness). After all, it has also been shown how the relation of will to cognition proper to each soul expresses itself very distinctly in the respective condition of the nerve-structures of the brain, and even thereby again, craniologically considered, in the cranial vertebrae, so that strength of the will ordinarily reveals itself decidedly through stronger development of the hindbrain and spinal cord and of the occipital vertebra.

Hereby, moreover, it must also have become clear that, just as there is no question of will without a developed consciousness, so too the developed will is unable to extend its influence into the absolutely unconscious realm of the soul — or rather, that it is capable of reaching it only indirectly and mediately, as the details concerning the influence of the conscious upon the unconscious life of the soul have already been set forth in an earlier section. In this connection, however, it must not be forgotten that the will also finds very definite limits already within the circle of actually conscious life. Not only can we not carry out without limit what we will, but even to will continuously is denied us on account of the ever-recurring sinking back of all conscious life into the unconscious. It therefore leads to peculiar reflections when one considers how this fettering of the will then extends even into the dream-world, and nothing is more frequent in this night-side of the life of the soul than some futile striving, a wanting-to-free-oneself and not-being-able-to, an anxious oppression under the impossibility of liberating oneself, and so on. If one attends, I say, well to all these strange phenomena of our inner life, it can scarcely be doubtful that already in that myth of the fettered Prometheus, which moves us so peculiarly, nothing else is meant than this struggle of the soul, this torment of a will that is, after all, hampered and bound in so many respects. Hence the high pathos of this myth, in which it becomes pictorially intuitable how the peculiar divine foresighted spirit of the human being, in its bondage to the rigid law of nature, labors in anguish within, until through a higher intervention the flesh-rending eagle is slain and, in the development of the spirit to the height of perfected cognition, in the free beholding of the divine, Prometheus is released from his fetters and led among the blessed.

[p. 357] If we wish here first of all to concern ourselves with the developmental history of the will, this can only be understood as its emergence from the initially entirely will-less state of the soul, and its gradual formation toward free will. In this, however, a more precise determination is first required of what we are entitled to call free will. The preceding reflections on the manifold limitations and hindrances of human willing already<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> "Deep in brooding, I gnaw my heart sore!" says Prometheus in Aeschylus (Prometheus Bound, v. 435).

[p. 358] show that a completely perfect freedom — that is, unlimitedness — of the will is entirely unthinkable; we must therefore begin by associating with the word "freedom" a different sense than that of absolute arbitrariness, and this can be none other than the sense of being-freed from what is not appropriate. The proper meaning of freedom of the will would accordingly be: freedom from a stimulation of willing by the merely accidental, inessential — that is, ungodly — and instead a complete satisfaction of willing in the direction of the primordially essential, the eternally self-same, the thoroughly divine. From this it follows, then, that just as all voluntary willing already presupposes some kind of conscious cognition, the true freedom of the will can only be conceived insofar as the most complete consciousness and the purest cognition of the divine have been attained, so that only the direction toward this divine becomes the determining element of the will.

A closer observation of the human course of development can now show through how many different stages, through manifold forms of willing — forms we call now desire, or when it appears with vehemence, craving, or which are called self-will and caprice, the less this willing is illumined by cognition — the soul educates itself toward the actual freedom of the will. In all these different stages of the will, cognition is the decided regulator, and much of what we expressed in the preceding section about the development of cognition therefore also finds its application here. But even if cognition is the properly directing, legislating element of the will, it is by no means the only stimulating element, the only determinant of the energy of the will. In this respect, rather, that unconscious element once again asserts itself with power which, on the lowest level,

[p. 359] where along with every kind of consciousness the will too was lacking, had alone determined all counter-action; but it asserts itself not as the unconscious simply, but in the form of feeling. Essentially, however, it is those feelings that we have for this very reason called the active ones — hatred and love — which, and indeed again in very different stages and degrees, stimulate the will and make it appear now stronger, now weaker. Even if, therefore, the saying of that Frenchman — "To love is to will, and to will is to love" [Aimer c'est vouloir et vouloir c'est aimer] — is too general, the close relation between the active feelings and the energy of the will is nonetheless utterly evident. Indirectly, to be sure, the influence of cognition is also here unmistakable, since it depends on cognition whether those feelings are moved by an archetype or by semblances, and whether more and more the higher, the positive feeling alone — love — can assert its determining influence, or whether hatred and grief are to exert a determining influence through similar lesser feelings. Since, however, as we have found, the freedom of the will can be nothing other than its freedom from every inappropriate determination, and its being ever more, and ultimately solely, determined by the unconditionally highest — the divine — and since, further, the divine cannot be recognized without at the same time exciting the love of the soul to such a degree that only in it does this primordial feeling reach its highest aim and the entire fulfillment of its essence, we now indeed understand why the actual perfection of the will must be conditioned just as much by love as by cognition. We therefore cannot conceal from ourselves that the will, for its true perfection, requires love just as much as cognition, and we therefore explain that an act of will — a deed — that is weighed and decided only by cognition<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> D'Arpentigny, Chirognomonie.

[p. 360] will always stand one level lower than an act of will and a deed that, in addition to cognition, were at the same time determined by a higher love.

In general, however, much that is remarkable emerges in this determining influence of feeling upon the will; and when we recall the immediacy of feeling, by virtue of which it in a certain sense rises above cognition, while through its obscurity, its often wavering and highly individual character it in turn significantly subordinates itself to cognition, we well recognize how a willing and doing stimulated solely by immediate feeling can on the one hand come forth with just as much greatness and beauty as in other cases it will appear as something precipitate, unbeautiful, and base. A remarkable circle from the unconscious through consciousness back again to the unconscious here manifests itself in a decided manner. On the lowest level of the still entirely unconscious soul, the stimulus of sense-sensation brings about the reaction — the deed. With developed self-consciousness, on the other hand, the sense-sensation is measured and weighed against thought, and thereupon the will decides, deliberating, in what manner the promotion of a state more appropriate to the soul — a certain happiness — can more securely be attained. With the inner elevation of the life of feeling, which in its essence is always conditioned especially from the unconscious, finally, the sense-representation that presents the situation for the decision of the will is at first likewise weighed in consciousness, but the will itself is conditioned by the heightened feeling, and thus essentially once again by means of the unconscious.

Just as it therefore depends on the fact that the highest stirring of the conscious spirit must always revert into the unconscious, and the finest and purest articulation in the

[p. 361] cognizing spirit is all the more conditioned by the preceding unconscious the more the spirit is what we call a genius, a primal spirit, so this circle also always acts back upon the proper appreciation of the different acts of will. For indeed it is perhaps to be granted for the greater number of cases that the deed which, without being determined by immediate sense-perception and emotional stirring, is willed and done solely through deliberation — that is, inner spiritual movement according to the standard of higher cognition — stands higher than the deed called forth immediately by sense-perception and feeling. Yet it must not be forgotten that, however highly in general that free willing may stand above the last-mentioned willing, which to that extent becomes once again a kind of compulsion, there exists yet above all free, deliberate willing a still higher willing that is compelled by the immediate self-announcement of the idea — that is, of the divine — one that everywhere asserts itself with a higher inner necessity, and from which the highest deeds of the spirit have always proceeded and always will proceed. This last, namely, is the kind of will that is moved by a higher unknown; it is the kind that has most often been named inspiration — divine enthusiasm; the kind that, with an inner force of which no further account can be given, irresistibly urges that the goal of willing, hovering now more brightly, now more dimly before cognition, be attained; the kind of willing, finally, that through the co-operation of feeling also in a remarkable way gives and heightens the power for accomplishment, and thus explains the already ancient saying: "The Lord is mighty in the weak." That, therefore, with respect to the will, too, there is such a progression from the immediately "compelled" to the free will, and then to the will that once again surrenders its freedom in a higher unconditional compulsion, reminds us thoroughly of the remark communicated much earlier, which referred to the fact that all ability becomes art only by ceasing to be an entirely conscious ability — a remark we illustrated by the example of the pianist, who only then truly and completely possesses the art of playing when the individual finger-movements and motions, each of which was at first separately willed and executed with consciousness, have become entirely unconscious again, and appear, as it were, involuntarily only when the idea of the music comes alive in the soul.

[p. 362] In these phenomena of the life of the soul, moreover, if we consider it more closely, only the relation manifests itself again that also repeats itself in manifold ways in nature in other respects — namely, the relation of a partial organism to a higher total organism.

Just as on the living sea-pen the individual animal-blossom has certain stirrings, sensings, and movements of its own, while others are governed only by the life of the entire polyp-colony, so too in a far higher sphere of life the conscious spirit of the human being has only partly its cognition, feeling, and willing for itself; in the other part, however, it shares these stirrings with the sphere of life of humanity, indeed with that of the world and all the divine revealing itself therein. Here the remarkable relation emerges that the conscious spirit of the human being on the one hand comes to development, to independence, and thus also to free will, only through communal life with humanity, and that yet on the other hand it is precisely in this communal life that the element is given whereby its independence is, as it were, once more suspended, and with respect to the will, a higher compulsion intervenes in the apparent freedom of choice, and does so all the more

[p. 363] the higher the soul has developed, and the more it was from the outset endowed. This latter circumstance is quite especially important and has hitherto by no means been sufficiently heeded. All too commonly it has been held that higher development must demonstrate the most perfect possible self-determination, a will as free as possible in this respect, and people have not sufficiently considered that what has been done that is great in humanity has not come about so much through free choice and self-created purpose, but according to a certain higher compulsion, according to an undeniable necessity — called now inspiration, now the compulsion of the daemon, now the urge of genius. What is certain, on the other hand, is that this inner freedom of the will — the having-become-conscious of a voluntary self-determination — must have preceded as a preparatory stage, if that higher compulsion is to enter in the right way and involuntary self-determination is to take place in a higher sense. In an individuality that does not yet recognize itself and is therefore not yet capable of free self-determination, a higher compulsion not merely conditioned by the idea of one's own existence can also operate, and does in the end always operate, since any one-sided development guided solely by one's own idea is impossible; but the result will be a different one, and essentially lesser in personal value, than where in the truly free spirit the higher divine necessity of a determinate direction of the will unconditionally asserts itself.

It remains at present to investigate, first, the relation of the will to the unconscious self-manifestation of the organization; secondly, to consider what is to be regarded as pathological aberration of the will; and finally, to take into consideration the effect of the will upon the inner growth of the idea.

[p. 364] In the first regard, one must recall how already in the unconscious self-manifestation of the idea, in the development of all the organs given for making the inner willing outwardly manifest, the relation is expressed that the reactive, self-determining side of our psychic essence must have upon the organization. Just as it will decidedly be the case that where, in the first unconscious formation, the organs of the reactive side are more strongly present in their disposition, the energy of the will in the spirit will also emerge more powerfully, so too, conversely, much exercise of the will will elevate and further develop these sides of the organization. Special consideration is required here, however, of the relation of the will to the reactive side in the sense organs; the eye in particular, this organ so thoroughly imbued with soul, tends to be of special significance in this regard. This activity of the eye is essential to all seeing, for without it no determinate seeing is possible; this activity, however, reveals itself to the observer especially in the gaze, and for precisely this reason the eye is at once, just as it announces feeling and cognition, also a mirror of the higher or lower willpower. For precisely this reason, too, there lies in the gaze of the eye that strange daemonic power, that element so determining and so completely dominating of so many others, indeed even of animals. For this reason the eye is often that which most immediately proclaims the will of the human being, and it may well happen that one who might still resist the words and deeds of a person of powerful will can be completely overcome by a single glance. Indeed, I must here call attention to the remarkable and at the same time antagonistic gradation that, according to the higher or lower nature of the human being, manifests itself in the ways in which his will reveals itself in the organization, inasmuch as what in the lower nature expresses itself in strong spatial movements through the

[p. 365] mechanics of the general skeletal and muscular system, in the higher nature announces itself through the governance of the finer musculature of respiration, in speech, and finally steps forth commanding outwardly already through the power of the soulful gaze. Whoever possesses the higher will then make all the less use of the lower.

If one then considers in detail how the influence of the will and its now stronger, now weaker exertion manifests itself upon the organic development and elaboration of the structures particularly subject to it, one notices once more a remarkable relation between conscious and unconscious life. The further formation and organic development of these structures — for example, of the muscle fiber and motor nerves — is entirely and solely, with respect to the particular micrological processes of growth, a matter of unconscious life, and therefore entirely independent of the processes of will as such, since it has already been noted above that to add or take away anything directly from our formative life lies beyond the reach of the conscious will. Nevertheless, in the case just mentioned, we see that the will in itself imperceptibly and gradually acts upon organic formation at all times, causing a voluntarily more exercised structure to grow more than an unexercised one, and so forth; and so we find here as everywhere that most limitations are only apparent, and that ultimately everything moves in but one single stream of becoming. Indeed, even the boundary of the direct influence of the will upon certain regions of formative life is nowhere an entirely absolute and immovable one; for human beings have been observed who exerted an influence of the will upon their heartbeat, who could voluntarily bring about certain secretions, and so forth.

What we can further adduce as pathological aberrations of the will includes, on the one hand, only reactions without sufficient guidance of cognition or that are inappropriate to it, and on the other, reactions that are too much and too directly determined by the unconscious where they should be determined only by the conscious. We call it childish and self-willed when in the more mature human being impulses of the will appear that are estranged from the light of higher cognition — when, for example, unsuitable and inappropriate foods are craved with vehemence, when inclination or aversion immediately break out into violent, inappropriate impulses of the will, and so forth — and this for no other reason than that in the child this higher cognition is still lacking, so that impulses of the will must continually appear which, unregulated and inappropriate as they are, cannot be otherwise here, but in more mature cognition appear as morbid. In all these respects, therefore, the original inner unity of cognition and will emerges with the greatest distinctness.

[p. 366] The other kind of irregular impulses of the will, which quite especially deserve the name of pathological aberrations because they tend to occur only in generally morbid states, are those that, where consciousness alone should determine, are determined by the unconscious and do not obey the conscious. To this category belong all involuntary movements called forth by the stimulus of disease, which we call convulsive, and of which one form especially deserves mention here because, within the unity of the spirit, it points very distinctly to that divergent radiation, or certain duality, which we distinguish as will and cognition — indeed, which in part also gives occasion to recognize a particular difference between the will and the execution of the will. To this category belong the cases when either, quite contrary to the judgment of cognition, a willing emerges that in the normal state could never [p. 367] Der

follow upon this judgment of cognition (for example, when despite clear cognition of one's relation to children or loved ones, an unconquerable willing to kill them emerges), as well as those in which, upon a willing of this or that kind, a quite different actual execution follows (when, for example, instead of one word being spoken, another is uttered, or instead of walking in one direction, the feet involuntarily move in another direction). The former condition is always the consequence of general mental illness, and these conditions require a special consideration from the whole, of which more later. Here the mention of it was indispensable only insofar as it shows what strange displacements between the most essential radiations of the spirit can indeed occur. The latter condition always rests on some disturbance of the statics of the innervation currents. In all of this, it is a very remarkable phenomenon that the spirit can actually experience such confusions within the realm of its own structures. For if one considers what a series of very complicated nerve-currents and muscular movements the utterance of only a single word presupposes, then for a word involuntarily inserting itself in place of the intended word to be spoken, just as regulated a sequence of movements is necessary as for uttering the actually intended word. Nevertheless, this inner "reaching for the wrong thing" occurs not infrequently and is always to be adduced as one of the strangest abnormalities of the will.

We hear further of a will that is too weak, of one that is too violent, and of an evil will, and have to consider whether these too must find a place in the consideration of the pathological will. More closely considered, these states of the soul belong by far for the most part to the spiritual direction of cognition and of feeling. This holds most decidedly for the evil will — a cognition guided by hatred, which with the utmost circumspection deliberates how the soul, contrary to its own true happiness, may bring about the misfortune and harm of others, and when it has reached a conclusion on the matter, wills and executes it quite in the manner of a higher, reason-governed action. As for the too-weak and too-strong will, a distinction must here be made as to whether one has in view the measure of spiritual firmness and freedom, or merely the power of reaction. The former falls entirely within the domain of cognition and is utterly inseparable from it. The inner power of willing to persist in what has once been recognized as right, and to reject what is thoroughly inappropriate, can only be determined by the vitality of the clearest consciousness and the fullest conviction; and likewise in the opposite case, it is the weakness of the understanding and the imperfection of discrimination which in many cases and in many persons appears simultaneously as weakness of the will, inasmuch as it permits no sufficient cognition of either the right or the wrong, thus removing the ground for any consistent conduct of life and presenting the individual as a weak reed bent hither and thither by every wind. To be sure, however, in measuring the strength or weakness of the will, there is also a second side that depends on the measure of the organically grounded capacity for reaction. The same individual, and at the same degree of cognition, may in the moment when he is conscious of his full inner health and strength have the most decided power to will and accomplish only what is right and appropriate, and in another moment, when the strength of the organism is broken — after a severe loss of blood, or under the feeling of illness, or in the greatest general exhaustion —

[p. 368] [p. 369] the power of the will is also broken; the soul allows the inappropriate to pass over it, and it is no longer possible for it to strive with earnestness and consistency toward what has been recognized as right and appropriate. Occurrences of this kind then point again with great definiteness to acknowledging the will as a quite particular radiation of our life of the soul, and the knowledge of them is very important for understanding and explaining many an otherwise problematic case of life.

The last thing that should occupy us in considering the processes of the will is the influence of this active, reactive side of the life of the soul upon the promotion of the inner growth of the idea. I have already said earlier that the whole life of the human being can have value only insofar as it leaves behind some result — that is, some re-attunement, some further development of that eternal and divine element which we have designated as the fundamental idea of our existence, or the idea simply. If we did not or could not assume that at the close of a fully developed human existence that something — that higher divine image of our being among all being, that idea to whose power alone we must attribute the formation and development of our entire organism — had become in some respect different, more mature, better, or also worse, more blessed or also more unblessed than it was at the beginning of its thus human and temporal self-manifestation, then this entire existence would be a semblance, a nothing in itself, a lie. It has further been shown that the actual existence of the spirit — that is, that mode of being in which now also a promotion or hindrance, a growth or a regression of the idea, may be assumed — only commences with the wonder of consciousness. I think, therefore I am [Cogito ergo sum]. This existence now, this true being of the spirit, is from the

[p. 370] will inseparable, for only so long am I truly conscious of myself as I deliberately — that is, through continuous activity of the will — think; and precisely for this reason it would be thoroughly erroneous to restrict the doctrine of willing and its actual execution only to action directed outward, since every self-conscious thought, every freely and deliberately stimulated thinking, always and in every case comes about only through an act of will. The more powerful, therefore, the will is, the more clearly it governs all representational life, and the more it causes the inner life of the soul to move continuously according to the highest results of cognition, the more does the soul increase in inner sovereignty, and the more does the energy of the idea heighten. Likewise, the same necessarily declines with willpower that is not at all or only weakly exercised; the unconscious again becomes overwhelming, and the spirit, the less it thinks, the less it exists. But it is not thinking alone in which the will manifests itself and through which the growth of the fundamental idea of our existence can be promoted or hindered; the spirit proves its peculiar, divinely creative power especially through the effects and transformations that proceed from it upon the world in which it appears — it proves it through the deed of life, not merely through the deed of thought. How very much, however, through the deed of life the innermost human being, the fundamental idea of our existence, increases, his own consciousness must tell everyone who has had the good fortune of completing some greater, capable work; it must tell him that in his deed he himself has become a different person; and just as through the free deed of the woman, when she gives herself to the man that he may know her, something in her own existence has forever become different, so the man truly becomes that which we call a man in the full sense of the word only when a free deed that truly

[p. 371] deserves this name has succeeded for him. All of this, then, has already been more closely considered where the growth of the idea was discussed.

If one, moreover, considers more closely the effects of the will upon the innermost being of the human person, one may well accept as an illuminating parallel a certain correspondence of this spiritual relation with the effect of muscular movement upon the outward form of the human being. Just as sustained and significant exercise of the musculature gives the contours of the form a certain firmness and ultimately even hardness, whereas a very little-used muscular system seems, as it were, to soften the outlines of the form and readily occasions yielding, less determinate forms, so it is with the influence of the will upon the inner spiritual formation of the human being, upon what is designated as his character, as his personality. Spirits that think continuously, strictly, and deliberately, and even more those who, as one says, devote themselves continually to practical purposes — that is, who constantly exert themselves to pursue the outward deed of life — gradually acquire a certain hardness and robustness that, often before they themselves notice it, makes them qualitatively other beings, robs them of the softness of feeling, and the more they actually plunge into the deed of life, ultimately deprives them also of the deed of thought. Conversely, spirits that present themselves as almost without will, or who deliberately forgo the exercise of the will for a long time, will assume a softness in their self-manifestation, will sink more and more into the region of feeling, and will increasingly lose every power of resistance. Proof enough of the power the will has to gradually re-attune and transform the innermost core of the human being. The highest task of the true art of life can therefore only be to continually live forth the perfect harmony of

[p. 372] feeling, cognition, and will, attuned by pure self-consciousness and consciousness of God, and thereby to promote and attain an ever higher development of the fundamental idea of our existence.

### h. Von dem Verhältniß der Seele zu andern Seelen, zur Natur und zu Gott — h. On the Relation of the Soul to Other Souls, to Nature, and to God

Among the many strange representations to which people have attempted to give a scientific coloring, surely the strangest is that of the most consistent idealism, which takes the I, of which alone we have immediate experience, as the sole existing thing, and accepts the entire world of appearances not as something existing in itself but only as a representation of this I. I have already mentioned earlier that this worldview, held with full consistency, is truly irrefutable in itself; and that this is so could clearly show how little that radiation of the soul which we call cognition is alone capable of grasping the actual truth completely. The innermost conscience for truth of every purely and unselfconsciously unfolded soul, the deciding that lies still deeper than mere cognition, will nevertheless at once recognize that assumption as thoroughly erroneous; it will rather give certainty that infinite ideas in general, and also infinite ideas destined for development as soul, fill the world, and that our own innermost fundamental essence is only a particular monad in the circle of that infinite multiplicity — that multiplicity, indeed that infinity, in which it has pleased a highest eternal mystery to reveal itself.

In the history of cognition it has now also been shown in detail how we come, although we can have immediate experience only of the state of our own idea, to know of, or rather to infer the existence of, other ideas than our own. The most wondrous, highly complex process of this inference repeats itself in our spirit at every conscious moment, and we deal with it as unconsciously as, say, the thousand complicated nerve-currents and muscular contractions repeat themselves in the arm of the pianist while he plays, and while he, without specially representing to himself those individual acts of will, thinks only of the course of the melody and of not a single one of those thousandfold movements. Having now arrived at the point where we are reflectively considering precisely the relation of our own monad to other ideas, and where the manifold relations, now hindering, now promoting, that arise toward other souls are to become clear, it seems in any case necessary to bring before ourselves once more in brief the whole process in its essential moments by which we receive notice of the existence of an outer world, indeed of an outer world determined by particular ideas. Let one therefore recall, first, that everything we call a perceived outer world is indeed only the re-attunement produced in the unconscious material of our organism by the conflict of an outer world with it — a re-attunement that is at once, through a relation to the conscious sphere mediated by the nerve, elevated to a representation. Secondly, let one bear in mind that it is only through a certain particular sequence of those re-attunements and of our representations of them — a sequence for whose particularity and distinctiveness we find no determinate necessity given within ourselves — that we are compelled to attribute to these thus acquired representations the thought of something not merely belonging to us but operating from elsewhere and determining us, and accordingly to assume an actually existing outer world, external to our being, that is related to us. Thirdly and finally, let us recall that only that distinct concordance that certain externally stimulated representations, in their peculiar sequence, deeply cause us to feel with the lawfulness of our own spirit, can determine us to foresee that those influences, too, were not merely accidental in themselves, but that underlying them, as underlying our own innermost being, is a peculiar inner necessity, a particular inner divine element — in a word, a distinctive idea different from our own. If one thinks through, I say, this whole, so very complex process, one will soon recognize how peculiar and how manifoldly conditioned the operation is that gives rise in us, for example, to the thought that there really exists outside our soul another soul that acts upon us, that there really stands before us another thinking, feeling, willing human being. It may then excite a certain astonishment to realize that the spirit is capable of dealing with all these so complicated relations quite as with a simplicity. I see the kindred human individuality beside me, act upon it and let it act upon me, and ordinarily by no means think of all that was necessary for this quite determinate cognition of the existence of a foreign individuality to dawn upon me, even though I really never immediately feel anything but myself as something quite re-attuned. Once, however, I have made all of this perfectly clear to myself in this way, I may then also — as it were leaping over all these intricate processes — immediately feel and hold fast the relation of my soul to others, and it may now become my task to consider more carefully and set forth more clearly the distinctive character, the significance, and the influence of this relation.

[p. 373] [p. 374] But it is a truth already expressed multiple times in these pages: the development of the soul into

[p. 375] spirit, the emergence of higher consciousness, and, in a word, the actual becoming-human, is conceivable and possible only under the influence of humanity -- that is, under the influence of one or several other souls upon the individual; and so I need now only further remark that with these words is at once designated the entire immense weight that the relation to other souls perpetually has and must have for the soul itself.

If one now wishes to make clear to oneself in detail the particular manner in which this relation of soul to soul is established, one must immediately look back upon the everywhere significant distinction that all life of the soul manifests itself partly in unconscious and partly in conscious activity, and if we consider this properly, four kinds of relation between souls will at once emerge from it: 1, interaction of the conscious of one soul with the conscious of other souls; 2, interaction of the unconscious of one soul with the unconscious of others; 3, interaction of the unconscious of one soul with the conscious of others; 4, interaction of the unconscious of one soul with the unconscious of others. The manifoldness of stirrings, processes, encounters, elevations, attractions, repulsions, and depressions that occur in these various relations is truly immense, for all history and all human life plays out essentially only in these various radiations. It cannot therefore be our task here to go too much into detail, but only to draw the attention of the observer of human relations to the many different threads from which the relation between soul and soul is spun -- a relation that one is often inclined to regard simply and straightforwardly as a simple one.

1. The relation between souls from conscious to conscious is that which is commonly presented as the most essential, and by some unphysiological psychologists indeed as the sole one. That it is in a certain respect the most significant may not be denied, for all formative, instructing, teaching, ordering, learning, spiritually advancing, and error- as well as truth-disseminating communication occurs only when conscious and willed representations of one soul pass over into the cognizing consciousness of another; -- whether this communication, however, is in and of itself to be called the most powerful, is very much open to doubt, for to say nothing of all the wondrous and mysterious relations which, rooted entirely in the unconscious, ground the manifold relations of sympathy and antipathy in life, one need only recall what half-unconscious and half-conscious influences must strike the unconscious in the child so that a consciousness can develop in it at all. Now even if, for these reasons, it must be conceded that in the moment of the effect itself, the relation between different consciousnesses does not possess the power and depth that characterizes the relations of the unconscious, there nevertheless emerges quite unexpectedly in the relation of conscious to conscious a new and extremely remarkable aspect of the relation of soul to soul; namely, the capacity to step out of the confines of temporal existence and to achieve an effect of the soul, or rather of the spirit -- eternal by human reckoning -- no longer merely upon a single kindred soul, but upon the spirit of humanity as a whole. Just as it was mentioned earlier that spirit is truly born in the soul with the very first thought, and that only from this point onward does the soul, or its fundamental idea, become capable of unlimited subjective growth, so now also, with the possibility of a conscious effect of

[p. 376] [p. 377] the soul upon other conscious souls, there is at the same time truly given the possibility of a certain objective infinity -- that is, of a wholly unlimited continuation of life and influence within the circle of humanity, reaching into quite unthinkable spans of time. How many spirits do we count in this way in history who, as persons, have long since departed from the number of the living, and who in their thoughts, their world of feeling, and in their willing, continue to be active even now and far into the future. The entire immeasurable circle of what we call the inner higher cultivation of humanity rests upon the unceasing reverberation and continued life of spiritual effects and spiritual deeds which, far above the region of the quickly passing lifetimes of individuals, have risen to the concept of the timeless, and alone ultimately make possible a steady growth of the idea of humanity itself. Of what power and of what value this conscious effect therefore is, requires no elaborate exposition, for everything great and significant, insofar as it belongs to the history of our species, rests upon such a basis alone; but we must not therefore forget -- what after all that has preceded is self-evident -- that all this influence in its root by no means belongs solely and exclusively to the conscious and willed thought, to pure cognition, but that it too, in its first ground, must each time again be acknowledged as conditioned by an unconscious governance of the idea directing the development of an organization, and by thousandfold reflections of unconscious soul-life (the world of feeling) that continually fall upon the world of thought in every life. Indeed, the most remarkable relations present themselves here to the investigator of the human world of spirit, and it has from of old been regarded as an important psychological task, in examining the living conscious effect of a significant spirit upon other self-conscious spirits, to ascertain and discover what therein was conditioned by the absolutely unconscious (or, as one was otherwise accustomed to say, by the constitution of his bodily organization) and by the events of his world of feeling, and what, on the other hand, for the most part and insofar as one may at all make such a separation, truly belonged to the fully conscious and freely willed activity of his spirit. To be sure, it requires a very fine hand to disentangle these ever so manifoldly interwoven threads and trace each one individually! -- Moreover, what has been said here naturally applies, just as to the soul from which the conscious effect proceeds, so also to the soul or souls that receive it. In the latter as well, this conscious influence first affects the conscious spirit, but in many cases, and likewise in an inseparable manner, at the same time the unconscious side of their existence, their world of feeling, and all of this must always ultimately refer us back to the inherently inseparable unity of the soul. -- So much then may be said in general about the effect of the conscious spirit upon conscious spirits, which, insofar as it communicates itself through sound and word, image and deed, is the best known and is that which mediates human communal life altogether. We now turn to the other, less noticed relations.

[p. 378] In the most complete contrast to the foregoing stands, next, 2, the effect of the unconscious of one soul upon the unconscious of another. Whereas the previously mentioned relation presents especially and at its highest the relation of one monad to many, and of many to one, the one now to be considered shows essentially and at its highest only the relation of soul to soul; and whereas the former extends even to quite distant and alien souls, indeed showed itself bound to no time whatsoever, so that a soul whose body has been scattered to dust for millennia can still act upon me as if present, this latter is bound to the most immediate and simultaneous presence possible, and will be all the more intimate the more decisively the deep kinship of two souls is established. This contrast is in itself extraordinarily remarkable; but if we pursue it to its furthest extent, a certain reversal of the relations then again emerges, in that on the one hand the highest awareness of conscious effect is alone capable of bringing about that closest relation of soul to soul in which alone the unconscious interaction is most powerfully active, while on the other hand it becomes apparent that precisely and especially through the unconscious overflowing of soul into soul the relation of one soul to many is conditioned. These apparent riddles will resolve themselves at once when we approach the matter more closely.

[p. 379] It requires only brief reflection to convince oneself that the highest and most intimate relation of the unconscious of one soul to the unconscious of another must always be tied in some way to the feeling of love. What is not connected by some deeper sympathetic attraction -- even if this be founded purely upon organic contrast -- will act only weakly as unconscious upon unconscious, whereas the deepest convulsions of these regions must always emerge where soul is bound to soul by a particular living feeling of love: indeed, it is very remarkable that relations have been observed which prove how little even a wide separation of the bodily existence of two souls has the power to disturb these connections.
