Something of this kind comes to cognition from the dark region of feeling by means of certain reflections upon the conscious life of representation, which we designate now as presentiments, now as dreams, and remarkable examples, in which spatially distant but inwardly close persons felt mutual effects -- where illness of the one produced anxiety or illness in the other, or where other particular moods of the world of feeling manifested simultaneously -- have indeed occurred frequently enough. It is also not necessary that only the love between the sexes should have established such a relation; close bonds of friendship, or the relation between twin siblings, and so forth, likewise furnish such examples. That, however, the most intimate relation from unconscious to unconscious occurs between the different sexes is beyond question. Its pinnacle appears in procreation, for precisely here the essential inner process -- that is, the very process whose conditions and nature only the most recent physiology has been able to bring to the light of consciousness -- is entirely given over to unconscious life. It is for this very reason that even science can indeed determine whether in the union of two natures the conditions are given upon which the possibility of calling forth a new individual depends, but whether in a particular case, under these completely fulfilled conditions, a new individual actually arises or not will nevertheless remain for the present entirely withdrawn from consciousness. It is then also remarkable in this connection that this productive relation from unconscious to unconscious, precisely because it has nothing whatever to do with consciousness, becomes in a certain sense quite independent of what one may call, in the higher sense, the love between the sexes. For this latter, although always essentially co-conditioned by an unconscious attraction, is, as we showed earlier, in and of itself quite inconceivable unless consciousness has first given it its proper consecration, for only to the extent that consciousness is capable of perceiving the beauty and worthiness of the beloved can love be kindled. Therefore, while love for its part does not exclude the real productivity of the wholly unconscious in the multiplication of the species, it is by no means essentially connected with it; on the contrary, it is of the most essential influence upon the heightening of the blissful feeling of the fullest mutual devotion. Mere procreation, however, as an effect solely between unconscious regions, can everywhere take place entirely without love, indeed almost without consciousness, and for that very reason also entirely without that blissful happiness -- solely through the united contrast of the sexes. It even seems at times that a very intense love, forever striving toward union, is rather counteractive than conducive to that real productivity. Be this as it may for now, what has been said is already sufficient, on the one hand, to show that the most vivid and intimate interaction of two unconscious regions, precisely because at its highest level it is possible only through love -- which in turn cannot be conceived without higher consciousness -- ultimately always depends again upon this very consciousness; and on the other hand, to demonstrate that a calling forth of the multiplicity of humanity itself -- which multiplicity in turn contains the condition of all conscious spiritual cultivation of the individual human being -- is everywhere and thoroughly conditioned by certain processes that themselves, however, belong only to the relation from unconscious to unconscious. Here too, then, there is ample occasion to observe how in all directions the most peculiar entanglements between conscious and unconscious occur in the history of the soul.

[p. 380] [p. 381] A particularly remarkable effect between the unconscious of two souls further emerges: partly in the relations we have already mentioned several times as antipathy and sympathy, and partly in the relations of diseased life that we designate by the words contagion and magnetic healing.

[p. 382] As regards sympathy and antipathy, this is rightly called a mystery, because, precisely insofar as it belongs to the unconscious, it will forever remain hidden in its root from conscious soul-life and will become noticeable only in its effects. But in the fundamental essential quality of the idea, and in the way the finest threads of the organization have been spun and woven in consequence of this, in a first wholly unconscious formation, it must ultimately always be sought, when, quite independently of all reflection, an attraction or repulsion develops between different persons. Any further explanations are generally quite impossible here, and only to indicate certain remarkable relations as facts can be our task. But who has not experienced something significant of this kind in his life! The silent attraction that draws us, often we ourselves do not yet know why, toward some individuality, that most inward feeling that causes us, upon chance encounters with people, often in the very first moment, to sense: with this person a deeper rapport is destined for you, and with that one you will never be able to truly befriend yourself -- these are very remarkable, and are often peculiarly confirmed by life experience; especially when we attempt, because understanding persuades us that a relation with the latter is after all possible, and that it would be better to turn away from the former, actually to follow this counsel, but life afterwards proves only too well how right the first feeling was. In individual cases, too, in sensitive<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> Not only with other persons, but with entire directions of life can such a sympathetic or antipathetic presentiment occur.

[p. 383] natures, a particular sensibility emerges that makes it remarkably difficult for them to endure the close proximity of other individuals at all, except precisely that one in whom alone they feel a perfect suitability to their own being, and with whom a decided attraction of sympathy connects them. The proximity of this latter will then, to be sure, act all the more powerfully and refreshingly upon them.

It is moreover easily understood that what remains to be said here about contagion and magnetic healing can properly be adduced only as a particular modification of what has just been discussed as sympathy. Both contagion and healing influence can therefore take place only when a certain sympathy is present. Where no relation of this kind exists at all, the healing effect will not even be sought, but contagion likewise will not occur. Thus, for example, no case is known of the epidemics among the American natives passing to Europeans. Just as therefore on the one hand a disease is sometimes communicated at the slightest touch, indeed merely through action at a distance, so in other cases even the most virulent and easily communicable diseases -- such as the plague -- can, when that relation is absent, remain without contagion despite the closest contact.

It must however be added in mentioning these cases -- I was led to think of these processes when I heard the account of the earliest life-history of a well-known extraordinary talent for arithmetic. This man, in whom the immense inner practice of calculation has absorbed all other and also the higher directions of spirit, so that the higher philosophical and poetical human being has entirely disappeared in the calculator, experienced as a small boy, when the first numerals were set before him in school, a true horror of them, so that he would not come to school, and only later, compelled by blows to attend school and to calculate, he became entirely a calculating machine. This initial horror at something that later completely absorbed his higher direction necessarily appeared to me at once as significant in the above sense.

[p. 384] that here it is not the individuality of the person alone, but also -- since a disease is in a certain sense a self-enclosed organic whole -- the individuality of the disease that must be taken into account. Not every disease will therefore be communicated from one individual to another. It can happen that two individuals who have a close rapport with each other communicate one disease to each other but not another, because the one individuality had an affinity for the one form of disease (what physicians call predisposition) but by no means for the other.

Likewise, both with respect to the transmission of contagion and the transmission of healing influence, it deserves to be emphasized that it is not exclusively and solely the immediate contact between the organisms that mediates them, but that foreign substances too, insofar as they have absorbed something of the atmosphere of the communicating organism, can mediate this transference. Thus a child that a sick person has carried can produce the disease in another person, and thus a cloth that the magnetizer wore, or the water that he touched, can excite and help heal the somnambulist. In these cases it seems at first striking that an unconscious psychic effect should be capable of adhering even to a foreign inanimate body. To understand these phenomena, two things must be considered: on the one hand, as regards the transmission of diseases, a detached primordial formation -- a cell -- of the diseased organism can actually be transferred to another through a foreign intermediary, and can then continue to germinate there and produce the same disease. Thus, for example, the cells floating in the inoculation matter of smallpox, even after they have been dried onto glass or threads, after they enter a receptive organism, act by reviving and multiplying

[p. 385] there. On the other hand, it is not detached formations but the continual vaporous dissolution of an organism itself, and thus principally its dissolution into the surrounding air, by which both healing influence and contagion are explained. Every organism, and the human one above all, forms through this perpetually imperceptible evaporation -- or, so to speak, decomposition -- its own small atmosphere around itself, whose substance belonged only shortly before to the organism itself and still in some degree preserves its peculiarity. This atmosphere retains in particular the special chemical proportions conditioned by the idea of the organism, which betray their peculiarity especially to the sense of smell; it also preserves its characteristic electrical tensions, and since these latter are after all only a product of the innervation currents, something of this innervation tension itself passes over to this atmosphere and forms what Humboldt already called, at the nerve, a sensible atmosphere. As soon as one has formed a clear conception of this, it will also become clear that by means of this atmosphere, which in turn adheres to other more solid substances (so long as it has not itself entirely dissipated), a certain unconscious vital influence of one organism can indeed be transmitted to the unconscious of another organism. One may therefore consider what was said further above about the peculiar significance of magnetic influence and magnetic manipulation as having been to some extent completed thereby, and at the same time herein lies the understanding of everything that one has to regard, in the history of relics and amulets, as that kernel of truth which remains when one strips away what at all times superstition has heaped around them in abundant measure.

Finally, it is still to be mentioned that just as in the case of

[p. 386] contagion the interaction between two unconscious regions is almost always involuntary and itself unconscious, with the exception of deliberate inoculation of disease -- so the healing influence, the communication of health and invigoration, almost always takes place as magnetic influence only through conscious volition. There is, however, also in the latter regard an unconscious and involuntary communication, and what has long been known belongs here: namely, that the atmosphere of young, vitally fresh persons exerts a

beneficial influence upon older weakened individuals, just as conversely the very young and delicate child, in restlessness and pain, often immediately feels the soothing influence when the mother takes it close to herself. And so much, then, for the consideration of the immediate or mediate transference of the unconscious of one soul to the unconscious of another!

We come now, thirdly, to consider the effect of the conscious of one soul upon the unconscious of another. This effect emerges with particular power in the developmental history of the human being, inasmuch as, the less consciousness is yet unfolded in the person, all the more can the unconscious be directly affected by the conscious influence of another. This may be observed most acutely in the relation of the mother to the child nourished in her womb or at her breast, and the most striking example of all is the so-called maternal impression, or the direct influence of a representation that has come to the vivid consciousness of the mother upon the organic unconscious formation of the fetus. In this power -- that it could directly alter the structure of the body -- the influence of the conscious soul upon the unconscious of another nowhere else emerges, and that something of this kind is possible is explained only by the so very close rapport that two souls of this kind have to one another. There then

[p. 387] namely, the one soul enters into a relation to the other similar to that which, in one and the same soul, the unconscious has to the conscious; and just as, for example, we unconsciously and involuntarily feel a watering of the mouth when we think of a sliced lemon, or just as we can feel a pain in the eye when we vividly think what consequences a knife thrust into the eye would have, so there the vividly aroused imagination of the mother sometimes decidedly affects the formation of the fruit ripening in her womb. Already more remote is the influence during the nursing of the child at the breast, although examples have been adduced showing that the character of the conscious soul-life, as it presents itself in the character of the wet-nurse, was not without influence upon the development of the infant, first unconscious and then also making itself felt in consciousness.

Apart from this kind of closest rapport between two human individualities, in ordinary life the effect of a conscious soul upon the unconscious of another mostly emerges only as a mediated one, mediated either through the unconscious of the giving soul or through the conscious of the receiving soul. The former occurs especially in every kind of so-called magnetic influence, as well as in the sexual, inasmuch as a conscious willing of the giving soul first produces a retuning in its own unconscious region, which latter, now intentionally brought into contact with the corresponding region of the other, communicates its own excitation to the other. The latter, where the conscious effect in the other first affects the conscious region and through this the unconscious, is the most commonplace kind of such transference, and the remarkable circumstance has already been mentioned above that influences of this kind are by no means bound to space and time any longer, for in this manner the thoughts of souls whose bodily appearance on earth has already been over for centuries still arouse in us series of representations that even now, through our consciousness, can act upon our unconscious life, upon our circulation, upon our secretions, and so forth. Even now the songs of Homer and the tragedies of Sophocles and Shakespeare arouse now the unconscious quickened beating of our heart, now altered forms of our breathing, now the secretion of our tears; even now the voluptuous depictions of Ariosto and Boccaccio excite our senses, and even now Dante, with the story of Ugolino, fills us involuntarily and unconsciously with a shudder of our sympathetic nerves -- proof enough that these effects belong to those that are truly immeasurable.

[p. 388] How far, apart from those relations between mother and child, the possibility extends of a direct effect of the conscious sphere of one soul upon the unconscious life of another is difficult to decide in general terms. It is certain that between closely bound souls that have matured toward a shared existence, the relation can become very intimate; yet it is hard to establish anything about this directly in the form of a psychological fact. If it really occurs that, for instance, the thoughts of one soul can directly produce a quickened heartbeat and a peculiar mood of feeling in another, and if it occurs that the deed or the acute suffering of one soul, although nothing of it has become conscious to the other, can nevertheless directly call forth in that soul a peculiar stirring of feeling that we term "presentiment," then it is thereby proven that in this way too a direct transference from the conscious sphere of one soul to the unconscious sphere of another is possible. I am convinced that many sensitive souls are not strangers to decided experiences of this kind; just as surely, however, am I convinced that to selfish, merely intellectually developed souls such things will scarcely occur, and must for that very reason be regarded by them as illusion and superstition. In any case, however, here too it is to be kept firmly in view that all phenomena of such transferences become explicable only if one holds fast to the thought that humanity is in itself a higher ideal organism, an organism in which all the particular individualities are enclosed as members. Only when one individual is thought of in a relation to another similar to that of one organ to another in the living body do the last-mentioned emanations, as well as the contagions and healing influences mentioned above, become comprehensible.

[p. 389] The last of the relations to be considered here will now be, fourthly, the influence of the unconscious of one soul upon the conscious of another. Properly speaking, there belongs here the entire powerful impression that the configuration of the organism, developed through unconscious life alone, can make and in the most manifold ways actually does make upon the conscious spirit of another. Everything we call bodily form, features of the countenance, the glance of the eye -- it is only the outward symbolism through which the deepest inwardness of an idea unconsciously governing these formations expresses itself, and how great, how in many respects often determining for an entire life, can be the influence that this outward appearance of a soul, formed without its doing and without its knowledge, exerts upon the conscious region of another! On this point it is not necessary to adduce further elucidations and examples. It is, however, not merely the fixed, enduring form; it is perhaps even more the silent, deep trembling of unconscious feelings that mirrors itself in the outward appearance, that unconsciously resonates in the tone of the voice,

[p. 390] and that announces itself in warmth, scent, and electrical tension, whereby the conscious spirit too is moved. Moreover, on this occasion it is once again to be recalled how it is that aspect of the conscious spirit's sensory cognition which we designate by the name of smell, wherein -- precisely because the organism dissolving in the air always becomes perceptible to it -- the perception of the quality of the unconscious existence of another soul is especially afforded. -- There is a passage in the second part of Faust that remarkably brings to light this peculiarity, rarely yet understood and not yet at all articulated. It is where Paris must appear in the circle of the ladies:

"What fragrance mixed with incense-vapor blows, That so refreshes my heart in its innermost!" another: the third:

"In truth! a breath pervades deep into the disposition -- it comes from him!"

"It is the blossom of growth, In the youth prepared as ambrosia, and atmospherically spread all around."

And so there is decidedly present a truly remarkable series of various transferences of unconscious soul-life not merely to one's own self-consciousness but also to that of another soul. Strictly speaking, one could also divide these transferences into mediate and immediate; the former would be those in which the unconscious as such communicates itself to the other's unconscious and only through this acts upon the sphere of higher consciousness. In this way many stirrings of dark feelings first pass over magnetically into another individuality and only there come to the perception of consciousness as well. Most clearly this emerges again in disease-producing, magnetic, and sexual influences. There the effect flows over unconsciously from both sides and reaches consciousness only by this circuitous route. The immediate kind is in part that mentioned above, where conscious sensory perception teaches us to know what unconscious stirrings are at work in the other soul; in another part, it could also well occur, where an intimate relation exists between two souls, that directly in the one soul that would arise in the form of thought -- that is, in consciousness -- which in the other soul had been stirred only as an unconscious feeling.

[p. 391] We now pass to the consideration of the relation of the soul to nature. Strictly speaking, we should have combined this section with the immediately following one on the relation of the soul to God, for what we call nature -- the world that surrounds us, the ground on which we live, the water that nourishes and carries us, the air we breathe and that in thousandfold reflections and cloud formations can enchant us, the forest that shades us, the flower that scents us -- these are after all, at their highest, only infinitely changing appearances of the eternal thoughts of that one supreme mystery which we call God. -- To be sure, that would lead still further, for in the end everything -- we ourselves, the humanity that acts upon us and for and through which we develop -- is again only a revelation of the thoughts of this One, this Highest, in whose deepest being all distinction and all manifoldness ultimately dissolve into the mere unity of the absolute. Here, however, where it is precisely our concern to trace the life, the revelation of the soul in its most varied refractions of color, we must avoid this metaphysical standpoint; we must hold fast to the most characteristically human one, and we shall soon recognize that, taken in this sense, a remarkable and most powerful interaction indeed exists between our soul and the nature surrounding us.

[p. 392] Here too it will first be indispensable to distinguish between the relations of nature to the unconscious and to the conscious life of the soul. To the first domain belongs the entire flood of innumerable influences through which at every moment of our existence we find ourselves sustained, nourished, disturbed, enlivened, exhausted, attuned and distuned. Hygiene, dietetics and pharmacology, physiology and cultural history ordinarily enumerate and weigh all these things, and it lies entirely beyond our scope here to enter into any special appreciation of them; but it must nevertheless be said that none of these influences can touch the finely articulated bodily revelation of the soul without in some way, now more weakly, now more strongly, yet resounding up into the region of the fullest conscious spiritual existence and activity. Just as, for instance, a wagon rolling past an observatory always somewhat disturbs the telescope of the astronomer observing from above, even if the building otherwise rests upon the firmest foundations. Here too the absolute separation of a bodily and a spiritual element, as two things utterly and in every respect different, had formerly rendered psychology quite helpless, for the effect of the one upon the other thereby became incomprehensible -- a difficulty that can no longer trouble us now. Moreover, we may indeed allow a definite distinction between the influences of nature that essentially affect only the unconscious side of our soul-life and those that turn entirely to consciousness, and we attend here especially to the latter, because it alone belongs to the pure domain of psychology. The former has already become clearer through what was said earlier about the life of the individual organic systems and the diseases of the feelings. In the discussion of the latter, however, it is likewise to be noted that it too is for the most part mixed

[p. 393] with influences upon the unconscious region of our existence.

As the mightiest relation of this kind we may regard the manner in which the bodily living-out of our soul and our spirit is situated with respect to the vital conditions of the planet. We are accustomed to summarize this under the concept of the nature of the soil and the climate, and how significant these conditions are for the development and efficacy of the spirit -- who does not know this! Whether our senses are early addressed by the cheerfulness and brightness of the sky, the purity and beauty of the air, mild temperature, and the grand formation of land and water, or whether they are surrounded by cold damp fogs and stunted by lack of light and pure air -- this will always produce a quite essentially different formation of the spirit! -- Indeed, this difference would stand out much more sharply still, were it not that the inventive spirit of the human being knows how to create means that are suited to replacing and improving the imperfections of the climate to a high degree. Remarkable, however, is how even these means of creating, so to speak, a new and better climate for oneself (through the invention of our heating systems, lighting, household arrangements, and so forth) -- in short, this in a sense artificial nature -- in turn exerts its own particular influence upon the development of our spirit. The ever-increasing artificiality of our social modes of thinking, much of the unhealthiness and feebleness of modern intellectual tendencies, the conventional modes of representation that deviate ever further from what is simply and purely natural -- these become for the most part explicable only through the factitious nature in which human beings have more and more settled and continue to settle ever more deeply.

Whoever pays closer attention to these things will then easily convince himself that that striving, which has emerged only in our time, to rush out

periodically, as if into a kind of nature-adoration, into forests and mountains, into valleys and upon rocks, is really a kind of instinct, so to speak, to seek a remedy against the malady of artificial life and its influence upon spiritual development. Certainly there lies a peculiar truth and significance in this striving, and such periodic immersion in free nature can indeed act in a truly refreshing and powerful way upon the spirit; but unfortunately this tendency too, through the manner of its execution, often enough becomes a decided caricature and then entirely misses its salutary effect.

If we now consider more particularly how the nature surrounding us addresses the soul and develops the spirit, it will be understood that this effect is partly brought about in general through the side of feeling and through the resonance of feeling in a thousand different strings and from the most varied directions, and partly is promoted especially as spiritual cultivation by the fact that in nature infinite parables appear, at the study of which the human being, like the child with fables, is destined gradually to ripen toward the cognition of higher truth. The former influence, which engages consciousness through feeling, passes through infinite nuances. How manifold alone is the influence of the seasons, which thereby grant to human beings in temperate climates a certain universality of terrestrial nature, in that at one and the same spot they lead past in ceaseless rotation, as it were, the conditions of the most varied zones -- from the hottest equatorial regions to the iciest polar regions -- and thereby act upon the person quite differently, one may say with a more earthly universality, than if one and the same state of the atmosphere always surrounded him. Certainly! what a peculiar influence this ever-recurring cycle -- from the more severe inward-turning of wintry conditions, through the enliveningly stimulating effect of spring, to the beautifully saturated fullness of summer, and to the half-dying, half-future-preparing results of autumn -- produces upon disposition and spirit can scarcely be calculated. -- A weaker but similar influence is by no means to be denied of the alternation of the times of day. The refreshing coolness and brightness of morning acts with a peculiar elasticity upon the activity of the spirit, and many an idea previously unconscious to it emerges new and sharp under the influence of the newly risen sun. A decided contrast to this is then formed by the more poetically reverie-laden influence of the sinking evening, while the darkness of the night is destined to lend the unconscious a momentary sovereignty over the conscious, whereas the height of the day particularly and most manifoldly favors the conscious intercourse of human beings with one another.

[p. 395] Similar relations make themselves known between the various moods of the weather and the spirit of the human being. Persons of weaker spiritual individuality can thereby be addressed with a force that is capable of partly robbing them of the free self-determination of their activity, while stronger natures, inasmuch as they increasingly regard the external world merely as the element in which they have to carry out and give form to their own particular purposes of life, are also accustomed to measuring the value and the cheering or depressing influence of these celestial courses only according to whether they are conducive to these purposes or not.

Another aspect from which a special relation opens between the human soul and nature is that of cognition, and in this respect the influence is quite incalculable that the contemplation of nature

[p. 396] has upon the spiritual development of the soul. One may say: just as within itself the soul develops the miracle of spiritual consciousness only under the condition of the formation of a certain natural organization, so too the spirit itself is able to unfold the wealth of sound thoughts and insights only when it faithfully and purely takes into itself the history of the nature around it in the essence of its inner sequence. This pure relation to nature has therefore from of old been revered by the best among humanity as something sacred, and quite singularly in this connection there is a passage in Euripides which, in a manner such as could come only to an ancient Greek, expresses this mystery naively and grandly. It is the passage of Hippolytus to Artemis:

"This freshly woven wreath, O sovereign queen! For adornment from inviolate meadows I bring to thee, where the shepherd dares not graze his flock, nor iron ever entered -- nay, the bee alone flits past the inviolate spring meadow, and where Innocence tends gardens with the dew of springs, that whoever holds not learned knowledge, but by nature the pure sense for all wisdom, may gather from it; but to sinners it is forbidden."

Only from this vantage point is it therefore to be understood why language also sets so high a value upon the expression "natural" as applied to the world of thought, and recognizes it as the seal of something perfectly appropriate. It is indeed, for careful and close consideration, something extraordinarily remarkable that the same inner organic sequence, the same deep necessity of nature, which determines that in such and such a direction the currents of the seas and the movements of the stars proceed, and that the progress of the development of a moss as of a palm can only be thus and not otherwise -- that this must also reign where a truly beautiful series of thoughts is to unfold and establish itself in the spirit. Just as there are therefore sequences of thought that we call harmonious and beautiful precisely because our innermost consciousness tells us that the same lawfulness and necessity and the same fine articulation reign in them that we admire in a healthy, natural organism, so there are also others that, through their obscurity, artificiality, and perverseness, give us the impression of a crippled, dislocated, unfinished, or corrupted organism, and which we then rightly designate as unnatural. These kinds of natural or unnatural sequences of thought are indeed conditioned and given to the highest degree by the relation of the soul to nature, and it is here in particular that external influence and education can do much to shape and guide the soul, and from this point especially what is called the study of the natural sciences acquires a higher psychological significance.

[p. 397] It must indeed certainly have an essential influence upon what we have called the natural in the sequences of thought of the spirit, when we early gain a healthy, pure intuition of the inner, actual life of nature, of the higher, divine, unconscious reason that reveals itself in the unfolding of a plant, as in the crystallization of minerals and the articulation of a developing animal creature; and this must have such influence because here too that earlier remarkable relation between conscious and unconscious comes to the fore, in which it emerged that if, on the one hand, the unconscious indeed first reaches its height only by rising to consciousness, on the other hand the conscious must also, in a certain respect, return to the unconscious in order to reach its height; -- for was not everything that we have called the free deed of the conscious spirit as art found to be possible only insofar as much spiritual

stirring and doing had first become wholly unconscious again. Thus the conscious spirit nourishes itself, so to speak, and especially during its earlier development, gladly and profitably upon the high unconscious governance of nature, and grows by it and from it, just as our organism bodily nourishes and grows itself by the intake of natural substances. It is in this way, then, for the most part, that the spirit cultivated upon the life of nature can gain that beautiful sequence and pure naturalness which then pervades, regulates, and animates all its thoughts, which is capable of spreading a wondrous charm over it, and which particularly distinguishes the spirit of Greek antiquity through such a singularly lofty beauty. From this vantage point it is therefore also very clear how great the significance of the study of nature truly is, and why, at a time when the human being on the whole and in externals is more and more being pushed out of the pure relation to nature and threatens ever more to fall prey to the influences of its own artificial and unfruitful nature, precisely upon the study of natural science so powerful a consolation, so great a hope must rest. It is this study, namely, which -- rightly pursued -- alone is capable of providing a helpful counterweight against that unnaturalness, of offering a conscious guidance back to nature, and of becoming, in the truest higher sense, a gymnastics of the spirit, and hence its high significance! To be sure, this study can also be entirely spoiled, misdirected, and turned to the very opposite of its goal -- namely, when it is turned entirely to artificiality, and when the memorizing of systematic names and characteristics and similar material, which properly serves only as a secondary matter and a makeshift for the immeasurable, is made straightaway the chief task of all endeavor!

Where therefore the study of nature avoids such wrong paths

[p. 399] and truly directs the conscious spirit only toward that divine and great element in the unconscious of the world, there it can fully become the palladium ensuring that the soul, even amid all the artificiality of outward existence, does not lose the truly "natural" quality of its being and its thoughts, but rather ripens precisely thereby ever more toward inner perfection. In this respect too, as in so many others, the soul will undergo a certain circuit from the unconscious through the conscious back to the unconscious, and the relation and connection of the soul to nature can in any case -- thus mediated through science -- become a higher and more intimate one than it was when merely a dark unconscious, though deeply grounded, attraction bound the spirit to nature. In this regard one may therefore well consider natural science as a guarantee that that beautiful naturalness of thought, which is the fairest seal of a truly cultivated spirit, cannot perish, however far a hyper-refined and over-artificial culture may ceaselessly strive to enmesh and sweep humanity along. -- May therefore the right direction and right manner always be preserved for this science!

The last and at the same time the first, the highest and at the same time the most intimate of all relations of the soul, is finally the relation of the soul to God. This too divides itself at once and necessarily into an unconscious and a conscious one.

"In Him we live, and move, and have our being" is the ancient, true, and eternal saying that must at once fully bring before us the unconscious relation of the soul to God! -- Strictly speaking, nothing further can really be added to this sentence, "in Him we live, and move, and have our being," when it is the unconscious relation that is under discussion;

[p. 400] for that highest, eternal mystery, that manifest secret of the Godhead, which creates, sustains, pervades, enlivens me as an atom in the infinite, the immeasurable, and lets me grow from an unconscious and bound being into a conscious and inwardly free one -- we are from the very beginning so wholly its own and so thoroughly initiated and united into it, that the "relation" here becomes an "inborn-being," a "being-part." The entire splendor and beauty of human organization, of unconscious human life-development and unconscious life-relations, the infinite richness of our inner articulation, the thousandfold relations in which our unconscious life stands to other things, to the surrounding nature and to sun and planets -- all this is only a particular revelation of that eternal highest mystery in and upon us, and I may therefore here only refer back to so much that has already been the subject of the preceding considerations, for in all that, in a mystical manner, the unconscious relation of the soul to God has actually been treated at the same time.

Let us therefore now turn to the conscious relation of the soul to God. Here the most remarkable and most singular relations surface. The spirit of the human being, the soul that has come to consciousness and knows itself -- just as it actually arrives at existence, at true presence, only through the thought (cogito ergo sum [I think, therefore I am]) -- so it now also strives, in contemplating and cognizing the world, to rescue itself from all the eternal flux of the world, from the constant flight of time that appears half only as past and half as future, from the sea of the ceaselessly perishing and ceaselessly renewing actual, and to attain to the grasp of an eternal, an immutable -- in a word, of an objective primordial spiritual being as the highest original ground of all that actuality and of its

[p. 401] own existence, and this we call the "seeking of the soul after God." This seeking, this yearning, this longing, runs as a fundamental tone through the entire history of humanity that has come to consciousness, and the spirit of the human being recognizes in its inmost depth that, just as the attainment of the first thought and of self-knowledge on the one hand, and the understanding and most complete cognition of a closely kindred soul in love on the other, are the two first conditions for satisfying a need deeply inborn in us, so finally the grasping and utmost penetration of the eternal divine mystery alone can grant the third and highest condition of the perfect satisfaction of our existence. But here, however, the most wondrous relations emerge; -- just as the longing for the most complete self-knowledge may be called a deep and immeasurable one, and just as the longing for the full sufficiency of loving cognition must also pass through many stages and yet will never receive a satisfaction complete in every respect, so too this yearning, this seeking after God, is not only tied to the most manifold stages of development and made difficult by the most various illusory images interposing themselves, but is at the same time in its very nature unfathomable and infinite. If we sum it up in one word, then in this respect too the opening words of these pages are to be recalled: "the key to the cognition of the nature of the conscious life of the soul lies in the region of unconsciousness." -- The unfathomable and actually unattainable character of the longing for God lies namely in this, that it here becomes in the truest sense the task of the thinking spirit: to let the highest of the conscious spirit sink, or rather rise, into the deepest depth of what is for us an unconscious. For we may straightforwardly declare: the highest

[p. 402] divine consciousness, the consciousness of the spirit of God in and of itself, is to be conceived by us only as something so immeasurable, so infinite, so all-encompassing, that for so thoroughly conditioned and finitely bound a consciousness as the human one, it must ultimately always completely coincide with the mystery of the unconscious itself; conversely, however, what we have called the divinity of the unconscious lies precisely in the immeasurability and incomprehensibility of a highest divine consciousness.

Inasmuch then as the conscious spirit of the human being, when it would give itself over entirely and most intimately to this relation to a highest, to something for it immeasurable, unconscious, finds itself irrevocably and perpetually immersed in a peculiar mystical antithesis of consciousness and unconsciousness, it also cannot but here, with all its longing, find itself confronted by a great mystery, an eternal mystery -- a mystery against which it can just as easily shatter and perish beyond rescue as it is capable of elevating, enlightening, and forever redeeming itself thereby to the highest degree. The former will occur if it either lets itself be led astray by illusory images of this highest and eternal, or if it lets itself become confused through the brooding that leads to madness -- the attempt to fathom what is in itself unfathomable; the latter, however, can succeed only if, on the one hand, it has the courage to behold the unconscious as such and to give itself up intimately to the infinite as such, and on the other hand if it is able to fill and conquer the abyss and the groundlessness of something utterly immeasurable and mysterious through the power and depth of love, and in this way to attain a perfectly beautiful and pure relation to this mystery. Both now require a somewhat more detailed development.

[p. 403] As regards the first point, concerning the illusory images of the divine, all and every attempt of humanity to compress that highest, immeasurable, and absolute mystery into the concrete concept of anything personified has always remained just so many instances of the aberration of the human spirit, and the strife and discord that then arose when individual peoples and persons created a deity in their own image, and each party declared its own to be the sole right and true one, have inscribed their traces in bloody lines upon every page of history a thousandfold. How coarsely the illusory images for that highest mystery are often chosen is proven by the fetishes of savages and the monstrous representations and idols of so many times and peoples, which are always only the faithful mirror-images of the state of such a portion of humanity. Although one attempt of this kind may be more ingenious than another, one also more poetically deeply conceived and more beautiful than another -- a painting of the divinity by Raphael nobler and mightier than a Chinese idol, and the deduction of the attributes of the person of the divinity by a monotheistic theologian more sensible than the invocation of a priest of the Dalai Lama -- yet strictly speaking and in the most earnest and unconditional truth, the one weighs just as lightly as the other, and the one is just as far removed from that submersion, or rather ascension, of consciousness into a highest and for us absolute unconscious -- which was described above as the soul's own proper relation to God -- as the other.

It is moreover something very remarkable and often touching to follow all the various phases through which humanity in this regard had to pass; -- in particular, nature-worship, the veneration of certain powerful natural phenomena, first gains a deeper significance from this standpoint. It was indeed wholly natural and fitting that, as soon as the spirit of the human being began to become aware of the high reason that silently reveals itself in all the unconscious around us, there arose in him the presentiment of a highest divine, and, since in the unconscious governance of nature the enormous power of this highest divine mystery had first become in some degree perceptible to him, the natural phenomenon in and of itself necessarily became for him first an object of veneration. The return of the conscious to the unconscious announced itself here first, I would say, symbolically, and precisely because behind this symbol, once again unconsciously, a high truth lies concealed, it is always something moving when we see the worship and veneration of a still half-childlike humanity turning now to the sun, now to the electrical stirrings of the atmosphere manifesting in the thunderstorm, now to moon and stars. This nature-worship, indeed the veneration of springs, rivers, and trees, therefore has for us something even more innocent and more justifiable than the worship of animal creatures or of forms that are supposed to be human -- and this for the sole reason that in the former a pure turning of the conscious toward a highest unconscious is still perceptible, whereas in the latter this highest unconscious already appears unnaturally compressed into an inappropriate concrete consciousness. The same applies also in favor of Greek mythology, for there too it is in no way actually attempted to force the one highest immeasurable mystery into a concrete concept, into a person represented in any particular way; rather, their divine figures are only personifications of individual natural phenomena -- it is ever the nature-worship itself, only that the individual expressions of the unconscious in nature are, as it were, raised to consciousness in such a way that the history of the human being himself is poetically attributed to them, he who, in the development of his soul, likewise experiences each time the upward formation from the unconscious to the conscious; and in this way only the elevation of the nearer, particular unconscious to the conscious takes place, but the inadmissible attempt is avoided of forcing the one highest and eternal mystery into the limited form of a kind of human consciousness -- a misstep from which monotheistic religions have seldom kept themselves free.

[p. 404] [p. 405] It requires no detailed description to show how all these illusory images, and the cruder and more inadmissible the more so, have brought manifold suffering and bloody strife and discord upon humanity; it is also to be noted as remarkable that that false monotheism, for example of the Jews and Turks, has spread far more harm and discord than the mild nature-worship and veneration of the nature-gods. Among the ancient Greeks not merely tolerance but also reverence for other religions was prevailing, and how this contrasts with the wars of extermination and the hatred toward those of other faiths that the worshippers of Jehovah and Allah have so often displayed; and were the wars of extermination of so many who called themselves Christians of a different significance than those atrocities? -- Always the more absurdly, the more idolatrously the worship of the Highest was pursued, the more loveless and brutal the persecution of dissenters! It is indeed a peculiar trait of humanity that only the rare and the few are capable of enthusiasm for the truly high and true, while infinitely more often a false enthusiasm for error and for the base is found!

Not only the interposition of these illusory images, the false objectification of the unconscious as a conscious, works in a disturbing and calamitous way upon humanity, but also the false subjective application of consciousness to the highest unconscious -- the pursuit through ceaseless straining of the understanding of what is not accessible to understanding, what we call brooding, and which can easily lead to madness -- works in a disturbing, indeed destructive way upon the soul of the human being. There is a certain false endeavor to force into concrete form what cannot be grasped in such form, which one might well compare to that of an arithmetician who would strive to grasp the secret of number by attempting, through ceaseless counting, to find the highest and final number. In any case, a false relation of the soul to God always arises in this way, for where the soul should receive composure, elevation, and rest, there arise in it instead distraction, exhaustion, and restless, futile toil.

[p. 406] Let us now turn to the consideration of the other side of the relation of the soul to God -- that is, to the beatifying, elevating side! It has been said that two things are indispensable conditions for this: first, to have the courage to behold that highest unconscious as such and to give oneself over most intimately to such an infinite, immeasurable reality; and secondly, to possess the wealth of love and through its power and depth to conquer and fill the abyss and the utter groundlessness of what appears to us merely as a mystery of the immeasurable. As regards the first, it necessarily has something overwhelming for the more limited spirit of the human being when it attempts to concentrate all the force of its thinking, feeling, and willing upon something absolutely unfathomable, utterly unlimited, a highest unconscious, and to surrender itself to it entirely; it is in a sense a venture, it is, grasped in its full scope, the highest deed of which the human being is capable; but precisely for that reason a certain higher maturity is also required if it is to succeed. Every human being therefore traverses within himself, so to speak, the history of humanity in this as in so many other respects; only late and only in the case of a truly attained maturity does he give up the illusory images in which this infinite initially compressed itself for him, and venture to gaze more clearly and more firmly upon something infinite in itself -- a representation that will easily appear to him at first, like a boundless desert, as something horrifying, more terrifying than elevating. A great inner elevation of the soul must therefore be added if this darkness is to brighten, if the feeling of necessary unconditional surrender to this highest divine is not to appear to it as something dreadful and destructive of all its own existence, all repose upon itself. Yet indeed the more mature and greater the spirit becomes in itself, the more a wide and circumspect cognition of the world illuminates it, the closer this mystery draws to it, the more the sublimity of the reason pervading and determining all that is unconscious, the beauty of all pure revelation of that eternal secret, fills it with joyfulness and veneration, and the more what at first seemed a desolate desert can become for it a source of blessedness. For all that, how could cognition alone suffice to make possible that unconditional surrender to something never wholly knowable, which we have designated as the highest deed of the human soul, were there not added here the second element: the wealth and fullness of love. It must therefore be unlocked, this power of love in the soul; it must be cultivated through its various stages, the higher of which always includes the preceding ones; this yearning impulse must be awakened, which drives the spirit out of its egoistic repose upon itself and can draw it with a power still greater to its own and the primal source of all that otherwise fills it with love, than was the earliest impulse that bound the child's soul to the mother's -- if the highest relation of the soul to God is to become possible: namely, the love of God.

[p. 407] [p. 408] We must first have learned to love the concrete before we become capable of the love of the abstract, and only when the soul has thus ripened through love in general can the inner necessity dawn upon it to strive with ardent love also toward the primal source of everything that we can ever cognize and love -- that mystery in which we live, and move, and have our being. Only in this connection does it become clear to us why the "Gospel of Love," when it entered the world, could have this lofty significance for the history of all humanity, and in what sense an inspired proclaimer of this message could say: "Pursue love!" Only when this ardent impulse has awakened in full force does the courage arise in us to overcome the terrors of that mystery and to fill with the power of love the abyss of a dark immeasurable, and only so does the chasm that at first seemed to separate the soul from its primal source close again; only so can perfect satisfaction and rest come to the soul in its restless striving, and only so is it brought to completion that the soul is truly granted "redemption" through love.

It moves one to singular reflections when one surveys the life of the human being and perceives that it quite properly, and even in the happiest case, presents a chain of deprivations, insufficiencies, and imperfections mixed with few perfectly satisfying moments, that it seems truly impossible that existence, in and of itself, could so shape itself that the higher need of the soul might be truly and lastingly satisfied! -- Necessarily, the cognition thus gained then stirs us to

[p. 409] the question: "why must these imperfections, these agonizing insufficiencies exist? why cannot life itself fulfill the hopes and wishes to which it might almost declare itself entitled through the involuntarily unfolding expectant need? why should the highest goods be merely shown to the human being and never lastingly attained by him?" But from all the distress of these questions, to which an answer fully satisfying to the understanding is simply altogether lacking, just as from all the pain over the suffering of life itself, there is only that "redemption" through the awakening and maintaining of the unconditional love for that divine highest mystery, in and from which we ourselves have proceeded with precisely that higher need, and whose bottomless abyss we can fill only through the power and force of love. It is this love, therefore, that makes the soul certain that that mystery, precisely because we must decidedly acknowledge it as the primal source of all that exists, is also the primal source of all truth, beauty, and of love itself; it is this love that therefore fills us with the consummate trust that a real answer and justification to those questions is indeed present, even if it cannot be wholly present within our cognition; and it is this love, therefore, that in all our restlessness and doubt alone can grant us sufficiency and inner rest. Such rest, however, once gained, then acts in part to open the spirit more brightly, so that it may recognize ever more, even through the veil of that mystery, of the grandeur and reason of the world-order; and in part it also first grants the soul the possibility of truly enjoying, with such composure and so sure a grasp of the present, whatever real happiness and favorable encounters life is from time to time capable of bringing about, and thus of partaking also of that, so to speak, earthly or temporal blessedness that in a restless, despairing mood must necessarily and unconditionally be lost. Both perhaps require a somewhat closer discussion in order truly to arrive at clarity regarding the effects of the attained beautiful relation of the soul to God. In the first respect, there is to be recalled especially the cognition that had already been disclosed to us earlier by another path, and which taught us that the morbid and the evil, the purposeless, the painful, and the deficient -- which brings about the essential suffering of life and constrains, burdens, and torments the free higher spirit -- is by no means grounded in the unconscious of the world, but arises only with the light of our consciousness. The unconscious in and of itself, then -- precisely the pure revelation of a divine -- could not fall ill at all, was free from evil, and was thereby entirely without guilt for that suffering of life that becomes all too keenly felt by the conscious spirit. In any case, it has something far more reassuring and satisfying when we convince ourselves that the evils and torments of life come about, as it were, only artificially through the entry of human consciousness, and that the human being, in his often singular struggle to work his way through to higher development, truly creates all the diseases and adversities that afterward cause him such severe torment, than if we were compelled to seek sickness and evil in the nature of the unconscious itself. The painfulness of life in itself, even so, will admittedly often enough be felt no less keenly, yet on the one hand there remains the hope that the farther not just the individual but humanity as a whole cultivates itself, the more of this perverse, burdensome, tormenting element can be cleared away, and more and more a higher freedom of existence can be established; and on the other hand, this very standpoint promotes an insight into the circumstances of life that must infallibly provide the most correct insights for the perfection of a true art of living.

[p. 410] [p. 411] As for what would secondly be said regarding the higher and right apprehension of that which the fragile life can bring to the soul in the way of happiness and harmony -- or, as the language beautifully puts it, blessedness -- it is clear, on the one hand, that only an already acquired calm and clarity of the soul's attunement can enable one to feel a favorable outward constellation, because it is itself already a happiness and only happiness can receive happiness (for true misfortune will no more receive true happiness than shadow will receive light); and on the other hand, this higher repose -- which, to be sure, must always in itself be the highest vitality -- will, by means of the more freely granted overview and survey of life it gives the soul, essentially contribute, through more correct assessment of the circumstances of nature and of human beings, to bringing about much of that happiness which necessarily and irrecoverably is lost to the soul that is inwardly unfree and has stepped out of its right relation to God. The old saying, "to those who love God, all things must work together for good," here finds its full application, and it gives occasion for singular reflections in life when we observe the Tantalean torments of so many persons to whom from many sides favorable circumstances seem to incline in abundance, and who, without that inner composure, languish amid plenty and lose their lives in a kind of restless despair; -- the whole of what in recent literature has been repeated ad nauseam under the caricatured name of Weltschmerz [world-pain] rests upon this very basis alone.

[p. 412] But the most important and at the same time the most mysterious element in the doctrine of the relation of the soul to God -- that which we should approach even in thought only with a certain reverence -- now remains to be considered: namely, what we may call the interaction -- the transference from the soul to that highest mystery and the transference of this highest mystery to the soul. That such a transference is certainly true and present, that the soul can be something to God and that God directly influences the soul that has developed to consciousness -- for this speaks a priori the law of the equality of essence existing in a certain measure, and for this speaks the higher instinct of all humanity that has awakened to consciousness. As regards the equality of essence existing in a certain measure, we infer it by the same right by which we are led to regard the soul itself as a divine and in its innermost, deepest ground likewise as a mystery. But it cannot be a divine without standing in an actual relation to the original ground of all that is divine -- just as certainly as, by way of analogy and entirely in the material realm, the stone cannot be an earthly thing without being attracted by the earth and for its part also attracting the earth in turn. -- Although therefore, in respect to the active interaction between the soul and God, a relation may exist even more immeasurable and far more unspeakable than between the falling stone and the earth -- which, in attracting the stone and making it fall, must also at all times be attracted by it in some degree --

yet that some active transference is present even in this highest spiritual sphere is utterly undeniable before the tribunal of the highest cognition and the purest conscience of truth. -- As for the higher instinct running through all the history of humanity that was mentioned, it manifests itself partly in what has been said from the most ancient times about the "power of prayer," and partly in what has been praised with manifold reverence in the words "divine aid," "election of grace," "act of the grace of

[p. 413] God." If we pursue these profound teachings further in detail, it must above all be emphasized that here the degree of growth and spiritual development (of inwardness with God) that the soul has reached can infallibly be the measure of the more or less intimate interaction only in the same way as that attraction and being-attracted of the falling stone is all the more powerful the greater and denser its mass. -- Composure of the soul, its purification through ever higher and purer concentration upon what alone is truly appropriate to it, a more vigorous growth of the fundamental idea of its being and activity -- this alone can grant it the possibility of attaining a more living reciprocal relation to the Highest. To be sure, here too everything ultimately dissolves into the mist of a mystery; no one lives who could say thus and so far goes what the purest, most composed direction of the soul toward God is able to accomplish and actually achieves -- partly to strive for a peculiar higher resonance in the nature of the divine itself, partly to gain a special counter-effect of this divine upon itself -- and thus and so far it does not go; but that something of this kind exists, that most singular processes of this kind have from time to time become noticeable in the deepest innermost life of the soul throughout the history of humanity, this stands firm and will always again be acknowledged by individual enlightened ones, even though the truthfulness of it could be so manifoldly forgotten among the mass of people immersed in everyday life.

What may now be said about this from the purely psychological standpoint might be the following. The directing of the entire collected soul that has developed to consciousness -- as reason, as feeling, and as will -- upon and toward that highest divine mystery we designate as worship, as prayer. It would be the utmost presumption to declare what this direction of the soul is to that eternal mystery itself; that it must, however, be something to it, is certain -- if only because everything and anything, and therefore also the worshipper, lives, moves, and has being in it, and the entire act thus in a certain respect becomes an inner process of the divine. What, however, this directing of the soul is and must be to itself is something about which somewhat more can indeed be said. Such composure, such -- I would say -- condensation of the highest bloom of the spirit, when it truly occurs with full inner freedom, beauty, and truth, must have a special counter-effect upon the growth of the soul, upon the purification of feeling, the strengthening of the will, and the clarity of the spirit in general. This is therefore precisely the growing and strengthening through worship, the appearance of which runs through the history of all higher human natures, and which growth through this approach to God has been depicted under the most diverse symbols and in the most manifold, now profound, now abstruse allegories and parables, in the most diverse religions and in the writings of all mystics, mostly symbolically -- symbols that will soon find their resolution for those who have clearly and simply made their own what follows from the above considerations.

[p. 414] As for what still concerns in particular the counter-effect of that highest mystery upon the direction of the worshipper's own life of the soul, it is for this reason almost impossible for the human being to distinguish anything particular of this kind from the general pervading, sustaining, and enlivening, because everything that one is accustomed to summarize under the names of "special guidance," "answered prayer," "particular working of providence," and so forth, even though in the concrete case it appears to us as a quite special influence and a particular act of divine grace, can nevertheless equally well be conceived as a precisely so necessary link in the general organic chain of the universal world-order. Precisely in this respect there lies in everything unconscious, when we now carefully follow it in consciousness, that high infinite wisdom! Whether we consider the mysterious course of the formation of a plant, where microscopically cell attaches to cell until the splendor of the blossom emerges from the abundance of leaves, or whether we trace the wondrous crises of a disease, in which with an astonishing apparent calculation the processes of unconscious life order themselves in such a way that, through sometimes quite unexpected phenomena, they save the life of the patient -- always we have before us something for us unconscious, which seemingly governs with the most remarkable calculation and the most profound deliberateness.

[p. 415] Just as, partly unexpectedly, partly also well presentient, those remarkable crises of diseases occur, so it is also with the often so quite unexpected, and yet often seemingly with special deliberateness guided, events in the destinies of souls in the organism of humanity! Upon wondrous secret threads move the events that reshape lives, move souls and develop souls, and often such a clear deliberateness, such a seemingly definite calculation of circumstances emerges, that we then well believe we may assume here the case of an extraordinary influence from above, although strictly speaking, mindful of that wisdom of the unconscious, we by no means require the assumption of anything extraordinary here, and the waiting longing of prayer only presses toward these events much as that presentiment of the patient precedes the approaching crisis. -- In all of this, however, what befits the human being is grateful reverence rather than an interpretation that goes too far into particulars! On the whole it may not be denied that, just as we must indeed admit the possibility of a certain influence of the more highly developed soul upon that highest mystery, so too a special answering of this Highest to the particular soul by no means lies outside the circle of possibility; for we see elsewhere too that an unconscious divine governance can be modified by the influence of the conscious spirit (if we retain the above examples, we observe that the course of a plant's development and the formation of the blossom can be altered by a changed position toward the light, and that the course of the crisis of a disease can be accelerated by the magnetic influence of a healthy soul, and so forth); but to what extent any such answering is an exception and to what extent it is a link in the general chain of the world-order will in the individual case ultimately always remain an impossibility to decide. Let us only acknowledge, moreover, that it can in itself already perfectly suffice for the soul, apart from any definite decision of that question in the individual case, merely to have penetrated to the clear conviction of an unconditional repose in that highest mysterious primal source, to the most decisive certainty of the highest truth, beauty, and love within it, and to the most complete trust that the more beautifully, truly, and lovingly the mystery of our own soul is developed, the more powerfully, intimately, and reciprocally the relation of the fundamental idea of our own existence to that eternal primal being will shape itself -- indeed, must shape itself. It is therefore not subject to any doubt that, precisely for this reason, the summit of the entire development of our

[p. 416] [p. 417] higher life lies in the relation of the soul to God, and it can be fully comprehended that only from this height can that higher breath of life pour itself forth not only over the region of consciousness but also over all the unconscious within us -- that breath which gives the first condition for that earthly happiness which we call the health of the soul, and which the investigations now following will have to illuminate still further.

### i. Von der Seelengesundheit und Seelenkrankheit — i. On the health and illness of the soul

Perhaps in no part of psychology has the neglect of the remarkable relation of the conscious to the unconscious spread so much darkness and difficulty, and so many errors, as in the investigations concerning health of the soul and illness of the soul. We, in the course here chosen and justified, may at once set forth a quite different and very simple fundamental view by saying: just as in the highest sense all our life is a life of the soul, so also in this sense no other illness is conceivable in us than illness of the soul. For that very reason, naturally, also no other health than health of the soul!

For both are mutually conditioning concepts. Only what can fall ill can also lay claim to the attribute of health. Of God and of the immediately divine, and likewise also of the absolutely unconscious -- for example, the stone -- it can no more be said that they are ill than that they are healthy, and for precisely this reason the innermost monad of the soul, insofar as it is a divine, is likewise unconditionally free of both.

If one now asks more precisely: what then is that which can be healthy or also ill in the soul? then from

the foregoing it is first clear that here its innermost abstract being-in-itself may never be named -- this, as our investigations of the growth and decline of the soul showed, is capable only of an increase or a diminution of its divine energy; -- rather, what can be ill and also healthy in our soul is: the appearance of the soul in life, that entire spectrum, as it is cast by the one divine ray of light into the dark, ethereal, substantial existence, thus presenting the concept of the human being. -- In these words one may already consider the essential key to be contained for obtaining the right answer to the old and much-discussed questions: whether the soul can fall ill? how bodily malformations can affect the processes of the soul? and how even external influences, medicinal substances, and food can have the power to make the spirit ill? and so forth. Still clearer, however, will the concepts about this become when one attends to the peculiar relation that exists between the essence and the appearance of the soul in general. For just as the former is indeed the highest ground also of every physical, bodily appearance of the organism -- of heaviness, warmth, solidity, and so forth -- but in and of itself has absolutely nothing to do with these appearances themselves, so too that modification of the life-appearance of the soul which we call illness can in and of itself have absolutely nothing to do with the innermost essence of the soul. Is it not indeed the same with the evil of the world and that highest divine mystery in which all things and we ourselves live, and move, and have our being, and which, although indeed the original ground of each and every existence, yet in and of itself cannot be called partaking of what we call the evil and illness of the world. Indeed, if one wishes to make this relation of

[p. 419] essence to appearance still clearer through an example, one need only consider how the concept of a mathematical figure, say a triangle, although it contains the original ground of the possibility of all actual triangles, yet has absolutely nothing to do with whether a real triangle is made of iron, of wood, or of stone, and just as little with whether some triangle has been bent or compressed.

Let so much then be said in general about the relation of health and illness to the essence of the soul! We now have to consider in detail what actually occurs in the life of the soul in the way of health and illness.

Here too the distinction between the conscious and the unconscious of the soul will immediately yield important and new results. Unconsciously the monad of our existence first lives itself out; the unconscious is everywhere the primitive, it is the basis upon which consciousness is only gradually built up, and we have become manifoldly aware that the appearance of conscious soul-life can be developed in its purity and completeness only when beforehand a purity and perfection of the development of the unconscious had been achieved. The first significant consequence that we may therefore draw for the subject occupying us here from this cognition is the following: "The health of the entire circle of unconscious soul-life is the first condition for the development of a perfectly healthy conscious soul-life." Before we may go further in this, however, it is to be asked: what is to be understood by health of the absolutely unconscious soul-life?

Above all, one will here have to look back upon that earliest period of life when all the existence of the human being is still a thoroughly unconscious one -- upon the period of its first formation. We call it a healthy course of development when gradually, in the right manner corresponding to the peculiar idea of precisely this existence, through ever-continuing transformation, the organs grow and develop without being disturbed by the influence of particular diseases, whose impact precisely here is most apt to manifest itself through disruption of normal formal relations. Here now lies a subject in regard to which it is especially to be wished that whoever wants to concern himself with the science of the psyche should have a conception of the origin, conditioned by formative diseases, of all that is called congenital defective formation, and which later often shows so essential an influence upon the kind of development of the spirit. Here too the truth of that Aristotelian word makes itself felt again: "And for this reason [dia touto], then, the consideration of the soul belongs to the natural philosopher, either entirely or as so constituted" [e pasa e e toiaute]. Certainly! whoever is entirely unfamiliar with the history of such formative defects (they are not without reason called vitia congenita [congenital faults], thereby also intimating their moral influence), who does not know how small the disturbances often need to be that produce quite false formations of the embryo, to whom it is unknown that inflammation of the brain in the fetus usually causes the child to be born with water on the brain, or that similar diseases of the thoracic organs developing in this period tend to become the cause of congenital heart defects -- malformations that lead to a life full of anxiety, a quite literal anguish of the heart, just as the previously mentioned ones lead to a life without developed higher intelligence -- how shall it become clear to such a person in what manner already in the world of unconscious soul-life the essential basis is given

[p. 420] [p. 421] from which later either a full health or a morbid condition of the spirit can proceed!

At this point it is naturally impossible to pursue and expound all these things in detail; but it is certain that only the greatest possible understanding of all these remarkable formative processes can give the clearest possible insight into how much the health of the conscious spirit is conditioned by a healthy unconscious bodily development. Always only where, in accordance with the peculiarity of the monad of the soul, through its peculiar unconscious working, a truly healthy formation and maintenance of the organism has emerged in the fetal period, will the possibility be given that in the later periods of existence too that lawful, disease-free course of life may develop which we designate precisely as bodily health, or as health of the unconscious soul-life.

To be sure, the results of a healthy first fetal development are not alone sufficient to preserve the health of the unconscious life in later times as well; rather, suitable conditions from without must favor the maintenance of health, and in particular it will later become an important task of awakened consciousness to preserve and protect the unconscious through the intelligence of the spirit. From that point on, consciousness in us has, so to speak, the debt of gratitude to discharge toward the unconscious out of which it has developed, and just as all beauty, all power, all significance of the spirit has been achieved only because beforehand an unconscious unfolded itself beautifully and powerfully and significantly, so now the spirit must in turn apply all circumspection and all wisdom to continually maintain the so easily disturbed course of the unconscious life of its soul and guard it in its integrity.

[p. 422] Moreover, between the later course of life and the first development there is, with respect to the unconsciously performed formative processes, no truly essential difference. All our life, insofar as it unconsciously maintains the formation of the organism, is an ever-continuing procreation; we live only inasmuch as we perpetually die and are perpetually newly generated, and insofar the art of living of the conscious spirit, which later watches over this ceaseless dying and generating within us proceeding with lawfulness and beauty, has essentially nothing else to do than what the mother accomplishes who, when she carries a newly germinating life in her womb, watches over it so that it is not prematurely separated or otherwise harmed.
