After such discussions, we may now answer the question "what is to be understood by health of the absolutely unconscious soul-life?" quite briefly as follows: it is the relation, perfectly appropriate to the archetype of precisely this human being, in the ceaselessly continuing generation, destruction, and constant regeneration of his temporal bodily appearance. Everything that we call intake of nourishment, blood-formation, nutrition, respiration, secretion, sexual productivity, heat-production, conditioning of innervation currents and muscular excitation, and so forth, is encompassed by this relation, and how much the health of conscious soul-life depends upon all these processes, which the divine monad of our idea directs long before any becoming-conscious and also with developed consciousness perpetually without its cooperation, will presently emerge.

One circumstance must, however, first be particularly mentioned: namely, that the concept of this health by no means has anything special to do with the lesser or higher dignity of the soul in and of itself. To the nobler and more significant nature it is just as applicable as to the lower and common one, and indeed something similar applies to the animal. It is therefore expressly to be noted that the designation archetype of the human being here refers only to the particular monad underlying each individual, and that, however greatly the ideas may differ, yet for each of them only what is particularly appropriate to it in appearance can be meant. It is, however, to be observed that every organism, according to its particular individuality, through that condition which we have presently come to know as health of unconscious life, always comes to outward appearance in an especially favorable manner; thus, even if it cannot thereby assume a different, nobler type, it will nonetheless receive a peculiar radiance of precisely its existence -- a radiance that is absent when health is disturbed, and that will always betray itself to the conscious spirit of another soul through a particular kind of beauty, as if through an outward symbol. Thus, for instance, the peculiar radiance and bloom that we see spread over a still quite tender healthy child, or over a sleeping healthy adult (where in both cases the effect of inner spirit can by no means make itself felt), will immediately be to the knowing observer the sign of the healthy unconscious soul governing there; and a peculiar pleasure, a certain delight, will infallibly arise in us from such contemplation. Moreover, the counter-effect of such a condition upon one's own life will be a very powerful one; only by virtue of it does the organism become as self-sufficient as is at all possible for it, only through it does it resist more powerfully the violent destructive influences from without, only through it does it gain significant power to act determiningly upon external nature in its turn, and later, in the service of the

[p. 423] [p. 424] developed consciousness, to carry out what the will of the spirit resolves. Naturally, this counter-effect of health is also one more ray to heighten the radiance that this condition diffuses over the organism, and since the organism thereby also becomes capable of a more salutary influence upon other organisms, it can contribute to presenting a more joyous appearance.

Now that we have thus considered the characteristics of the health of unconscious soul-life, the consideration of the health of conscious soul-life will presently form a second major task of these reflections. In order first to establish the concept of this health, one must recall that the conscious world of the spirit is likewise something that develops in a peculiarly organic manner, something that manifests its life in constant inner movement, something never finished and self-enclosed, indeed something infinite in itself, and that, just as of unconscious life it was said that it perpetually oscillates between ceaseless dying and ceaseless new generation, so of the conscious it is to be said that it perpetually oscillates between sinking back into the unconscious and being born anew to consciousness. This view is so important because only when one has learned to regard in this way the inner phenomenon of consciousness as something ceaselessly in motion within itself, perpetually perishing and newly arising, constantly developing itself further or declining, will one be capable of forming a conception of how much something analogous to what we said of the health of unconscious soul-life holds true at the same time for the health of the conscious. By health of conscious soul-life, or as health of the soul pure and simple (as one is wont to designate it in contrast to bodily health, or the health of unconscious soul-life), is accordingly to be understood: the relation perfectly appropriate to the archetype of precisely this particular psychic individuality, of the vital movements of thinking, feeling, and willing, in ceaselessly continuing generation, submersion, and re-summoning of the conscious world of the spirit. In this definition one will recall what was said further above about the "natural" in the life of the conscious soul. It is precisely this natural quality that also distinguishes the thinking, feeling, and willing of a healthy soul. Herewith again, no particularly noble and significant character of the soul is meant or designated; rather, it can occur and actually does occur that this health of the soul appears at the most various levels of spiritual energy, in the crude soul of the savage as in the deeply cultivated European, in the child as in the man and in the aged; but just as we said that in the health of unconscious life this condition spreads its own peculiar bloom and radiance over the bodily life and heightens its self-sufficiency and energy, so we find also that this pre-eminently so-called "health of the soul" not only always invests the individuality of the spirit in which it is observed, as far as it is capable thereof, with a certain charm, but also elevates precisely the self-sufficiency and the capacity for action, the capability, of this spiritual individuality to its maximum. Only when one understands the doctrine of the health of the conscious soul in this way -- that one comprehends under it the normal course of all the inner vital movements of the spirit -- does it acquire a definite meaning and higher significance; one then looks upon the great inner manifoldness of this entire region, one considers the constant alternation of the sinking and rising of consciousness, and only thus first gains, so to speak, the right to distinguish a healthy and a morbid condition in this alternation of appearance, quite as in the unconscious sphere of life. Indeed, one could in truth rediscover and demonstrate all the vital movements of the unconscious -- such as circulation of the blood, respiration, sexual productivity, digestion, assimilation, nutrition, and secretion, as well as muscular force and sensory life -- in the spiritual realm, and could establish a dietetics of the spirit entirely upon these premises, whereby the concept of actual spiritual health would have to be made ever more vivid. In this sense it at once becomes palpable that the healthy spirit, if it is to maintain its health, must take in nourishment, and indeed appropriate nourishment; that an inner circulation of representations must proceed neither with feverish intensity nor with sluggish slowness; that a certain breathing-through and refreshment in pure, higher feelings remains indispensable for the conscious soul, even apart from spiritual nourishment; and that an actual spiritual productivity, an outpouring of the soul, appears indispensable if that inner alternating life of the psyche is to persist in a truly healthy course.

[p. 425] [p. 426] Only now, after we have in these reflections arrived at clear and -- when I consider the older psychologies -- indeed quite new concepts regarding both the health of the unconscious and that of the conscious soul, is it time to dwell longer upon what might be said about the relation of these two forms of health to one another.

With the well-known expression "a sound mind in a sound body" [mens sana in corpore sano], that particularly desirable condition is generally designated where conscious and unconscious activity of the soul simultaneously remain in a perfectly regulated and natural course, and herewith the ideal of perfect health would indeed be given. Inasmuch, however, as all health can only be individual, and for each individual person represents a particular relation of the manifoldness of his appearance, the various kinds of perfect health would never be entirely alike among themselves, and given the constant transformation of the organism that it undergoes in the development of life, even in one and the same individual this condition, when it occurs at all in manifold instances and over longer periods, is never throughout and always the same. This completely regulated state, then, this wholly and thoroughly natural quality -- in short, this ideal of health -- is in general, like everything perfect in life, when it does approximately occur here and there, a great rarity; rather, in this respect too, human existence finds itself consigned to a perpetual and most manifold oscillation -- an oscillation that will manifest itself partly in imperfections now of the health of the conscious, now of the unconscious soul-life, and partly in a certain unequal state of the health of both spheres relative to each other. A perfect equilibrium between these two spheres is among the things that almost never occur; rather, we find almost always now the one, now the other, predominating. If one now observes such cases more closely, one can arrive at establishing many an important law in this respect. Thus it is first unmistakable that for the growth of the actual inner monad of the soul, or of the being-in-itself of the idea, in the earliest periods of life-development a certain predominance of the health of the unconscious is just as important and conducive as in the later periods a certain predominance of the health of the conscious spirit becomes. Precisely because the unconscious represents the primitive, upon whose ground the higher blossom of the spirit is first to unfold, and precisely because this latter will emerge powerfully and significantly only when a maximally healthy organization and function, especially of the higher nerve structures, has been achieved, there is needed, as it were, for a beautiful human development first the predominance of health in the unconscious. Yet it is easy to see that a continued condition of the same kind can no longer have a favorable effect where, as in later periods, the predominance of the conscious spirit over everything unconscious within us has more and more become the very task of existence. It gives occasion for singular reflections here when one finds that, even in the regular course of life-development, at a certain height the energy of the health of the unconscious is, in the majority, destined to diminish somewhat, while on the other hand the clarity and maturity of the conscious spirit should and will, if its health is preserved, ceaselessly increase. More of this will yet emerge when we come to consider what in the soul is altogether perishable and what is eternal!

[p. 427] [p. 428] A second law in this respect may then be expressed approximately thus: Given the infinite diversity of the inner quality of souls -- now a higher and more divine, now a lesser and properly human peculiarity -- the predominance of the health of now the one, now the other sphere of their appearance is of very different significance. For the remarkable behavior reveals itself that where the quality is a lesser and, I might say, a coarser one, the health and beauty of the conscious soul during the more mature periods of life can actually be elevated by the occurrence of a lesser, indeed a disturbed health in the unconscious. The peculiar charm and the certain healthy beauty and force of the spirit that in such individuals often first emerges during bodily illness, and also sometimes disappears again when bodily health is once more in full bloom, is a very remarkable psychological fact. How frequently, for example, has it occurred that young, vitally robust country girls, when the so-called developmental diseases befell them, fell into peculiar auto-magnetic states in which a fineness of spirit, a power of thought-sequence, and a depth of feeling emerged that were soon lost again once these illnesses had passed. Similarly one observes the same in the male sex. A case in which, after a brain injury with loss of brain substance, a finer kind of intelligence and a higher expressiveness of speech appeared is known to me in particular. Here too this higher spiritual health was lost as soon as the healing of the injury had taken place. Moreover, everyone feels that the massive health of the unconscious in an athlete does not at the same time coincide with the higher health of the spirit in a deeply thinking sage.

[p. 429] If, then, the foregoing explained a certain elevation of the conscious spirit through illness of the unconscious, a third law must on the other hand declare that a too imperfect health of the unconscious will yet also impair the health of a higher consciousness, and will not permit the organism of thinking, feeling, and willing to appear in its vital movement with the beauty, force, and naturalness that it would have been capable of displaying under the condition of a greater bodily health. In all very significant natures one will therefore find that a high degree of health also in the unconscious furnishes, so to speak, the support so that everything that such a psyche, in the productivity of its rich existence, has to bring to revelation can actually be carried through, and thus herein lies once again the indication that it must always be and remain one of the most important tasks of the spirit to watch carefully over the maintenance and preservation of unconscious life as well.

[p. 430]

### β . Von der Seelenkrankheit — b. On illness of the soul

The illnesses of the soul in our sense will again divide into those of the unconscious and those of the conscious soul-life. Here too we must begin by making clear to ourselves what is to be understood by illness of the unconscious soul-life.

The manifold life-manifestations of the unconscious, as they continually reveal themselves in the ceaseless transformation, generation, re-destruction, and regeneration of the substance of the organism -- this so highly multifarious steady doing which we designate now as respiration, now as nutrition and growth, circulation of the blood, secretion, reproduction, and so forth -- can and must, since they constantly stand in conflict with innumerable influences of the world, often be disturbed in the most manifold ways. Such a disturbance, such a hindrance, is however not yet an illness. The pulmonary life may be deprived of the necessary pure air, and breathing will become imperfect and constricted, but the person is not yet ill; the digestive life may be deprived of its nutrient substances, the person hungers, thirsts, and yet is not yet ill; and so throughout all these individual life-manifestations that we call unconscious or merely bodily functions! Illness is therefore something certainly new, a something that arises and lives itself out according to its own laws in an organic manner, as a product of such conflicts of the organism's own life with the alien life of the world, and indeed lives itself out upon the individual life-manifestations, the bodily or spiritual functions themselves. This new something, this idea of disease, which has been generated as something in a sense parasitic between the idea of life on the one hand and the ideas of the world on the other, grows, lives itself out, multiplies, dies itself or kills the organism according to definite and very remarkable laws and relations, of which further discussion cannot be had here, and which were to be discussed here only insofar as they are capable of giving us information about the disease-phenomena also in the circle of conscious soul-life. The organism, then, in whose life-circle such a disease-idea is living itself out, we call ill; its own life-idea is disturbed, offended by this alien life, but nevertheless the inner monad of its peculiar existence persists just as certainly and securely as, as we said above by way of example, the idea or the concept of the triangle remains undisturbed no matter how many actually bodily represented triangles I may dissolve or bend. All illness therefore affects only the appearance of the original divine idea of a certain life, not the idea itself. However, in the disease itself too we can, in sharper thinking, distinguish its fundamental idea, its monad, and the signs or symptoms of it that emerge in the altered appearances of the organism. This monad of the disease is naturally one which, since it lives itself out only in the appearance of other ideas, can never itself come to consciousness; it will always remain an unconscious one, and for that very reason it can also be generated and born only in the unconscious of our life. Our conscious spirit cannot generate something unconscious; it generates and gives birth only to thoughts, which themselves exist again only for a conscious being. The consciousless, then, is generated only by the unconscious, and thus the in-itself-unconscious idea of disease too can proceed only from the unconscious of our being. These reflections are very remarkable and consequential for all understanding of

[p. 431] [p. 432] disease in general, and especially also for its relation to the conscious spirit, for from them it follows first of all that, since illness has its actual root only in the unconscious soul-life, and the idea of disease can be generated only here, a disease rooted solely in the conscious spirit is impossible, although it will never fail that the radiations of every diseased state must spread themselves at once over the entire soul, precisely because the soul is in its fundamental nature through and through a unity. Precisely for this reason, then -- because the root of disease is always to be sought in the unconscious -- we already instinctively associate with the expression "disease" simply and solely the concept of the disease-idea living itself out in the governance and upon the appearance of the unconscious soul. If, on the other hand, a special reflex of such diseased unconscious life strikes over upon the conscious spirit that has come to development, in such a way that the disturbance of the spirit becomes a chief symptom of the illness, then we at once also distinguish this illness by a special name: we call it disturbance of the soul, mental illness, and so forth. From these grounds it is therefore clear that one must absolutely separate from the concept of actual disease what, as abnormal conditions, is generated purely in conscious life -- namely, the conditions of error, callousness, and vice -- and that at most these conditions may be regarded in a figurative sense as "moral diseases."

At present it is our concern to give an overview of the essentially different forms of illness of the soul: first, how it arises in the unconscious and also spreads principally in this region; secondly, how, while also originating in the unconscious, it lives itself out essentially in the appearance of the conscious spirit. The former opens the vast realm of the actual, or so-called bodily diseases, and this realm divides itself according to the following three fundamental forms: the first encompasses the numerous host of "fevers." Here we see, as it were, the primordial disease, which the great world-historical epidemics represent, the one that can spread over all other disease-forms and from which so many others arise. The second encompasses the various forms of "inflammation" (the secondary disease, which repeats the general fever-process in the localized organ), and the third presents "malformation" (the tertiary disease, which tends to be added later to the foregoing and mostly permanently alters the formation of the organism). In all these diseases, then, a certain particular idea foreign to the organism's own life appears and lives itself out parasitically, and indeed always essentially upon the appearance of the unconscious soul, whether consciousness has not yet developed at all (as in the fetus) or whether it has already actually been disclosed (as in the adult). As a consequence of the peculiarity of such a special idea, and according to its particular nature, everything that the soul otherwise unconsciously regulates according to its own inner law is now determined otherwise and in a new particular manner; respiration, circulation, digestion, nutrition, and so forth no longer proceed as before; rather, through their manifold, often very strange modifications, precisely the alien new idea now brings itself to appearance and completes its life-cycle upon them, so that only when it has traversed and concluded this cycle can the proper life of the inborn idea once again manifest itself through the accustomed former modes of appearance. It is in any case important that one should first have gained at least a

[p. 433] [p. 434] reasonably sufficient overview of these disease-forms that manifest essentially in the unconscious -- which in their immeasurable manifoldness constitute the chief study of the physician -- so that one may then also better learn to understand the second realm: the realm of those diseases that manifest principally in the conscious spirit and are commonly called "illnesses of the soul" exclusively.

In order to grasp this field correctly as well, it must be borne in mind that, just as in unconscious life the circulation of the blood, transformation, respiration, reproduction, and so forth form their own circles of existence, so too in the conscious spirit feelings, representations and trains of thought, as well as acts of will, represent life-circles that, in whole or in part, can likewise suffer manifold disturbances and hindrances in their conflict with the world. Here too such individual disturbances, which we call suffering, errors, false willing, are not yet illness in and of themselves; but they can and must be symptoms -- signs

all that is morbid which seizes the spirit. It has already been stated above that true diseases — those in which a peculiar unconscious idea of a special parasitic life manifests itself — can no more arise within the conscious spirit than a shadow within a light, or a second centre within a circle, and that only as an analogue of them do the so-called moral diseases, the "addictions" and "vices" and "delusion," occur. How, nevertheless, the conscious life of the soul can indeed be seized in a truly morbid manner, although a special unconscious idea of disease is never generated there, becomes comprehensible when one considers the following: the whole soul is in itself always a unity — a monad —

even though within it, as something higher, the free conscious spirit is able to develop itself out of what remains as the unconscious. Every disease, even one whose symptoms manifest themselves almost exclusively in bodily processes, therefore acts — when in this soul

[p. 435] a conscious spirit has already unfolded — always to some degree upon this spirit as well; it casts its shadow over it, as it were, unsettling, clouding, altering in some respect the conscious life of the soul. This clouding, however, will neither disturb the essence of the spirit nor will it be lasting; rather, it vanishes completely together with the disease. On the other hand, it also happens that diseases occur which not only momentarily and faintly cloud that highest blossom of the soul, the conscious spirit, but that certain ideas of disease, likewise originally manifesting themselves in the unconscious, behave in such a way that they continually direct their full force toward producing lasting disturbances of the life of the brain, thereby persistently altering the cognition, feeling, and will of the spirit in a qualitative manner, and thus bringing about, as it were, displacements of the spirit. To make this relationship clear through a simile, one may recall that in the plant the root is always an essential and original part, but that, just as some plants are almost nothing but root, in others almost everything seems to go into stem and leaves, so that finally (as, for example, in Fucus) the root can become a formation scarcely noticeable in itself. Just so is it to be said of the idea of disease that its actual generation always belongs to the unconscious soul, and that the root of all disease phenomena always lies here, but that the phenomena of disease themselves differ insofar as in many cases they too remain essentially concentrated only upon the unconscious, while in others the entire spread of the disease turns toward the conscious spirit, and there produces in thinking, feeling, and willing the most violent displacements, or, as one says, derangements, while the life and phenomena of the actual root manifest themselves outwardly only to a slight degree. The most faithful image of such relations lies in this simile, and one will, if one thinks oneself into it, easily comprehend in what sense that which is commonly designated by the name of mental diseases or disturbances of the soul must be grasped — namely, not as an actual disease of the spirit, but as a disease-phenomenon manifested at the spirit, a disease-phenomenon which will always emerge the more easily, the less the spirit had previously risen to its healthy height and power, and the more it had already been estranged from its truly divine direction by error, impure feeling, and action.

[p. 436] It is now not difficult to understand that just as transiently almost every fever or inflammation must generate certain phantasies and delusions — a passing mental illness, as it were — so certain chronic malformations, alterations of the blood mass, and so forth will occur which, by permanently altering the generation of innervation in the brain, the conductivity of the nerve fibre, and so forth, also produce a lasting retuning of the spirit, continually altering and displacing cognition, feeling, and will in the most peculiar manner, and thus conditioning what we call the second realm of illness of the soul, or the disease-phenomena of the spirit — madness, melancholy, and so forth. As has been said, whether or not this effect upon the spirit shall occur will also depend upon the height to which the growth of the soul, the development of the spirit, has already advanced. As it was shown earlier that a height of the spirit exists from which a descent is scarcely possible any longer, so must it also be repeated here that the freer and greater the spirit had already unfolded itself, the less power the influence of disease upon the spirit has to produce true disease-phenomena at the spirit

[p. 437] It follows from this at once how great the difference is between the susceptibility to disease of the unconscious and the conscious soul. The unconscious, although resting through and through upon something divine and eternal, is, because referred in its manifestations to the perishable, something everywhere easily vulnerable and easily falling ill: the strongest organism of a human being does not, in its formation resting upon the working of the unconscious, resist a few drops of prussic acid, or a few grains of arsenic, or a very small heart wound, and if these influences destroy it outright, there are a hundred others sufficient to generate an idea of disease, which then, according to its own peculiarity and no longer according to the organism's own idea, directs a life that is thenceforth to be called diseased. It is entirely different with the conscious spirit once it has attained its maturity: it is in this respect invulnerable like the air; it can endure fierce sufferings and pains; it can struggle in the most tremendous exertions — but insofar as it is a divine idea that has attained self-consciousness, it has grasped the thought of its own eternity, and thereby it becomes immortal even in its manifestations — indeed, what particularly concerns us here, it is also for this reason in and of itself entirely beyond the reach of disease. Only in this sense can what has always been revered as the higher power of the spirit become fully intelligible! One will comprehend from this why this higher conscious spirit often seems to remain so entirely untouched by the fiercest sufferings and diseases of its own unconscious soul, and to what extent it enjoys a freedom that has been so manifoldly admired in the destinies of great, enlightened natures often severely tested in life! Nevertheless, we need only reflect more sharply upon the development and relations of a spirit that has attained even such a height to become aware of how precise and intimate the connection is in which it too remains throughout life with all unconscious radiations of the soul. As we said earlier that there is no stronger stirring of the unconscious which does not in some way spread its vibrations up into the heights of consciousness, so we recognize that even the most vigorous, brightest spirit must incessantly remain inseparable from the unconscious of its soul and be continually and decidedly influenced by it. A poison that destroys the unconscious will also benight consciousness, and a disease that develops in the unconscious will always in some way alter the feeling, thinking, and action of the conscious spirit.

[p. 438] To be sure, the power of the spirit strengthened in consciousness also has, conversely, a great influence upon the unconscious; from it proceeds, as was already shown above in the consideration of the health of the soul, that through its vigilance the unconscious is guarded and preserved, and one can say with certainty that this power will absolutely be able to protect the unconscious at least to the extent that, if not all disease can be kept away from it, yet that disease is truly kept away from it which could work over from the unconscious upon consciousness, upon the thoughts, feelings, and actions of the spirit, in such a way that lasting derangements, true disease-phenomena, might assert themselves there. The bright spirit of an Aristotle, Kant, Leibniz, and the like will therefore not abolish the possibility of disease, but will be a guarantee that phenomena of mental illness will certainly not occur in it. Where, on the other hand, a lesser energy of the conscious spirit is present, where it, in that period of life in which it should have matured and strengthened in action, feeling, and thought, is still weak and sunk in error, false feeling, and unhealthy action — there diseases that develop in the unconscious will not only always work over upon consciousness with greater force and produce true disease-phenomena at the spirit, but diseases will in themselves also arise more easily, because the power that is destined to guard the unconscious presents itself only imperfectly and in a weakened state.

[p. 439] Before we consider that second realm of illnesses of the soul, which encompasses those manifesting at the conscious spirit, in as much detail as is necessary here for an overview of the entire developmental history of the soul, it remains to draw attention, on the one hand, to the relationship in which certain very elevated and healthy phenomena of the spirit come into very close contact with certain actual disease-phenomena, and on the other hand, to indicate in particular from which originally merely agitated states of the spirit the transition to actual mental disease-phenomena can most easily be made. With respect to the former, it is very remarkable that certain great, independent directions of the healthy spirit occur which, precisely because they too concentrate the power of consciousness in individual directions to the highest degree, have often given occasion for it to have long been acknowledged, and already since Plato, that between some of the most sublime workings and visions of the spirit and some decided forms of madness there exists a very great approximation. The boundary of such states is certainly harder to determine than it appears at first glance, for neither the internally correct sequence, nor the height and beauty of thoughts and feelings alone, can always offer sufficient distinguishing marks. Between the visionary who has fallen into religious madness, who beholds the Madonna surrounded by angels, and a Raphael, to whom she appears in the moment of artistic inspiration and who retains her image permanently, the difference at first seems by far not so great as between the mentally ill and the mentally healthy it is usually assumed to be. Upon closer and sharper consideration, however, a considerable difference soon emerges, and it reveals itself, apart from the different internal development of the states, also outwardly, particularly in that the diseased direction always utterly lacks the capacity for complete and sustained productivity. Never will the sick visionary leave behind a picture of his vision which, like the immortal creation of a Raphael, brings to intuition for centuries the power of a great, healthy thought, and never will the declamations of a mad nun who sees hell and purgatory produce a work like the Divine Comedy of Dante. When Plato therefore says: "Inasmuch as that prophesying is holier and more honourable than this soothsaying, both in name and in fact, so much more excellent also, according to the testimony of the ancients, is a divine madness than a merely human understanding" — what is meant here is not a diseased condition, but that higher unfreedom of the spirit in which it, itself seized by a higher divine idea, gives up, as it were, the working for itself and according to chosen purposes, and is driven onward, half unconsciously as it were, no longer by self-chosen but by given purposes. In short, the higher, or, as Plato quite rightly calls it, the divine madness arises only when in the spirit of the human being — that is, in the soul that has arrived at its own true centre, the self-consciousness of the idea — the sway of an idea that has penetrated from the great mystery of the world, unconscious to us,

[p. 440] [p. 441] asserts itself and becomes dominant; the lower, or properly morbid, madness, on the other hand, comes about when a special, alien idea of disease that has arisen in the inner world of the unconscious brings itself to dominance and directs the appearance of the organism not according to the laws proper to the soul but according to alien laws, and directs it in such a way that from this unconscious realm consciousness too is overrun and disturbed. As for the transitions which lead from states of the soul that are still healthy in themselves but passionately agitated to mental diseases, the following may yet be remarked. According to their different natures, emotions are indeed, and especially, capable of acting as truly disease-generating moments through the strong resonance they always have in the unconscious, and it may be regarded as a general law that diseases whose idea was first stimulated in the unconscious from the sphere of consciousness also always tend in their inner spread and development particularly to cast their shadow back toward the conscious world of the spirit. This is to be regarded as blow and counter-blow: disturbances of the blood and nutritive life, for example, which were occasioned by violent and sustained emotions, will in their course always more readily encroach again upon the phenomena of consciousness, and thus more easily produce true disease-phenomena of the spirit, than, for example, similar disturbances of the blood and nutritive life which were originally conditioned only by physical causes — colds, dietary errors, and so forth. A case of this kind thus occurs, for instance, when prolonged grief and sorrow have already disturbed the unconscious life and when some violent excitement, sudden fright, pain, or something of the sort, then generates a disease

[p. 442] which now immediately strikes over into conscious life and conditions melancholy or mania. Ophelia is such an example, and every asylum presents a multitude of unfortunates who became ill in precisely this way. Indeed, from this standpoint one will also understand why what we called moral disease above — the sinking of consciousness into error and vice — so often likewise prepares the transition into madness and frenzy. The less the higher intelligence appears as the light of the soul, the more aberrations of the spirit, now here, now there, direct the course of unregulated representations, the more violent the agitation of feeling will become, the more pain, hatred, love, joy, and sorrow will rage through one another, and the more easily, from this quarter, true disease-phenomena first of the unconscious and then of the spirit will be added to the moral disease. We perceive accordingly that the observation of these transitions from healthy mental states to diseased ones affords many important insights into many individual occurrences of the diseased spirit and into disease-phenomena at the spirit in general. — We now proceed to the

### y . Besondern Erwägung der Krankheitserscheinungen am Geiste — y. Special Consideration of the Disease-Phenomena at the Spirit

For these diseases, however, a quite similar classification will first present itself as the one according to which those belonging to the unconscious alone were divided — for we have shown that these too take root not in the soul that has attained higher consciousness in and of itself, but only in the unconscious of the same. There will accordingly be: 1. Primary disease-phenomena of the spirit; these are the general ones, those which impair all radiations of the spirit simultaneously, those especially similar to fever and which

[p. 443] often combine with it — to this category belongs mania, raving. 2. Secondary disease-phenomena; these are the partial ones, similar to inflammation, and here belongs madness (monomanias, folly, and melancholy). 3. Tertiary disease-phenomena; these are those that invariably accompany a significant malformation of the brain: imbecility, idiocy, cretinism. Frenzy, madness, and imbecility thus relate to one another entirely as do fever, inflammation, and malformation, and the kinship of the corresponding states is unmistakable. Fever, like mania, is, as one customarily says, an acute condition, runs its course in certain periods and in fairly definite times, and also soon leads to a definite outcome — either in recovery or death, or by transition into chronic states. One will also never find a mania that does not combine with fever symptoms, particularly with violent agitations of the vascular system, so that here it always emerges especially clearly how profoundly the unconscious life is primitively affected in this illness. It is remarkable, moreover, and has not previously been sufficiently utilized for the history of so-called mental diseases, how several of them, and mania in particular, can be evoked by way of experiment, and indeed from stimulations of the unconscious life. Several poisons, especially those obtained from the pollen of hemp, or also opium smoke, will not fail in mediocre spirits to produce the phenomena of mania in their full terribleness, and thereby provide an especially clear insight into the genesis of those diseases which manifest themselves at the spirit. Precisely the same is the case with the other extreme of this series, imbecility, in which likewise the conditioning by states of the unconscious life emerges so evidently. This sad condition, in which the brain has always become, either through deficiency of initial formation or through later disease, one that is in some way malformed (stunted, hydrocephalic, altered in substance), therefore also presents itself as a twofold one, in that either no ray of the spirit has developed at all, or one that had already developed has been dimmed again. Whether the one or the other, however, some alteration of the brain structure produced by unconscious life will always be clearly recognizable here, and thus these cases always serve to confirm what we said earlier about the conditions of consciousness in general. For even though it had already become clear that the development of consciousness in and of itself can always only be grasped as a miracle, and that we are only capable of measuring the conditions under which this miracle occurs, we see here it now definitely proven and demonstrated that the impairment of these conditions also immediately inhibits, indeed often truly abolishes, the manifestation of the spirit.

[p. 444] Among these conditions, the first was the development of a special, peculiarly impressionable system — that is, the nervous system — and the third: that for the possibility of preserving these impressions and comparing them, sufficient field be always given in a rich and vigorous brain formation. In this respect, the cases of idiocy appear especially suited to show how essential that third condition in particular is. Here too it would, moreover, be easy (if experiments of such a kind were at all conceivable) to produce imbecility itself by experimental means, through influence upon the initial, in itself unconscious formation, and thus to confirm our theory; for, if in any case the artificial compression of the skulls of newborns among American savages is already unfavourable to the development of a higher consciousness, it could be asserted with certainty that an all-sided forcible restriction of the growth of the brain (somewhat after the manner of the restriction of the growth of the feet among the Chinese) would always and just as necessarily bring about idiocy, as dullness, imbecility, and finally the fading of consciousness arise when in later years softening of the brain or hydrocephalus of the brain has developed.

[p. 445] If we now consider all this, it remains remarkable enough that, while in the case of mania as well as idiocy the fact could not be denied that in these two endpoints of the series of mentally diseased states the root of the evil lies in the unconscious — or, as one usually says, in the corporeal — in the other afflictions standing in the middle, that is, in the various forms of madness, it was so difficult to arrive at a settled view on this matter. The abandonment of a strictly genetic path is here too the sole cause of so many errors. Indeed, it is also truly a strange appearance, one easily misleading even the observer himself — that of a soul caught in madness! All the processes of the unconscious life seem at times, to superficial observation, in perfect order: the person breathes, nourishes and expends himself like a healthy person; his pulse beats the right measure; and likewise whole provinces of the mental realm seem in perfectly lawful condition. Thinking and feeling appear undisturbed in many directions; the spirit cognizes, draws conclusions, judges; it often devises definite plans with great acuteness and can even prove itself with consistency in carrying them out in action. But suddenly, in other directions, something entirely alien, something irreconcilable with all sense of truth, something decidedly morbid, now emerges all at once! The actual middle member of this series, monomania or the so-called fixed idea, is among these manifold forms the especially characteristic one and deserves above all to be examined when the question concerns the nature of these states. However, just as in the soul, which rests through and through upon a unity, there can be no talk of anything merely local, truly sharply separated, merely partial, so too the more precise observation of every such case of monomania shows that by no means is only one ray of the psyche diseased and all others healthy, but that in truth these conditions too are conditioned by a peculiar idea of disease that manifests itself in all this life and only becomes more clearly apparent on one side — an idea which, being itself unconscious, can only take root in the unconscious. In these conditions, it never escapes the eye of the sharp-sighted, more deeply investigating physician that, despite the appearance of health which so manifoldly deceives the layman, a true and peculiar disease is always present here. Indeed, the entire constitution of the unconscious life-phenomena is modified in patients of this kind in a special way: the distinctive features of the face, the altered gaze, the different bearing, by which such persons strike even laymen, their often very noticeably altered odour, and their strangely fluctuating temperature, betray the condition in a manner similar to the way a hectic condition betrays itself by those circumscribed red cheeks and hot palms which the ignorant are accustomed to take as signs of the fullest health. The modality, however, of making clearer how diseases that appear so very concealed in the corporeal are yet capable of so remarkably retuning the mental — perhaps the following will suffice for this.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> [p. 446] A work which is very well suited to make such individual actual conditions vivid to the reader is the "Biographies of the Mentally Ill, by Dr. K. W. Ideler." Berlin 1841.

[p. 447] First and foremost, one must necessarily recall how remarkably every ray of conscious life is indispensably bound to a certain formation pre-shaped by the unconscious working of the idea. Only through the fact that the most delicate nerve substance of the brain has developed in primitive cellular mass and primitive fibres is an essential condition of all consciousness given, and no thought, no feeling, no stirring of the will can occur in the spirit that would not be connected with some fine retunings in the tension of the innervation currents of these substances. One could almost say: just as the galvanometer, in the degree to which it shows the magnetic needle deflecting, indicates the strength of the electric current in the galvanic apparatus, so in the soul that has attained consciousness every single stirring of the spirit is the intellectual side of some particular change of state in the nervous apparatus of the brain. These relations, I say, one must keep in mind in order to comprehend how definitely certain morbid states of the unconscious must mirror themselves in consciousness in a peculiar way, or must resound there, through the brain formation! Indeed, it can contribute to the clarity of these altogether difficult representations if one remembers how the sense-sensations in the larger sense organs remain bound in just the same way most intimately to the condition of their nervous structures. What extraordinarily fine processes in the distribution of the auditory nerves may it be, for instance, which we sense as ringing in the ears! And likewise it is the finest retunings in the retina of the eye and its innervation tension that, as so-called ocular spectra, produce sense-representations to which no external action of light at all corresponds. Far more delicate still, however, and entirely beyond the reach of anything we call weighable and measurable, one must regard those processes, in themselves unconscious, in which we must presuppose the corporeal condition of every mental stirring! Here too, these matters previously discussed at sufficient length are to be returned to only insofar as is necessary to make clear how any disease, if it has produced in certain provinces of those elementary nerve substances particular lasting retunings, must at once also bring about in a certain way lasting retunings in mental stirrings. Roughly in the same way as the eye, in whose retina a perhaps ever so small transformation has taken place, always and always sees certain colours, certain figures, granular serpents, floaters [mouches volantes], and the like, so it is clear how for a human individuality in whose unconsciously forming brain-life certain lasting alterations have taken place, certain representations corresponding to them can always and always be brought forward again, so that thereby healthy, pure, voluntary thinking must appear just as hindered as, in another way, such thinking would not be possible if through continual external noise and agitation certain alien representations were constantly forced upon the spirit.

[p. 448] Only from this standpoint will one then be able to understand to some degree how all those peculiar states are produced which not infrequently occupy in a remarkable way the middle ground between erroneous thinking and the mentioned deceptions of the sense organs. To the latter, of which we cited as examples the ocular spectra and the ringing in the ears, what is known as "hallucinations" comes closest: the hearing of alien voices (a ringing in the ears of the brain, as it were; figuratively one could also call all madness so), the seeing of phantasms, and so forth. To erroneous thinking — that of the healthy person who had merely given his train of thought a wrong direction, from which, however, illuminating, clarifying representations can soon convert him — the monomania, or so-called "fixed idea," on the other hand approaches through manifold transitions from both sides, and even within the latter a distinction can again be made as to whether the spirit predominantly surrenders itself to diseased feelings or whether it strives after confused, wretched actions. Examples of the former are religious monomania, false love-madness (erotomania), the monomania of representations of fear and persecution, and so forth. Examples of the latter are the monomania of stealing, of murder, of sexual lust, and so forth. How much, moreover, all these forms of disturbance of the spirit are to be regarded and understood precisely in this way alone also emerges from the consideration of their often so sudden termination; for those strange delusions which pursue a person often for years and could not be dispelled by any rational persuasion however reasonable — and could not be dispelled because for the patient they did indeed rest upon an inner reality and truth — sometimes vanish all at once as soon as some important and essential inner change in the morbid condition undergoes a crisis. The entire effort of the physician should therefore here too be directed mainly toward bringing about such changes, restoring accustomed but now suppressed secretions, eliminating diseased states of the unconscious life — and hundreds of cases show how suddenly the clarity of the spirit often re-emerged and these cloud-images of delusion were dispelled as soon as it was possible to restore the true integrity and norm in the unconscious formative life of the brain substance. Must one not also, moreover, by looking back from these diseased states to the healthy,

[p. 450] immediately and completely convince oneself that the former cannot possibly be rooted in the spirit itself, in the consciousness of the soul! The spirit is, as it has itself become — that is, as it has through thinking attained the eternal present — also at the same time the only thing in itself certain, and thus one also calls this most solid thing in the spirit the conscience [Gewissen], and it is precisely this inner certainty that utterly contradicts the notion that from the spirit itself a state of derangement could arise; for that is precisely the difference between error and derangement — that the former always gradually vanishes of itself, or through given illumination vanishes at once, whereas derangement, because it is rooted in the unconscious, can only disappear when the unconscious itself has once again become normal, either in general or at least insofar as it is the organ of the spirit. May what has been said suffice to give an overview of the diversity of the individual forms of these disease-phenomena of the spirit, and let us now cast a glance at the remaining moments important for our purpose! First, the manner of their more frequent or rarer occurrence in certain souls may come into question. In general, it has already been remarked above that the sinking of the spirit into error and wild action and feeling does indeed increase the disposition to these disease-phenomena, in any case because, working back from the conscious upon the unconscious, the formative life of the brain itself is already led astray by such false mental activity, and that therefore comparatively among dull, crude, uneducated natures more mentally ill persons must occur than among more highly educated and more nobly developed ones. On the other hand, however, it is also remarkable that only on the condition of a certain degree of mental development do the disease-phenomena at the spirit emerge, and that to that extent, but also only to that extent, greater culture does indeed favour the increase

[p. 451] of mental diseases. In tender childhood there is for this very reason only one form that makes itself noticeable early, and that is precisely the one in which, owing to a disturbance of the unconscious formative life in the brain, no mental development at all becomes possible — imbecility. Just as little can disease-phenomena at the spirit be observed in the crude savage, and all this indicates that without a certain height of mental development the reflex of a disease of the unconscious upon the conscious cannot occur — which is also very natural, for in order that something may undergo any change, it must first of all exist. We can after all observe precisely the same thing in the sphere of entirely unconscious life: thus pulmonary diseases tend to develop only at an age when the development of the respiratory organs has been completed, diseases of the sexual organs tend to appear only after puberty, and so forth.

These considerations lead us further to the origin of the disease-phenomena of the spirit, and here a moment must immediately be mentioned which very clearly demonstrates their rootedness in the unconscious, namely the so frequently observed heredity of these conditions. It is not uncommon to find families in which from generation to generation several persons always succumb to this deplorable condition, and naturally this too would not be possible insofar as that unconsciously developed formation, which is precisely the first thing that can be transmitted from parents to children, did not ultimately always contain the essential cause of them. The free action of the spirit belongs to each individual as such, but the unconscious development of one's organic existence is not something one has given oneself. The remaining moments that can condition the origin of mental diseases lie partly in the conscious, partly, and essentially in the last analysis always, in the unconscious sphere; for upon closer reflection it will immediately be evident to everyone that in an individual whose unconscious life is perfectly healthy, under occasioning external circumstances and through a false voluntary direction of conscious life, the most violent agitations of feelings and aberrations of cognition and will of many kinds may indeed occur, but never what we quite properly call disease-phenomena at the spirit.

[p. 452] In what way aberrations of the conscious life of the soul (or so-called moral diseases), and especially by means of violent emotional agitations, can occasion mental diseases has already been pointed out earlier. What it is, on the other hand, by which disease-phenomena at the spirit can be brought about from the unconscious or so-called corporeal side — this belongs too much to the province of the physician for it to be considered here at length. Only quite in general let it therefore be noted that all moments of this kind must have reference, either directly or indirectly, to the formation and life of the brain. It is therefore indeed something very frequent to find, through autopsy, peculiar and manifold malformations of the brain not only in idiots but equally in incurably insane persons. Where, however, such malformations truly cannot be found, it is either because the disturbance of the formative relations is so fine that it escapes the senses (and this, in an organ whose microscopic structure is still for the most part a riddle and whose most important life-phenomena — that is, the retunings of the innervation tension — can no more be made visible than the magnetic current in a piece of iron, will occur most frequently), or because the brain-life was only indirectly affected. The explanation of the latter is given by the following. Since the brain has the significance of forming the hearth where all primitive nerve fibres converge at the highest point, disturbances of even the most remote organs, or, as one may better say, subordinate life-circles, necessarily make themselves felt in that hearth at once and everywhere. At the same time, one must especially recall the different psychic signature of these individual life-circles, draw attention to how the life-circle of the biliary organs, the organs of circulation, of sex, of digestion, and glandular life each has its special significance in psychic respects, and how in particular the world of feeling receives its distinctive colouring only from this manifoldness — and through all this it will become more and more clear how from these regions indeed the strangest disease-phenomena at the spirit may well come about. Whoever in this regard has reflected more extensively on what was earlier remarked in the history of the feelings and in the doctrine of the influence of the unconscious upon the conscious will also here more easily arrive at clear representations, and it will be evident to him how a prolonged illness, and especially actual malformations — of the liver, for instance — by virtue of the altered innervation tension of those primitive fibres which place the biliary organs in rapport with the brain, may well occasion a sensation of genuine illness in that cerebral structure which directly belongs to the life of representation; likewise, how bad, morbid proportions of the fluid mass, since innervation itself is through and through a constant result of the life of the blood, must everywhere, and hence also in the brain, develop an innervation that is not healthy and normal, and which must necessarily be reflected in consciousness as a morbid mood. (Hence in chlorotic girls derangement and monomania occur so frequently, and so forth.) Always, therefore, the more such dissonances in the unconscious life have taken place which are reflected in the brain, the sooner will disease-phenomena at the spirit come about, and naturally this effect will occur all the more readily when to a certain inborn, abnormal condition of unconscious life an accidentally acquired one is added.

[p. 453] [p. 454] To what extent, moreover, a ground for the origin of madness may also lie in the unconscious life of the soul has likewise already been indicated earlier. In general, one will not be able to deny that here too the side which contains more of the unconscious — that is, feeling — is also capable of becoming a more essential disease-moment; for every direction of feeling, be it joy, sorrow, love, or hatred, as soon as it appears at its ultimate height, gives a state of the soul which, by its exclusiveness and, as it were, its annihilating character, stands on the line of madness — just as the highest spiritual inspiration does — and which only then and insofar distinguishes itself from madness as it contains truth within itself. Where this is therefore not the case, where feeling is not moved by something truly appropriate to the soul, where accordingly from this side already inner certainty and truth are lacking, there all the more easily will a falling ill of the unconscious life influence the conscious, and the soul will at once cross that narrow boundary between emotional agitation and madness, so that genuine disease-phenomena at the spirit are immediately produced. In this way one sees, proceeding from emotional agitations, a great many cases of madness develop; yet it must not be overlooked that from the side of cognition and of the will as well, the disposition to madness can be given, for on the one hand a long-continued, frequently repeated exertion in pursuing a certain train of thought (for example, mathematical problems) will ultimately just as surely disturb and make diseased the process of innervation in the brain, as the prolonged outstretching of the arms of Indian holy men causes the muscles and tendons of the arms to atrophy; and on the other hand, the complete abandonment to a certain willing and doing can likewise lead to madness — yet always only because it is such a doing as also makes the unconscious fall ill, by not granting it the protection and vigilance which, as we said above, the conscious owes to the unconscious. Thus, for example, early sexual excesses frequently establish idiocy, and thus the asylums are filled with madmen who represent the sad victims of habitual drunkenness. In both cases it is always precisely demonstrable how, through certain conscious actions, the unconscious became diseased in general and especially in certain systems, and how from there the brain-life was bound to find itself shaken.

[p. 455] If we now further direct our gaze at the duration of these disease-phenomena at the spirit, the general statement to be made is that, for the most part, they extend over longer periods of life. There is indeed also brief, truly acute derangement — namely, that which occurs with violent fevers in brain inflammations, in opium intoxication, and so forth; but since here the illness always predominantly runs its cycle in the unconscious, one customarily, though quite unjustly, does not reckon these cases among the so-called mental diseases at all, although through such a separation no true advantage could ever accrue to science, since all disease actually always lays claim to life as a whole, only now one side, now another more prominently, and since the history of individual diseases will only be completely understood after this recognition. We would thus, with respect to duration, have to distinguish from the madness of one intoxicated by hemp-drink or opium smoke, scarcely to be measured in hours, to the somewhat longer, still acute madness of the one suffering from nervous fever, to be counted in days, up to the madness of the fully deranged, lasting years. As stated, the latter kind is on the whole the more frequent, because quite fine dissonances in the unconscious of the brain and nerve life, which must inevitably be present wherever what is commonly called mental illness is to emerge, will mostly be of a chronic nature; indeed, if these dissonances have advanced to truly significant malformations of the substance, they may well extend over an entire human existence and ultimately condition a lifelong duration of derangement. All unconscious life, however, moves according to certain secret laws of rhythm and periodicity, and so we shall also in its diseases everywhere observe similar lawful movements, and for this reason the duration of any derangement must also be measured entirely according to such unconsciously traversed periods. Just as mists fall, so those strange derangements often suddenly fall away from the circle of representations, and all at once the spirit again freely exercises itself in that measure of representations and in that energy which were proper to it before the illness.

[p. 456] If we therefore rightly grasp all this, it will now also at once become clear to what extent mental diseases can end in such entirely different ways. As the first thing, however, to be noted about the various outcomes of this illness, and what clearly follows from the peculiarity and power of the spirit considered earlier, we must adduce the recognition that the morbid condition of the spirit in and of itself never becomes, nor can become, fatal. This simple truth too has, like so much that relates to these strange conditions, never yet been definitely stated, and not infrequently the expression is used that a mental disease has killed a patient, although the natural feeling of peoples has always rightly guided language insofar as one never used to say that a person died of madness, but always only that he died in madness. The truth of the matter is that in such cases death proceeds only from the unconscious life, as in all other diseases, and this recognition also leads again to the perception of a new and peculiar law, namely: that just as life always begins with the unconscious manifestation of the soul, so it can also end only with this unconscious manifestation, and in the natural course of things will also always end only thereby (for when the conscious spirit in suicide intentionally brings about the conditions that destroy the unconscious, this is always a relation contrary to nature). If it therefore happens that the mentally ill die, this will occur either because some special disease — a fever, an inflammation, an apoplexy, and so forth — supervenes, or because the same suffering of the unconscious which, as an illness of the brain, had cast its reflex upon the processes of the spirit, intensifies to such an extent that now all other life-phenomena must be impaired thereby and death is occasioned in the form of hydrocephalus, paralysis, and so forth. Just as the dying of the mentally ill thus proceeds from the unconscious, so necessarily the second possible outcome — that of recovery — must also be essentially conditioned by the unconscious. An understanding that has fallen into error can find within itself the path to truth or be led to it; a will that has sunk into wild, vicious action can, touched by the voice of conscience, abandon this course, or be improved by education; but for the madman there is no path to recovery in this manner. Only the deviation that proceeded from consciousness can also be led back to the right through consciousness, and this too points once again to the fact that the root of the disease-phenomena at the spirit lies absolutely outside the spirit — that is, in the unconscious.

[p. 457] [p. 458] Recovery can therefore in these diseases, as in all others, come only from twofold sources: first, from the inner, unshakeable divinity of the idea of the individual in general, which persistently and with constant power strives to restore the peculiarity of life, the normal; and second, from the course of the disease disturbing that normal, which, like all temporal life, is a finite one — that is, must unwind itself in certain periods and then expire. It is the cooperation of these two moments which, sometimes supported and furthered by a purposeful, intentional influence from without, in the fortunate case allows the spirit to return to its full clarity — a process which can be most clearly and simply surveyed in those acute cases cited as opium intoxication or fever-madness. Here, where the corporeal illness so utterly predominates, the moments which condition the fading of those benightings of the spirit can be distinguished with more clarity. One recognizes more distinctly the periods in which the disease withdraws and subsides, and in the same measure the light of the spirit re-emerges. Nevertheless, the history of recovery from madness lasting years will in essence be no different from the recovery from those acute cases, except that here in months what there transpires in days.

[p. 459] As a third possible outcome of the disease-phenomena at the spirit, mention must finally be made of the transition into other diseases. This is a pure metaschematism. Just as the physician often observes that, for instance, an asthma disappears when gout appears in the joints, or just as certain other chronic ailments vanish when a fever breaks out, so too a disease which also burdened the organs of thought can change its form in such a way that now other provinces of life become the seat of the suffering, and one indeed also observes in this way that changes of form of the illness emerge in which, for example, skin inflammations, articular gout, discharges of blood, and so forth, suddenly release the captivity of the spirit and let it work again in its integrity, although the unconscious life nonetheless continues to remain diseased on the whole. One sees, then, that this outcome of the illness, which is not at all rarely observed in the insane, psychologically reveals no new side of the condition, that it rather likewise only points back to the fact that all disease-phenomena at the spirit are rooted in the unconscious, and that thus these processes deserve a special study in practical respects more for the physician than for the psychologist. Now, after all the preceding considerations, it will also emerge of its own accord what is to be said here about the possible cure of madness. The most important question, which has much occupied alienists and which could never reach a proper decision as long as the actual nature of these states themselves remained unclear, is: "whether the cure is to be achieved through direct influence upon consciousness, or through influence upon the unconscious?" The former procedure was called the purely psychic one, and it was to confine itself to a kind of pedagogy; the other was the purely medical one and comprised a medical treatment as in all other diseases. Whoever has followed us with attention in the investigations set forth will find the decision on this matter not difficult. As was already remarked above, the purest and sharpest scientific deductions about the erroneousness of the trains of thought pursued in such states avail the madman nothing at all; one may try to convince a madman with the best reasons of his madness, and he will never allow himself to be convinced, for he is truly in his own way in the right; he can only argue as he does — and this too confirms the far less absolutely firm nature of cognition, of which there was already mention in the history of cognition. Just as, therefore, every appeal to conscience must also remain futile in these cases, because truly something alien, something grounded in the unconscious, holds the spirit captive and forcibly pushes it in another direction. It thus becomes clear that the first and essential task of such a cure must always be for the physician to penetrate as deeply as possible into the mysteries of the unconscious life of the patient, to make clear to himself in what directions the peculiar life of the disease, developed there, has drawn its threads ensnaring the healthy like an uncanny web, and then to endeavour to dissolve these threads and to counteract this alien element in an appropriate manner. Just as fever-madness vanishes when the fever itself is overcome through appropriate curative measures, just as the confused thoughts of opium intoxication disappear when the effect of the poison has been neutralized by suitable antidotes, so chronic mental disturbances often vanish at once when the hepatic-portal congestions, decompositions of the blood, or suppressions of accustomed secretions conditioning them have been remedied. To be sure, in many cases it is infinitely difficult to discover the fine threads to which the first seeds of disease attach themselves; indeed, even if they were found, they often lie in such depths of the organisation that the curative actions of the physician cannot directly reach them; and finally, in still many more cases, malformations have set in within the organ of thought itself that can in no way be removed — reasons enough to make comprehensible why those unfortunate states so often mock the physician's efforts. That nevertheless a continued influence upon consciousness can also contribute to furthering the cure is very natural and not difficult to understand. Just as an instrument, through an unskilled player who now here, now there strikes a note that sounds inharmonious with the others and improperly sets the other strings vibrating, is easily put out of tune, so too a disordered, wandering thinking, by also working detrimentally upon the in itself unconscious brain-life, can give occasion for madness; for this very reason, however, a restraining of the deranged spirit, insofar as it is at all capable, to think in an ordered manner must also have a beneficial and healing effect, and thus certainly everything that promotes ordered thinking, feeling, and willing in the insane will work as an essential moment for facilitating the cure, even though it can never in and of itself effect the cure. One can, however, concede in general that since the identification of precisely that illness in the unconscious which casts its reflex upon consciousness is so difficult, and often the medical remedies can scarcely reach there, it may hardly be subject to doubt that on the whole far more cures of deranged and still curable conditions are brought about by nature alone — that is, only through the salutary, ever quietly norm-restoring striving of the unconscious — than by art, and that

[p. 460] [p. 461] [p. 462] therefore indeed in many cases the arrangement of a regimen relating to conscious life that is perfectly appropriate will remain one of the most essential tasks of the physician. But let these considerations suffice, the further pursuit of which belongs entirely on the ground of psychic medicine. The actual "organon" of these doctrines seems to me to be hereby given. It is important, however, that we here make clear to ourselves in what relation these diseases stand to the innermost further development of the soul. In the section of this work in which we took into consideration the important question of the growth of the soul, we had arrived at the momentous result that only through the emergence of consciousness in the soul — that is, only from the appearance of the spirit onward — does a growth, or also a regression, of the fundamental idea of our existence become possible. How essentially detrimental, therefore, every condition which makes a free development and further cultivation of the spirit impossible, or at the very least significantly impedes and delays it for a longer time, must work upon the growth of that mysterious monad in which we must recognise the fundamental condition of every stirring of the soul, follows from this without further ado. In the states commonly called disturbances of the soul or mental diseases, the free will, free thinking, the truly free action of the spirit is no longer possible, and thus representations and thoughts, feelings and desires constantly revolve in one and the same circle. The reason why this freedom is no longer possible lies in the fact that the conscious life of the soul is held, as it were, under a spell by a reflex that the unconscious has cast upon the spirit; but that it is so — that something alien, not an idea that has emerged from the conscious spirit itself, has laid this spell over all radiations of the spirit — is of the most essential influence upon the result that a disease of this kind must have for the condition of the innermost monad of the soul, a result whose peculiarity emerges all the more clearly when we compare it with the result of merely corporeal disease, or with the free aberrations of the spirit that are figuratively called moral diseases. In the latter two, a peculiar development proceeds freely from the interior of the soul itself; a certain striving, even if abnormal, is nevertheless carried through in its own peculiar way with strength and resolution, and the consequence is that when such a condition has finally run its course and concluded, the soul feels itself purified, refreshed, and strengthened — in the one case in its conscious, in the other in its unconscious life. How often do we see that after a regularly run, even very violent, febrile disease the person appears, as it were, rejuvenated and newly strengthened, that small or even larger abnormalities with which the organism had perhaps long been burdened are completely lost after such a storm, so that one can say in truth that the disease has heightened the health. Likewise, it is an old saying that "a sinner who repents is worth more than many righteous" — that is, that any error however grave, or the losing of oneself in however reprehensible an action, if the inner magnetic needle returns to its true meridian and the abnormal pursuit has truly been cast off from regained, pure consciousness, thereby at the same time strengthens the energy of the innermost being and promotes a true growth of the soul. The same significance therefore also attaches to Goethe's words: "Error stands in the same relation to truth as sleep to waking. I have observed that one turns from error, as if refreshed, back to the true." Such purification and

[p. 463] [p. 464] refreshment, however, by no means resides in the wretched, hybrid condition of mental illness. Just as this can already be perceived in the most swiftly passing, most acute mental disturbance that we call "intoxication" — that it, far from leaving the spirit elevated and strengthened, tends rather to leave behind a duller, benumbed state — so this holds also for the longer, chronic, so-called mental diseases, and we recognize from this that they are in and of themselves entirely hostile to the growth of the idea. For this very reason, persons who have been mentally ill for a longer time usually retain, even when they may be considered as restored, a certain aftertaste of their former diseased condition; and whether in the most terrible of these diseases, in complete imbecility, that frightful sinking in the energy of the idea itself must not gradually set in which we designated, in the case of moral depravity, as a depth from which no elevation is any longer possible (no redemption from the nether world [nulla redemtio ex infernis]) — who will vouch for this! Consoling in this regard must therefore be what we recognized in the earlier considerations about the peculiar power of a more highly developed spirit: on the one hand, that at a certain height of consciousness it becomes more and more free from the power of the unconscious altogether and of the diseased in particular; and on the other hand, that a certain height in the growth of the energy of the idea makes an ensuing decline just as impossible as, at a certain depth, the renewed ascent can be. And at these norms let it suffice in order to develop for oneself in detail the further considerations regarding the influence of mental diseases upon the growth of the idea.

[p. 465]

## III. Von Dem, was im Unbewußten und Bewußten der Seele vergänglich und was darin ewig ist — III. On What Is Perishable and What Is Eternal in the Unconscious and Conscious of the Soul

Of all questions that have been raised in the field of the science of the soul, none has occupied the inquirers more, indeed moved humanity more, than that concerning the eternal in the soul.
