A presentiment, a certain belief that here something eternal is truly present, is deeply rooted in humanity; but for all belief in it, the longing for decisive certainty remained unsatisfied, and the representations of it actually always only reflected back the respective state of culture of the individuals. Almost, just as we may say that the human being most manifoldly created his God in his own image, so he created the representation of the eternity of his soul according to the development of his spirit. From the crudest, most massive representations of states in which all externalities of the soul were to be permanently preserved, to the most sublime refinement, indeed almost volatilisation, of every distinctive soul-existence in eternity, the thoughts of human beings wandered restlessly from one mode of representation to another. In any case, if the possible is to be gained from the subject, we must above all seek to establish what the concept of the eternal is in general and what we are to understand by the expression "eternity." It is not un- Carus, Psyche.

[p. 466] important to attend first to the word itself in its origin and its root. The root-word, however, is Ewa, and remarkably this already includes, alongside the concept of infinite duration, the concept of "law" (whence also the word Ehe [marriage]). Already through this linguistic form we are thus led to recognize that "eternal" can only be predicated of that which, like the concept of law, does not so much have a real, sensibly appearing existence, but rather of that which is something thought — something abstract. Thinking further, we then also truly find before long that everything that becomes — that is, that in some way begins, arises — can by its nature be transformed and changed, and therefore appears only in an existence conditioned by time, and as such may not be called an eternal but only a temporal thing. We thus arrive at the recognition of the truth that in general not that which "becomes," but only that which truly "is," may lay claim to the predicate of eternity.

What therefore truly "is," what entirely withdraws itself from the constant, absolutely restless flight of past and future in which all so-called reality is enclosed, and persists in a true and infinite present — only of this can we say that it is eternal. Eternal in this sense is accordingly, first of all, only the highest divine mystery itself, and eternal insofar are the emanations of this mystery, the ideas; and becoming as such — that is, the infinite, constant manifestations of the idea in the aether, or that which becomes purely and in general. Everything, on the other hand, that becomes in particular, that in some way first arises in time, that only in time can become something real, something other, bears for this very reason the seal of transience upon it.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> W. Graff, Old High German Thesaurus, vol. 1, p. 506.

[p. 467] After we have thus recognized what being eternal means in general and wherein the eternal differs from the perishable, we may now also venture to examine more closely what is eternal in the human soul. In all the preceding considerations it has become clear to us that the essential ground of the soul had to be called a divine archetype of its being — an idea — of all its being. That the soul, therefore, insofar and to the extent that it is precisely such a divine archetype, must be eternal, is thereupon immediately clear: for the simplest reflection shows us that to an idea — be it one of the highest or one of the least — the concept of time finds no application at all. One need only consider, for instance, the idea, or, as it is called there, the law of a mathematical formation — for example, the idea of the triangle — and it immediately becomes evident that, although all conceivable and actually realized triangles must necessarily be perishable, the idea, the law of this formation, has absolutely nothing to do with transience, with time at all — that it is eternal. Now, however, the soul that manifests itself in a human being is, as everything earlier has shown, by no means merely some general idea like the idea, or the law, of a simple mathematical formation; rather, it is always the idea of precisely this particular and absolutely distinctive personality; for all that is accidental and external in life may indeed participate in the further development of such a personality, but it can ultimately be created only by the idea thought precisely so, and only so, in the highest divine mystery. From this it then follows without further ado that the fundamental idea of a human soul is not merely eternal as an idea in general, but that in the above the cause of the eternality of the essence of precisely this particular soul, different from all other souls, is given.

[p. 468] That accordingly the predicate of eternity belongs to the innermost essence of the soul, and not merely to this essence as a soulless and characterless divine archetype of the human being in general, but precisely as the archetype of this particular human individuality, can thereupon no longer be subject to any doubt. Before we then think further, however, about how matters stand with the perishable in the soul and in what relation this stands to the eternal, we must enter more closely into a subject which has hitherto been entirely passed over in philosophy and psychology, and which yet precisely here requires detailed appreciation, because only on this ground will the question of the relation of the perishable to the eternal be truly answerable. This subject, however, is the essential difference which must necessarily dawn upon the more deeply thinking spirit between two great infinite series of divine ideas in general. Where the growth of our soul was discussed, it was already shown in detail that in the innermost essence of the higher soul destined for consciousness and attaining consciousness — that is, in the divine idea conditioning its being — there is by no means an everlasting eternally-self-equal state, but rather, as soon as its free consciousness has developed, a constant progression, a certain steady movement, an activity inseparable from its freedom, either approaching the primordial divine or distancing itself from it, takes place and must take place. Just as definitely, however, the spirit also allows us to recognize that there are infinite other ideas that exist and continually manifest themselves in the world, of which this activity, this freedom, this progression cannot be predicated — which, however infinitely many times they manifest themselves, that is, express themselves livingly in the reality of the world, always make themselves known only as the same, which thereby reveal a state of being bound and belong to the realm of necessity. Everything that we recognize as idea, or law, of regular formation, and all ideas that condition the becoming of infinite living yet never conscious creatures, belongs here.

[p. 469] To make this difference perfectly clear to oneself is important in every respect, but quite especially when it is a question of determining what in the conscious spirit must be recognized as eternal. We may state it thus: all the infinitely repeated manifestation of unconscious ideas can, since they never become aware of themselves, are never led to the contemplation of their own essence, also in no way benefit themselves, alter nothing in their eternal being — or, as one may also express it, from their manifesting themselves nothing specially self-perpetuating will ever remain behind. All the infinite, ever-renewed manifestation of unconscious ideas will therefore never have significance for themselves, but only for the highest eternal mystery of God — that is, for His will to express Himself always in eternal "delight of becoming" [Werdelust] — and we thus recognize what has already been expressed several times earlier, that the realm of necessity in and of itself never and nowhere admits of a progression, an inner heightening or diminishing of its divine energy. It would therefore be entirely futile to try to conceive, of any of the ideas assigned to necessity (beginning with those which appear merely as laws of mathematical formation, up to those which manifest themselves livingly in the infinite species of unconscious creatures), a further development, a becoming-otherwise, a further-determining of themselves. With unshakeable firmness into all eternity, everything here remains self-identical; never in the most infinite time or in the most diverse forms and manifestations of existence will the fundamental law of a formation, the essence of any unconscious creature, become otherwise; the concept of freedom is unknown in these regions. It is different, however, with those ideas whose significance it is to become aware of themselves, to attain self-consciousness, to create themselves once more, as it were, and thus to be capable of a greater or lesser development of the essence of their own divinity. Such an idea too will, driven by its own inner divine delight of becoming, strive to manifest its own eternal essence infinite times; it too, as something higher, includes something of the lower — that is, something of the necessity and the boundness of the ideas that eternally persist in unconsciousness — and its initial self-manifestation also appears once again as merely an unconscious one, but as<sup>1</sup><sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> The only passage of a more recent writer in which one might find such a distinction in some measure indicated, though perhaps more with the poet's own half-unconscious foresight than with the philosopher's knowledge, is given in the remarkable words in the second part of Faust, where it is said of the Mothers — that is, precisely of the archetypes, the divine images of all being, of all existence: "A glowing tripod tells you finally / That you have reached the deepest, very deepest ground. / By its light you will see the Mothers; / Some are sitting, others stand and walk, / As it may chance. Formation, transformation, / The eternal mind's eternal recreation, / Encompassed by images of all creatures — / They do not see you, for they see only phantoms." The distinction expressed here among the Mothers (archetypes) between sitting, or standing and walking ones, can philosophically only be understood by interpreting the former as the eternally self-identical ones bound in necessity, and the others as the progressive ones destined for freedom in self-contemplation. A difference whose importance one will come to comprehend more and more.

<sup>1</sup> [p. 470] This beautiful word, also first coined by Goethe, well deserves introduction into philosophical discourse. The impulse, the innermost loving aspiration of the divine, the eternal, to manifest itself temporally in ever new and infinite ways, can in no case be better designated.

[p. 471] an unconscious which, at each new self-manifestation, as we showed earlier, "Promethean-like" presages consciousness, by creating with certainty the conditions for that mirroring from which consciousness emerges and by means of which the soul is to develop itself into spirit. Only, then, when in a life-manifestation of such an idea those conditions are completely fulfilled — when with the first thought the essence of the spirit reveals itself and the spirit, in an ever more complete becoming-clear-to-itself, also ever more decisively grasps once more the eternal present of its essence — does the essential peculiarity of this idea, freedom, now also emerge, and only as a free being is it now able to rise in its divinity or to sink therein. If, therefore, the idea, eternal in itself, of an unconscious being that is never destined for and never attains consciousness — though driven by the universal delight of becoming of all that is divine, it may manifest itself livingly ever so manifoldly — never arrives at the contemplation of itself, never at the actual awareness of the eternity of its essence, one must also recognize that in it and in all its possible life-forms absolutely nothing is eternal except itself, as something existing-in-itself [an-sich-Seiendes]. Everything that it experiences in its self-manifestation — or rather (since "experiencing" [erfahren] already presupposes a knowing of oneself) unconsciously lives through — passes by in the flight between past and future, and it itself is with iron necessity eternally unchanged the same. Herein lies also the reason why the representation of a world without conscious beings raised to spiritual existence [Da-sein] and freedom [Frei-sein] is such a dreadful thought, and therein we conceive the necessity for the highest mystery itself to create free, higher beings called to the contemplation of the eternal. Indeed, one could in a certain sense say: the world itself only first becomes a world by mirroring itself in the spiritual freedom of an eternal being — somewhat as one can say: light first arises only when there is an eye that perceives it as light; or, more precisely expressed: it is not the sun that illuminates the world, but the eye! Having now in the foregoing pointed to the great and essential difference which we must recognize between infinite ideas subject to bondage and unconsciousness, and infinite ideas destined for freedom and their own consciousness, a feature common and identical to both infinite series must now also be brought out before we turn to the conclusions that may be drawn from the above for the recognition of what in the soul is perishable or eternal. What is common, however, to the ideas of both kinds is: that the one as well as the other, all eternal in themselves, are all also destined and able, by virtue of possibility, to manifest themselves temporally in infinitely manifold ways. Accordingly, the world is therefore not to be conceived as arising and existing in such a way that to each individual manifestation of any life-form a peculiar and new idea would always underlie it, and that in each such renewed appearance a new idea, multiplying itself again into infinity, would also emerge; for a representation of this kind, in which one would imagine the ideas multiplying anew with each new generation of all that becomes, would be no less contrary to reason than if one were to dare to think of a multiplication of the one Godhead merely because it ceaselessly brings itself to appearance in the world in infinitely manifold and ever new ways. At the same time, it would in truth mean raising the in itself undeniable infinity of the world to a manifold infinity (that is, to a contradiction, for of any multiplicity it can be said only once that it is infinite) if one wished to attribute to each new life-form, as a condition of its being, a peculiar, new, and yet eternal idea. Of a divine archetype it can never be conceded that it emerges in and of itself as something new, since it is understandably contrary to the essence of the eternal to think of it as something that had not existed before — which every idea would have to be thought of as, if it were assumed to have arisen from division or multiplication of other ideas. Let it therefore be made perfectly clear here once more that not merely for all unconsciously self-manifesting ideas, but also for those destined for the freedom of consciousness, the example holds which we took above from the law of a mathematical formation: that, namely, just as the law of the triangle remains one and the same although represented innumerable times, always and again, in actuality as a temporally perishable triangle, so too they, although in themselves always essentially the same, are yet able to manifest themselves infinitely many times in appearance.

[p. 472] [p. 473] In this way, then, the infinite manifoldness of the world persisting in unconsciousness first arises and perishes and arises ever again. Systems of celestial bodies with all their immeasurable creatures persisting in the night of unconsciousness, where generation upon generation is ceaselessly linked — they show only so many repeated manifestations of one and the same infinite ideas thought in God, and when the law of a plant or animal form reproduces itself immeasurably in ever new formations, the archetypes nonetheless remain the same for all eternity. In entirely the same way, however, the infinite manifoldness in the world of the conscious spirit is also to be conceived! Already what we earlier recognized concerning the eternity of each idea conditioning not merely a soul in general but each individual personality in its essence makes it comprehensible that such an idea, precisely by virtue of its eternity, cannot manifest itself livingly in one single human existence alone, but must be destined for an infinite series of forms of existence. The oldest intuitions of humanity have therefore also always received something of this recognition and reproduced it in manifold, often curious symbols and parables; but it is always the ancient Indian teachings of the infinite perfecting of the soul through infinite forms of existence that, in this respect as in many other supreme results of philosophy, have shown the truth in its most transparent garment, and it is not to be said how much more material the teachings of Greek philosophers already become, who attempted to make intuitive the eternity of the ideas destined for the possibility of consciousness in the thought of a so-called transmigration of souls. In general, one will, upon the consideration of every such attempt, become convinced that our spirit is indeed capable of recognizing the eternity of its essence in and of itself, but that it is utterly futile to try to create for oneself any particular and more detailed representation of the manner in which the fundamental idea of our soul and our spirit, in accordance with its eternity, could manifest itself outside the bounds of the present human existence.

[p. 474] After we have thus in the foregoing set forth what is in general eternal and what is perishable, after we had recognized that the fundamental idea of precisely the most distinctive individuality of the soul must necessarily be conceived as eternal, and after we had drawn attention to the enormous difference which exists between the eternally self-identical ideas subject to necessity and the ideas destined for freedom and progression, it was further necessary to bring out that an equality of these two series exists insofar as both the ideas destined for consciousness and those persisting forever in unconsciousness, by virtue of their eternal essence, can and must ever anew and infinite times manifest themselves. Only then, therefore, when all these thoughts are deeply grasped and understood in their entire content, shall we be far enough to be able to strive toward the solution of the problem concerning the perishable and the eternal of a particular human soul, and shall be entitled to hope for satisfying results. Let us therefore fix our spiritual eye with the sharpest possible focus upon the history of our own life of the soul, and let us first recall that the divine idea manifesting itself in our being is not one that under all circumstances knows of itself, that is always self-conscious, but rather that it first manifests itself as an unconscious, insofar as it, in accordance with its eternal divine archetype, first conditions and calls forth the temporal, perishable image of the body. To this extent, it is thus also in truth an eternal, but one not knowing of its eternity. If, therefore, the development of the soul were to reach only as far as the appearance in the embryonic body, in which the conditions for that self-mirroring from which self-consciousness marvellously arises are not yet given, it would stand on entirely the same level with all those ever self-identical repetitions of the self-manifestation of divine ideas from whose essence the entire unconscious world-appearance proceeds. The simile of the idea of the triangle, which infinite times conditions the becoming of a real triangle that, to be sure, because of the ever-changing conditions of appearance, will always be somewhat different from a preceding one, yet can never essentially depart from the concept of the triangle, would then hold for the manifestation of the essence of a human soul in infinite human life-forms just as well as for the appearance of celestial bodies, plants, and all other unconscious creatures — that is, here too there would always only proceed endless perishable copies of one and the same eternal, but of its eternity unknowing, archetype.

[p. 475] [p. 476] Now, however, the relation of the idea of the complete human being manifesting itself temporally is not so, but of a higher kind; it truly conditions, at a certain maturity of life, that self-mirroring and thus that self-consciousness in which the spirit can grasp the thought: "my essence is eternal." With this thought it then also steps at once out of the flight of past and future in which all other creatures are confined, and embraces within itself something abiding — a present. That such an attainment of an inner, higher present is at the same time a true return to the eternal, a special intuition of the divine, as it were a renewed living-into the divine (an inluiare [to be en-godded], as Dante very beautifully says with a newly coined word), and that for precisely this reason the energy of the idea must increase therein — of this there has already been detailed discussion in the doctrine of the growth of the soul. Now we have only to express ourselves on what special influence and what peculiar change this awakening of consciousness is capable of producing in the eternal being of the soul. If, however, we hold fast that everything that becomes is perishable and temporal, everything that truly is, eternal and imperishable, we find indeed first of all that each and every particular manifestation of the fundamental idea of our essence — whether unconscious or attained to consciousness — be this manifestation in the unconsciously formed shape of the body, or in certain particular representations of the soul, always bound to specific forms of corporeality, whatever names they may have — whether they occur as sense-impressions and their remembrance, as sound- or sign-memories (words) in which we think, as particular reflexes of the unconscious upon the conscious (feelings), or as particular determinations toward action against the external (stirrings of the will) — they all, as they come into being in time, cannot as such be eternal. Seemingly mercilessly, the highest contemplation of the soul in the spirit thus indeed strips the soul itself of a certain richness, an enormous manifoldness that served as often for the happiness as for the pain of life. The soul says to itself: everything and anything that you think, how you think, what you feel, how you feel it, what you live and as what you appear, is only a temporary manifestation and can lay claim to eternal duration just as little as it was from the beginning. After all, the earlier considerations on the conditionedness of the conscious by the unconscious already showed how precisely all particular representations of the spirit are bound to the so very perishable formation and innervation current of the brain. But when the spirit itself is at first seized by a certain shudder at this recognition, and when we cannot at first resist a certain sadness at so infinitely much that falls prey to transience, and at so much at which we felt our development had been cultivated in the course of life, there will decidedly be found a comfort, a reassurance, first in the fact that from the history of life itself we draw the conviction that everything that arose for us in existence, feeling, thinking, and willing could only have arisen because another existence, feeling, thinking, willing was there before, and that this preceding thing also perished as something temporal — thus it has always been precisely this perishing, this loss, this restless being-destroyed that was the

[p. 478] condition of every new gain, every new further development and reception, and only in this way, and only in the infinity of time, can a constant — if I may express myself so — rejuvenation of the idea become possible. A second consideration to keep in mind, moreover, is that precisely the conscious spirit, which alone can feel the pain at this constant flight of the perishable (for everything unconscious is of course also free from all pain at the constant passing of its appearance), is also precisely the one that essentially conditions, receives, and preserves the effect of this perishable upon the being-in-itself of the soul, and precisely thereby raises this perishable, if not in and of itself, yet in its consequences, to something eternal, as it were. It is therefore indispensable in this respect to recall constantly what was said earlier about the growth of the soul through life-innerization and life-exteriorization; and if we wish to make clear to ourselves in what relation the constantly changing and utterly temporal element of our conscious life of the soul in its particulars — in its manifold representations, feelings, and acts of will — stands to the deeply innermost abiding element of the idea, we need only think back upon our own psychic development. Doing so, we perceive in our interior that thousands and thousands of feelings, representations, and acts of will have absolutely vanished from our consciousness — which in our earlier life represented the means by which our soul gradually matured. We recognize all that individual as truly vanished, because even the unconsciously formed structures of the brain, to which for a time that perishable element adhered, have since, in the constant revolution of life, become

so entirely different. Nevertheless, all this individual, though in itself passed and vanished, has also not entirely vanished and been lost; for it has in truth left us behind as another. It has, just as in general the first conscious thought, and with it the spirit, arose upon a perishable formation of the unconscious, so too through its perishable element altered and furthered the growth of the imperishable being-in-itself of the idea; and we thereby receive yet another proof and example of how something can be at once perishable and yet at the same time in a certain sense eternal. But if we acknowledge this of what we had previously mourned as temporal and perishable in our soul, then at the same time, by our arriving at the conviction that all this flight of time moves upon the ground of an eternal, a powerful counterweight against such grief is undoubtedly given. If, moreover, one wishes to subject such difficult matters, with a truly collected spirit, to a longer and sharper reflection, one may come to recognize that in particular the here-discussed over-working of the perishable into the eternal — an over-working which, at least in its most general results (especially insofar as it made itself known within the developmental course of the soul itself), could never be entirely denied — has at all times been the most important reason for the fact that an unerring, deep feeling of humanity, even if still very caught in a certain childish helplessness of representations, could not be dissuaded from granting even to the most perishable expressions of the soul a certain participation in eternity. If, therefore, for example, the North American savage believed that even the perishable form of the body — which already in life constantly decays and is newly generated — would be eternalized after death and must even then still be nourished in a human manner; or if among the Greeks the departed wandered immortal in the last life-form among the blessed or the damned, and so forth — these were, as it were, only allegorical applications of that intuited recognition, while of course the science that has matured to manhood (when speaking of a being of the soul outside this existence) on the one hand, to be sure, seems mercilessly to annihilate everything that arose as newly become in the soul only in the course of life, yet on the other hand inscribes the truly eternal becoming of the soul in God with a firm and indelible stylus.

[p. 480] The difficulty for the comprehension of the spirit in these matters lies in any case mainly in the fact that, while the soul longs for the certainty not merely of its eternity in general (which it would share with all its even unconscious ideas), but quite especially and essentially for the certainty of the eternity of its self-conscious spirit, it must yet at the same time tell itself that all the representations and all the equivalents of the representations of the spirit, by which this very consciousness has developed and by means of which the soul has first become manifest to itself as spirit, must absolutely, together with the perishable appearance of the organism, fall prey to universal transience. Certainly, however, whoever has properly thought through what was said above about the self-perpetuating in its essence even of the perishable appearance at the spirit will already thereby get beyond this seeming contradiction, in that he recognizes and comprehends that in the question of the eternality of the conscious spirit it is not so much a matter of the passing reflections of what is in itself constantly changing and therefore also utterly perishable, but rather that the conviction gained about the consequences which these reflections must necessarily have for the being-in-itself of the fundamental idea of the spirit gives complete assurance that, to be sure, the kind of this consciousness can and must change, but that concerning the abiding and imperishable ground of this consciousness no doubt whatsoever can exist.

[p. 481] It is, however, not solely what was said above about the self-perpetuating of even the perishable of the soul in the essence of the self-conscious spirit from which and by which the conviction of the eternity of what is truly highest in us can grow; rather, there is a second important series of considerations which, in another direction, can and must likewise lead to this conviction, to this longed-for certainty, and this arises for us when, the question being of a being of the soul outside this existence, we endeavour to make perfectly clear to ourselves how we are to conceive the contrast of the highest degree — the eternity of a fully completed consciousness and freedom — then how we are to conceive the deepest degree of something entirely unconscious and bound within itself, and finally how we are to conceive a middle between both as the properly human relation. Here it will then emerge that that highest degree of universal, completed consciousness can only be presupposed in the highest mystery of the divine itself. This divine, whose essence can appear to our cognition always only as a great secret, as a highest universal unconscious, is in and of itself nevertheless absolutely to be foreseen as everywhere and absolutely beholding itself in each and every manifestation of its essence, recognizing itself as free and eternal. It cannot in such a being Carus, Psyche.

[p. 482] depend exclusively upon certain individual manifestations forming themselves in the aether that this divine beholds and recognizes itself; rather, it is absolutely to be foreseen that in each and every new manifestation in the aether this divine also always beholds and recognizes itself in a new way, and even though within it an eternal change of all temporal manifestations does and must take place, the highest ground of all this change is always to be sought and found only in the necessity of ceaselessly manifesting the entire infinite depth of its eternal essence in ever newly emerging appearances, without the perfection of its self-recognition being in any way endangered by this change. In every transformation of being, therefore, despite the restless annihilation of all that previously existed and despite the ever-new emergence of what never before existed in this way, an eternally clear consciousness mirrors itself without ceasing and independently of all that change; and that we are able at least to conceive of such a highest of all blessednesses is the most essential seal of that level of blessedness to which the human spirit too is called and chosen. The complete opposite to such highest self-transparency and self-consciousness and such completed freedom was then shown by all those ideas which, though by higher necessity, as thoughts in God, manifest themselves in infinitely manifold ways in the appearance of the world, yet in themselves never rise to consciousness. If we take, for instance, the idea of a certain crystalline formation, it is clear that, however thousandfold this archetype may manifest itself in infinite copies, we are never justified in conceiving it as recognizing itself and attaining freedom in consciousness, and that therefore for such an idea all those infinite manifestations of its essence will absolutely and eternally remain without any result. Between these two extremes, then, which we have just designated as the most complete unconsciousness on the one hand and the highest consciousness on the other, there lies finally as a middle the mode of being in which that divine archetype which manifests itself livingly in the form of a human soul is destined to reveal itself. A peculiar mixture of the unconscious and the conscious is thoroughly fixed and determined for it, and a predominance now of the one, now of the other, an oscillation between both, and also a constant conditionedness of the one by the other, is decidedly what must present and reveal itself in all our considerations, in every stirring of life, as quite essential to it. The unconscious in it is that which, like all unconscious ideas of the world, appears as an integral part of that eternal mystery itself, and which for this very reason will have the creative power to produce the temporal, perishable copy — the organism; (for all creating, all organic generating, can proceed only immediately from the highest mystery; to the conscious spirit there is always allotted only an artificial creation). The conscious, on the other hand, is that which, when it mirrors itself in this partial, finite creation of the body, is destined to recognize itself as something particular, as something free, as something divine. This individually conscious also shares, to be sure, in a certain measure the peculiarity of the highest consciousness — of being independent in its self-beholding from the transience and changeability of the manifestation in the created bodily form — and it proves this in that the spirit recognizes itself as one and the same despite the rushing metamorphosis of the body. The body does not cease to perish; this was already clear to Plato; and nevertheless, despite this constant dying of the organisation and its restless renewal, the one conscious spirit mirrors itself in this constant change always as

[p. 483] [p. 484] one and the same, and acknowledges only that change which, within one and the same individuality, can be given through the growth of the soul as we discussed it earlier. Here we thus have a truth, a fact deeply grounded in our spirit and making itself felt at every moment, which we must always bring forward again, to which we must always look back, if we wish to arrive at definite concepts, at complete certainty, concerning the eternity of our spirit! If we therefore now gather once more into one result what all these preceding considerations have taught us, I would say: the fundamental idea of our soul — that is, that eternal divine archetype of all our being — contains in its one being a twofold radiation of the highest primal essence, of which the one, as unconsciously creating divinity, conditions and ever again creates the restless metamorphoses of our appearance, while the other proves itself as the spirit abiding in inner constant presence, and as the freed, higher half — the blossom, as it were, of the other. The permanence of the latter mirrors itself temporarily in the constant becoming of the former, and this mirroring so presupposes the former that one must say it can emerge in actuality [actu] only under the condition of the creation of the former, although in potentiality [potentia] it is certainly always and eternally to be acknowledged as present and existing within that idea, that eternal archetype of the human being. Now, if the unconsciously creating one radiation of our essence must be presupposed as an eternal because it is a divine one and as such proves itself by its creative, ceaselessly organically creating, organism-preserving and ever-again-regenerating power, it follows that it cannot manifest itself merely once, and merely in that small, finite circle of existence which we call a human

[p. 485] life, but that from it, as an eternal, infinite further life-circles must be conditioned — life-circles of which, within the present existence, we are by no means capable of reaching any closer experience. Now, this established and clearly thought through, it further follows that that potentiality of the ever-present objectivity of the I, or in a word, this essence of the eternity of consciousness, must stand in relation to the infinity of all possible forms of appearance of the unconsciously creating in our archetype precisely as the abiding unity of our present spiritual consciousness stands in relation to all the fleetingly passing appearances of this particular bodily existence. That eternal spirit, then, which is the freer, higher, self-beholding ray of the divine in us, will necessarily experience the kind of its consciousness in very different ways, depending on the life-circles in which the unconsciously creating of its archetype manifests itself (just as already in the one form of appearance we call a human life the consciousness of the spirit is different as child, as man, and as old man); but the actual core of the I, the actual individual of the spirit, must for this very reason also in all possible forms of the self-manifestation of the idea, apart from the heightening or diminishing of energy, remain essentially one and the same in itself — precisely just as during a single life this our spirit, despite the constant transformation of our body, yet fundamentally everywhere and ceaselessly manifests itself only as one and the same, although at the inner energy of its being-in-itself very definitely experiencing a constant diminishing or increasing. It seems to me that everyone who has followed with concentration and circumspection the train of thought that has led us to this result must find that it is

[p. 486] extremely simple and clear, and leads us with such certainty that our innermost sense of truth can in no way remain in doubt about its result. Nevertheless, it is important, partly to clarify this result further to oneself in multiple ways, and partly to observe how recognitions of this kind have at all times stirred in the most diverse forms among humanity. In the latter respect, a glance back at the doctrine of the Hindu and at the Zend system is especially important, as in these the essence of the above recognition, though in manifold veiling, yet very definitely reveals itself. Whoever were able, equipped with the above recognition, to approach the sources themselves here, and did not have to rely upon the manifold later excerpts and translations, would in this regard infallibly arrive at remarkable results. This much is certain: that the Zend scriptures and Hindu doctrine establish the existence of the essence of the human soul not merely, as the later Jewish and Christian myths, after the metamorphoses of this earthly life, in death, but also before them (before its generation). A representation of what we designate by the name of the archetype of the idea appears, however, in those scriptures under the name Fravashi [Feruer]. It says in Rhode 1: "Human beings, like every other earthly creature, have their Fravashis, which were brought forth by Ormuzd in the primordial beginning of things and which, as independent beings, assisted Ormuzd in the battle against Ahriman at the beginning of the third period and secured his victory; at the appointed time these Fravashis descend from heaven and unite with a human body and live as human beings." For this reason, the word Fravashi and Oroue or Oroneetche, which means life or soul, is also used in several places as one and the same, just as Die heilige Sage des Zendvolks [The Sacred Legend of the Zend People], p. 395.

[p. 487] we too recognize in the soul the archetype, or the idea, as its most essential element. The Fravashi is, however, by no means merely the archetype of the soul, but also, as the following shows, and as it alone must be rationally conceived of an archetype, at the same time the archetype of the body. For it is further stated 1: "Under Fravashi one conceived the entire archetype of the human being also with regard to the body; one therefore also ascribed to the Fravashi, before its union with the actual body, a human form, and consequently also a body, though an infinitely fine one." In the latter there again emerges a childlike mode of representation, unable to think the entirely abstract. On the other hand, soul and Fravashi are again taken as one in that passage of the Bundahishn also cited by Rhode, where it says 2: "When a human being dies, the body becomes dust and the soul returns to heaven." In an essentially similar way, the teachings of the Hindus also regard the eternity of the divine idea which temporally manifests itself as soul. I cite the following passage from von Bohlen (Ancient India, part 2, p. 324) in this connection: "Unborn and eternal, the individual or embodied soul (Sarira) too is a part of the world-soul and emanated from it, as sparks separate from a burning, blazing flame; enclosed in the body it becomes active through the organs, as an artist takes his instruments in order to work; through the body and its organs it is likewise moved by sensations, and has through these same organs an inclination toward good or evil, which in infinitely manifold form, without God's cooperation, has existed from eternity. The affects, however, which strike the individual soul have no influence upon the supreme being of which it is a particle, just as the sun is not affected when its image trembles in the moving water; for through embodiment the particle of the world-soul isolates itself, and reunion with this takes place only after the completed wandering." In the further development of the representation of such transmigration of souls one then, to be sure, immediately encounters much that is abstruse. The soul is supposed to be clothed with various sheaths; wrapped in the finest of them it leaves the deceased, and so forth. It is only remarkable to observe with what necessity the human spirit at all times has been driven to the thought that the eternal divine idea of its essence could not possibly be destined to manifest itself livingly in the one short human existence alone. These representations are therefore also by no means peculiar to the Orient alone; everywhere, even in the dull soul of North American savages, they emerge, and von Bohlen cites a representation of this kind where he says 1: "Among some savages of North America, children's corpses were therefore buried along the highways, in the hope that passing women might catch the young souls." All this can of course not be cited here as any kind of authority, but it is always remarkable how certain great truths deeply grounded in consciousness, which to present in the greatest possible purity and sharpness can only be the task of the strictest science, manifest themselves in dark concepts and manifold symbols at every period of the life of humanity. To that extent one can then indeed also say that for the spirit longing for the certainty of recognized truth, so long as it has not yet become entirely firm in its contemplation, a kind of proof a posteriori lies in studying the manifoldly veiled forms of that recognition as they disclosed themselves at different times in other souls. Is<sup>1</sup><sup>1</sup><sup>∗</sup>

<sup>1</sup> op. cit., part 1, p. 170.

<sup>1</sup> Ibid., p. 397.

<sup>∗</sup> Ibid., p. 396.

[p. 488] [p. 489] it not even so with the greatest and most significant task of the spirit, with the recognition of a highest divine mystery! For the soul not yet fully established in itself, in God-consciousness, it is a kind of comfort and uplifting to learn in what way the recognition of God has mirrored itself now more brightly, now more dimly, in millions of other souls; for indeed there always lies in this the mysterious quality of all such recognitions, that one must soon become aware that it can always only manifest itself with a certain limitation in humanity — quite as Plato says 1: "To find the maker and father [poieta] of this universe is difficult; but having found him, it is impossible to declare him to all mankind." Let us then look with a certain joyfulness upon the testimonies of the earliest times which decidedly express the recognition of the eternity of our innermost essence; yet at the same time everything must appear doubly unsatisfying that in later times has become manifest concerning a theory which was merely endeavouring to represent what in us is born and temporally arisen as immortal and imperishable. This doctrine, which sought to vindicate for a soul that had only come into being with the generation or birth of the child an immortality, or, as one also quite illogically expressed it, an eternity, could before the verdict of a pure science of the spirit show absolutely no staying power, and could only as a dogma remain commended to faith. That what is truly to prove itself "eternal" may not have a beginning in time was bound, upon somewhat sharper reflection, to be clear in and of itself, and so there remained only the dilemma of either conceiving an unconditional eternity of infinite ideas within the highest eternal mystery, or of granting to the idea itself only a temporal existence.<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> Timaeus, Steph. 28.

[p. 490] We feel, however, at once that it is absolutely irreconcilable with what we understand by the sublime essence of the idea to conceive it in itself as merely something temporal, and so the unconditional necessity exists — since to assume a half-eternity would be something quite absurd — to acknowledge the eternity of the idea in general and also the eternity of the being-in-itself of the human soul. What more beautiful and more significant result can the human spirit, gazing into the depth of its own essence, win for itself than that there should dawn upon it brightly and irrefutably the certainty of the eternity and divinity of its innermost being, and that within this temporal attainment of consciousness it should become perfectly clear to it: the possibility of an infinite development through ever-new emergence of a distinctive consciousness from the unconscious, which always sinks back again into the universal! While, moreover, in this sense and at this depth we grasp the secret of the eternity of the being-in-itself of the human soul, another essential recognition also comes to meet us, as it were, of its own accord — namely, that this eternal, once it has again cast off the form of a temporal life, must in its quite pure being-in-itself be conceived not as a conscious but as an unconscious. In the pure being-in-itself of the soul, every kind of disclosed consciousness is inconceivable — and inconceivable for this reason, because the preceding investigations have demonstrated with the most decisive certainty the conditions under whose presence alone the miracle of consciousness can disclose itself, and with whose cancellation the manifestation of consciousness must as surely vanish as the luminous pinnacle of a dome collapses when the foundation gives way and crumbles. If, therefore, these be

ditions are certainly and absolutely without doubt given and fulfilled only gradually while the idea of our essence manifests itself livingly in some particular living development, and if they are just as undoubtedly cancelled again through the annihilation of this life-form, then the being-in-itself of the idea itself must necessarily be conceived as outside all individual consciousness. If, therefore, it cannot be otherwise than that, upon the complete cancellation of its temporal self-manifestation, the idea returns purely to its being-in-itself — retaining only what it has gained or lost in immediate heightening or diminishing of the energy of this idea during its free manifestation as spirit — then one must also see and clearly comprehend that this truly eternal of the soul, this pure being-in-itself of the idea, from which it proceeds and to which it always returns, that eternal which ever again gives birth to the temporal and ever again also relinquishes it — that this, as such, may not be called a conscious but only a divine, primordial, unconscious. Nevertheless, it is clear that the divine archetype of a soul, in this its unconscious being-in-itself, must be something other and higher after a beautifully completed human life than it was before this self-manifestation, and that the same, as soon as it once more manifests itself anew in some particular life-form, will now also infallibly manifest itself in a higher sense than it could authenticate itself in that preceding human existence — and conversely. Only, as stated, all these things lie entirely outside the circle of human experience and therefore allow only the saying: they must be, but not how they must be. It is further clear that what the legends of the peoples designated in the most diverse forms as "resurrection to new life after death" is accordingly quite undeniably only the symbolic representation of the new manifestation of an idea in some new life, and indeed always emerging again from the state of pure being-in-itself, just as our own life once emerged from a similar manifestation. Even the thought of a certain "judgement upon the soul after its resurrection," equally widespread in the history of humanity, can only gain a higher reality when we consider that the manner in which the idea must manifest itself in a next-following life-form will necessarily always be conditioned by the kind of growth that was possible for it during its preceding conscious existence — that is, that a sunken idea can manifest itself only in a lesser, a risen idea only in a higher life-form in the following existence. As for that pure being-in-itself of the fundamental essence of the soul, we would of course quite vainly endeavour, within our conscious life of the soul, to find a form expressible in the equivalents of words by which it could be completely designated, and we can therefore do nothing other than acknowledge that being as an unknowing unconscious. If, however, we all already recognize the pure, healthy, dreamless sleep — that is, a state of our soul which, compared with being awake, is also a kind of non-being — as something desirable, something peculiarly blissful in itself and refreshing for the new awakening, this can serve to some extent as an intimation of what the being-in-itself of the monad of our soul, both before and after a temporal form of life, may promise and truly bestow. So much, perhaps, and no more than this, the science of the soul is permitted to intimate and express. Every attempt to extend further upon this matter in some manner of fanciful<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> [p. 492] Already Oken said (Naturphilosophie, p. 258): "Every awakening is a resurrection from the dead. A new sympathizing."

[p. 493] images can only be regarded as an aberration. Just as little, however, may one finally venture to say anything about the highest inner periodicity of the idea with respect to its awakening to manifest itself anew as soul and the duration of its then-returning state of being-sunk-within-itself as idea in and of itself. We may have presentiments that here too, just as a definite norm is set for the duration of an earthly life of the soul, some norm also exists for the ensuing being-in-itself; indeed, we may have presentiments that in the successive individual life-states of the idea, remarkable lawful sequences and progressions take place, in which it would not be impossible that the already recognizable, profoundly meaningful relations of the systems of celestial bodies might emerge as being of essential significance for these progressions. But everything, everything dissolves here into mysterious mist, and, while we may permit the poet to range freely here in his own way, it befits science to close its volumes here in modesty.

[p. 494]
